# Proverbs

# ANNOTATIONS ON PROVERBS

## A PREFACE CONCERNING THE ARGUMENT OF THIS BOOK, AND THE USE OF PROVERBS IN GENERAL

*As the author of this book is easily known by his name, so famous and frequent upon holy records, *Solomon*; and by other titles and attributes, as *son of David*, and *king of Israel*, at the beginning: so may the argument of it as easily, by the inscription it bears, of *Proverbs*: a word not unknown unto any that understands common English: of both which nevertheless (both of the author, and inscription) somewhat more shall be said afterwards in due place. In the meantime it will be worth our labor to look into the origin, and to show the right use of *Proverbs*, in general: which to do, as it ought to be done, will put us upon a consideration of differences of times, and ages of the world; and of different tempers and judgments of men; as also of different kinds, or degrees of wisdom, according to variety of times: the knowledge and consideration of which things will very much conduce, as to a right apprehension concerning the use; so, to a right judgment concerning the valuation and estimation of this particular book.*

*We will not go so far as Adam, and the originals of mankind; of which times there has been, and is yet great controversy among the learned, whether men were then wisest, and afterwards degenerated, as mankind increased: or whether that was man's infant-time in point of knowledge and worldly wisdom, which in after-ages by degrees came to ripeness of understanding: it would be besides our purpose here; and elsewhere we have handled it at large. Without any such reflection to primitive times we say, that the same country and kingdom, according to diversity of events and alteration in matter of government; of peace, or wars; of liberty, or subjection; may have, in several ages, several ebbings and flowings in matter of human knowledge, and wisdom, and differ from itself, as in outward shape and customs; so, in matter of wit, judgment, and capacity, (depending most from different education) and so in its ways, and degrees of wisdom, and understanding.*

*There was a time among the Grecians, (in whom we instance rather than other nations, because better furnished of ancient records concerning them, than concerning other nations:) a time, I say, when all wisdom (as is observed somewhere by *Aristotle*) consisted in parables and sentences: and to be the author of a wise saying, was enough to purchase a man in the world both admiration and authority. A sentence, and an oracle were then thought to have great affinity, and ordinarily entertained with almost equal honor and reverence. Either none but those that were of high rank and place, took upon themselves (of which more upon chapter 1, verse 1) to speak sentences: or if any others did, they were reputed worthy of greatest honors. Some sentences were consecrated in temples, and attributed to the gods, because they were thought to surpass the wisdom of mortal men. So was that famous Γνῶθι σεαυτόν, Nosce teipsum: or, *know yourself*; of which so much has been written by the ancients. Others were engraven upon statues, or pillars of stone, erected of purpose, and set in highways, that travelers, as they passed by, might at the same time, both learn, and admire. Such were those called Ἑρμαῖ, mentioned by *Plato* in one of his dialogues, erected by *Hipparchus*: whereof one had this saying, Στεῖχε δίκαια φρονῶν; that is, *think of righteousness as you go along*; or, *practice righteousness in your life and conversation*. Another, Μὴ φίλον ἐξαπάτα, that is, *deceive not your friend*. And it is observable, that such an opinion this *Hipparchus* had of those his sentences, that to every one of them, as ancient painters and statuaries to some of their choicest pieces were wont, he did add his name: Μνῆμα τόδ᾽ Ἱππάρχου*, *Μὴ φίλον, etc. Which was not done by him only, but by some others in the same kind: as by *Phocylides*, who lived much about that time; not the author of that Ποίημα Νουθετικόν, now extant under that name: a very choice piece otherwise, we confess; but not of that antiquity: but another *Phocylides*, of whom there remains but little; to wit, some few sententious distichs, here and there, scattered in ancient authors; who also bear witness of him, that to every such distich his manner was to prefix his name, in these words, Καὶ τὸ δὲ Φωκυλίδου: *this also is Phocylides his verse, or, sentence*. By which does appear the simplicity of that age, compared with later times. *Synesius*, a very responsible author, cites *Aristotle*, (but not to be found, I doubt, in what now remains of *Aristotle*:) as, saying, that Proverbs, were ἐγκαταλείμματα: that is to say, *the relics, or remains*, of that ancient wisdom and philosophy, which through public broils and confusions of nations and kingdoms, (such as we have seen; and I pray God, we do not see the same fruits:) was perished. However, if that passage of *Aristotle* be not now extant, yet by many that are, it does appear how much he has adscribed to ancient Proverbs. Himself had made a collection of old Proverbs and sentences, as *Diog. Laertius* in his life, does report. In the second book of his Rhetorics, he treats of them largely: and in the first book, where he treats of testimonies and evidences; he gives Proverbs the next place after oracles. But that which is much more wonderful is, the power and efficacy such sentences and parables (for they were often joined) had, to persuade men; as either to reclaim them from some vicious course, or purpose; or to allay passions of either anger or sorrow, or the like; whereof there be divers examples in ancient histories: insomuch as some maintained, (of which subject *Seneca* has written one epistle, or two,) that no other kind of philosophy was needful, (as particularly, no *exhortations*, which we call now, *sermons*;) to persuade men to goodness, but this kind, which they had known to be so successful and available in former times. But now was the case much altered, in *Seneca*'s time, and long before: mundo jam ad summam solertiam perducto; as he speaks somewhere: when moral philosophy, through continual use and exercise, was become so popular, that every fool, or varlet, (yea every *carnifex*, as is said in the comic) could utter in ordinary talk more sentences, than formerly the wisest philosophers, or statesmen in their most serious discourses. A man would have thought the world, in point of life and morality, through this improvement of knowledge, should much have amended. But it proved quite contrary. For those sentences, which once, when rare, and of difficult access, as it were, were entertained as oracles; and accordingly wrought upon the souls and minds of men; were now become a mere complement of language, and a delight of the ear only; without any influence at all on the heart; so that the very name of ἀρητάλογος, or *sentence-speaker*, became the name of a juggler, or impostor. Antiqua sapientia nihil aliud quam facienda & vitanda praecepit. Et tunc longe meliores erant. Postquam docti prodierunt, boni desunt, says, *Seneca*.*

*From all that has been said, hitherto; concerning these *Proverbs of Solomon* (not to speak of them as canonical Scripture, which is a common consideration; but according to human judgment,) I would infer particularly, first, that to value them to their worth, we must not consider them by what we see in these days, when learning (so far as to talk, and make a show) is become more common than silver was in *Solomon*'s days, (*1 Kings 10:21, 27*) when every peddler can make a preacher; and many beardless boys out of shops, and from the plow, with little reading, and a great deal of confidence can find words enough (which some call the Spirit) to lead whole multitudes. Certainly, had *Solomon* himself lived in these days, he would have been thought in the judgment of many men but a very ordinary man, in comparison of some of these inspired tradesmen, and confident youths. Secondly, that some things are delivered with that simplicity of words, and style, as did well agree with the constitution of those times, to which they have more particular relation, both to please and to profit: though not so well perchance, with that over-ripeness of wit, and subtlety, that most men are born with in this age. But lastly, that if men could withdraw themselves a little from that φιληκοΐα, that immoderate hunting after the pleasure of the ear, (of all other senses, the most insatiable: the proper temper, shall I say, or distemper of all learned ages;) and betake themselves to more sober retired reading, with answerable serious meditation; (as necessary, as the very reading itself;) they may find the power and efficacy of these Proverbs and sentences, (even of those which at first hearing, promise but little;) as much as others have done in former ages, to the amendment of their lives, and freeing of their souls from the bondage of many unreasonable lusts and passions: than which, in very deed, no greater bondage or slavery is in the world.*

*Some things besides whoever shall read these Proverbs, had need to know beforehand. First, concerning coherence of matter and words, that they must not look for it; though it is very good sometimes, and apparent enough for divers verses together; yet it is not to be expected, as a thing not endeavored by the author. Neither indeed was it the fashion in those days, to write such things otherwise than loosely and promiscuously. It may appear, as by some others, so by *Theognis*, one of the ancientest *Gnomologues*, or sentence-writers now extant, who also lived not many ages after *Solomon*, and in many things does agree with him, as may appear to them that shall diligently compare them together. Yet of the first 9 chapters, for the most part, the subject is but one; to wit, the commendation of wisdom. And because there is not anything that does more strongly or dangerously either withhold, or withdraw from the study of wisdom, than bad company; but especially, the company of women: having said something in the first chapter of the company of bad men; he does often insist upon that of women, as the most dangerous of the two, (so he found it himself;) and does very lively and poetically set out their allurements, and the fearful end of those that are deluded by them, and fall into their nets. The last chapter contains (for the most part) the character of a virtuous woman, and a good wife. In all the rest, there is but little coherence of matter, except (as I said before) here and there of some few verses, that hang together.*

*Secondly, that some things, yea many, are often repeated. And this will bring us to a further consideration of the author. That *Solomon* is the author of this book of *Proverbs* in general, is generally acknowledged: but the author, as *David* of the Psalms, not because all made by him; but because either the maker of a good part; or collector and approver of the rest. It is not to be doubted but that many of these *Proverbs* and sentences, were known and used long before *Solomon*: so wise and understanding a nation could not be without many pithy common sayings and sentences, which are accounted the best evidence of every nation's wisdom; and though it might have many more than *Solomon* (guided by the Spirit of God) did think fit to canonize, by admitting them into his collection; yet that he would admit of none, is not a thing likely. Of them that were collected by others, as *Solomon*'s, but long since his death, from chapter 25 to 30, and then of those that bear *Agur*'s name, chapter 30, and *Lemuel*'s, chapter 31, see upon those several chapters. If not all *Solomon*'s, then but partly his, and partly collected by him, and partly by others, at several times: no wonder, if divers things with little or no alteration, be often repeated. However, *Solomon* might have some end in it, in repeating some things of purpose. We shall find repetitions in that kind in the writings of some ancients. Sed quia nimis indociles quidam, tardique sunt, admonendi videntur saepius; says one of them. Dicentibus; Quousque eadem? Responde: Ego debeo dicere; Quousque eadem peccabitis? Remedia ante vultis, quam vitia desinere? Ego vero magis dicam; & quia recusatis, perseverabo. So another.*

*A third thing very necessary to be observed, is, that there be few moral maxims or sentences, of such catholic and universal truth, (which is the privilege of mathematical theorems) as to hold always: or that the contrary should never be true. The reason whereof is well showed by *Aristotle* in his Ethics. If you will have it in the words of an ancient grammarian, that has written upon *Virgil*: Sententiae non semper generales sunt, sed interdum pro negotiorum qualitate formantur. As for example; Differ, habent parvae commoda magna morae. A little delay may prove a great advantage; says one. Semper nocuit differre paratis: delays are always dangerous; says another. Both may be true, as the matter may be stated by different circumstances of time, place, and the like. So, our Savior in the Gospel; *He that is not with me, is against me*, in one place, *Matt. 12:30*. And, *He that is not against us, is for us*, in another, *Luke 9:50*. And *Solomon* himself, in the same place, Answer not a fool according to his folly: and, *Answer a fool according to his folly*, *Prov. 26:4, 5*.*

*As for the word *Proverbs*, of the several uses and notions of it, and how here to be understood, see upon the first verse of the first chapter.*

1 Kings 4:33*, we find amongst other things recorded of *Solomon*, as an effect of his extraordinary divine wisdom, (verse 29) that he spoke 3000 proverbs, (not four thousand, as I find in some English Bibles, printed at *London*, 1620) part of which, so many as the Holy Ghost thought fit for canonical Scripture, are contained in this book of Proverbs, and Ecclesiastes; and some perhaps in the Canticles. It is not unlikely, that many of the rest are to be found in *Ecclesiasticus*: which by some ancients is attributed to *Solomon*, not only because written in imitation, as one of the prologues there does acknowledge; but also because partly taken out of this book of Proverbs, as may appear by the parallel collected and exhibited by *Cornelius a Lapide* in his *Prolegomena* upon this book: and partly (as in all likelihood) out of the remainder of those 3000 before spoken of. This *Ecclesiasticus*, or author of the book we commonly call *Ecclesiasticus*, which was written in Hebrew, and afterwards translated into Greek; lived some 500 years after *Solomon*, and some 250 before Christ, in the days of *Ptolemeus Philadelphus*: by whose procurement the Hebrew Bible was first translated into Greek, by those we commonly call the *Septuagint*: and it is thought by some learned men, that the author of this book (one *Jesus Sirach*:) was one of those *Septuagint* interpreters. The reading of *Ecclesiasticus*, and diligent comparing of parallel places, will give light to many obscure places of the Proverbs; which has made me the more willingly to take notice of the book.*

*It has been the opinion of some, as appears by the *Questions Hebraicae*, ascribed commonly to *Saint Jerome*, that all those 3000 before mentioned proverbs, were comprehended in this book of Proverbs. The words out of the said book (whoever be the author of it) to this purpose cited by *Cornelius a Lapide*, are; In Proverbiis versus nongenti quindecim continentur: in quibus etiam continentur tria mille parabolae.*

*The ancients indeed were wont to divide all books into verses, and by the number of verses to estimate their bulk and bigness; of which much has been written by later critics. But how 3000 parables can be contained in 915 verses, (taking verses for lines, as they did;) I cannot imagine; but rather believe there is an error in the copy. But if not in the copy; yet, however, an error in the man, certainly, if ever any did so believe, or write.*

## CHAPTER 1

**Verse 1.** *{The Proverbs}* Heb. *משלים* or, in construction, as here, *משלי*. The word is derived from a verb, that has two significations; both which may have relation to Proverbs, or sentences. The first signification is, *dominatus est; dominium exercuit*: that is, he has been lord, ruler; or governor. Hence sentences might be denominated, as among the Grecians, *ἀξιώματα*: *κυρίαι δόξαι*, and the like: thereby to set out the worth and dignity of moral sentences and apophthegms, tending to instruction, and direction, in matter of life and manners: as among all other subjects deserving the preeminence; so also among all men, to pass with authority, and without control. This is the common opinion; which I am not against. But it may be as probable, that sentences were at first so called, from the rank and place of the authors themselves. For in ancient times (whereof somewhat has been said in the preface:) they were for the most part governors, or such at least, as were thought fit to govern, whose sayings were received for sentences. Whence it is, as I conceive, that sentences, in general; or more particularly, choice sentences, are called *נגידים* chapter 8:6, that is, princes; properly; and 22:20, *שלישים* magnates: tribuni; that is, great men, or chief officers: as we shall say there. See also upon chapter 16:10. *A divine sentence is in the lips of the king*, etc. And Quintilian, an ancient master of rhetoric, one of the most judicious that ever wrote; where he treats of sentences, says well: *Magis enim decet eos in quibus est auctoritas, ut rei pondus etiam persona confirmet. Quis enim ferat, puerum, aut adolescentulum, aut etiam ignobilem*, etc. That is, it becomes them best (to use sentences:) that are men in or of authority, that the dignity of the person also may add some further weight to the matter. For who would endure a child, or a youth, or some obscure mean fellow, to pronounce sentences in his ordinary talk, and to take upon him the authority of an instructor, or teacher? The Grecians also called sentences, *γνώμας*, *quod similes sunt consiliis*, (I think he meant *scitis*: for so is the word *γνώμη* used:) *aut decretis*: as the same Quintilian. Certain it is, that anciently many were the authors of sentences, who also were of laws: *Hi non in foro, nec in consultorum atrio, sed in Pythagorae tacito illo sanctoque secessu, didicerunt jura, quae florenti tunc Siciliae, & per Italiam Graeciae ponerent*; says Seneca, speaking of some ancient lawmakers. The second signification of the verb *משל* (but in another conjugation:) is *Similis factus est*: and (in *Hiphil*, as they call it;) *comparavit, assimilavit*: that is, he was made like; he did liken, or compare. Hence some conceive that the proper signification of *משל* or *mashal*, is, similitudo, parabola: a similitude, or parable. And because most parables end in proverbs, or proverbial sentences, and admonitions; therefore, say they, the word came to be used for proverbs, and sentences: first, for such as were expressed figuratively: then, afterwards more generally, for all manner of sentences, or moral precepts, though (for the language) never so plain and direct. But I will not further inquire into the reasons: certain it is, that both here and elsewhere in Scripture, the word *mashal* is so taken: not for a proverb, properly so called, which implies a common speech, sententious, with somewhat extraordinary (according to the propriety of every language, some affecting rhymes; some, allusions; and others, some other thing) in the expression; but also any moral precept, instruction, or observation; such as are many in this book, which bears the title of Proverbs. However, it does not follow, that because of the inscription, everything in the book, should be proverbs: since it is ordinary enough to make inscriptions a majore parte. The first nine chapters are by many accounted rather as an introduction to the whole book, than as part of the Proverbs. Indeed they do not contain many proverbs, but the praises and commendation of wisdom, in general: that is the subject; whereof see what has already been said in the preface and upon chapter 10, verse 1. *The Proverbs*, etc. From the tenth chapter to the end, there is little else but proverbs and sentences. Again there be, who think these first nine chapters to be here exhibited as they came from Solomon, himself, without any alteration of either matter or method: but those that follow, to be collections: of which more shall be said in due place. Others, with more probability, that the six first verses of this chapter, are not Solomon's, but added by way of preface or argument by them that made this collection, and set it out abroad. This, as of itself it is not improbable, because the like we know has been done to many books; yet if there be no further ground, as I know none, for it, it might as well have been spared, there being as much and more probability for the contrary. For that Solomon himself should prefix his name and titles himself, is very suitable to the custom of those ancient times. There be few books, of any great antiquity, whether in verse or prose, but begin with the author's name, his country, and pedigree, and other like circumstances; and this by the author himself, as all men agree. It is commonly interpreted by them that are well versed in ancient books as a mark of great antiquity; if it be genuine, and not suppositious, or counterfeit. But now for conclusion of all we have more to say upon this title, before we leave it, the consideration of titles being a principal thing in all expositions: we may reduce the several uses and significations of this word *משל* (as it relates unto proverbs:) to the several properties and conditions belonging to a proverb. Which though they be not found but in few proverbs, all: yet all belong unto proverbs, in general; as appears by them that have written of the nature and constitution of a proverb; which also might be showed, (as by them it is, who professedly treat about it;) by divers examples. A proverb then we say, is an ancient speech (the more it has of antiquity, the more of luster and authority:) commonly used (that too, adds to the authority: and the Greek *παροιμία*, for a proverb, imports properly, a common way, speech or admonition: from which some fetch the Latin word, adagium, too:) which for the most part, has somewhat of acuteness, obscurity, or concinnity in it, more than other speeches; whose end is, instruction, or information, in point of life and conversation. Now we shall find, that what has some of these qualifications and conditions; yea though but one of them, if notably and eminently, is in the Scripture called, *משל* a proverb. So, for example: *Wickedness proceedeth from the wicked*: *מרשעים יצא רשע*: 1 Sam. 24:13, a very plain speech, which might seem the interpretation of a proverb: (as, *Can the Ethiopian change his skin, or the leopard his spots?* Jer. 13:23. *The tree is known by his fruit. Men gather not figs of thorns, nor grapes of a bramble-bush*, Luke 6:44, or the like:) rather than a proverb itself. But yet a proverb, and so called there, because an ancient common speech: *As saith the proverb of the ancients, Wickedness proceedeth*, etc. Again, Ezek. 24:3. [Son of man:] *Utter a parable unto the rebellious house* (in the Hebrew, *משל* a proverb; and more full and emphatical there, than can well be expressed in another tongue: *משל אל בית המרי משל* as if you should say: parable a parable: or, proverb a proverb: that is, proverbially set out, etc.) *and say unto them; Thus saith the Lord God; Set on a pot: set it on: and also pour water into it: 4. Gather the pieces*, etc. unto the 12th verse. There is nothing in all this, that is properly proverbial, as the word is more commonly used: but as it is a similitude, darkly and obscurely set out; that entitles it to this appellation of a proverb. Whence also it is, that *משל* a proverb: and *חידה* a riddle, in the like case, Ezek. 17:2, (*חוד חידה ומשל משל* very emphatically here too, in both words:) are joined together. Again, Psalm 78:1, 2. *Give ear, O my people*, etc. *I will open my mouth in a parable: I will utter dark sayings of old.* In the whole psalm there is not anything properly proverbial, or sententious: no, nor very obscure, and allegorical: but a plain historical narration, or commemoration of things that had passed in old times, between God, and their forefathers, in Egypt and elsewhere, since their coming out of Egypt: where the power, and constant goodness of God towards them; and their as constant unthankfulness, and manifold provocations, are manifested and declared. Why then a proverb, or a parable? Or, why riddles? For so the original word (*חידות*) which in our English is rendered *dark sayings*, does properly import. First, because of the argument: ancient things, very old, and very strange: and in that partaking of the nature, as of proverbs, so of riddles. But especially, *משכיל לאסף* that is, a psalm of, or for Asaph, to give instruction. Instruction and warning, by such examples, so emphatically, not in ordinary language; but poetically set out, of those things that had happened in old times, and by an extraordinary power, or hand, to their forefathers, by reason of their stubbornness and many rebellions, being the main (to glorify God, another; but that the most immediate:) end; therefore called a parable, or proverb: and, a riddle. Lastly, particular men, or a whole people is made a proverb: (a proverb, and a byword; *שנינה* an acute, or witty saying, properly: they are so put together, Deuter. 28:37, and elsewhere:) when their names are become the object of common talk, (and wit too: for some men are most ready to show their wit, upon such an occasion of other men's calamities: which nevertheless argues as little true wit, or wisdom, as it does charity:) for a warning to posterity. We have showed the extent of the Hebrew word, as it is used in the Scripture. Now what a proverb is properly in its own nature; and how to be distinguished from a parable, or sentence: or apophthegm: how from an apologue: a riddle, etc. because I think it not so necessary here, and would put us to much arguing, to determine it: I must refer the reader that desires to be satisfied, to them that have written of that subject, of purpose; but to Aristotle especially, where before quoted; in whom much good matter to this purpose will be found.

As more short, so more pertinent it may be, to take notice, that as the Hebrew *משל* is used in the Scripture of divers speeches that are not proverbs in the common sense and use of the word: so the Greek word *γρῖφος*: *griphus*: (properly, a riddle:) is sometimes taken more generally, for any question, or problem, though it have nothing in it, either of wit, or obscurity. See in the tenth book of Athenaeus, by Clearchus etc. as by the learned that have taken pains upon that author, is well observed.

*{The Proverbs of Solomon}* Heb. *משלי שלמה* I am not very prone of myself, to adscribe much unto derivations, or allusions, in, or of proper names. Yet we find many in the Scriptures, both of New, and Old Testament. But the Old especially. No proper name there almost, (at least, of any note:) but has its allusion, or mystery. Abraham: Isaac: Jacob, etc. who knows it not? Why then may not we observe, (though I find it not observed:) that *משל* *mashal*, a proverb: and *שלמה* Solomon: by transposition (wherein consists all anagrammatism:) only of one letter, is but one word? And there be many such transpositions of letters in the Scripture, not in allusions, and etymologies only; but even in the same word, bearing the same signification, to be found. So that if we make a verbal of *משל* the verb; the same word may stand for Solomon, and for a speaker of proverbs. But I shall not further insist upon this.

**Verse 2.** *{To know wisdom and instruction, to perceive the words of understanding}* Solomon by these, and many other words, heaped in the 3, 4, 5 and sixth following verses, tells his reader what the end is of this book, and what benefit he may reap by reading of it. The world is, and has been of old, (though never more than now) full of writers, and as full of readers: but variety, or curiosity being the only end of most, the world were better without them. An ancient grammarian in Aulus Gellius, when some professed philosophers came to him, being a man of great account in his profession, to be resolved about some curiosities in matter of language, broke out into a great passion; *Ego Grammaticus, vitae atque morum disciplinas quaero: vos philosophi*, etc. I that am a professed grammarian, now in my old age, am wholly set upon those arts and studies by which my life and manners may be benefited: and you professed philosophers, (*mera estis mortuaria Glossaria*:) are wholly turned into dead and rotten glossaries (or curiosities about words and phrases, from which no benefit can accrue, either to you, or to anybody else, in point of life or action.) It made him wish, that rather all men had been born dumb, than that there should be so much ado about words. As therefore the consideration of the end, beforehand, in all actions and undertakings, do extremely become a rational wise man; so, if seriously thought upon, is there no end much considerable, that has not reference to this end, here spoken of, *To know wisdom*, etc. that is, to the improvement of man in his better part, in matter of life and action. And this was the way that the ancients did use to teach wisdom; as before in the preface has been observed, by proverbs, and parables, and sentences; such as this book does contain. Which way in those ancient times was found so effectual, that some in later ages, did not only prefer it before any other way, but would admit of no other, as expedient. Concerning which, Seneca has two epistles, wherein the matter is argued on both sides very largely, and learnedly. But here a question, not impertinently, as I conceive, may be moved; whether the law and the commandments, comprehended in the five books of Moses, were not sufficient to that end, without these proverbs of Solomon. For that that was the end of the law also, appears by many passages: as by Deut. 4:5, 6. *Behold I have taught you statutes and judgments*, etc. *Keep therefore and do them; for this is your wisdom and your understanding in the sight of the nations, which shall hear all these statutes, and say; Surely this great nation is a wise and understanding people. For what nation*, etc. To this, our answer is; first, that the end of the law, was, to instruct men in those things, that did more immediately belong to the service of God; as also in matters of right and wrong, which come within the compass of laws and public administration of justice. Other precepts of morality and good manners, were rather set out in types, partly; and partly, in examples of the law, than in express terms, most of them. However, the effect of most of them might be deduced out of the law, by them that were very well versed in it, and applied themselves earnestly to the study of it: and many questionless of these very proverbs, here commended unto us by Solomon, as tending to this end, *to wisdom and instruction*, etc. were then extant and in use among the Hebrews, being transmitted from fathers to children, by them that were wise themselves, and careful of their children's education. Thus was the law, with those other helps that God had provided for them, very sufficient to make them wise and perfect, that carefully sought after wisdom, according to that degree of knowledge and perfection, which God, in his heavenly wisdom, had allotted to that infant-age of the Church. Afterwards, when God was pleased that moral wisdom should be more popular and common, he raised Solomon to that end and purpose, who not only collected into one, what former ages afforded in that kind; but himself, being endowed by God with extraordinary wisdom, added many particular instructions of his own, as he was inspired by God. These words therefore, *To know wisdom*, etc. must not be taken in opposition to the law, as of a different way here proposed: but upon a supposition of those means, the law, the Psalms, or whatever else God had already provided unto that end. See more upon verse 4. *To give subtlety*, etc. and verse 8. *My son*, etc.

*{To know wisdom and instruction, to perceive the words of understanding.} ***3.*** {To receive the instruction of wisdom}, etc.* Here be many words put together in these, and some following verses, some of which in common use, are equivalent; (otherwise called: *synonyma's*) and others, that are of very near signification. Nevertheless, it were no hard thing to devise some difference, might we but take the liberty to look into the divisions of subtle philosophers, concerning the manifold habits and faculties of the soul; as either into Aristotle, concerning the differences of those five habits, as he reckons them, of the soul, *τέχνη*, *ἐπιστήμη*, *φρόνησις*, *σοφία*, *νοῦς*: or unto others of the differences between *νοῦς*, *λόγος*, *λογισμός*, *διάνοια*: and the like: no hard thing, I say; could we but as easily persuade ourselves, that Solomon writing these things for common use, and even where he invites common people to him, could think it fit and seasonable, to tie himself to such philosophical niceties in his expressions; which though they may have some ground in nature, yet are so remote from common apprehensions, that but very few are capable of them. To let therefore all curious distinctions pass, as rather the conceits of interpreters, than any part of Solomon's, or the Holy Ghost's meaning; that which we conceive most probable, is, that by so many words, perfect or true wisdom is intended, which presupposes a competency of light in the understanding, (as the foundation;) concerning things divine and human; and a conformity of the will and affections to that light. Variety of words does the better set out the excellency of this wisdom; and serves also to make the deeper impression in the mind of the reader. Yet for all this we deny not, but that according to the propriety of the words, *חכמה* may refer to speculative knowledge and sciences, more particularly: *בינה* to practical abilities, which we call prudence and discretion; as *מוסר* does import discipline properly, that is, such improvements of knowledge and manners, as comes by institution, and good education.

*{Of wisdom}* We may note, that the word *חכמה* in Scripture, is a very general word, and comprehends all kind of wisdom: as the word *σοφία*, in Greek authors. Sometimes, it does import mechanic craft and wisdom: which though of an inferior degree, yet is from God too, as we are taught Exod. 28:3; 31:3, and Isa. 28:23, 24 unto the end. Sometimes, worldly wisdom, whether practical, which we call policy, prudence: or speculative, as the knowledge of things natural, etc. as 1 Kings 4:30. And lastly, heavenly sanctifying wisdom: as Deuteron. 4:6. See Job 28:28, and Psalm 111, last verse: of which see more on the seventh verse of this chapter. All these kinds as they are all from God, so they may all concur and meet in some one man. One and the same man may excel in mechanic craft; whether by practice, and actual operation: or by showing his ready fancy, quick apprehension, and sound judgment in all such things: and may be very prudent and cautelous in worldly things; both at home, and abroad: and besides all this, a very godly man too. But though this be not impossible, yet it does not often so fall out: except a man (as some are) be naturally, wise and politic. But they that attain to worldly wisdom by long use and practice, and diligent observation; (which is the more ordinary way) they are such commonly, as make it their only end, and business, in this world: who propose to themselves worldly ends, and achievements, as chiefest happiness. Whereas, they that have a right apprehension of the vanity of this world, and all worldly felicity; and can make a difference between things momentaneous, and eternal; and have a faith answerable to their judgment, by which things future appear unto them with equal certainty as things present: it is not very likely, or very possible (excepting, as before:) that such men should be very worldly-wise and prudent: not because they are incapable; but because being so intent upon the prosecution of heaven, (that precious pearl:) they cannot afford unto such things which they have no better opinion of, so much time and tendence, as they require: it being very difficult, if not impossible, even in this sense, to serve two masters, God and the world: to apply himself effectually to the pursuance of worldly designs and advancements; and to the purchase of heaven and eternity. Aristotle makes it, among others, the property of a *μεγαλόψυχος*, or truly magnanimous man, never to be seen very earnest or eager about anything: and particularly never to speak, or to walk hastily: because, says he, this world does not afford anything, that he does so much value, as to put himself out of his ordinary pace, or to raise his voice for it. For what should he think much considerable in the world, to whom life itself is not a considerable thing? So he. How much more does a true Christian, whom thoughts of eternity have once thoroughly possessed, think the study and earnest pursuit of these things beneath him? Yet true it is nevertheless; (as we shall clear afterwards) though a man does not set his heart upon the world, or make it his study to be worldly-wise; yet (some natural towardliness that way presupposed) he may much advantage himself even so (in worldly wisdom, I mean) by the diligent reading of the word of God, and especially, by being well versed in this book of Proverbs.

**Verse 4.** *{To give subtilty to the simple, to the young man knowledge and discretion}* David says the same of the law of God, before this book of Proverbs was extant, Psalm 19:7. *The law of the Lord is perfect, converting the soul: the testimony of the Lord is sure, making wise the simple.* See also Psalm 119:98, 99, 100. *Thou through thy commandments hast made me wiser*, etc. whereby that is confirmed which we said before, that these commendations we have here of the Proverbs, and those effects that are ascribed unto them, do presuppose, or include a knowledge of the law likewise.

*{Subtilty}* The original word *ערמה* here translated *subtlety*, is sometimes taken in a good, and sometimes in a bad sense. There be divers examples of both significations in the Scripture. Though it be desired, and much commended, I hear by them that must have *praemansum cibum*; and would not willingly be put to the labor of turning more books than one (for that they cannot, through ignorance, were too gross to suspect:) yet I cannot persuade myself that it is fit to be done, or indeed, profitable to any, to transcribe, upon every word, whole concordances and dictionaries. So a man may be long enough, and yet take no very great pains. My observation upon the word is; that whereas there is nothing more natural unto man (as philosophers observe, and we partly know by the sad story of Adam's fall:) than the desire of knowledge: and among the men of the world, (that is, the greater part,) no kind of knowledge more valued, or desired, than craft and subtlety: therefore wise Solomon does here most wisely multiply words, as *subtlety, knowledge, discretion, learning*: and before, *wisdom, instruction, understanding*, and the like, the more powerfully to draw and to persuade, if possible, all kind of men, to the love and pursuit of heavenly wisdom: being very confident himself, (and would have all men to believe, as by this accumulation of words he does intimate:) that heavenly wisdom, is the wisdom of wisdoms: and heavenly knowledge (that is godliness, or the fear of God) the knowledge of knowledges: to which therefore all titles, attributes, and expressions of wisdom and knowledge, as subtlety, prudence, discretion, and the like, in request among men; do properly belong. Which may be the comfort of every true Christian, that knows God in Christ, sincerely, and experimentally; that though perchance he knows little else, yet he knows as much as will make him eternally happy; in comparison of which all other knowledge is inconsiderable and of little worth. See also upon chapter 8:12, *prudence*. But this, not to undervalue any kind of human knowledge, or learning, which may be useful unto men; than which nothing, (no worldly thing) is, or should be more honorable among men: much less, that part of human knowledge, which does very much conduce to the better understanding of the word of God, upon which true faith is grounded; confuting and confounding of errors and heresies, falsely pretending to the said word: without which help of human knowledge and learning, there would be great confusion in the world; atheism and superstition greatly prevail: nor yet to conclude from these words, that all kind of knowledge must be had from the Scriptures; all learning thence to be derived; which some blind zealots, and enthusiasts would have: to none of these ends; God forbid: but to give heavenly knowledge her due; and the better to understand, why here expressed by so many words. See also upon chapter 2, verse 9, *and judgment*.

*{To the simple}* By *simple*, the foolish and ignorant, such as want knowledge, and instruction, are commonly understood, as by the annotation upon the beginning of the verse may be gathered. But the word will bear another interpretation also, which may be as proper to the place, as the former: simplicity, is sometimes taken for integrity, and sincerity of actions opposed to fraud and hypocrisy. Without this simplicity; or at least, except a man apply himself to it; he can reap little benefit by reading. A corrupt life, and a corrupt judgment, go commonly together. See more upon Psalm 51, verse 16. *But unto the wicked, God saith.* But more likely (if here to be taken in a good sense:) that by simplicity, is meant tractableness, and lowliness of mind, opposed to pride and haughtiness of spirit; which makes men to have a high conceit of their own abilities, and to despise others. Such kind of simplicity is commended by Christ, and required in them, that aspire to have faith and Christianity: Matt. 18:2, 3. *And Jesus called a little child*; and by Saint Peter, 1 Pet. 2:3. *As newborn babes.* Pride and self-conceit, are the very bane of religion. Let a man be never so well qualified otherwise; yet if proud, and highly conceited of himself, he is as far (in the eyes of God, whatever he be in his own, or the eyes of other men:) from true religion and Christianity; as the most vicious of men. It is to be hoped, that God in his infinite mercy, may pity them, in time that know themselves (being thoroughly sensible of their own defects and misery:) an object of pity and compassion: to which tends the parable of the proud Pharisee, and humble publican, Luke 18:10, etc. But *God resisteth the proud*: James 4:6, and 1 Pet. 5:5. (See here upon chapter 3, verse 34:) as them, of all men, who most particularly and eminently usurp upon him, while they ascribe to themselves anything, wherein they can find matter of glorifying. For glory belongs unto God only: and he will not *give his glory unto another*, Isa. 42:8. Of this sense of the word *simple*, and of interpretation grounded upon it, see more upon the next words.

*{To the young man}* The word *נער* though more commonly it signify a youth, or young man: and because young men are most fit for service, a servant also: yet some make it most proper to a child in the womb, ripe and ready to be shaken off; that is, to be born: and so they would have it taken, Judg. 13:7. That it is taken for a newborn babe, Exod. 2:6, is without all doubt. Now according as we interpret the word *simple*, either for ignorant, or for innocent and meek: (whereof so in the precedent annotation:) so must *נער* here be translated, either, young man, or, babe: (or, little child.) Why ignorant, and young, should be put together, and both called upon to learn: needs no further explication. See also upon chapter 10, verse 1. *The Proverbs of Solomon.* Neither is much more difficult to conceive, why innocent or meek; and babes, or little children, should go together. See the former quotations, Matt. 18:2, 3, etc. to which may be added, Psalm 131:1, 2. *Lord my heart is not haughty*, etc. *Surely—as a child that is weaned*, etc.

**Verse 5.** *{A wise man will hear, and will increase learning}* Because the young, and the simple were particularly mentioned in the foregoing verse; to whom also the word of *instruction*, in the former, might be thought to have most particular relation; lest any should think the use of this book to belong unto such only, he presently adds concerning those that have already attained to the reputation of wise men; that such also, though in comparison of others, wise already, in some degree, may by this book improve themselves, and increase their learning. In which words we have also a pregnant character, or *κριτήριον* of one that is truly wise; which is, that such a one being most sensible of his own wants, (which is the case of them that are most perfect in this world) will despise no means, no opportunity to improve himself. Whereas a fool, (that is, one that thinks himself wise) because he thinks he knows all things better than any man can tell him; as he is not solicitous to seek the means of himself, so if good counsel be given him by others, he has not the patience to hear; or if he must hear, yet it goes no further than his ears, and does not enter into his heart, because of his frowardness and self-conceit. It is the propriety of a wise man, absolutely to despise no man, though never so mean, or simple otherwise; because there is no man so simple, but may speak to the purpose sometimes; according to that ancient saying, *Saepe etiam est olitor valde opportuna locutus.* And that great oracle of nature, Hippocrates, whose sentences, after Solomon's, for worth and excellency, may challenge the next place; his advice in a place is, *Μὴ ὀκνέειν καὶ παρὰ ἰδιωτέων ἱστορέειν*: that is, that we should not scruple, or be slack, to learn, even from them that are counted idiots. See also upon chapter 11:12, *despiseth his neighbour*.

*{Shall attain unto wise counsels}* Or, *possess wise counsels* according to the original word, which signifies both. By *wise counsel*, I understand ability to give good advice upon urgent occasions and necessities, whether private, or public; but public especially. A very high commendation, which belongs unto few men, who have been famous in their generations for it, and have been admired also by posterity. Such were the counsels of Achitophel; whereof 2 Sam. 16:23 we read, that *the counsel of Achitophel which he counselled, in those days, was as if a man had inquired of the oracle of God*: though he found in the end the truth of that which is observed by Solomon in this book, *That there is no wisdom, nor understanding, nor counsel against the Lord*: chapter 21:30. Which if he had well understood before, he would not have been so impatient when his good counsel was rejected, as to hang himself for it: 2 Sam. 17, verse 12 and 23. But he justly suffered for his pride. Such also is the counsel of the poor man, spoken of Eccles. 9:13, etc. by which the city was delivered; though he was not regarded for that, because poor: as it falls out sometimes in the world, that they that have done most considerable service, are least regarded: and others reap the benefit of their labor, or wisdom. But though it may so fall out sometimes; yet generally, no men are more honored, either living, or dead, than they that are able to give good counsel: in Homer, Agamemnon the king, being engaged in a troublesome war, wishes that he had but ten such counselors as Nestor, and makes no question but he should bring things to an end quickly. Upon which the Greek commentators have several observations of the excellency of good counsel. This I conceive to be the most plausible, and truest sense (though I have not found it in others) of the words; grounded, in part, upon the propriety of the Hebrew word *תחבלות* which comes from *חובל* a pilot; and does most properly and immediately import (*artem nauticam, & solertiam illam, qua gubernator in nave regenda utitur*, says Mercer:) that skill and dexterity which belongs to a good pilot. And what that is, who knows not? And accordingly, the LXX here translate it *κυβέρνησιν*, and the vulgar Latin, gubernanda. The Chaldee paraphrase also, I should say, if the Latin translation of it were altogether to be trusted where we find it *gubernaculum*. But the truth is, the Chaldee word imports no more than *solertiam*, or *prudentiam*: or at the most, *prudentem gubernationem*, in general: which I think cannot be well rendered by *gubernaculum*. But this as a caveat by the way only. I wonder, therefore, that Mercer, (who is followed by most interpreters) should make these words to cohere with the next verse, in sense: *and a man of understanding shall attain to wise counsels, to understand a proverb*, etc. which is very contrary to this exposition, which we embrace, and have hitherto insisted upon. For so, wise counsels are confined to that intellectual wisdom and understanding; (more properly called, wit and subtlety;) which is necessary to the understanding of proverbs, and riddles: which we rather interpret of politic wisdom and prudence. (See chapter 11:14. *Where no counsel is*, etc. It is the same word there; which does much confirm our interpretation of it here.) That which has moved them to it, as I conceive, because they do not see, what this infinitive *to understand*, etc. can otherwise depend upon. As for the former infinitives, *To know wisdom*, etc. they make them to depend of the first verse: *The Proverbs of Solomon*, etc. *To know wisdom*, etc. that is: the use and end whereof is, to teach wisdom, etc. But here, the coherence seems to be interrupted by the fifth verse, *A wise man*, etc. which therefore they fetch from these words. But besides that generally, and in this book of Proverbs peculiarly in which all things are so loose, to argue from the coherence, is no very good argument: it may be answered that there is no inconvenience at all, if we fetch the coherence, though somewhat interrupted by the fifth verse, from the first; according to the tenor and construction of the former verses: which kind of interruptions are frequent enough in other books; and may be helped, if need be, by a parenthesis. The Arabic translation turns all those infinitives into imperatives. Indeed, in the Greek, it is very usual, in this way of precepts, to use infinitives for imperatives. But that it is so in the Hebrew too, especially when the infinitive is turned into a gerund, is more than I can avouch.

**Verse 6.** *{To understand a proverb, and the interpretation}* Of the coherence, see upon the former words. The solution of riddles, and dark sayings, though sometimes rather an effect of wit, in general, than a necessary consequent of true wisdom, is here mentioned among other particulars, because it was a thing in ancient times, not among the Jews only, as appears by divers Scripture stories; but among the Grecians and all Oriental people, much practiced, and in great request, as is testified by divers ancient authors. This is further explained, and set out by the author of Ecclesiasticus, (of whom, see what is said in the preface) in these words; *But he that giveth his mind to the law of the most High, and is occupied in the meditation thereof, will seek out the wisdom of all the ancients*; etc. chapter 39:1, 2, 3.

**Verse 7.** *{The fear of the Lord, is the beginning of knowledge}* See the places quoted in the margin of the Bible, Job 28:28, and Psalm 111:10, and chapter 9:10, and the annotation upon Psalm 111:10. *The fear of the Lord.* And upon Psalm 130:4. *That thou mayest be feared.* Except then this foundation of *the fear of the Lord*, (or piety and religion) be had, all other wisdom, is but folly; yea more dangerous and pernicious in the consequent, than mere folly and simplicity. Ancient heathen philosophers dispute at large, that *non est virtus cui non constat ratio.* Let a man be never so liberal, just, or temperate, in his actions; if he be so rather by an instinct of nature, propriety of bodily temperamentum: or by the power of good education, habituated into custom; than upon any ground of reason, and judgment, they will not allow him truly to be liberal, just, or temperate. Nay Plato goes so far, that if a man does perform such things, such actions of justice etc. upon grounds of reason, as he does think, and believe: yet except his judgment be so well grounded and settled, that he be out of all danger of a relapse, that is, of contrary impressions, (a thing often seen, in the changes and revolutions of states and times, according to which the minds and opinions of light ungrounded men are apt to turn, and still to be confident that they are in the right:) upon any occasion; he will not allow him truly to be just, or anything else that pretends to solid virtue. It cannot be denied but Epicurus himself was the author of as many excellent sayings and sentences, as most philosophers before him, or since; nay and lived too, in some degree accordingly: though not as some would make us believe, who while they endeavor to make a saint of him (a thing not much to be wondered in such an age of saints, such as they are:) take the liberty to live like right Epicures themselves; but lived, I say, in some degree, according to his own precepts and sentences. But this, not in reference to God, or to another world; (or immortality of the soul:) both which, (though not so openly, the one, as the other:) he denied; but in reference to the pleasure to be reaped, of a quiet calm life; pleasure being the thing, which he proposed to himself, as his only felicity. If therefore neither fine sayings and sentences: nor some outward performances, suitable to those sayings, could (even in the judgment of heathens,) make Epicurus to be well thought of; but contrariwise, his name and his memory was generally, in horror and detestation among them, because of his principles: how much less is the show and outward practice of any virtue, and the possession of any knowledge, that is not grounded upon religion, and *the fear of the Lord*, to be regarded in any that profess Christianity? See also upon chapter 3:3, *mercy and truth*. But let us not mistake in words. Religion, and *the fear of the Lord*, with most men at this day, is taken for opinion, fancy, confidence, zealous words; yea, certain tones, faces, gestures, and the like. Let a man be never so religious in his life and conversation, and sound in his faith, that has not these marks; he is none of the godly. God keep sober men from such delusions.

*{The beginning}* Although *ראשית* here is expounded by divers, as Psalm 111:10. *The fear of the Lord*, etc. to which we have already referred: to wit, for excellency and perfection: yet because Solomon afterwards, chapter 9, verse 10, does seem to expound himself, using there the word *תחלה* which properly signifies *beginning*, and is not found in that other sense, as *ראשית* is; therefore it is more plausible, to keep to it, here also. But whereas some expositors make a doubt concerning this *fear of the Lord*; whether to be understood of filial, or servile fear: I should rather question the warrantableness of that distinction, (though ancient enough, I know:) if we might be so long, as to discuss it, and search it to the bottom. We have done it elsewhere, and here, if by *the fear of the Lord* we understand religion, and the worship of God in general; (as Psalm 130:4. *But there is forgiveness with thee*, etc. which I think is most proper to this place: see the annotations there;) there is no great need of it.

*{But fools despise}* The original word *אויל* does not only signify a fool, simply; but a stubborn, strong-headed, self-conceited, incorrigible fool: as by comparing of places may easily appear. Now if with the LXX we may translate it here, *ἀσεβεῖς*: that is, ungodly men: then the sense (and coherence) will be: the fear of the Lord, (that is, godliness and religion:) is true wisdom: and if godliness be true wisdom indeed, it must needs follow that they that reject or despise the one, reject and despise the other also: that is in effect, that all ungodly men, that will not submit to *the fear of God*, are fools. So, this latter member, is both a consequent, and a confirmation of the former; both tending to one end, to set out the excellency of the fear of God, or religion. There be many in the world are not afraid, or ashamed, to be accounted irreligious: there be, that glory in it; and think the better of themselves for it. They cry themselves up, and are cried up by others, for the wits of the times: when far better wits without this mark, of being atheistical, are not in comparison, regarded. But there be not many in the world, that can so well brook, to be thought unwise: much less fools: and thereupon Solomon grounds his exhortation to piety, by averring, that the fear of God, is in very deed, true wit, and wisdom; and that they that despise, or reproach piety and godliness, do at the same time proclaim themselves egregious fools. But taking, *מוסר*, for discipline and chastisement, which seems to be most proper to this place: then the coherence will be this. The fear of the Lord, says Solomon, is wisdom: a great commendation, and very taking, since all men generally are ambitious of wisdom. But there is somewhat that goes along with it, with this fear of the Lord, besides wisdom; and without which, wisdom cannot be got. And what is that? *My son*, says Jesus the son of Sirach, (and he too, a very wise man, and supposed to be Solomon himself by many) *if thou come to fear the Lord, prepare thy soul for temptation. Set thy heart aright, and constantly endure*, etc. But this is it, that fools cannot endure. It makes them hate religion, when they are told of suffering. So that by these words Solomon seems to give a reason, why more men do not apply themselves to godliness; even because they cannot endure the instruction, or chastisement of wisdom. For so the words may import, by a figure common enough in the Bible: *wisdom and instruction*: i.e. the instruction, or chastisement of wisdom. *μαθήματα*: *παθήματα*: instruction and castigation, must go together: such is the order of God; and Christ himself, though the Son of God; his natural Son; yet was he not exempted from this law *ἔμαθεν ἀφ᾽ ὧν ἔπαθεν* Heb. 5:8, *he learned—by those things that he suffered*. And who would not, except he be a very fool or an infidel, suffer for a while, in this, so short and transitory; that he may be happy eternally, in another world?

**Verse 8.** *{My son, hear the instruction of thy father, and forsake not the law of thy mother}* These words may be understood, either of that obedience in general, which all children owe to parents; which after the fear of God, is the next duty; (both duties being comprehended by one word, Pietas, by the Latins) and is the first command in the second table of the Decalogue: or they may have more particular reference to Deut. 4:9 and 6:7, and other like places, where parents are strictly enjoined to teach the law of God to their children, with all possible care and diligence. According to the first interpretation, the obligation of children to parents, is general: they owe respect and reverence to their parents, whether good, or bad; and in worldly civil things, whether at home, or abroad, that are not against the law of God, perfect obedience: but according to the second interpretation, that properly relates to God's worship, and religious duties; so the obedience of children, or this command concerning their obedience unto parents, must be understood with a limitation, or restriction: it presupposes their obedience to the law of God, concerning the education of their children: from which if the parents swerve, or apostatize, children are not bound to obey them. Most interpreters, interpret *father* and *mother* here, allegorically, or metaphorically: some understanding by it, God; some, God and the Church: others, Solomon himself. This they were put to, because they could not find any literal sense, which they could like, or maintain. But how likely it is, that Solomon, in these popular admonitions and exhortations, directed to the simple, as well as others, would express himself so obscurely, or ambiguously, let the understanding reader judge. I see no reason at all why any man should question the literal meaning here, more than in Saint Paul's words, *Children, obey your parents in all things*, Col. 3:20. All precepts in this kind, being to be understood with certain limitations, which no man almost is ignorant of; but not allegorically to be frustrated and avoided. He may also call the law of God, *the instruction*; or, (as chapter 6:20) *the commandment of his father*; and *the law of his mother*; to endear it unto him the more, and the more strongly to oblige him to the observation and profession of it, because in matter of laws and religion, men ordinarily do most willingly embrace whatsoever is *πατροπαράδοτον*, as the Grecians are wont to express it; that is, that which they have received from their parents: the laws and religion of their fathers, and forefathers. How superstitious and pertinacious men have been in that kind in all ages, though that which they received from their fathers, were never so absurd and false, ancient stories, both ecclesiastical and others, will teach. A great engagement therefore it is to them that receive the truth from their fathers and mothers, never to apostatize from it, for any temporal loss, or advantage.

*{The instruction of thy father}* St. Paul observes it, that *Honour thy father and thy mother*, etc. is *the first commandment with a promise*, Eph. 6:2. We may here observe, that after the commendation of piety, which Solomon does begin with; that is, our duty to God; the next thing he does insist upon, is the duty of children towards their parents, as most immediately flowing from the former, and the very foundation of all other moral duties. Insomuch, that the very word *εὐσέβεια*, and pietas, is sometimes appropriated to this one duty. And herein the sayings of ancient wise heathens, and the dictates of Scripture, do agree. It is furthermore observable, that the second part also of this book of Proverbs does so begin; to wit, at the tenth chapter, where again the same inscription, as at the beginning, *The Proverbs of Solomon*, is prefixed; that even there, I say, the very first precept, or sentence, is, concerning the duty of children: *A wise son maketh a glad father*, etc. So ancient Gnomologues; who as they are of greatest antiquity, so they agree most with the Scriptures: *Πρῶτα θεὸν τίμα*: *μετέπειτα δὲ σεῖο γονῆας*. So did once Phocilides begin; though since, some other verses have been set before. Pythagoras, his verses, also, *Ἀθανάτους μὲν πρῶτα θεούς*, etc. then presently: *Τοὺς τε γονεῖς τίμα*. So prose writers too: as may appear by Isocrates his *Paraenesis*. Plutarch has a most excellent passage to this purpose, by which the power of reason grounded upon that remnant of natural light in men, since Adam's fall, and the consent of it (reason I mean, and natural light:) with revelation and religion, may appear. All men, says he, in all places, that are sober and rational, unanimously, do speak and sing, (alluding to poets:) that both nature, and the laws of men, grounded upon nature, (or, that are not contrary to nature:) after the worship of God, give the first and chiefest place to parents: and that the most acceptable service that men can do unto God, is a continued course of respect, and service, daily renewed and increased, performed by children unto parents. See more, upon chapter 10:2, *maketh a glad father*: out of Plutarch. Lastly, we may observe, that not in the Decalogue only, *Honour thy father*, etc. is the first of the second table; but also in that solemn repetition and confirmation of the moral, or natural law, pronounced on Mount Ebal, with curses against the transgressors; the same precept, (though expressed there in other terms:) has the same rank: *Cursed is he that setteth light by his father, or his mother*, Deut. 27:16.

**Verse 9.** *{An ornament of grace unto thy head, and chains about thy neck}* This is expressed popularly; not that the Scripture does, or would have us to ascribe much to these outward dressings and ornaments, which are proper to the body; (whereof see 1 Tim. 2:9, 10. *In like manner also, that women adorn themselves*, etc. and 1 Pet. 3:3. *Whose adorning let it not be that outward adorning*, etc. whom nevertheless I do not understand, as though they did absolutely forbid all such ornaments, but only prescribe and commend what those ornaments are, which those that are religious and wise, should chiefly desire, and endeavor) but the better to set out the inward beauty and excellency of piety and virtue, in terms and expressions, most suitable (because ordinary) to vulgar capacities.

**Verse 10.** *{My son, if sinners entice thee, consent not}* Nothing can happen more pernicious to one, who through want of years, or otherwise, is not yet settled in the ways of godliness and piety, than bad company. *Be not deceived: evil communications corrupt good manners*; 1 Cor. 15:33. Saint Paul had it out of an ancient Greek comic, then extant, called Menander: whereby it appears, what reckoning he made of those ancient sentences and sayings of wise heathens. One of them, ancienter than Menander, by many years, delivers it with much confidence, that wickedness (among men) does chiefly spring from this root, even from evil examples and company. And therefore in some ancient commonwealths, not only lewd and riotous men were severely punished themselves; but also laws made for the punishment of them that frequented their company. Charondus his law *περὶ κακομιλίας* was famous to that effect. O Christianity! I may say: O Reformation! Christ says, *except our righteousness exceed the righteousness of Scribes and Pharisees, we cannot enter into the kingdom of heaven*, Matt. 5:20. What then if it come short of the righteousness of heathens? But this is not a place to preach: though just indignation, in these licentious times, may extort some words. Of the danger of evil company, see on Psalm 1:1, *walketh not in the counsel* and the references there.

**Verse 11.** *{If they say, Come with us; let us lay wait for blood}, etc.* He does here, and in the following 12, 13, 14 verses, by a figure called *mimesis* or, *prosopopoeia*, represent them speaking, and using their enticements, or rather enchantments, to pervert a young man from the right way, and to draw him into their nets: not that we should think that they use these very words, (neither indeed do they use one kind of bait always, but can fit it to several tempers and dispositions, and other circumstances of place and time, and the like) but that from these words in general, we should understand with what boldness and impudency, such use to insinuate themselves, under pretense of love, and plausible hopes and achievements: when nothing but hell and destruction, both of body and soul, is in all their ways. Neither are the words, here used, so to be taken, as words of enticement properly, but rather as words of aggravation: as when he makes them say, *Let us lay wait for blood; Let us lurk privily for the innocent without a cause*, etc. his meaning is not, that they use those, or any like: they are too subtle and cunning, to set out so plainly the horror of their wickedness and villainy: this were not to entice, but deter: not that his meaning therefore; but this, that when they use other language, which does extenuate the wickedness of their practice, (as what is so abominable, but may cunningly be extenuated, and made, if not plausible, yet tolerable, or excusable?) they must (by them that are wise and rational) be understood as though they plainly said, *Let us wait for blood: let us lurk privily for the innocent without cause*, etc. But of all bad examples and illaudable courses, to which a young man may be drawn, why Solomon should pitch upon, and begin with this of bloody thieves and robbers; which may be thought the utmost of wickedness: a thing not so incidental, neither to any ingenuous nature nor education, as some other vices are: why then with this? If it be asked; I must confess I find nothing at all about it in any commentator: but that which seems to me most probable, is, either that Solomon had a more particular aim and respect to his own times herein, when it may be thought that this practice was more usual and ordinary, even among the better sort in the world: or that he intended here, at the very first, to set before the eyes of young men, the danger and horror of a licentious life, not regulated by the fear of God, and the dictates of a tender conscience: which though it begin not ordinarily with such extremities, as are here expressed; yet very commonly it proceeds in time to such, and ends in them, or the like: so that whoever, in his younger years, takes a liberty to lie, to swear, to pilfer, or to drink, or to frequent lewd company, whether of men or women: any of these, or the like; he may justly suspect himself, that in time, he may prove an arrant cutthroat rogue, such as is here described by Solomon.

*{Let us lay wait for blood}* Jer. 5:26. He speaks not only of the shedding of blood with the hand, but also of all crafty practices, which tend to the detriment of our neighbor's life, or livelihood, Psalm 10:8 and 17:12. And it is observable, that the very word *דם* which ordinarily signifies blood; does also in the Chaldee as ordinarily signify money: even as *βίος* in the Greek tongue, both life, and livelihood. And Cato, that wise Roman, being asked what it was, foenerari: that is, to give money to use; answered: what is it to kill, or, to murder? But there be many ways besides that, invented to suck the blood of the poor: the fallacy is, in the difference of words and terms only. *Fures privatorum furtorum, in nervo atque in compedibus aetatem agunt: fures publici, in auro atque purpura*: said the same Cato.

*{For the innocent without cause}* Whose words, and how to be understood, see the annotation upon the first part of the verse. They that would have these words intended by Solomon, for the very words used by the enticers, are put to many shifts, to find somewhat that may be plausible. Some devise one thing, some another: but I find nothing in all their devices and inventions, that bears (with me) the least color of probability. Can any man be so barbarously wicked, as to propose to himself the shedding of innocent blood; as it is the blood of an innocent? Or if himself by long use, has attained to that degree of immanity; would he be such a fool, as to use these words, as an allurement to them, that are yet raw in such practices? And not to content himself neither to say *innocent*, but to insist upon it, as if no other motive more plausible and popular, could be found; by adding, *without cause*? O but, say some, *innocent and without cause*: that is; we will fall upon such, that never did us any wrong, either in word or deed, and have no occasion at all to suspect us; and so shall we have the better opportunity to accomplish our desires. Their innocency, shall be our security. I do not deny, but such thoughts murderers may have: but that they would express themselves or Solomon their thoughts in such words; who can believe? Others therefore, they would have this *without cause*, to be spoken by these enticers, in reference to the event: *without cause*, that is, say they, what we do, we will do it so warily, and cunningly, that we need not fear any ill consequent: though death, ordinarily, (a public death) be the reward of murder: blood for blood: yet we shall provide for our security well enough. Justice shall have no hold of us. The effect of justice shall be frustrated in us. Or if that will not do, because it takes away only the offense of the words *without cause*: that is, in effect, of the repetition: and leaves the *innocent*, the original, as it were, still liable to the same censure: then, say some others, (more plausibly, I confess, if the words would bear it:) *innocent without cause*: that is: those men, that trust to their piety and innocency: that think themselves very safe, under the protection of God, whom they pretend to worship: but *without cause*: it is but a vain confidence. For God (if any God at all) does not take notice of such things: it being by daily experience, clearly evident to all men, that the good, as soon as the bad; the just, as the unjust, are exposed to all manner of mishaps. Folly therefore it is, to stick at these things. This indeed lays well open the very root and foundation of all lewd practices. See Psalm 10:11, 13, and Mal. 3:16. *Ye have said, it is vain to serve God: and what profit is it?* etc. and elsewhere; so many places also, Wisdom 2:13 and following verses. And I think probable enough, that such enticers do usually begin there, to possess their novices, with such principles. For till that be done, they cannot go on in their villainies without some reluctancy, and remorse of conscience. And for the words; David's expression may seem to come somewhat near: *Verily I have cleansed my heart in vain, and washed my hands in innocency.* Psalm 73:13. To wash his hands in innocency in vain, is no more than to be innocent in vain; and that the word *חנם* here translated *without cause*, may also signify, *in vain*, is not denied. However, let us make the best of it, that can be made: it is but a forced sense, in this place; the other, as more literal, in itself; so more natural to the coherence. For all the words, verse 11, 12, manifestly tend rather to aggravation, such as would become a judge, or a reprover; than to extenuation, such as is ordinary to men of that profession, whereby they endeavor to delude both themselves and others. We therefore stick to our first interpretation. It is very ordinary to the Scripture, to set out wicked men and sinners by words as by them, immediately and personally uttered: but rather, indeed, containing, or importing the effect either of their bare thoughts, or of other words spoken by them; in common construction, very different; but in reality of truth, that is; in consequence of matter, or event; and in God's interpretation, the very same. It is a figure of speech common enough upon other occasions too. So David, *But if they be the children of men, cursed be they before the Lord, for they have driven me out this day,—saying, Go, serve other gods*, 1 Sam. 26:19. His meaning was not, that his enemies had ever used any such words; but that such was, or might probably be, the effect of their persecution: so that, if he should become an idolater in a strange land, the guilt of it should partly lie upon them. We have been the longer, because the vindication of the right sense in this place, may give light to, and serve for many other passages, of this book.

**Verse 12.** *{Let us swallow them up alive}* Without any sign of blood, as if they had died a natural death: or, so as no man shall know what is become of them. Thus they provide for their own safety. So they, that would have these words to be the very words of these personated enticers. But more probable, that they are Solomon's, the author, his words, taken from the effect, or event of their doings, and therefore not without cause adscribed unto them; tending to aggravation of their cruelty; thereby the more effectually to deter from their society, and fellowship. Of all kinds of death, there is none (naturally) more dreaded, than to be swallowed up quick, or alive, either by terrible monsters, as Jonas was: or, by the earth itself, suddenly gaping, and closing again, as Corah and his fellows.

*{As the grave}* The Hebrew being the very same, as Numb. 16:30, etc. is used in the history of Corah, etc. if the English word, *grave*, will not fit there; as indeed indeed it will not: I wish we had had some other word here too, which might have fitted both places: it being very probable, that in the expression here, there is some allusion to that which is there recorded. The English word there, is: *pit*: and that cannot be here (no more than *grave*, there,) because by it, the next Hebrew word, *בור* fovea: is rendered. The truth is; we have no proper English word for *שאול* in this sense; the Greek, *ἅδης*: and the Latins; infernus. Hell indeed we have, for a place of torments: but for a place common to all that are dead, and gone out of this visible world, without any reference (as *ἅδης*, and infernus, often:) to pain, or pleasure; I know none. I can say therefore, that *hell* is no proper word, in this place: but where to find a more proper, I know not. As for the word *שאול* so much, by so many learned, and unlearned, even unto books, and volumes of no small bulk, has been written of it; as that I rather expect thanks, that I can pass it over so quietly; especially, when there is no need: than reproof, as though I had been willing to spare my pains, to the prejudice of my reader. I take no notice of chapter 30:16, because I do not conceive, that there is any allusion here, to the unsatiableness of the grave, there mentioned. I note it, because some others have thought otherwise.

**Verse 14.** *{Let us all have one purse}* He shows whereby the wicked are allured to join together. Every one hopes to have part of the spoil of the innocent.

**Verse 15.** *{My son, walk not thou in the way with them}* Chapter 4:14.

*{From their path}* That is, have nothing at all to do with them, Psalm 1:1.

**Verse 16.** *{For their feet run to evil, and make haste to shed blood}* Isa. 59. Rom. 3:15.

**Verse 17.** *{Surely in vain the net is spread in the sight of any bird}* About the sense of this, and the following verses, to verse 20, and their coherence, there is no small controversy among interpreters; among whom (them that have written at large, I mean:) a man shall sooner lose himself in the variety of expositions; than find what to stick to, in that variety. If we will content ourselves with this translation we have here, and the interpretation which is grounded upon it, it has as good authority as any other; some principal rabbins, and Mercerus (the best of all expositors) his approbation. According to them, this is the sense of the words: as the net is spread in vain; though not in vain, in regard of him that spread it, who when the bird is caught, has his end in it; but in vain, in regard of the bird, which might have taken warning by this spreading of the net: as (indeed some do, and avoid it; according to that of the poet: *Quaeque nimis patent retia, vitat avis*:) that some evil was intended; but does not, for the most part being allured by the bait, which it is greedy of.
