So these, (notwithstanding the examples of so many that have perished by these unjust courses and so many gibbets, and gallows, which are set up everywhere, as nets before the birds, to warn them:) being compelled through their own covetousness, and desire of prey; they run on to destruction, as if they had *laid wait for their own blood, and lurked for their own lives*. This is their exposition, which Cornelius a Lapide also after divers others mentioned, though he term it profundiorem & involutiorem, that is, the most remote (in sight) and most intricate of all; yet he prefers before all the rest, as most agreeable to the Greek and Hebrew. For my part, to speak my mind freely, but for the authority of those I have named, it gives me very little content: remote it is, I am sure, and intricate as Cornel. a Lap. judges of it, and may agree with the Greek better than any other: but that it does so with the Hebrew too, I will not easily grant. The Hebrew words, without any violence or supplement, may be thus also translated: *Surely without cause the net is spread within the sight of any bird: Yet men lay wait for their blood, and lurk privily for their lives*: or, rather, *So they lay wait for their blood*, etc. According to this translation, this; with reference to the former verses, will be the sense: as men ordinarily (for sometimes it may be otherwise, perchance; when the net is spread to catch and destroy birds that do hurt: but that is not ordinary; whence is that of the comic, *Non rete accipitri tenditur neque milvio, Qui male faciunt nobis. Illis qui nil faciunt tenditur; Quia enim in illis fructus est; in illic opera luditur*:) without any provocation, or injury, seek to catch birds, that they may eat them: so these wicked ones spoken of before, lay in wait for the blood of innocent men, that have done them no wrong at all, for mere gain and lucre sake. This translation being so agreeable to the original; and the interpretation so proper and literal without any supplement, whereas in the former (that we have here the word *own*,) is twice to be supplied, to bring it to that sense they would have: what should make it less plausible, (though not without all authority: for some rabbins, mentioned by Mercer, be for it:) or probable, I know not; except this were it; because it was thought somewhat improper, and perchance ridiculous, that laying in wait for blood, or to speak more expressly, that the murdering of innocent men, should be compared to catching and killing of birds; which seems rather to extenuate the matter, than to aggravate it; yea in some manner, to justify it; which no man can think could be Solomon's meaning. But if this were it, indeed; there was a great mistake, and misapprehension of Solomon his aim. For certainly, the words, if rightly understood, will afford as great an aggravation, as any that is, or can be used, upon that occasion. For what can be said, or conceived more horrid of the most Cyclopean men, as poets call them, than that, they should kill men as freely, and with that indifferency, as others kill birds, not out of any malice, or revenge; but for food, which no man thinks unlawful (except it be a Pythagorean) or makes any conscience of? And so may Psalm 14:4. *As they eat bread*; be understood: that is, they make no more of it, than they do, or any man usually does, to eat a piece of bread. What Solomon does here express, under the similitude of birds, is by the prophet Habakkuk expressed by fishes; in those bitter words of his expostulation with God, in point of providence: *and holdest thy tongue*, (says he) *when the wicked devoureth the man that is more righteous than he. And makest men as the fishes of the sea, as the creeping things that have no ruler over them: They take up all of them with the angle; they catch them in their net*, etc. Hab. 1:13, 14, 15. There is another interpretation, which I make less reckoning of, than of the two former; yet for the number of expositors that follow it, not inconsiderable. They refer this, *in vain*, to the plots and ambushes of these ruffians; to this effect: as men spread the nets before the birds in vain, because the birds what by some natural sagacity, what by former experience, know well enough how to avoid it, and to frustrate the expectation of the fowler: so these wicked men, though they hunt after the blood of the innocent never so greedily, and lay their ambushes never so cunningly: yet their labor shall be in vain, and their hopes frustrated; because God himself will deliver the innocent and the righteous out of their snare, and make the mischief which they plotted against others, to fall upon their own heads, so they. There be places of Scripture enough I confess, to confirm the sense of this latter part, as Psalm 9:16 and 57:6, 9, etc. that God will deliver the righteous, etc. but how probably it may be said, or supposed to be said by Solomon, that the net (in this sense they would have) either before birds, or men; (in a general sense:) is spread in vain: and how this will cohere, and agree with the 19th verse, concerning men; and with chapter 7:23, *as a bird hasteth to the snare*, etc. let the reader consider. I said before, they are many, that follow this sense; which is all I have to say for it. There is yet another interpretation of these words, which for the acuteness of it, not altogether groundless, (for else I should not think it worthy of that title) I cannot pass by without some notice. It is Salazar's, who, to give him his due, has done very well upon the Proverbs, and is the largest that I have seen; but that he takes too much liberty, and is extravagant sometimes. First, he observes, and shows by divers examples, that unjust gain is often expressed or set out by Solomon, by similitudes taken from birds, and fowling: but the most considerable place to that purpose, is chapter 23:4, 5. *Labour not to be rich*, etc. *for riches certainly make themselves wings, and flee away as an eagle towards heaven.* Now the Hebrew here (as the margin has already told us) is, *Surely in vain the net is spread, in the eyes of everything that hath a wing.* By *everything that hath a wing*, he would have riches, (of which before, verse 13. *We shall find all precious substance; we shall fill our houses with spoil*:) to be understood. Two arguments, says he, Solomon does use to deter men from this wicked course of life; the first is, because they shall miss of that booty, which they propose and promise themselves, as their end; they shall either miss of it altogether, or not long enjoy it, because it *hath wings*, and will not long abide with them. Then secondly, while they so greedily seek after prey; not sparing innocent blood, that they may compass their desires, they at the same time *lay wait for their own blood*: their mischief will fall upon their own heads. *His own iniquities shall take the wicked himself, and he shall be laden with the cords of his sin*, Prov. 5:22.

*{In vain}* Or, *without cause*, verse 11, chapter 3:30.

*{In the sight of any bird}* Heb. *in the eyes of everything that hath a wing.*

**Verse 19.** *{Which taketh away the life of the owners thereof}* Heb. *את נפש בעליו יקח* which words may either according to the first and last interpretation which we have spoken of upon the former words, be translated, as here, *which taketh away*, etc. to which agrees in part, Prov. 15:27, *He that is greedy of gain, troubleth his own house; but he that hateth gifts, shall live.* Or, according to the second, which we conceive to be the most literal; *who taketh away*, etc. relating to the person of the covetous, *every one that is greedy of gain, who*, etc. Prov. 5:22. See the former annotation at the end.

**Verse 20.** *{Wisdom}* Heb. *wisdoms*; that is, excellent wisdom. Or, *wisdoms*, that is, all kind of wisdom: which may have reference also (as Mercer is of opinion) to the several ways of proposing wisdom in several ages, and places. *Ἡ πολυποίκιλος σοφία τοῦ θεοῦ*, *multiformis Dei sapientia*, Eph. 3:10, that is, *the manifold wisdom of God*. See also, Heb. 1:1.

*{Wisdom crieth without, she uttereth her voice in the streets.} ***21.*** {She crieth in the chief place of concourse}, etc.* See chapter 8:1, 2, 3, etc. Some think, because anciently proverbs and sentences were wont to be sung in the streets, (they might have added, and to be proposed in writing too, upon pillars, etc. whereof see in the preface) and marketplaces, that these words might have reference to that custom. It is very possible they might, to that also; but chiefly and principally, to the public schools and synagogues of the Jews, where the law of God was daily taught, and expounded, both to young and old; as many as either were studious of it themselves, or took care of their children's education. But if we take the words more generally, as extending to all men, and nations, in general; then must we say with that acute, and of all others, the most scholastical and philosophical rabbin, Levi Ben. Ghersom: this divine wisdom, says he, calls upon all mortal men with a loud voice, while it infuses itself into their souls, prompting them by certain natural instincts, motions, and propensions, to goodness and virtue, and to shun the contrary. These be those natural *ὁρμαί*, or *ἀφορμαί*; or *ἀρχαί*, as Aristotle calls them, which all ancient philosophers have acknowledged, concluding thereupon, that all vicious wicked men are damnable, (I mean it not of eternal damnation, which many of them were ignorant of) because of the neglect of these first motions and invitations of nature. Aristotle treats of it at large in his Ethics, l. 3, cap. 5, and maintains, that though it be not in the power of many wicked men, when gone very far in wickedness, and habitual sinners, to reclaim and reform themselves; yet for all that, that they are inexcusable and worthy of punishment. As one (says he, among other things to that purpose) that does cast a stone, after he has cast it, the stone is no more in his power; but he might have chosen whether he should cast it, or no: *ἡ γὰρ ἀρχὴ ἐν αὐτῷ*, for the beginning, or original power, (of casting, or not casting) was in him: *Οὕτω δὲ καὶ τῷ ἀδίκῳ καὶ τῷ ἀκολάστῳ*, *ἐξ ἀρχῆς μὲν*, *ἐξῆν τοιούτοις μὴ γενέσθαι*· *διὸ ἑκόντες εἰσί*, *γενομένοις δὲ*, *οὐκέτι ἔξεστι μὴ εἶναι*: that is, so of unjust and intemperate; it was in their power at the beginning, not to be such; and therefore they are willfully such; but now that they are become such, it is no more in their power to be otherwise. Another, in moral philosophy, not inferior to Aristotle; but in point of life, far above him; but a heathen too; expresses it thus: it is upon occasion of error and ignorance that he speaks it; for nature, (says he) had furnished him at first with certain instincts, notions, and opportunities, by the help whereof he might have attained to the knowledge of truth: which having neglected, hence it is, that he is now not able to discern that which is false, from that which is true. Ant. Med. 9. B. n. 1. Again the same Rabbi Levi, (as I find him quoted by Cornelius a Lapide; for in the copy I have, I find it not; but it should be he by the matter, and somewhat he has to that purpose upon the 23rd verse, and elsewhere) *Haec omnia, inquit, eo spectant*, etc. that is, all these words of Solomon tend to this, that the divine wisdom does most evidently and miraculously manifest itself by the works of creation, wherein his incredible wisdom, if they apply themselves to take notice of it, is incurrent into the eyes of all men. Which is in effect no more than what is affirmed by St. Paul himself, when he says; *For the invisible things of him, from the creation of the world, are clearly seen, (being understood by the things that are made, even his eternal power and Godhead; so that they are without excuse*, Rom. 1:20. See also upon Psalm 19:1, etc. *The heavens declare the glory of God*, etc. and Wisdom of Solomon, (as is commonly called; and like enough it is, that some part of it, as before was said of Ecclesiasticus, in the preface, might be Solomon's) chapter 13:1, 2, etc. and expositors upon John 1:9. *That was the true light, which lighteth every man that cometh into the world.* But now if it be asked, (and I wonder it is not, that I can find, at least, by any expositor) how all this can be reconciled with what, with so much rhetoric and eloquence (hardly to be matched) is delivered in the 28th chapter of Job, from the beginning of the chapter, to the end of it; *Surely there is a vein for the silver, and a place for gold*, etc. *But where shall wisdom be found? And where is the place of understanding? Man knoweth not the price thereof; neither is it found in the land of the living. The depth saith, it is not in me*, etc.—*Whence then cometh wisdom, and where is the place of understanding? Seeing it is hid from the eyes of all living*, etc. To this may be answered: first, if we take wisdom there, as in this place of Solomon, of such wisdom, as is requisite to lead a man to the knowledge of God, and to embrace virtue, that the book of Job, (as most are of opinion,) was written before the law was written. That Job was a stranger, (an Arab, or Idumean) no Israelite, and might complain the more, for want of those helps and prerogatives which God had afforded to his chosen people. But the most certain answer is, that that wisdom Job speaks of, is not the same, here intended and described by Solomon. By wisdom there, Job intended especially the wisdom of God in his works, and the ordering of his visible creatures: so far manifest and visible to all men, that men may by the consideration of the creatures, understand the power and wisdom of the Creator to be infinite: but otherwise, to the wisest of men so unsearchable, that there is not the least creature of the world, but is able to pose (in the exact and philosophical consideration of the nature and properties of it,) the ablest philosopher that ever was. That he intended this wisdom especially, may be gathered, in part, by verses 23, 24, etc. *God understandeth the way thereof*, etc. but especially by the last verse, and conclusion of the whole chapter; *And unto man he said, Behold, the fear of the Lord, that is wisdom: and to depart from evil, is understanding*: by which words, compared with the former from the beginning of the chapter, it does appear, that having treated hitherto of the divine wisdom, known unto God only, to man incomprehensible; he does now pass to, and conclude with that wisdom which mortal man is capable of, by God himself commended unto mankind: *And unto man he said, Behold the fear of the Lord*, etc. See also upon Jer. 9:23, 24. *Thus saith the Lord, Let not the wise man glory in his wisdom*, etc.

*{In the opening of the gates}* See upon Psalm 127, last verse, *They shall not be ashamed, but they shall speak with the enemies in the gate.*

**Verse 22.** *{Ye simple ones}* Wisdom reproves three kinds of men: simple ones, which err of ignorance; mockers, that scoff at knowledge; fools that hate instruction.

**Verse 24.** *{Because I have called, and ye refused, I have stretched out my hand, and no man regarded}* Isa. 65:12 and 66:4. Jer. 7:13. Ezek. 3:18.

*{I have stretched out my hand}* A gesture of such as made proclamation, to get attention, Isa. 65:2. Acts 21:40. Of those also, that would have one to come unto them, and again of those, that offer themselves to help one in great danger and distress. In all these senses it will well agree to the present occasion. None are in greater danger and distress; and want present succor and relief more, than stubborn fools, and infidels.

**Verse 26.** *{I also will laugh at your calamity}* This is spoken according to our capacity, signifying, that the wicked, which mock and jest at God's word, shall have the just reward of their mocking, being requited in the same kind.

**Verse 27.** *{When your fear cometh}* That is, your destruction, which you feared, chapter 10:24.

**Verse 28.** *{Then shall they call upon me, but I will not answer; they shall seek me early, but they shall not find me.}* Job 27:9. Isa. 1:15. Jer. 11:11 and 14:12. Mic. 3:4.

*{But they shall not find me}* Because they sought not with sincere affection to God, but for ease only of their own grief.

**Verse 30.** *{They would none of my counsel}* Psalm 81:11, 12.

**Verse 31.** *{Therefore shall they eat of the fruit of their own way, and be filled with their own devices}* Chapter 14:14.

*{Of the fruit of their own way}* They shall feel what commodity their wicked life shall give them.

**Verse 32.** *{For the turning away of the simple shall slay them}* Hebrew *כי משובת פתים* here in the text, *For the turning away of the simple*: may be translated also, (as in the margin,) *For the ease of the simple*. According to the first, *the turning away of the simple*, is, their refusing to hearken to *the call* of wisdom, and *her counsel*, (*Turn you at my reproof*, etc. verse 23. *Because I have called, and ye refused*, etc. verse 24. *But ye have set at nought all my counsel*, etc. verse 25.) *And the prosperity of fools*, (that is, of wicked men; whereof see upon Psalm 14:1. *The fool hath said in his heart*, etc.) shall be the cause of their utter destruction, while they looking no further than to the present, and finding that they do well in their wickedness, and enjoy themselves with comfort; either their judgment is perverted concerning themselves and their action, so that they see not what all the world besides does see, and judge of them: or if they know themselves to be wicked, yet their heart is hardened through success, and either fear nothing, persuading themselves that there is no God, or that he does not see and regard; or desperately conclude with themselves, that it is better for them, however, to enjoy present certain comforts, than to bereave themselves of them, for uncertain, whether hopes, or fears. Demosthenes, the ancient orator, has an excellent saying to this purpose; *Τὸ δ᾽ εὖ πράττειν παρὰ τὴν ἀξίαν*, says he, *ἀφορμὴ τοῦ κακῶς φρονεῖν τοῖς ἀνοήτοις γίνεται*; that is, for a man to prosper in the world above his desert, is very apt to beget strange opinions, and to pervert the mind of those who otherwise are not very wise of themselves. If instead of *turning away of the simple*, we like better to translate, *the ease of the simple*; the sense, as to the whole sentence, will come to one; only the two causes before assigned of the final and utter destruction of men, their averseness from good counsel, and their prospering in their wicked courses, will be now reduced into one, their *ease* and *prosperity*: the other, though not expressed here, may easily be supplied, and presupposed, from the former verses, as has already been showed. Some rabbins, as we are told here by Mercer, (who also calls it egregium sensum:) would have this affix pronoun, *them*, not reciprocally taken of the same; but as intended of others; to this effect: that that which makes wickedness to prevail in the world, is, the prosperity of some known impious, ungodly men: upon whom while others cast their eyes, and take notice, that notwithstanding their impiety, they thrive; nay, thrive and prosper (many of them, in sight:) by their impiety; they are encouraged to follow their examples, hoping to speed no worse, than such and such, whom they have before their eyes. This indeed is the more certain and general cause of the two. For there be many wicked men, that do not prosper in their wickedness; and yet wicked still: so that their own prosperity cannot be said to be the cause. But there would be but little, or no wickedness at all in the world, were it not for the prosperity of some, in all places, and at all times, some; but sometimes, in great number: and then does wickedness proportionably increase in the world:) whose wickedness, unworthiness, impiety, lewdness, hypocrisy, and the like, is as known and visible, as their prosperity is. And this is it that Solomon himself elsewhere does avouch it to be the cause of all evil, in these words, *Because sentence against an evil work is not executed speedily*, (that is, because wicked men, traitors, extortioners, oppressors of the poor, murderers, Epicures, and the like, are not rewarded by God in this present world, (or presently) that all men may take notice of God's justice and just judgments; but contrarily, prosper often, and become great by their wickedness,) *therefore the heart of the sons of men is fully set in them to do evil.* Eccles. 8:11. This excellent sentence of Solomon, is excellently well expressed, (yet I will not say, that he ever read him) by an ancient Greek poet, of greatest antiquity, once so famous for his sentences, that his very name (*Priusquam Theognis nasceretur*,) became a proverb. In a place where he maintains that riches well gotten, are stable; ill gotten, though for a while enjoyed, sooner or later, will at last turn to our ruin; he has these words; *Ἀλλὰ τάδ᾽ ἀνθρώπων ἀπατᾷ νόον*· *οὐ γὰρ ἐπ᾽ αὐταῖς Γίγνονται* (some editions have it, *Γίνονται*: others, *Τίνονται*, but all to one purpose,) *μάκαρες πρήγματος ἀμπλακίαις*: that is, but this is it, that commonly deceives men, because the gods do not always appear (or, show themselves) as soon as any wickedness is committed. After this, he goes on; sometimes, says he, the same man (though long after) bears the punishment: sometimes his children, and sometimes death prevents the execution, that neither he, nor his children, suffer for it. Thus is the matter answered by a heathen: how it is by Ecclesiastes, or Solomon, appears by the 12th and 13th following verses, which should not have been divided from the former by a paragraph, as though new matter there began, as in most English Bibles I have seen, it is; *Though a sinner do evil an hundred times, and his days be prolonged*, etc. of which words, as also concerning this argument, of the wicked's worldly prosperity, in general, see what has been noted upon Psalm 37:1. *Fret not thyself because of evildoers*, etc. See also Malachi 3:13, 14, etc. *Your words have been stout against me, saith the Lord*, etc. *Ye have said, It is in vain to serve the Lord*, etc. *And now we call the proud happy*, etc. to the end of that chapter.

**Verse 33.** *{Shall dwell safely}* Psalm 25:12, 13. *What man is he that feareth the Lord*: etc. and Psalm 34:12, 13, 14. *What man is he that desireth life*, etc. confirmed by St. Peter: 1 Pet. 3:10, 11. Objections, and answers concerning this matter; see the annotations there, and the references.

*{And shall be quiet from fear of evil}* See the notes upon Psalm 91. *He that dwelleth in the secret place of the most High*, etc. and Psalm 107:6. *And he delivered them out of their distresses*, etc.

## CHAPTER 2

**Verse 1.** *{My son, if thou wilt receive my words, and hide my commandments with thee}* Chapter 7:1.

*{Hide my commandments with thee}* That is, keep them in your heart, as treasure. *Keep them in the midst of thine heart*, chapter 4:21. See more upon Psalm 40:10. *I have not hid thy righteousness within my heart*; which might seem contrary, but it is not, as is there showed.

**Verse 2.** *{Apply thine heart to understanding}* If you give yourself seriously to seek the true knowledge of God without hypocrisy.

**Verse 3.** *{If thou criest after knowledge}* It is not enough to be willing to learn wisdom, but we must pray earnestly to God for it, and spare for no cost or labor, whereby it may be obtained.

*{Liftest up thy voice}* Heb. *givest thy voice.*

**Verse 4.** *{If thou seekest her as silver, and searchest for her, as for hid treasures}* Matt. 13:44.

**Verse 5.** *{Then shalt thou understand the fear of the Lord}* He had said before, chapter 1, verse 7, that *The fear of Lord is the beginning of wisdom*: now, he makes wisdom and understanding, to bring in religion and the knowledge of God; which is in effect to tell and teach, that these two are inseparable, or if you will, that true wisdom, and true knowledge, are but one thing in reality of nature, though distinguished by names. And this is a very high point, and of greatest consequence: and therefore not to be wondered, if he insist upon it with variety of expressions. For what man is he, if not much degenerated into a brute through continued lewdness and wickedness; that does not desire wisdom, and knowledge, in general? *The Jews require a sign*, says St. Paul: *and the Greeks*: what Greeks? The Gentiles he means: all the nations of the world, in general; more, or less: all men, of all nations of the world *seek after* (somewhat that they call and account) *wisdom*. 1 Cor. 1:22. We have touched upon it before, and it is acknowledged by writers of all ages and professions, that it is natural unto man to desire knowledge. If therefore men could be persuaded that all true knowledge is in *the fear of the Lord*: that all other knowledge, without the fear of the Lord, is mere vanity and folly: it would be a very strong motive to induce men (rational men,) to the pursuit of piety and religion: and to bring them unto a good opinion of it. Whence the devil on the other side, labors what he can, to possess all men with this opinion, that religion turns wise men into fools and idiots. But that such as either Solomon, or David, or others, eminent for their piety, and nearer relations unto God; should so speak of godliness and religion; is not so much to be wondered: but what should prompt ancient philosophers of greatest antiquity, as Pythagoras and Plato, and some others to deliver and maintain the same, almost in the same words? *Ἡ μὲν γὰρ τούτου γνῶσις*, says Plato, *σοφία καὶ ἀρετὴ ἀληθινή*: *ἄγνοια*, *ἀμαθία*, *καὶ κακία*: the knowledge of God, is true wisdom, and virtue: (or rather, prowess: which is the original signification of the word.) The not knowing of him is ignorance and baseness. In another place he maintains, that divinity is the only true liberal science: all other art and profession, vulgar and mechanic: his meaning is, vile and inconsiderable in comparison. Many such passages there be in ancient authors, by which it does appear, that the true wisdom, spoken of in the former chapter, was not, at no time, confined to one nation: according to the annotations there, upon verse 20. *Wisdom crieth without*, etc.

*{And find the knowledge of God}* How then says Christ: *I thank thee O Father, that thou hast hid those things from the wise and prudent, and hast revealed them unto babes.* Matt. 11:11? It will be answered, that there is a double wisdom, as practical, (of which Jer. 4:22. *They are wise to do evil, but to do good they have no knowledge*) so speculative: to wit, a worldly, and a heavenly wisdom. *From the wise and prudent* therefore; not absolutely, but according to the world; or, *according to the flesh*: as the apostle speaks, and distinguishes, 1 Cor. 1:26. *Brethren, ye see their calling, how not many wise men after the flesh, not many noble*, etc. A good answer, I grant: but not so full and satisfactory, as it may be made. For what shall we say of speculative human knowledge, and learning? Is there no use of it; no advantage, to the attaining of heavenly wisdom? Yea, very great certainly. There is no question but the consideration of nature, and moral philosophy, especially, have brought many unto Christ; have been the more visible means, I mean of their conversion; as by ancient learned fathers has been more fully disputed and demonstrated: who also make the Platonic philosophy particularly, to have been a kind of preparation to the Gospel. But here we must again distinguish. There is no kind of human literature but may further unto piety and godliness, two things being presupposed. First, that it have some relation unto God, as the author, or the end; or both, wherein men may observe great variety among ancient philosophers and wise men. Some, so frame their tenets, and whole body of doctrine; what unto moral, or natural; that they altogether either by silence, or direct assertion, excluded God, and his worship. Others again, though they had no knowledge of the true God, and his worship by revelation, no knowledge of the Scriptures; yet by the advantage of their natural light were guided to refer all unto God, and to divine worship: they will acknowledge him the author of all goodness, happiness, and virtue: and consequently thought themselves obliged to worship and serve him. The second thing is, humility: I do not mean that humility, that Christians teach: proposed unto us by Christ as the crown of virtue; but such humility I mean, as wise heathens at all times acknowledged, and commended for a virtue, and a principal help to the acquisition of truth: otherwise by them termed, modesty: which makes a man sensible of his own defects and ignorance; and willing to embrace any means offered unto him to benefit himself in the pursuit of truth: opposed unto that vice, or kind of pride (for it is not pride in general) which they most properly call *δοκησισοφία*: or, self-conceitedness. This was Socrates (so famous and venerable in all ages among heathens: as never was any man before, or after him:) his business, and all his philosophy tended unto this, to teach men that they knew nothing, as they ought to know; and they least, that most took upon themselves. As for himself, all he could brag of, was; that he knew himself to know nothing. Moreover, that all right knowledge, must be expected from God. Indeed, human knowledge of itself, is very apt to *puff up*; as the apostle speaks, 1 Cor. 8:7, and when a man is once *puffed up*, he becomes incapable, as to reality of wisdom, of any further proficiency. In such a case, an idiot, a plain rustic, has the advantage of him. Otherwise, as I have said, knowledge, and learning of itself, is a very great advantage.

**Verse 6.** *{For the Lord giveth wisdom: out of his mouth cometh knowledge and understanding}* 1 Kings 3:9. James 1:5. This was a very controverted point among the wise heathens, and divers learned treatises and books (whereof some yet extant) have been written about it. The Platonists affirmed, that virtue and wisdom, were the gift of God: the Stoics denied it, whence is that horrid blasphemy of Seneca and some others of that profession, that a wise man excelled God himself, because God was good and wise by necessity of nature; man, by free election. See more upon the former words.

**Verse 7.** *{He layeth up sound wisdom for the righteous}* Heb. *תושיה* here translated *sound wisdom*, properly signifies *essentiam*, *substantiam*, (*οὐσίαν* in Greek) that is, substance, essence, or whatsoever has reality of true being. Mercerus translates it here *essentiam*; Junius, *quicquid revera est*; that is, whatsoever has reality of being. Another equivalent word to this (as by some rabbins is well observed here) which also comes from the same *radix*, is *יש*, which word we have, chapter 8:21, to the same purpose, as *תושיה* is here. *That I may cause those that love me, to inherit* (*יש*) *substance: and I will fill their treasures.* The word is there translated by Mercer, esse perpetuum; that is, an everlasting being: by Junius, *id quod est*; that which is: expounded by him in his notes, *quod nunquam esse desinit*; that never ends. Much might here be said out of Plato and other ancient philosophers, concerning the nature and properties of true being; who bring many arguments to show, that nothing that is material and visible; nothing that is temporary, has a reality of being, or existence, properly, but an appearance only; or, at the most, an improper, imperfect, equivocal (as logicians are wont to speak) being, of another kind; and quite different from true being: to which purpose, Seneca, *Quaecunque videmus ac tangimus*; and presently after, *Omnia ista quae sensibus serviunt, quae nos accendunt & irritant, negat Plato ex iis esse, quae vere sint.* Some Greek fathers also (as well as philosophers) are very copious upon this subject, showing, that no worldly things have that *ὑπάρχειν*, that implies reality of life or being: St. Chrysostom, among others, has bestowed much of his eloquence upon it. How well all this does agree with the Scriptures, which call God, Jehovah, as if you should say, He that is, and of all others, make that his chiefest name and title; might easily be showed, and confirmed by many reasons and authorities if need were. But because it is a high point, which all men are not so capable of, we shall content ourselves with some plain passages, which may, in part, suffice us, to ground that interpretation of those words of Solomon upon, which we shall commend unto the reader. First, St. Paul says *We look not at the things which are seen, but at the things which are not seen: for the things which are seen, are temporal*; (*πρόσκαιρα*; temporary; but for a time, properly) *but the things which are not seen, are eternal*. Secondly, the same apostle (it is the opinion of many ancients, that he is the author of that epistle, though it is much opposed by some others) in his epistle to the Hebrews, chapter 10:34, does most elegantly oppose *τὰ ὑπάρχοντα* (as commonly but most improperly, even in the judgment of divers ancient heathens, called) that is; worldly goods, wealth, or substance; to that *ὕπαρξις*, (so he calls it; which is the very word of Solomon, *יש* or, *תושיה* interpreted in Greek) or, substance which is permanent and eternal. *For ye had compassion of me in my bonds*, says he; *and took joyfully the spoiling of your goods*, (*τὴν ἁρπαγὴν τῶν ὑπαρχόντων*) *knowing in yourselves, that ye have in heaven* (*κρείττονα ὕπαρξιν ἐν οὐρανοῖς*) *a better and enduring substance*. Then lastly, Solomon himself, of riches and worldly goods, says directly, that they are not, chapter 23:5. *Wilt thou set thine eyes upon that which is not? For riches*, etc. just as the ancient Greek philosophers, and others, speak of them when they style them, *ἀνύπαρκτα*; things that are not. All these things, put together, I conclude, that by *תושיה* in this place, and by *יש* in that other, Solomon does intend somewhat permanent and eternal, that may truly be said, to have reality of existence, in opposition to transitory worldly goods. And what should that be, but eternal life? Most rabbins, I know, by these words, understand the law, which is in effect, as much, as *sound wisdom*, because sound wisdom, is an effect of the knowledge of the law: but that sense is not so proper here, neither to the place, nor to the word, taken in its propriety, neither is there want of rabbins, that have embraced this sense; which I prefer before others. The author of Zohar, (a book of great credit among the Jews: and of as great antiquity, if they may be believed; for which I will not pass my word) has these words: what is *יש*? That world which is to come, which shall never end. And certain it is, that accordingly, the word is used by some ancient rabbins, *pro futuro seculo*; that is, for eternal life. But it is not their authority that I trust to, or would have my reader to ground upon: if there be not reason enough for this interpretation besides, I shall not press him to embrace it. But such passages as speak, though but obscurely, of eternal life and rewards, I would make much of; because it is a subject in those days, among the Jews, but sparingly spoken of: whereof, see upon Psalm 49 and 37. Some further confirmation of this interpretation, the LXX also do afford, who render the word here by *σωτηρίαν*: salvation. It is thought by some that they read it, not *תושיה* as we have it; but *תשועה* from *ישע* salvavit, etc. which though plausible, at first hearing; yet well considered, may prove more plausible than true. For who knows not, that the LXX do often express the sense, which they apprehended to be chiefly intended; though it be much further sometimes from the very letter of the original, than *σωτηρία*, is from *תושיה* so that I think we need not go to *תשועה* for it; though it be granted that it is more proper to it, than to the former: yea, and granted too, that there be many examples of such whether mistakes, or varieties, in the Greek translation, that now goes under that name. See also concerning the verb *צפן* here used, in the next note. Nevertheless, if any man like better, by *תושיה* here, to understand *sound wisdom*, because that is conceived the most ordinary signification, that will best agree with most places; and that most expositors are for it here too; I am not against it. More concerning the word *תושיה*, and its different significations, see upon chapter 18:1. *Through desire*, etc.

*{He layeth up sound wisdom for the righteous}* *יצפן*. *He layeth up*; or *hideth*. I will not press either, as an argument for what has been said before of *תושיה*: I know the expression may be proper enough concerning worldly goods, or things (whether wisdom or wealth) that are enjoyed in this world: yet it may be some confirmation to my interpretation of that word in this place, that it is as proper, if not more, of things invisible and eternal; and so used by David, Psalm 31:19. *O how great is thy goodness, which thou hast laid up for them that fear thee!* And by St. Paul, more than once; as where he says, *for the hope which is laid up for you*, Col. 1:5, and, *there is laid up for me a crown of righteousness*, 2 Tim. 4:8.

*{He is a buckler to them that walk uprightly}* That under his protection they may overcome, (Rev. 2:7, etc. or *be faithful unto death, and so receive a crown of life*, Rev. 2:10 and 3:11. But because the verb substantive (as commonly called) *he is*, is not in the original; *buckler* is by some referred by apposition, as grammarians speak, to *תושיה* by which if we understand *sound wisdom*, or doctrine, the case is clear. See verse 11, *Discretion shall preserve thee*, etc. See also chapter 1, verse 24 to the end. And so the apostle, *Above all taking the shield of faith, wherewith ye shall be able to quench all the fiery darts of the wicked*, Eph. 6:16. But if eternal life; as we have argued it there; how is eternal life a buckler? I answer, this construction is but the conjecture of some, which we were not bound to take notice of; much less to embrace it; which scarce any man does. However, if we were bound to it, or any man like it better, than that which is more generally received; it will be no very difficult thing. First, Gen. 15:1. *I am thy shield, and thy exceeding great reward*, says God to Abraham, I will not question the sense, of *shield*, there, I only observe that *shield*, and *reward* are joined together, as *buckler*, and eternity, or eternal salvation (an exceeding great reward, indeed, and what is, to speak properly, but eternity? *καθ᾽ ὑπερβολὴν εἰς ὑπερβολὴν* 2 Cor. 4:17.) here. But then, Eph. 6:17 *the helmet of salvation*, we have the words of the apostle. And if we may say the helmet, why not as properly, the buckler of salvation, or eternity, and in very deed, the hope of salvation, or eternal life, is a great buckler, in time of distress, when all other helps do fail. *Therefore we faint not*, says the apostle, 2 Cor. 4:16, and Christ himself, (our example, *the author and finisher of our faith*: (*for the joy that was set before him, endured the cross*, etc. Heb. 12:2.

*{That walk uprightly}* A godly life is the best means, as to obtain, so to preserve a sound faith; of which depends eternal life. *If any man will do his will, he shall know of the doctrine*, etc. John 7:17. And, *He that keepeth the law of the Lord, getteth understanding thereof*, Eccles. 21:11. That it preserves also, St. Paul witnesses; *Holding faith and a good conscience, which some having put away*, (a good conscience) *κατὰ τὴν πίστιν ἐναυάγησαν*, *have made shipwreck of their faith*; or, concerning their faith, 1 Tim. 1:19. Thus in reference to, and pursuance of that interpretation of the word *תושיה* (of which upon the former words) may these words be interpreted. But if any had rather understand this, *buckler*, here, of protection from temporal evils, I am not against it; neither will it be strange; that promises belonging to this life, and that which is to come, should be put in one verse, or comprehended (as we deny not both *יש* and *תושיה* may bear likewise) in the same words.

**Verse 8.** *{He keepeth}* Heb. *to keep*, and accordingly as we translate *to keep*, or *he keepeth* (both which the Hebrew will bear:) the sense will be different. If, *to keep*; then it belongs unto *his saints*, and the meaning will be, that God does so protect and preserve the godly; or those that are his, and so effectually guide them by his Holy Spirit, that they are able *to keep the paths of judgment*: that is, the right way, both in matter of action, and belief. An obvious sense, often occurring in all parts of Scripture. But if, *he keepeth*: (which the coherence with the next words does seem to require:) then it must be referred unto God himself, and the sense will be, that God by protecting the godly, does manifest his justice unto men. For *God is known by the judgment which he executeth*: Psalm 9:16. So there; and Psalm 7:7, *return thou on high*. But this protection of God, with what limitations to be understood, see upon chapter 1:33, *shall dwell safely*; and the references there.

*{And preserveth the way of his saints}* *חסידו* in the original; of which see upon Psalm 86:2. *For I am holy*, etc. The word in this sense, is taken for mercy, not passive, but active, as Mercer notes here; who translates it misericordes; the merciful. Those therefore that delight in cruelty should not, of all men, take that title upon themselves, as those murderers in St. Augustine's time, (otherwise called *circumcelliones*) did. Optatus Milevit., in his third book against the Donatists, describes them also, and their actions. However, both significations of the word (active, and passive) may do well enough in this place. See the reference.

**Verse 9.** *{Then shalt thou understand righteousness and judgment, and equity; yea, every good path}* Chapter 1:3. The same word *צדק* in the original is there translated *justice*, here *righteousness*. I do not apprehend any great difference between justice and righteousness: to me, it is all one, which of the two is used. But men sometimes, that affect subtlety, find great mysteries in these differences, or varieties, for which there is no ground at all in the original: which might be prevented, if the same word were kept, when there is no need of variety.

*{Then shalt thou understand righteousness}* As wisdom will teach us how to carry ourselves well towards God, verse 5. So likewise towards man, in this verse: for holy hearts are best fitted to understand holy things, Psalm 25:12. John 7:17.

*{And judgment}* If we refer this to practical wisdom, particularly, which we call prudence: and other words here used, to different qualifications and abilities, as some do; whereof has been spoken before, upon chapter 1:2, 4, etc. where it is showed that there is no necessity of distinguishing them, and for what reason, so multiplied: yet I say, if we make a difference, it must be noted, that wisdom being a very general thing, which comprehends many species of different natures; Solomon's intention is not, that whosoever applies himself to godliness, or the *fear of God*, (that is, true wisdom:) shall be made partaker of all gifts, and graces belonging to wisdom in general: which is seldom, if ever, seen in one and the same man: but rather, to set out the excellency of wisdom in general: by these several gifts and graces, which those that apply themselves to it, reap; some one (besides the main, salvation:) some another: some more, some less, even as it seems best unto God, for every man's particular good, with a respect to the whole, or to the general: as it is at large prosecuted and disputed by St. Paul: 1 Cor. 12, chapter, and to the Ephesians, chapter 4. *There are diversities of gifts*, says he, *but the same Spirit*. Where, by *Spirit*, is intended by him, what is here comprehended by Solomon, under the notion of *wisdom*.

**Verse 10.** *{When wisdom entereth into thine heart}* This is very proper and emphatical. For a man may talk of wisdom; yea and be able to discourse of it, of the nature and properties; largely and learnedly and to write of it too, with commendation; as we know there be that preach, very plausibly, who are or have been known very lewd, (the more the pity such should be suffered) in their lives and conversations: and the reason is, because they have it well in their tongues, and in their brains what they have of it; but nothing in their hearts. And so long as that is not affected, our wisdom may do good unto others perchance, and sometimes, more hurt, than good: but as to ourselves, there is nothing done yet: nay our case is more lamentable and dangerous, than if we had no knowledge at all. So ancient philosophers speak too: Sen. *Consol. ad Helv.* c. 16. *Jacta sunt disciplinarum omnium fundamenta. Nunc ad illas revertere, tutum te praestabunt illae—si bona fide animum tuum intraverint*, etc. and so others frequently.

*{Knowledge pleasant}* Psalm 19:10 and Psalm 119:183. There is a superficial light pleasure, and rejoicing of heart, which many find at the very first entrance into knowledge: which as it comes suddenly, so is apt to vanish as suddenly. Of which Christ speaks in the parable of the sower. *They on the rock are they, which when they hear, receive the word with joy: and those have no root, which for a time believe and in time of temptation fall away*, Luke 8:13. But the pleasure that arises from sound knowledge, as it is not so easily found, so is it of a more solid nature; and though subject to many interruptions, yet not utterly failable, while knowledge lasts: and is a kind of firstfruits, or imperfect taste of those future joys in heaven, when we shall have attained to fullness of knowledge, (when imperfect knowledge, such as we are capable of in this world, *shall vanish away*. 1 Cor. 13:8.) in the sight of God.

**Verse 11.** *{Discretion shall preserve thee}* It shall teach you how to govern yourself, and to keep you from sinful ways, and dangers thereupon ensuing.

**Verse 13.** *{Who leave the paths of uprightness}* The right ways prescribed in God's word, which is the only light, they leave to follow their own fancies.

*{The ways of darkness}* John 3:19, 20. Eph. 5:11.

**Verse 14.** *{And delight in the frowardness of the wicked}* Perversely wicked, walking cross to God and good men, Lev. 26:21. Psalm 18:27.

**Verse 15.** *{Whose ways are crooked, and they froward in their paths}* Or, *and they crooked in their paths*. So Merc. *Quorum viae perversae sunt, & perversi sunt in viis suis.* I confess the Hebrew words for *perverse* in the first member, and *froward*, (or, perverse:) in the second, are different words, but, of one and the same signification, according to the exposition of the learned Jews. Now the 13, 14 and 15 verse (which is this) seem to tend most to one thing, to set out a perverse man: or rather, the crookedness and perversity of a wicked man, that has not the fear of God in his heart, nor makes any conscience; who has no rule to walk by, but his own lusts and desires. Solomon sets out divers words and expressions, the crookedness of their ways, that are such: a thing much insisted upon by ancient philosophers also: who also express it with much varieties, and divers similitudes: wherein (as in many other things) the good agreement of the authors, who are most copious upon it. Seneca: *Maximum judicium est malae mentis, fluctuatio, & inter simulationem virtutum, amoremque vitiorum, assidua jactatio.* And in another place: *Quid porro, nonne nunc quoque (etiamsi parum sentiatis) turbo quidam animos vestros rotat, & involvit fugientes petentesque eadem; & nunc in sublime allevatos; nunc in infima allisos rapit?* Besides many other places of the same author, to the same purpose. But there is somewhat in Solomon's words, which in a profane author whether poet, or orator would have been looked upon perchance as a piece of great wit and much commended. For while in this verse he describes, (he does endeavor to set out, I would say should I speak of a common author) the perverseness and crookedness of wicked men, he has made his speech or words somewhat like unto them, through a notable incongruity; joining a feminine (*ארחותיהם* and *עקשים*) and a masculine together. I know what is said by some to reconcile the words to grammar: but with such liberty, that a man may say there is no such thing at all as incongruity in any writer, or speaker. Now that Solomon made it so of purpose, to imitate them whom he speaks of (a thing done by divers authors,) I will not undertake: that it was an oversight, or a mistake in him, I hope no man (though some such, yea greater, if greater can be, have been observed, by learned men, in best authors) can be so absurd as to imagine. I thought I was bound to observe it: let the reader make what use of it he thinks best, with sobriety.

**Verse 16.** *{To deliver thee}* As discretion will deliver us from evil men, verse 12, so likewise from evil women, in this verse.

*{From the strange woman}* That is not your wife, no: you may not meddle with. So strange fire, Numb. 3:4. And no stranger (to wit, none that is not a Levite,) to meddle with the tabernacle; Numb. 1:51. And, *from the stranger*, (as follows here) that is, who is as bad as one of a strange nation, whom God had forbidden them to touch: 1 Kings 11:12. Or it may be, that such as prostituted themselves publicly among the Jews, being for the most part strangers indeed; that is, of another country, by birth, and other relations (we have the story of one, named Cozbi, a Midianish woman, Numb. 25.) from thence afterwards, in process of time, any public whore, or strumpet in general, whether a Jew, or a stranger, might be so called. So *זונה* in Hebrew, signifies both an hostess, and a whore, though some fetch it, according to difference of significations: from different primitives: yet it is but one word: and originally, as I conceive, signified, an hostess. And the English *harlot*, everybody knows, was once the name of a woman, a proper name, originally. See also upon chapter 7:20. *He hath taken a bag of money.* Now, what is here, as also chapter 5, and elsewhere, concerning an adulterous woman, is by Rabbi Solomon expounded of idolatry, superstition, heresy, false opinions, and the like, by which a man is seduced from the true knowledge and worship of God. Other rabbins, as Ben Gerson upon this place, and Rabbi Ben Maimon the most famous, (and not without cause) of all rabbins, in his excellent *More Nevochim*; apply to the material part of man, that is, to the body, and senses: or to the fancy, or imaginative faculty, by which (for they all come to one) the mind, or understanding faculty, which by God was appointed to rule and to govern, (therefore called by the Greek philosophers, *τὸ ἡγεμονικόν*, that is, vis, or, facultas imperatrix:) is by secret enticements in most men undermined, and enthralled: which in very deed (being so weakened by Adam's fall) is the original cause of all mischief, and wickedness in all men. To this ancient philosophers also agree, who though they knew not of Adam's fall, nor whence, originally, proceeded the weakness of reason, (his better part) in man, so apt to be seduced, and to yield itself to the senses; yet they generally ascribed the ground of all disorder to the material part, which they called *ὕλην*: and spoke of the baits and allurements of it, much to the same purpose, as Solomon does here in the person of a woman. This is also very excellently well prosecuted by an ancient holy man and martyr, called *Maximus Monachus*, in his divine meditations, which he inscribed, *Κεφάλαια*. Now to say with some, that Solomon in all this, had no further aim, than to dissuade from fornication and adultery, taken literally and properly; truly I think were to do him some wrong. It cannot be that so wise a man, so well acquainted with ancient parables and allegories, in so many reiterated expressions, should have no thought at all of a thing (to a philosophical contemplative man,) so obvious; and in very deed in an allegorical way, of all allegories I ever met with, most proper and natural. Yet that this was his primary aim or intention, considering to whom and for whose use he wrote these proverbs, I would not say: nor make it the literal immediate sense, as some do. As for that objection in Rabbi Sol. that this were no great commendation of the law of God, if it did only deliver from fornication and adultery; we answer; first, that it does not follow from the words here, or any other, where the same matter is spoken of. Why is this here more particularly mentioned, and insisted upon, has already been showed in the preface. Besides, that fornication and adultery, (especially by a strange woman, we understand properly, one that is of another country) might, by consequent, and so literally, include idolatry, and heresy, we easily grant: as it fell out in Solomon himself, who by *strange women* (his wives and concubines:) was drawn to idolatry in his old age, 1 Kings 11:4, etc. No wonder therefore if Solomon insist upon it often both for the foulness of the crime, in itself, altogether incompatible with true divine wisdom; and for the consequence of it, in these considerations: in this introduction especially, (these first eight or nine chapters as we have said before:) which was more particularly intended for them, who by reason of their youth were but strangers to the business of the world, and were yet to choose their way, (that of pleasure, or that of virtue:) as their own good genius, and God's Spirit should direct them. See also upon chapter 9:11, 13, *A foolish woman*, etc.

*{Even from the stranger which flattereth with her words}* Chapter 5:3 and 7:5.

**Verse 17.** *{The guide of her youth}* That is, her husband, who married her in her youth, whose office is to guide her, and to whom she ought to be subject, 1 Pet. 3:7. The Hebrew word *אלוף* does properly signify a captain of thousand men: whereupon some have grounded a conceit, that may pass perchance for a jest, or a piece of wit; but must not be seriously entertained by any sober man; to wit, that a young woman's husband was called *אלוף* because it was as hard a business for a man to rule a young woman, as to rule a thousand men, or soldiers. But that jest will be spoiled, if the truth be known, which is fit to be known however, to prevent the like conceits to which many are given, and oftentimes deduce great matters from them, affecting to build upon sand rather, than upon firm ground. First then we say, that though *אלוף* be most properly *χιλίαρχος* (a captain of 1000) and so rendered by the LXX in many places: yet more generally, it also signifies a captain absolutely, any captain, or guide without any reference to 1000. *ἡγεμών*: as by the LXX is expressed. I will not waste time in examples of either, which are very obvious. But secondly, we say that the same word, from the same root, but in another signification, signifies a friend, an intimate friend: from *אלף* copulare, to join together, which signification though it have lost in the Hebrew tongue, (as it has happened to many, or most Hebrew words, for want of store of books written in the Hebrew tongue, when it was purely spoken:) yet it still retains it in the Arabic (a dialect of the Hebrew:) as by some learned men has been showed. So that even here in this place, the Hebrew word might have been translated *friend*, as well as *guide*, and more truly, perchance, as chapter 16:28 and chapter 17:9, we find it not in the Chaldee paraphrase only, and Greek version: (observed long ago by Mercer:) but in others also, exhibited in that inestimable and unparalleled Bible, set out by Dr. Walton, in London: yea in our English Bible too *chief friends*, in the first place: *very friends*, in the second. And were this a fit place, I think I could argue it unto probability, that the very word *φίλος* in Greek, came from the Hebrew. Now, *the friend of her youth*, is very emphatical, because, naturally, those friends are dearest one to another, who have been such in their youths. *Veterrimae quaeque amicitiae (ut ea vina quae vetustatem ferunt) debent esse suavissimae*, says Cicero.

*{The covenant of her God}* The promise made in marriage, whereof God is both the author, and witness, Mal. 2:14 and Ezek. 16:60. Jer. 2:2, by way of allusion.

**Verse 18.** *{Her house inclineth unto death}* The courses used in her house, bring men to untimely ends, chapter 7:27.

*{Unto the dead}* Heb. *רפאים* as Psalm 88:10. The word signifies properly loosened: as in faintness: or dissolved. And so the Grecians also, by *κεκμηότες*, (properly), faint, or weary;) sometimes, understand the dead. See also upon 21:16, *shall remain*.

**Verse 19.** *{None that go unto her return again}* Those that are so entangled seldom get free again, Eccles. 7:26.

**Verse 20.** *{That thou mayest walk in the way of good men}* Chapter 13:20. Discretion will keep you from evil men verse 12, and evil women, verse 16, that you may walk in good ways, in this verse. Or if this coherence seem too remote, (though if we make a parenthesis of the 13, 14 and 15 verses: and another, of the 17, 18, 19, which I think must be, it will not appear so:) we must (with some other) supply the sense with some such thing: [Take good notice of what I have said;] or, [To this end I tell it you;] *that thou mayest walk*, etc. See also upon chapter 1:5, 6, *shall attain unto wise counsels. To understand*, etc. The Arabic translation of this verse may seem very strange; being to this effect; *for if they had walked (or, gone,) in straight paths, truly they had found, (or, they would have thought) the ways of righteousness smooth: (or, plain.)* But we may cease to wonder, if we take notice, that this is not made of the Hebrew text; but out of the translation of the Septuagint, as we commonly call them: whose words are, *Εἰ γὰρ ἐπορεύοντο τρίβους ἀγαθὰς*, *εὕροσαν ἂν τρίβους δικαιοσύνης λείας*. So before, verse 17, where we have it out of the Hebrew, *which forsaketh the guide of her youth*, etc. the Greek there is, *Υἱέ μὴ σε καταλάβῃ κακὴ βουλή*· *ἡ ἀπολιποῦσα διδασκαλίαν νεότητος*, *καὶ διαθήκην θείαν ἐπιλελησμένη*: that is, *my son, let not evil counsel take hold of thee, or, surprise thee:) which forsaketh the doctrine (or teaching) of youth, and forgetteth the divine covenant.* And just so the Arabic, but that instead of *νεότητος*, (which nothing could be made of:) read it, as I conceive [*νεότος*?], or, *νοετότητος*, for *νοέσεως* (supposing perchance, that from *νοετὸς*, a substantive in *της*, (as from *πιθανός*, *πιθανότης*, etc.) might be formed. However, we may not infer upon this, that therefore the Arabic (in this book of Proverbs) is altogether out of the Greek: except we shall say the authors of it have taken a great liberty even upon the Septuagint themselves: as may presently appear by the 2nd verse of the first chapter, *Abominanda res est*, etc. compared with the Greek, *Ὅσον ἂν χρόνον*, etc. I shall forbear such observations henceforth, which I do not conceive so necessary for these annotations. However this warning here I thought would not be amiss for their sakes that are so happy as to have the use of the said Bible, and know how to use it.

**Verse 21.** *{For the upright shall dwell in the land, and the perfect shall remain in it}, etc.* See upon Psalm 37:2, *For they shall soon be cut down*, etc. and more generally concerning temporal rewards and promises, upon the first verse of that psalm, *Fret not thyself because of evildoers*, etc.

*{The upright shall dwell in the land}* They shall prosper in the world, when wicked men, especially adulterers, waste their estate, and come to nothing, chapter 6:26. So it did often fall out in those days and does yet: but see the references upon the former words.

*{The perfect shall remain in it}* Heb. *shall be left in it*: to wit, when wicked men come to untimely end.

**Verse 22.** *{But the wicked shall be cut off from the earth, and the transgressors shall be rooted out of it}* Job 18:17. Psalm 104:35. See the references upon verse 21.

*{Rooted out}* Or, *plucked up*.

## CHAPTER 3

**Verse 1.** *{Forget not my law}* Forget not to do it, for God accounts those that do not his law, forgetters of it.

*{But let thine heart keep my commandments}* Deut. 8:1 and 30:16.

**Verse 2.** *{Length of days}* Long life is the blessing of God, which he gives to his so far forth, as it is expedient for them, Psalm 91:116. See the references upon chapter 22:21.

*{Long life}* Heb. *years of life*.

*{And peace shall they add to thee}* Psalm 119:165.

**Verse 3.** *{Let not mercy and truth forsake thee}* Deal mercifully and truly with all men. So we find these two joined together in divers places of Scripture, as Hosea 4:1. *Because there is no truth, nor mercy, nor knowledge of God in the land*; and in this book of Proverbs, as chapter 20:28, *Mercy and truth preserve the king.* Some men may be naturally compassionate; not out of conscience, or respect to God; but out of some natural tenderness: who therefore in their dealings with men, stick not at anything that is for their advantage, be it just, or unjust. Others again, make great boast of justice, and seem to be very strict and punctual in their dealings: but their fault is, that they are all justice, without any tenderness or compassion, be the case or cause never so favorable, in the eyes of other men. They please themselves in their rigor and inexorableness, as though that were an argument to them of their justice, whereas, in such cases, the old saying proves commonly true: Summum jus, summa injuria, that is, justice without mercy, (in due time and place) is greatest injustice. It is not therefore without great cause that these are so often joined together, *mercy and truth*; which if we separate in our practice, we may be merciful, or just and true, in our own conceits, or in the opinion of some: but in very truth, and in the eyes of God, we are neither. For since that God has so joined them, there is the same obligation for both: so that either of them, without the other, is not, nor can be in obedience to God, (it is but mere delusion to think so:) but out of some other cause. There be who by these words would have the whole duty of man comprehended, referring mercy, unto men; that is, to our dealings with men: and truth unto God: that is, to those duties, that have more immediate reference unto God; as faith, and a good conscience towards God. Indeed, a man may be both merciful and true, (or just,) in outward appearance, to get this praise of men; or for some other worldly end; hypocritically, not sincerely; or perchance, out of a good nature (in some degree) and by the advantage of good education, more really and sincerely; yet not truly, and acceptably before God; except it be for God's sake, principally, and in obedience to his commandments. See more to this purpose upon chapter 1:7. *The fear of the Lord*, etc. This sense is good, but not so proper here, as the former in my judgment. But there is yet a far different sense made of the words, by some chief expositors, as Mercerus, and others. They refer both these, *mercy and truth*, unto God, not as the object, but as the author, or fountain of both. And so indeed we find them, so joined, spoken of God. So in the Psalms often; and elsewhere. So in this book too: *Do they not err, that devise evil? But mercy and truth shall be to them that devise good*, chapter 14:22. And in what sense adscribed unto God, see upon Psalm 85:7 and elsewhere. Then the words (if we follow this sense) must be translated: *Mercy and truth shall not forsake thee. Bind them*, etc. But neither is the sense so proper to the coherence; nor so natural to the *particula*, *אל*, which rather implies an imperative. We shall therefore forbear to say more.

*{Bind them about thy neck}* Exod. 13:9. Deut. 6:6, 7, 8. Chapter 2:9. Keep those virtues as most precious jewels.

*{Upon the table of thine heart}* Have them ever in remembrance. It was the custom of many nations, to engrave laws, and other public acts of consequence upon tables of brass, or stone, to make them more authentic, and more lasting, and inviolable. *Nec verba minantia* (that is, laws,) *fixo aere ligabantur*, says the poet. In allusion to this custom must be understood the words of Jeremiah. *The sin of Judah is written with a pen of iron, and with the point of a diamond: it is graven upon the table of their heart*, etc. Jer. 17:1. See also 2 Cor. 3:3 and Job 19:23, 24. See also upon chapter 2:10. *When wisdom entereth into thine heart.*

**Verse 4.** *{So shalt thou find favour, and good understanding in the sight of God and man}* In the Hebrew an imperative: *so find thou favour, etc.* See Gen. 42:18. *This do and live*: that is, *and ye shall live.* And chapter 4:4. *Keep my commandments, and live.* It is ordinary in the Hebrew, and in other tongues too, usual enough.

*{So shalt thou find favour}* With God especially; that being the chief thing; yea the only necessary thing: *My grace is sufficient for thee*: 2 Cor. 10:9, yet, often, with men also: as of Christ, Luke 2:54. And, more generally, in this book, chapter 6:7. *When man's ways please the Lord, he maketh even his enemies to be at peace with himself.* And some observe here the propriety of the verb, *to find*, upon which they infer, that as a thing is properly found (*εὕρημα* in the Greek) that was not sought, or expected; so the grace of God, and his gifts, are altogether free, without any precedent meritorious acts of ours. The doctrine is good; but if it had no better ground, I would not oblige any man to believe it. It may seem, to put us in mind, not to ground our faith upon.

*{Good understanding}* Or, *good success*. The Hebrew verb, *שכל* not in different conjugations only, but even in the same, does signify to be wise: to be endowed with good understanding; and, to prosper, or to have success. And there is great reason for this communication of significations, because of the near dependency in the things themselves. That *good understanding*, (that is, prudence, and discretion:) does often beget good success, no man makes any question; and that want of understanding, is the overthrow of many public and private estates. But on the other side, that good success, which (sometimes by a stronger power is cast upon weak, yea, wicked attempts,) does entitle rashness and folly, yea mere madness sometimes, to wisdom and prudence in the opinion of the world, is the observation of many ancient historians, and other approved judicious writers. Among other, Demosthenes, has this observation: *Αἱ γὰρ εὐπραξίαι δειναὶ συγκρύψαι καὶ σκιάσαι τὰς ἁμαρτίας τῶν ἀνθρώπων*, etc. that is, prosperity and good success, is of great force to hide and shadow the faults of men: but if once they begin to trip, then all such things are discovered and come to open light. So that one word may very well signify both, and they must be men of solid judgments, that can discern between good success, by chance, and real understanding and prudence: and upright honest men too, that they be not carried, with the current, and suffer their judgments (as most men, when wicked men rule:) to be overswayed, and, in time, blinded by their affections. And in this place particularly, *good success* may do very well, since all men know, that not only God's favor, really is the cause of good success, for the most part: but even the opinion of it, though grounded upon error, or delusion, does often not a little prevail to that effect.

*{In the sight of God and man}* Some refer these words to *favour*; because often so joined in Scripture: and then bring in *good understanding*, after. I confess there be examples of such trajections; (as they call them) in Scripture; some that may be thought as harsh as this. But then we must say withal, that necessity of admitting such trajections in such places, does appear more, than it does here. All men that are wise, or endowed with a good understanding, are not known to be so, or generally accounted so. The credit of wisdom, in the world, is a thing that goes as much by favor, and good luck, as anything that I know. See what has been said of *good success*, upon the former words. Besides, envy and malice do often blast the credit of men of great ability; and there be artificers in the world, to cry up them for able and honest, that are most unworthy of any such commendation: even those sometimes, who if they had their due, a halter, and gibbet, would be their reward. *Ille crucem sceleris pretium tulit, hic, diadema.* God's favor may alter the case, and bring men, (those that fear, and serve him, cordially, when it is for their good) into credit: sometimes, it is an effect of God's great favor, that they are not (though best deserving) more regarded by men.

**Verse 5.** *{Trust in the Lord with all thine heart}* This *with all thine heart*, is of great force here; and very emphatical. First, because it is a principal duty of religion, and God is said *to take pleasure* in them, that trust to him, or *hope in his mercy*, Psalm 147:11, to endear this duty unto us. Secondly, *with all thine heart*, does import an earnest striving, to the utmost of our powers, against all impediments, and temptations to the contrary, and suggestions of the flesh, or sense. Such was Job's trust, and such his resolution, when he said, *though he slay me, yet will I trust in him*, Job 13:15. Such David's, when he said, that he would *not fear, though the earth should be removed*, etc. Psalm 46, and such the prophet Habakkuk's, when he said, *Though the fig tree shall not blossom, neither shall fruit be in the vines*, etc. Yet he *would rejoice in the Lord; he would joy in the God of his salvation*. Hab., last verses. These things may be thought impossible, by them that know not experimentally, what it is to trust, *with all their hearts*. Even in worldly things, a good heart, and a good resolution, will go a great way: even to amazement sometimes. How much more in God and for God: if a man be well grounded and prepared. See Rom. 8:31 to the end of the chapter.

*{Lean not unto thine understanding}* A seasonable caveat, and admonition, lest that having before set out the praise of wisdom, and understanding, as effects of the *fear of God*; foolish men, upon a persuasion of godliness and religion, should adscribe to their own wit and prudence, and grow presumptuous, as they grow (or persuade themselves they do:) religious. Neither is it enough for us to trust in God (not in ourselves:) for the means; that is, that in case things fall out as we wished and endeavored, to give him the thanks: but also, for the event, whether so, or so: that in case it prove contrary to our desires and endeavors, yet to believe that God is wiser than we, and does order all events for the best, though we cannot penetrate into the depth of his counsels.

**Verse 6.** *{In all thy ways acknowledge him, and he shall direct thy paths}* 1 Chron. 28:9.

*{In all thy ways acknowledge him}* Use God's counsel in all your actions, and do nothing contrary to his will.

**Verse 7.** *{Be not wise in thine own eyes}* Rom. 12:16. See before upon chapter 2:5, *and find the knowledge of God*.

*{Fear the Lord, and depart from evil}* Chapter 16:6. Job 1:1.

**Verse 8.** *{It shall be health to thy navel}* Some think, because infants, while in the womb, receive all their nourishment, from which depends the health and growth of the whole body, through the navel, which afterwards becomes, if not altogether useless, yet not so considerable, as to be reckoned a principal part: that therefore the navel is here used allegorically, or metaphorically, of infants, through spiritual regeneration, in the womb of the Church: but this seems to me too remote. I rather agree with them who think that the whole body is comprehended under these two, the navel, and the bones, the softer parts, under the one, and the harder and more solid, under the other. The navel is as it were the knot, in appearance at least, which hangs or fastens all the belly, the seat of the softest parts: and so is called by some, ligamentum intestinorum. It is likely, that it was common speech among the Hebrews; as also that of the bones, which we find also Job 21:24, speaking of a man in full strength and vigor of body, *His breasts are full of milk*, (some translate *mulctralia*; his milk-bowls, or vessels: but it is a figurative expression, to express vigor of body, though not so proper to men) *and his bones are moistened with marrow*. It is possible also, that there might be some allusion, in the expression, to arches in building; where one stone, that which is in the middle, called also sometimes, *umbilicus*, seems to bind the whole arch. *Unus lapis fecit fornicem: ille qui latera inclinata cuneavit, & interventu suo vinxit.* Whence it is, that author of the book *De mundo*, (entitled to Aristotle) how fitly, I know not; but very elegantly, compares God to these umbilical stones, as by whom the whole world, all parts of it, are sustained and preserved. The same ancient Latin author, whose words I had but now, speaks of it in another place; *Democritus invenisse dicitur fornicem, ut lapidum curvatura paulatim inclinatorum medio saxo alligaretur.* But he denies it, and maintains that long before Democritus such building was in fashion. However, among the Hebrews, (who might learn it of the Egyptians) though not among the Grecians, or Romans, it might be of far greater antiquity. So the navel, or *umbilicus*, being just in the midst of the body, (as is noted by physicians) it may appear as a knot, or binding of the whole body.

*{Health}* Heb. *medicine*.

*{Marrow}* Heb. *watering, or, moistening*, Job 21:24.

**Verse 9.** *{Honour the Lord with thy substance, and with the firstfruits of all thine increase}* Exod. 23:19 and 34:26. Deut. 26:2, etc. Mal. 3:10, etc. Luke 14:13.

*{Honour the Lord with thy substance}* That is done many ways. By deeds of charity especially. *He that oppresseth the poor, reproacheth his maker, but he that honoureth him, hath mercy on the poor*: Prov. 14:31. To build churches unto God, was once thought a very good way too: and that centurion in the Gospel, was thought worthy of Christ's favor, because he had built the Jews a synagogue. Now, according to the religion of the times, to pull down churches, is to *honor God*: and if soldiers had not been more merciful in the execution, than some ministers (who notwithstanding like well, that God should still be *honored* with payment of tithes:) have been in crying them down, and profaning them, so far as in them lay; (for which also some have been rewarded) it may be by this we should have had but few standing. God preserve them that yet have escaped.

*{And with the firstfruits of all thine increase}* As was commanded in the law: and by this they acknowledged, that God was the giver of all things, and that they were ready to bestow all at his commandment.

**Verse 10.** *{So shall thy barns be filled with plenty}* Deut. 28:8.

*{Thy presses shall burst out with new wine}* To pay or give what God appoints, is not the way to impoverish men, but to enrich them. The Hebrews have a very elegant proverb to this purpose; *תעשׂר בשביל שתעשׁר*, that is, pay your tithes, that you may be rich. The same word in the Hebrew, (the different placing of one little prick over one of the letters, makes all the difference:) signifies to pay tithes, and, to grow rich: which gave occasion of the proverb. Now these promises of temporal rewards, though they have many times a very literal accomplishment, and many men have been, and are repaid with great advantage, for their good deeds, even in this life: yet it is not the part of a Christian to fix his heart upon temporal rewards, or promises; but to prepare himself to bear the cross, and to think it highest honor and happiness, (in this world,) thereby to be made conformable unto Christ. See more of temporal promises, upon Psalm 37:1. *Fret not thyself.*

**Verse 11.** *{My son, despise not thou the chastening of the Lord: neither be weary of his correction}* Job 5:17. Heb. 12:5. Rev. 3:19.

*{Despise not the chastening of the Lord}* Do not set light by God's rods; but make good use of them: neither be too much cast down under them, for they come for your good. Though this seem but harsh doctrine unto flesh and blood; yet we find that natural reason prompted it to divers ancients, who had it not from either Job, or Solomon, or any other of divine authority, since them. Seneca, after many ancienter: *Hos itaque Deus quos probat, quos amat*: (these two words the very same almost, that are used by the apostle, *ἀγαπᾷ*, and *παραδέχεται*:) *indurat, recognoscit, exercet: eos autem quibus indulgere videtur, quibus parcere; molles venturis malis servat.* And elsewhere he uses the same similitude of a father: *Patrium habet Deus adversus bonos viros animum*, etc.

**Verse 12.** *{Even as a father the son, in whom he delighteth}* Deut. 8:5. The apostle, Heb. 12:6, quoting this passage out of Proverbs, as appears by the context, instead of these words, *even as a father the son in whom he delighteth*: he has it, *and scourgeth every son whom he receiveth*; according to the translation of the Septuagint, then commonly received among the Jews. The original Hebrew *וכאב* as it may be differently pointed, without any alteration of the letters, will afford either: either *even* (or, *and*) *as a father*: or, *and scourgeth*. The difference, as to the matter, is of no moment; and therefore the apostle was not scrupulous. Neither should we, where there is no greater occasion; and there would be less divisions and dissensions among brethren.

**Verse 13.** *{And the man that getteth understanding}* Heb. *draweth it out*: to wit, out of the well of God's word. The Hebrew word, does also signify, scaturire; to boil or spring up, as a fountain does: which sense of it is here followed by some rabbins. I like it the better, because of the words of our Savior, John 4:14. *But water that I shall give him, shall be in him a well of water springing up into everlasting life.*

*{The man that getteth understanding}* Heb. *the man that draweth out understanding.*

**Verse 14.** *{For the merchandise of it is better than the merchandise of silver, and the gain thereof than fine gold}* Job 28:15, etc. Psalm 19:10. Chapter 8:11, 19 and 16:16. Or, according to Mercer, *and [better] than the gain of fine gold*. The preposition which is set before gold, in the order of the words; he thinks does belong unto gain, in the construction. There be examples of such, whether anomalies, or elegancies we confess: but the sense is good either way. Nay, the sense is the same, in effect, so that there needs no further dispute about it.

*{For the merchandise of it is better than the merchandise of silver}* The gain that men get by using it, as merchants do by traffic.

**Verse 15.** *{She is more precious than rubies}* Learned men are not agreed about the proper signification of *פנינים*. Some translate it, *margaritas, pearls*; some, carbuncles, or rubies; others coral, but the most common, and surest (as I conceive until we know more certainly:) expression is, precious stones.
