**Verse 16.** *{Length of days is in her right hand}* He compares wisdom to a queen, that holds long life in one hand, and wealth and honor in the other, to give plentifully to her servants. The ancients had a way of reckoning (whether years, or anything else) by the fingers and hands. They began with the left hand; and when they were come to a hundred, then they passed to the right hand: whence is that expression of one of the poets, speaking of one that was very old; *atque suos jam dextra computat annos*; that is, he reckons his years with his right hand. Some think that Solomon in these words might allude to that custom; which is not impossible: for it seems to have been very general; and many ancients both fathers and others, speak of it, or allude unto it. But more probably that he does allude to what happened unto himself in a vision, dreaming, 1 Kings 3:5, etc. where we find the same particulars, as are here expressed: and which is most observable, *riches and glory*, (honor, there, in our English, which is the same thing: yea and that same word in the original *כבוד*) put together, as it were in one hand, verse 13, and *length of days*, by itself, as the principal and most considerable, and as it were in the right hand: verse 14 and wisdom, the ground and foundation of all, verse 12. So that there is no question to be made of the allusion. As for the thing itself that is here affirmed, see the references upon chapter 2:21, etc. and upon verse 18, *a tree of life*, of this chapter.

**Verse 17.** *{Her ways are ways of pleasantness}* The man that walks wisely, shall enjoy true pleasure, and felicity. And this, not hereafter only, in the world to come, (which must be our chiefest end, and aim, to which this is but a passage,) but in this life, also, in a good measure. *For the kingdom of God is not meat and drink, but righteousness and peace, and joy of the Holy Ghost*, Rom. 14:17. Neither is this contrary to what is elsewhere delivered by the same apostle, that *all that will live godly in Christ Jesus* (*in whom are hid all the treasures of wisdom*, Col. 2:3.) *shall suffer persecutions*, 2 Tim. 3:12, or that *through much tribulation we must enter into the kingdom of God*, Acts 14:22, since that spiritual joys, and temporal adversities are no ways incompatible: and even where there is no present sense of joy at all, yet the assurance we have of future joys in another world, is a kind of anticipation of joy, though not yet sensibly enjoyed. Again, the heathens themselves, though in their praises of wisdom and virtue, they come not much short of Solomon: yet they could say, (and their writings are full of such cautions, and admonitions) that Radices amarae, etc. that the roots, (or beginnings and first application) of wisdom, were bitter; but the fruits sweet and pleasant. See also Ecclesiasticus 2:1, 2, 3, etc. *My son, if thou come to serve the Lord, prepare thyself for temptation*, etc.

**Verse 18.** *{She is a tree of life}* Which brings forth such fruit, that they that eat thereof have life. He alludes to the tree of life in Paradise, Gen. 2:9. Rev. 2:7. Now when we read of life, length of days, and the like, in this book; though we exclude not the more literal and obvious sense which nevertheless more peculiarly belonged unto the Jews; as has often already been observed, yet it becomes us that are Christians, to remember what true life is, as we are taught by Christ and his apostles, and as true Christians, to set our minds upon that, which is the *hope of our calling*, Eph. 1:18, and the *end of our life*, 1 Pet. 1:9, in which all other promises, which were but as types and figures of this are fully comprehended, exhibited, and fulfilled. See the references upon verse 16.

*{A tree of life}* Not elsewhere mentioned in the Old Testament but in Genesis, and in this book. And it seems by comparing of places (in four several places he does use the words) that it was a proverb in Solomon's time applicable to anything, that is much desired, and longed for, and brings great joy and comfort when it comes whether it have any relation to life, (in the proper sense,) or no. So chapter 13:12. *Hope deferred*, etc. *but when the desire cometh, it is a tree of life.* Chapter 4:15. *A wholesome tongue, is a tree of life.* So is *ζωὴ ἐκ νεκρῶν*: life from the dead: used proverbially, by the apostle, according to best interpreters Beza, and others: Rom. 11:15. *For if the casting away of them be the reconciling of the world: what shall the receiving of them be, but life from the dead?* And so *ἀναβίωσις*: and vita altera, in profane authors.

*{To them that lay hold upon her, happy is every one that retaineth her}* The Hebrew words, here translated *lay hold*, and *retaineth*; are very emphatical, as by Mercer is well observed: importing the first, an earnest, and watchful catching at a thing: the other, a firm and fast holding of what is caught. As to the first, the words of our Savior may give some light, that *the kingdom of heaven suffereth violence*, (or, is taken by force; as the margin there:) *and the violent take it by force*. Matt. 11:12, as also the words of the apostle, *lay hold on eternal life*. 1 Tim. 6:12. To the latter; *happy is every one*, etc. the words of the same apostle, *Wherefore, he that thinketh he standeth, let him take heed lest he fall*. 1 Cor. 10:12, and Apoc. 2:25, *hold fast till I come*; may seem to have more particular reference. Certainly, security and presumption is the loss of many, if not most, that think themselves good Christians. Little do most men think, neither are they willing to believe, that it is a matter of great care, diligence, solicitude, continual watchfulness, and circumspection, to get eternal life.

*{Happy is every one that retaineth her}* Chapter 8:34.

**Verse 19.** *{The Lord by wisdom hath founded the earth}* Psalm 24:2. Solomon having spoken so much in commendation of wisdom, and attributed unto it such marvelous effects, as he has done; and all this, of human wisdom; or rather, divine, but such as is imparted unto man, by God; he proceeds now into admiration of divine wisdom, as it is in God himself, and shows the wonderful effects of it, to the end, partly, that we might the sooner be drawn to the love and esteem of so heavenly a thing; and partly, that we might the better understand the truth of what he had delivered before, that all wit and wisdom in man, does proceed from God, and is his gift; and therefore to be expected from him only. This I conceive is the best and plainest coherence. We may also understand Christ, the Son of God: *Λόγον ἐνυπόστατον*: the substantial, and consubstantial word of God, yea *the power and wisdom of God*: 1 Cor. 1:24, *in whom all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge are hid*, Col. 2:3. Him, I say, to be aimed at, and mystically set out by these words. *For by him were all things created*, etc. Col. 1:16 and John 1:1, etc. But this, rather as the mystical, than literal meaning, other reasons are given of the coherence; but such as I judge either too remote or impertinent.

*{Established}* Or, *prepared*.

**Verse 20.** *{By his knowledge the depths are broken up}* Those deep places, into which the waters were driven at the beginning, Gen. 1:9. So some others, have other interpretations. But the most probable of all, is, to understand it of fountains and springs, which rivers are made of, without which the earth would not be habitable, and therefore mentioned here as a great effect of God's wisdom, and prudence towards mankind. Now concerning fountains and springs, there be divers questions in philosophy; the resolution whereof would very much set out the power and wisdom of God in contriving things so usefully, and yet so hiddenly, that the wisdom of man is posed in the consideration of the causes. Besides philosophers, Plutarch, as I remember, has some considerations upon the cause of fountains and springs, in the life of Paulus Aemylius.

**Verse 21.** *{My son, let not them depart}* Here Solomon returns to the commendation of human wisdom, (such as man in this world is capable of, I mean, as was said before:) and much to the same purpose, as before. See therefore what has been said of repetitions in this kind, in the preface. Now for a nominative to this verb, some fetch it from the foregoing verses, 19, 20, *wisdom, understanding, knowledge*. Others, (by a transition, or transposition) from the following words, in this verse, *sound wisdom, and discretion*. This last would be more probable, were it not against the laws of right syntax that to substantives, feminine, an adjective masculine should be joined. But there be examples of that in other places of Scripture; so that the reader may please himself.

*{Sound wisdom}* *תושיה* see upon chapter 2:7, *sound wisdom*.

**Verse 22.** *{So shall they be life unto thy soul}* Without wisdom then, the soul lacks life, and the body wants ornament.

**Verse 23.** *{Then shalt thou walk in thy way safely, and thy foot shall not stumble}* Psalm 37:24 and 91:11, 12. See there.

*{Then shalt thou walk in thy way safely}* You shall prosperously effect all your business in the day, and after it is done, you shall sleep quietly in the night.

**Verse 24.** *{When thou liest down}* Psalm 3:5, 6.

**Verse 25.** *{Be not afraid}* Or, *Thou shalt not be afraid*, verse 24. Psalm 111:3.

*{Neither of the desolation of the wicked}* For when God destroys the wicked, he will save his, as he did Lot in Sodom: or, *the desolation of the wicked*, actively; that is, the mischief and destruction, which the wicked does plot and project against you. See (as before) on Psalm 91:5, etc.

*{When it cometh}* Or, *that it should come*, to wit, upon you.

**Verse 27.** *{Them to whom it is due}* Heb. *the owners thereof*. And who are those think we? Even the poor and needy, who by the law of God, have right to part of what we have; if we have to spare: that is, if we have more, than is needful for our present sustentation, and our children's. So the Greek translation here, (*ἐνδεῆ*:) and best expositors. And hence it is, (say some) that alms are called by the rabbins, *צדקה* that is, properly justice.

**Verse 28.** *{Go, and come again}* Pay your debts upon demand, if you be able, and defer not payment.

*{And tomorrow I will give, when thou hast it by thee}* Lev. 19:13. Deut. 24:15.

**Verse 29.** *{Devise not evil}* Or, *practice no evil*.

*{Seeing}* Or, *when*.

*{Seeing he dwelleth securely by thee}* Seeing he fears no ill from you and therefore cannot prevent it.

**Verse 30.** *{Strive not with a man without a cause}* Out of pride, or covetousness.

*{If he have done thee no harm}* The Hebrew words seem to import literally (and so Pagninus in his interlineary translation:) *si non retribuit tibi malum*. But it is a great mistake, not in him only, but in divers lexicographers, even Buxtorfius, (a man otherwise of extraordinary deserts, in this kind of learning) for one. For the word *גמל* does not only signify retribuere, etc. but as often almost, do a man a kindness or unkindness, without any provocation. The vulgar translation therefore is here deservedly commended, which expresses it: *cum ipse tibi nihil mali fecerit*: which our English does follow. But we must not here follow the inference and interpretation of some rabbins, who because of this addition, *if he have done thee no harm*, teach, that therefore if a man have done us harm, we may hate and persecute him with fools, etc. as once their forefathers did, upon the command, *Thou shalt love thy neighbour*; that therefore it was lawful *to hate an enemy*; for which they were reproved by Christ, Matt. 5:43, which is answer enough, to satisfy any Christian, that it ought not to be so. But neither would we say anything to favor Anabaptists, or any other, who shall hold it unlawful, to sue for our right unjustly detained from us, in courts of justice: so it be without malice, hatred, or too great animosity: but in the spirit of meekness, with a readiness to surcease, (though with some loss) upon any offer of accommodation otherwise.

**Verse 31.** *{Envy thou not the oppressor}* Psalm 37:1. Desire not to be like unto him in oppression, that you may be as rich as he.

*{The oppressor}* Heb. *a man of violence*.

**Verse 32.** *{But his secret is with the righteous}* Psalm 25:14. That is, his covenant, and fatherly affection, which is hid, and secret from the world. Or, *the secret of the Lord*, that is, that *תושיה* spoken of before, chapter 2:7. *He layeth up sound wisdom*, etc. that is, eternal life: of which see more there. *יצפן*, *he layeth up*, or *hideth*, there: and *סודו*, *his secret*, here. But of the different acception of these words in general, and in this place, *the secret of the Lord*, see more upon Psalm 25:14. *The secret of the Lord is with them that fear him*, etc.

**Verse 33.** *{The curse of the Lord is in the house of the wicked}* Mal. 2:2.

**Verse 34.** *{Surely he scorneth the scorners}* James 4:6. 1 Pet. 5:5. He will show by his plagues, that their scorns shall turn to their own destruction, as chapter 1:26. See more upon Psalm 18:26, *thou shalt show thyself froward*. And upon chapter 1:4, *the simple*.

*{Surely}* Heb. *if*.

*{But}* Or, *yet*.

*{He giveth grace unto the lowly}* He makes them to find favor with men, as Exod. 3:21 and 11:3 and 12:36. This saying of Solomon: *He scorneth the scorners, but he giveth grace unto the lowly*, is by the Septuagint (so called) turned into the Greek: *Κύριος ὑπερηφάνοις ἀντιτάσσεται*· *ταπεινοῖς δὲ δίδωσι χάριν*, and so quoted by Saint James, and Saint Peter, according to the reference above. Now every man will say, at first hearing, that the sense is well enough expressed, since that *the scorners*, and *the proud*, both in their nature, and according to Scripture, (as has been observed by expositors) come all to one. But I do not find the unexpressible elegancy and pregnancy of this translation taken notice of, which I wonder at. Observe then, that the authors of it taking it for granted, that by *לצים* proud men were intended by Solomon, such as the Greek call *ὑπερηφάνους* (wherein I think they were right enough) because they could not make a verb of it, as from *לצים* in the Hebrew, *יליץ* in lieu of that, they used *ἀντιτάσσεται*, which does signify *ex adverso aciem instruere*; as if we said, to counter-encamp; or, to stand in battle array in direct opposition, which does imply, that as they do, so does God, as they deal with him, so he with them, and that is in effect, (but, intimated only, not expressed,) that as proud men behave themselves proudly towards God, so God (according to that, *with the froward, thou wilt show thyself froward*: Psalm 18:26.) towards them. But the Greek is more emphatical, implying withal that the proud, (agreeable to some other places of Scripture) do as it were levy war against God himself; a great aggravation of their sin.

*{Unto the lowly}* Heb. *עניים* (not, *ענוים*) that is, unto the poor; not unto the lowly. But because it is most proper unto the poor and needy, to be humble and lowly: (I say, most proper it becomes them best: though it often falls out otherwise: there be proud beggars in the world; and generally, none more proud, than they that have been beggars, if once they come to be somebody: we see at this day:) therefore we find the words more promiscuously used in Scripture, (in sundry places,) of poor and lowly. But the rabbins note, that though the word be written with a *י* here; yet it must be read with a *ו* (or, *Vau*.) so that they make it rather a mistake of a letter, in the writing; than a community of signification. The reader may choose which he will. See more upon chapter 11:2, *with the lowly is wisdom*.

**Verse 35.** *{But shame}* See upon Psalm 119:6. *Then shall I not be ashamed*, etc.

*{Shall be the promotion of fools}* Heb. *exalteth the fools.* And this I take to be the best translation, *exalteth*, that is, proclaims, divulges, and makes them known, as things that are lifted up, are more in sight. There is no great difference of sense I confess: but so shall we make this to agree with chapter 14:29, where the same expression is in the Hebrew, *but he that is hasty of spirit, exalteth folly*: that is, does commit many things, by which his folly comes to be known and talked of, both far and near.

## CHAPTER 4

**Verse 1.** *{The instruction of a father}* Of me, who take on me the person of a father, and love all you young men, as if you were my own children. See upon chapter 1:8, *My son*, etc.

**Verse 2.** *{Good doctrine}* The Hebrew word comes from receiving, to show, that we are ignorant of ourselves; and are bound to receive good instruction, when it is offered by others.

*{Forsake you not my law}* My precepts, which are not only doctrinal instructions, but also rules of life appointed by God, as a divine law.

**Verse 3.** *{For I was my father's son}* My father taught me, and I hearkened to him: so I teach you, and expect that you should hearken unto me.

*{Tender and only beloved in the sight of my mother}* 1 Chron. 29:1.

*{Only beloved in the sight of my mother}* Beloved, is well added, to show her singular affection to Solomon, as if she had had no more children; yet had she three more; 1 Chron. 3:5.

*{In the sight}* Heb. *לפני*. But over against it in the margin, there is a different reading; *לבני*, to the sons, or, children: or, as some would translate, among the children. But Mercerus shows well here, that they do wrong to the Bible, that embrace that reading; since the Masoretes themselves that observe it, pass their censure upon it, that it is false, or spurious.

**Verse 4.** *{He taught me also, and said unto me, Let thine heart retain my words: keep my commandments and live}* 1 Chron. 28:9. In this chapter be many repetitions, of which (generally,) and the cause of them in this book, see in the preface to the book.

*{He taught me also and said unto me}* How far Solomon does here represent his father David speaking; or, (to be plainer,) how far the words of David, which are here set down by Solomon, (for that some are, there is no question made,) do extend, is a question not easy to be determined by any argument from the text itself. Some think that David's words go no further than this very verse, and that in the next, *Get wisdom, get understanding*, etc. it is Solomon himself that speaks. Others, that David speaks, till verse 7, *Wisdom is the principal thing*, etc. Others again, till 10. *Hear, O my son*, etc. I cannot give any reason why any of these opinions may not be true, or equally probable: but if any go further, the further they go, the more improbable to me their opinion will be. For if some of these chapters, one or more, had been penned by David, it is likely they would have had David's name prefixed in another manner. But that Solomon did learn many of these sentences throughout the book, from his father David as I make no question, so is it as little to the purpose here.

**Verse 6.** *{Love her, and she shall keep thee}* Seneca will give great light: *Haec si persuaseris tibi, et virtutem adamaveris (amare enim parum est) quicquid illi contigerit, id tibi, qualecunque aliis videbitur, faustum felixque erit: & torqueri, si modo jacueris torquente securior, & aegrotare, si non maledixeris fortunae, si non cesseris morbo; Omnia denique—in bonum abibunt*; etc. And could they so speak, that had no certainty of the immortality of the soul; no assurance of any better condition after this life; and Christians complain if they suffer in this world; or think that God is not in heaven, or regards not, if innocent men are afflicted.

**Verse 5.** *{Get wisdom}* In the fifth and sixth verses, he requires that we should purchase wisdom, lay it up in the memory, as in a storehouse, not go aside from it on either hand, but obey it constantly, out of love. See also chapter 3:14.

**Verse 7.** *{Wisdom is the principal thing}* Heb. *ראשית חכמה*, where no *regimen* is; (I mean no alteration of points or letters, to betoken a *regimen*: the first word not being capable of any such alteration:) and therefore they that would have it translated, *Principium sapientiae*, or *the beginning of wisdom*; and not *principium*, or *praecipuum sapientia*; that is, *wisdom is the first, or principal thing*: as if the one were more according to the Hebrew, than the other, are certainly mistaken. The Hebrew will bear either: and Mercerus has both; *Initio sapientiae*; and, *principium sapientia*: but prefers this latter, which we have here also in our English. And the coherence so, is both good and plain: that since wisdom is the thing, wherein our summum bonum, or chief happiness does consist, that therefore among other ends and purposes, which every man in the course of his life does propose unto himself, we should make this the chiefest, (which to attain, we should think no pains, no cost, Matt. 13:43, *and selleth all that he hath*, etc. too great) to get wisdom.

**Verse 8.** *{Exalt her, and she shall promote thee}* Have her in high account, and then you shall prosper. Worldlings do contrary, they make low account of wisdom, and seek it at last, or not at all. The words will bear this sense also: tread her ways diligently; and she shall, etc. and it is followed by some. But the other sense agrees better with the following words, according to that 1 Sam. 2:30. *Them that honour me, I will honour*, etc.

*{Exalt her and she shall promote thee}* *For them that honour me I will honour: and they that despise me shall be lightly esteemed*, 1 Sam. 2:30.

**Verse 9.** *{An ornament of grace}* Chapter 1:9. Some learned men think, that St. James his words, *But he giveth more grace*, James 4:6, are taken from this place. Indeed *לוית* may signify *augmentum*, or *additamentum*: and so the vulgar Latin, *Dabit capiti tuo augmentum gratiae*: but not so translated here by the Septuagint; but by *στέφανος*: a crown, or garland: both here, and chapter 1:9, where it seems to agree better, than *augmentum*, and, if there; it is likely, that the same was intended here too: so that I see no great likelihood, that it should be so, as Heinsius would have it. Yet if it be granted, there will be no great inconvenience: and it cannot be denied, that there be examples of more remote allusions.

*{A crown of glory shall she deliver to thee}* Or, *she shall compass thee with a crown of glory.* Not, *a corruptible crown*, (though that too, sometimes) 1 Cor. 9:25, which because corruptible, and intermingled with many thorns, many wise men have thought not worthy the taking up: but *a crown that fadeth not away*, 1 Pet. 5:4. See more of temporal rewards and promises upon Psalm 37:1. *Fret not thyself*, etc.

**Verse 11.** *{I have taught thee in the way of wisdom}* Solomon declares what care his father had to bring him up in the true fear of God: for this was David's protestation, which Solomon now repeats.

*{I have led thee in right paths}* Heb. *made thee to go*, that is, assisted you by calling upon you.

**Verse 12.** *{Thy steps shall not be straitened}* You shall walk at liberty without offense.

**Verse 13.** *{Take fast hold of instruction}* See before upon chapter 3:15, *to them that lay hold upon her*. Now, *to take fast hold*, we cannot; nobody can; except we proceed from grace to grace: from virtue to virtue. There is no standing still: either to advance, or go back we must. Ancient philosophers could tell us that; whose writings are full of exhortations, to the purpose. *Sublabemur, nisi ire et niti perseveraverimus, Si quicquam ex studio et fideli intentione* (that is, courageous earnestness:) *laxaverimus, retro eundum est. Nemo profectum ibi invenit, ubi reliquerat*, says Seneca.

*{And when thou runnest thou shalt not stumble}* Psalm 91:11, 12. See there the annotations on verse 5, etc.

**Verse 14.** *{Enter not into the path of the wicked, and go not in the way of evil men.} ***15.*** {Avoid it, pass not by it: turn from it, and pass away.}* Of the danger of evil company, and the seasonableness of such warnings, see before upon chapter 1:10. *My son, if sinners entice thee*: and the references there. Now, here by so many words in the 15th verse, for one thing: being all of one signification; as may seem to make a notorious tautology, or battology: though indeed it is otherwise. For the accumulating of so many words of one signification, upon such an occasion, is very emphatical. Solomon by this reiterated language, (which is natural in such cases) would have us to understand the eminency, and imminency both of the danger that young men are in, through ill company. As if passing by in the streets, we should descry fire breaking out of a house, which had not been taken notice of: or, upon the highways, we should observe some ignorant traveler, in a full career, to some terrible precipice: nobody would wonder to hear us often repeat the same words; as fire, fire: a precipice, a precipice: take heed, look to yourself: and the like. So is this unusual repetition of words here of purpose, to make us the more sensible of that, which though it be so true, as nothing can be more; yet is so little believed, that more young men laugh at it, when they are told of it; than are persuaded, that there is any danger in it. And when, in what age, or what place of the world; did men, whether heathens, or Christians (so called) generally, men and women, indulge to themselves more liberty in this kind; than they do, now, in these times, which we are taught to call (so little we make of mocking God:) blessed and happy times? And times of reformation? Such earnestness of language we may observe in Theognis, (that ancient Gnomologue, who comes nearest to Solomon, of any that I know now extant) where he advises his friend to avoid bad company, (which I think he repeats in little less than a dozen places:) and to apply himself to good. There he has several words together: stick to them, drink with them, eat with them, sit with them, and please them: for their power is great, says he, what? To frame you (by their example) to goodness; as follows there.

*{And go not in the way of evil men}* Come not into it at all, or if by force of temptation you be brought into it, go not forward in it, but get out of it quickly.

**Verse 15.** *{By it}* Heb. *through it*.

**Verse 16.** *{For they sleep not except they have done mischief}* Meaning, that to do evil, is more proper and natural to the wicked, than to sleep, eat, or drink, Psalm 36:4.

*{Unless they cause them to fall}* Either to fall, (in their estates, or persons) by their violence or violent hands: or to fall (in their souls, and resolutions,) by their temptations, which is as acceptable a prey unto them, (such is their wickedness, Rom. 1:32. *Who knowing the judgment of God*, etc.) as the getting of much substance. And thus are they also very lively represented by an ancient comic upon the very same occasion of dissuasion, from them, and their company. Solomon's words may receive some light by the collation: which I think is the best use we can make of such authors: and I profess that in many places of Scripture, both of the Old, and New Testament, where best commentators left me very doubtful, I have received much satisfaction from such collations: as Beza does profess somewhere, that he was beholding to Anacreon, (a very wanton poet otherwise:) for the right sense of a place of Scripture. Plautus is the man that I mean: who in one of his comedies, makes a father speak to his son in this manner:—*Nolo ego cum improbis te viris, Gnate mi, neque in via neque in foro ullum sermonem exequi*, (Here we have the substance of the 14th and 15th verses) *Novi ego hoc seclum moribus quibus sit; malus bonum malum esse vult, ut sit sui similis.* (Just so Solomon here, if we interpret this, *to fall*, of those whom they entice: which is as natural an interpretation as any other, not omitted by Mercer:) then he proceeds to a description of their violence:—*turbent, miscent mores, Mali, rapax, Avarus, invidus (avidus furti,) sacrum, profanum, publicum, privatum habent, Hiulca gens.—quod manu non queunt Tangere, tantum fas habent quae manus abstineant. Caetera, rape, trahe, fuge, late*, etc. How excellently well is their unquiet life, broken sleep, and continual endeavor to do mischief, which exposes them to perpetual flights, and dangers in those words, Caetera, rape, trahe, etc.) their haste, their greediness, their fear, their flight, in few words, set out, and acted.

**Verse 17.** *{For they eat the bread of wickedness}* Chapter 9:17 and 20:17. Some refer this *for*, to their unquiet sleep, spoken of in the verse before: as a reason in part of it. As if he said: no wonder if they cannot sleep quietly, who live by mere rapine, and know themselves in danger in all places, of being taken, and seized, as enemies to the peace and quietness of other men.

*{The wine of violence}* Heb. *violences*. That is; gotten by wicked means, and most cruel oppression, Amos 2:8.

**Verse 18.** *{Unto the perfect day}* Psalm 37:6. Signifying, that the godly increase daily in honor, till they come to perfection. Or rather, that their minds being enlightened by the Spirit of wisdom in this life, they shall proceed from grace to grace, from virtue to virtue in this world, until they be come to the end of their race, and be admitted to the presence of God; by participation of whose glory they shall shine themselves, as the sun does in the firmament to our eyes. *And many of them that slept in the dust of the earth shall awake*, etc. *And they that be wise, shall shine as the brightness of the firmament, and they that turn many to righteousness as the stars forever and ever*, Dan. 12:2, 3.

**Verse 19.** *{The way of the wicked is as darkness}* Isa. 59:9. Jer. 23:12. Some read it in the Hebrew, *באפלה* *in darkness* so; not, *כאפלה* (with a *caph*:) *as darkness*, as we have it. It may seem plausible; but there is no need. For besides that, there is *כ* veritatis, (as Hebrew grammarians speak) as well as similitudinis, and so either way, (with a *beth*, or *caph*;) it comes all to one: it is more agreeable to the first member of the verse, *as the shining light*,) with a *caph*, than with a *beth*. Now, although it is very true, literally (and that, therefore the literal and immediate sense) that wicked men live *in darkness*, in reference to their lives and conversations, and to their actions and purposes: (and I wish it might be said of the wicked only and that of the Latin poet, were not generally true: *O miseras hominum mentes O pectora caeca! Qualibus in tenebris vitae quantisque periclis Degitur hoc aevi quodcunque est!*) yet mystically the words may as truly be referred to eternal death, so frequently set out in Scripture by darkness, as every man knows: as, eternal life, by light. See also upon chapter 3:18. *She is a tree of life.*

*{They know not at what they stumble}* Like men in the dark they can foresee no dangers, much less prevent them, Isa. 59:10. John 11:10. 1 John 2:11.

**Verse 21.** *{Let them not depart from thine eyes}* Chapter 3:3, 21. Or, *let them not make them to depart*: that is, let not the wicked (mentioned, verse 19.) make you neglect obedience to God.

*{In the midst of thine heart}* See chapter 2, verse 1, *hide my commandments*. So Seneca, *aperto pectore haurire*, upon the like occasion.

**Verse 22.** *{And health to all their flesh}* That is, they shall have health of body: under the which, all other blessings of this life, promised in the law, are contained. See chapter 3:8.

*{Health}* Heb. *medicine*.

**Verse 23.** *{Keep thy heart with all diligence}* As men keep the heart most of all from wounding, because the wound of it is mortal; so must they, in spiritual affairs, look principally to their thoughts, and afterwards to their tongues, eyes and feet. See also Jer. 17:9. *The heart is deceitful above all things*, etc.

*{With all diligence}* Heb. *above all keeping*.

*{Out of it are the issues of life}* Thence comes life natural, spiritual and eternal. *For out of the heart proceed evil thoughts, murders, adulteries, fornications, thefts, false witness, blasphemies*; Matt. 15:19, and, *A good man out of the good treasure of his heart, bringeth forth good things; and an evil man*, etc. Matt. 12:35, and therefore the apostle would not have us to judge any man, (which must be understood of private judgment, and censures; and when the fact is not notoriously bad and scandalous,) but to leave all judgment to God, who only does see the hearts of men, 1 Cor. 4:5. *Therefore judge nothing before the time*, etc.

**Verse 24.** *{A froward mouth, and perverse lips}* Heb. *frowardness of mouth, and perverseness of lips*. Psalm 34:12, 13, 14.

**Verse 25.** *{Let thine eyes look right on}* Look to your way, like a traveler, and look not aside: weigh your actions beforehand by God's word: keep the mean, and avoid both extremes.

**Verse 26.** *{Let all thy ways be established}* Or, *all thy ways shall be ordered aright.*

**Verse 27.** *{Turn not to the right hand nor to the left}* Deut. 5:32. Josh. 1:7.

## CHAPTER 5

**Verse 2.** *{Discretion}* Heb. *discretions*: that is, all manner of discretion.

*{That thy lips may keep knowledge}* That you may not only be wise yourself, but also able to teach others wisdom, Mal. 2:7.

**Verse 3.** *{For the lips}* I warn you to hearken to my words, lest you be deceived by the words of the adulteress.

*{A strange woman}* See notes on chapter 2:16.

*{For the lips of strange women}, etc.* Chapter 2:16 and 6:24.

*{Mouth}* Heb. *palate*.

*{Smoother than oil}* By oil and honey, he means flattering and crafty enticements, Psalm 55:21.

**Verse 4.** *{Her end is bitter as wormwood}* That which will come upon you, if you meddle with the adulteress. So Plautus (as by some has been observed upon the place) *in melle sunt linguae sitae vestrae orationes Lacteque; corda, felle sunt sita, atque acerbo aceto: E linguis dicta dulcia datis, corde, amare facitis amantes*, etc.

*{And is bitter}* 2 Sam. 2:26.

*{Sharp as a two-edged sword}* A two-edged sword, applied proverbially to anything that is very penetrative, whether to hurt, or to do good. Hence, as here in a bad sense; so elsewhere, the word of God is likened *μαχαίρᾳ διστόμῳ* (which is the word here too, in the Greek:) to a two-edged sword, Heb. 4:12, and Christ himself mystically set out with *a sharp two-edged sword, coming out of his* *mouth*: Proverbs 1:16. and 2:12. to express his irresistible power: to which purpose also a sword is attributed unto God Deuteronomy 32. and Psalm 7:12.

**Verse 5.** *{Her feet go down to death}* Chapter 7:27. All her doings lead to destruction.

*{her steps take hold on hell}* By hell here, we may safely enough understand hell as in common use, for a place of torments. Though *fools make a scoff at sin*, chapter 14:9. and have devised (men and women that are given to this sin:) many plausible terms, (among themselves:) whereby to color and extenuate their baseness and villainy; yet they will find one day, that God is not so easily mocked, (Galatians 6:7.) as they are easily deluded by the devil. However, what *שאול* was more commonly among the Hebrews; and *ᾅδης* among the Greeks, has already been said upon chapter 1:12. as the grave. So that as we have here first *death*; and then *שאול* all in one verse, according to the phrase and language of those days, both among the Jews and heathens; without any tautology though intended of the same thing: so should I say (did not I fear to engage myself into an endless controversy; or at least, not to be ended without many words:) that in the Apostles' Creed (so it has been called of old:) *he descended into hell*; was no otherwise intended. And indeed there might be another reason for it besides the common; because Christ, by some heretics, was affirmed not to have died really. But this by the way only: I leave every man free.

**Verse 6.** *{Lest thou shouldest ponder the path of life, her ways are movable}, etc.* The words as Mercerus well observes, are somewhat obscure and admit of different interpretations. Some translate, *Semitam vitae forsitan si ponderes*, etc. to this effect: If you be about to consider of the right way; the way that leads to present happiness in this world, and to eternal life, hereafter? take heed of such; avoid them with all possible care, since nothing can be more contrary to such a purpose, or deliberation, than the ways and company of such lewd insinuating women. Whether you be already in the way, or upon a deliberation concerning the right way, take heed of such: their company, if you be right, will pervert you: if yet to resolve, and in the act of further deliberation (as most that are young yet) and inquisition, they will blind your judgment: you shall not be able to judge aright, of what is best and expedient: what leads to true happiness; what to certain misery and destruction. It is the saying of Aristotle in his Politics, that *οἱ ἐν πάθει ὄντες*, they that are prepossessed with any passion, (whether it be passion of love, or fear, or any other) cannot discern the truth. Aristotle teaches in his Ethics at large, that as men are habituated in their lives and conversations, so are their judgments inclined and swayed, concerning that which is morally good or bad: and that *τοῖς πολλοῖς ἡ ἀπάτη διὰ τὴν ἡδονὴν ἔοικε γίνεσθαι*: most men are corrupted in their judgment through pleasure and sensuality; which they might have prevented, had they not run out into lewd courses. Others would have this to be the sense; That if a man be once habituated, and inured to lewd courses, though afterwards, when he begins to find the inconvenience of it, he would fain get out, yet it is not in his power; *vestigia nulla retrorsum*: as one of the Latin poets has it; *None that go unto her, return again: neither take they hold on the paths of life*: chapter 2:19. The consideration whereof should be of great force, to make men wary, if not altogether stupid, or of themselves, very desperate. Others again, because the Hebrew verbs (*תנוע* and *תפלס*) (according to the propriety of that tongue) may be translated either by the second masculine singular, or by the third feminine singular also; they refer this to the adulteress, and translate: *Per semitam vitae non ambulat: vagantur ejus orbitae, ne sciat.* Certainly, though it be the profession of such creatures, to entice and cheat others; yet themselves were first cheated by the devil, before they could resolve to prostitute themselves to so much infamy, (besides the danger of eternal death, which few of that desperate crew, think of) before God and men. Other expositions there be, but reducible to these, as to the main: Yet I do not find in any, that which I should of myself, pitch upon, as most literal and probable. It is observed by some others too, but by comics especially, who of all others should be best acquainted with their customs and dispositions: that as there is nothing more effectual to disengage the mind of those that cleave unto such as are here spoken of, than to see them (their manners and their conditions) in their own natural shape and color, without any disguise: and to know their own unaffected carriage and behavior at home, when they are alone among themselves: so there is nothing, that such more avoid, than to be so seen and known. It made one of the comedy, out of his good will to his good friend and master, to applaud himself extremely, that he had found out a way, (—*quomodo adolescentulus Meretricum ingenia & mores possit noscere, Mature ut cum norit perpetuo oderit*) how he might make them known to him, betimes, making no question at all, but if once known, he would ever after abhor them: and after a pretty large description of their domestic nastiness and gluttony, so contrary to what they appear abroad; he concludes, (*Nosse haec omnia, salus est adolescentulis*:) That to know these things in time is happiness, and as it were deliverance from death to young men. I conceive therefore; that Solomon's purpose in this verse was no other, than to set out the cunning of such creatures, who knowing well enough, that nothing can be more prejudicial to them than to be known, do what can be done by art to prevent it. It is a very hard, if not impossible thing, to discern well those things, though we have them before our eyes; which are in perpetual motion: variety of shapes and objects, hinders the intention of the mind, and keeps the eyes of the body from fastening upon anything. Therefore says Solomon, to express how she avoids to be known, that *her ways are movable*: which may also import frequent change of places (which the Hebrew *נוע* will bear very well, which also signifies to wander up and down) and habitations, a thing often practiced by them that would not be known. And this she does, says Solomon, *lest thou shouldest ponder the path of life*: that is, lest by loathing of her person through some casual distaste first, you should come by degrees to a right apprehension of your case, and so save yourself before it be too late.

*{Lest thou shouldest ponder}* There is much weight in that word. It is not bare pondering that can do good alone, except after follow consent, or assent and resolution. But by pondering, he comprehends these also, as supposing that no man can be so mad or senseless, (the desire of happiness being natural unto all men) as that after he has seriously, again and again considered (which is understood by pondering: a thing not to be done, but with care and diligence) of his case and of the end and consequent of these courses he is in, that he should continue in them to his apparent destruction, both of body and soul; both temporal and eternal. The word *λογίζομαι* is used in the New Testament, to the same purpose, which properly signifies, putare, to cast accounts: a very proper and significant word too, as any can be. It is the word used by St. Paul, Romans 8:18. speaking of the sufferings of the godly in this world, compared with eternal rewards: *λογίζομαι γὰρ*; *For I reckon, etc.* Certainly, want of this sober, serious consideration and ratiocination, (the proper work of a rational creature) is the greatest cause of most men's perdition.

**Verse 7.** *{O ye children}* O Solomon, and the rest of my sons: for they are David's words. See notes on chapter 4:4, 11.

**Verse 8.** *{come not nigh the door of her house}* As from a house infected with the plague: come not nigh the door.

**Verse 9.** *{Lest thou give thine honour unto others, and thy years to the cruel}* By honor, he may understand his wealth and good name: by others, the strumpets themselves; so styled in scorn, in opposition to a lawful wife, which by God's constitution and ordinance, is *one flesh* with her husband, Matthew 19:4, 5. Or, her children and kindred. By *the cruel* some would have the husband of this adulteress intended, of whom chapter 6:35, 36. it is said, that *jealousy is the rage of a man: that he will not spare in the day of vengeance, etc.* that is, in effect (compared with verse 32. before, that *he that committeth adultery with a woman, destroyeth his own soul; etc.*) that he will slay the adulterer without mercy: him therefore by *the cruel* here to be understood, which is not improbable. Yet there will be more emphasis in the word, if it be understood of the adulteress herself, it being the propriety of such creatures, as they are described ordinarily by the comics, as to put off all sense of honesty, so of humanity. No highway cutthroats can be more cruel than such, when they find their time. Terence is noted to have done that which no man ever before him did: that is, to have represented some of that profession, doing some acts of ingenuity. But it was the poet's ingenuity, it is thought, to set them out so, rather, than that any did really deserve it. But besides, there is a manifest allusion in the words, between *אחרים*, (alieni) others, and *אכזרי*, (crudelis) the cruel. Such allusions are very frequent in Scripture. There is also somewhat observable between the words of this, and the next verse, which I leave to them that read the book in the original language.

*{thine honour}* There be many interpretations of this word, upon this place: but, wealth, credit, and reputation; as has been said, most proper to the common use of the word, and best agreeing here. Yet if unto these any think fit to add, vigor, strength of body, which adds no small honor to the body, and fits a man for honorable actions, I am not against it. And here somewhat might be said in vindication of that form of words, (in that excellent formulary) in the rites of marriage: *with my body I thee worship*. There was great reason for it in those days, when (as once by the law of God, and by the law of nations, or civil law): concubines were lawful, and had jus thori: but could challenge no honor, or worship, which was due to the wife only. This very word, worship, is in the ancient Jewish form, which is exhibited by Buxtorfius, and the word there, *פלח* is also used of God's worship, as our English word is. But this by the way only, because many men are so apt to cavil at that, which they do not understand.

**Verse 10.** *{thy wealth}* Hebrew, *thy strength*.

*{and thy labours be in the house of a stranger}* Lest she, her husband and her children spend those goods which you have gotten with hard labor.

**Verse 11.** *{And thou mourn at the last}* Roar like a lion: as if he were going to hell; he has more horror now, than joy before.

*{when thy flesh and thy body are consumed}* By some foul disease, which follows loose persons in that kind, Job 20:11. Some would make a difference here between flesh and body. But it has no ground: though indeed, flesh, properly taken, is not the same as the body which comprehends many things besides flesh: (or muscles, to speak anatomically; and according to Hippocrates:) as bones, sinews, marrow, etc. and as true it is, that there is no part of the body, which was not to be corrupted and consumed by diseases proceeding from intemperance in this kind. And again, we may with the LXX. and some other translations, by a figure called *ἓν διὰ δυοῖν*: (whereof there be many examples in Scripture:) take *thy flesh and thy body*, for, *the flesh of thy body*. But I think it more pathetical, and emphatical, if we take flesh and body, (as usually, in the Scripture) for the same: it being a precept of rhetoric, to insist with variety of words, upon a thing, which is effectual to our end, and is of itself plausible. And what more effectual, to reclaim a man (that is not quite given over to a reprobate mind) from these vicious courses; than the consideration of those both painful, and loathsome diseases, that use to accompany them? And what more plausible, than to persuade men to love their own flesh (which the apostle says, no man ever hated) and to make much of their bodies? which, in a wrong sense, and to the prejudice of their souls, (that better part) most men are too prone unto, of their own accord.

**Verse 12.** *{How have I}* How brutish was I to do so, Psalm 73:22. Now he sees his error, though late first.

*{hated instruction}* He shows the true cause of continuing in adultery, to be hatred of instruction, although no adulterer in his health will confess so much.

**Verse 14.** *{I was almost in all evil}* Whether of the evil of guilt, or, of punishment; or whether of both, (as most probable to me:) expositors agree not. I take the words to be spoken of one, that never went to the utmost, in these infamous practices; nor long continued in that abominable sin: who therefore now, as he does acknowledge his folly, that he went so far; yet withal the mercy of God, that he went no further; and was taken off, before his case was altogether desperate; and his disease incurable. But see upon the next words.

*{in the midst of the congregation}* I was made a public spectacle of misery for all men to gaze at, and almost utterly ruined. Or, (as an aggravation of his wickedness) so desperate and insensible of infamy was I, that I did not care who knew it, who saw it: I gloried (as now is the fashion; and many think themselves the better gentlemen for it) in my lewdness: so others. The first, is most agreeable to the sense we have insisted upon, upon the former part. But either of these, is probable enough. And because *קהל ועדה* here translated, *the congregation and assembly*: and by others, as by the vulgar Latin, Ecclesia & Synagoga: are also sometimes used, or applied to any ordinary company of men; yea of bad men also, as Psalm 26:5. *the congregation of evil doers*, (Hebrew, *קהל מרעים*) and Psalm 126:18. *a fire was kindled in their company*: (of Dathan and Abiram: Hebrew, *בעדתם*: and so in the verse before, *עדת אבירם* *the company of Abiram*:) there be who interpret these words of the company of evil men, as if he intended to ascribe all the evil he did (as it often, nay commonly falls out; whereof see more in the preface, and upon verse 10. of the first chapter:) to bad fellowship and company.

**Verse 15.** *{Drink waters out of thine own cistern, and running waters}, etc.* Of the meaning of particular words, in this, and the two next verses, there is no doubt made: but of the sense and meaning of the whole passage, there is. Some by cisterns, etc. understand lands, and possessions, or worldly goods in general; as if the aim of the words were to persuade them that have means, to live of, and upon their own; and not only contentedly to enjoy what God has bestowed upon them, but to endeavor that others also (the poor especially) may be the better for what they have: so to use their means, and not to waste them prodigally, and riotously with, and upon lewd company, whether of men, or women. So St. Basil, or to this purpose, of old; and Junius, among the later, do interpret this passage. But because women are spoken of immediately before, and presently after too, so that the whole chapter may probably be thought to be of one argument: to wit, to contain a serious dehortation from the company of lewd women, and to that end, first to set down the danger and misery of such a life; and then, the remedy, or antidote; to wit, lawful marriage: there being nothing heterogeneous, or improper (this one controverted passage excepted) to that argument in the whole chapter: this has moved the greater number of expositors, rather to interpret this passage also to the same purpose, making the said prescribed remedy, an antidote against fornication and adultery, (to wit, lawful marriage:) here to begin. Which is very plausible in opposition to that former interpretation: though in that also there is somewhat to the general scope and purpose of the whole chapter, to wit, against riotousness and lewd company; but nothing so direct and pertinent, as this second interpretation seems to be. Neither can any doubt be made of the propriety, or probability of those metaphors, waters, and cistern; and well, etc. so interpreted, which we have elsewhere also, to this, or the like purpose; where no question can be made of the sense: as chapter 9:17. *Stolen waters are sweet, etc.* and 22:27. *For a whore is a deep ditch, etc.* and Ecclesiasticus, (of which, what reference it may have to these Proverbs, see before in the preface) 26:12. *She will open her mouth—and drink of every water near her, etc.* Not to question therefore, the propriety or pertinency of the allegory, to this place; that which I stick at, is, how to make these three verses in the prosecution of the said allegory, or allegorical exposition, to hang together; which I confess is more than I can well do myself; or can say, that I have yet met in any other, what I thought satisfactory. *Drink waters out of thine own cistern, etc.* that is, say they; Get a wife of your own; and delight in her, to extinguish the fire of lust in a clear well; and not in dirty puddles, which strumpets are. *Let thy fountains be dispersed abroad:—and rivers of waters in the streets;* that is, say they; from you and your wife as pure fountains, let a numerous issue of well-nurtured children spring or flow; which may run, or spread in the streets abroad, as so many clear riverets; to water and comfort by marriages and otherwise, the whole neighborhood; and to be an ornament to yourself. Children, indeed, as they are the chief fruit of marriages; so in opposition to adulterous beds too; is the mention of them very proper; because neither so natural (and there is a natural cause for it:) to such; to such I mean, as make a trade of their prostitution; nor any part (ordinarily) of their intention, in their unlawful copulations. The sense therefore that they make of the words is good; but the expression seems to me rather enigmatical, than allegorical: and though there is apparent reason why Solomon should express those things rather allegorically, than otherwise: yet why so obscurely, and enigmatically, I cannot imagine. Neither is it the obscurity only, that I stick at, but the ambiguity. For what he says in the second verse, in a more obvious construction, may seem to cross the first; but especially the third; *Let them be only thine own, and not strangers' with thee*, to cross the second: *Let them be only thine own, etc.* that is, say they, let those rivers of waters issuing from those fountains; (that is, from your lawful wives: it being then lawful, or allowed to one man to have more wives than one) that issue, those children, be yours only; and undoubtedly; as they may be, if you meddle with no woman, but such as is your own: whereas, if unlawfully begotten; you may have the name, but the true father will be uncertain, and others may challenge an interest in them, to your shame and reproach: and some of them proving suppositious, strangers shall carry away part of your wealth and substance. This is good matter, if it can be made of these words: which, in ordinary construction, seem rather to contradict what was before delivered. And hence it is, that some of old apprehending a manifest contradiction in the words, to reconcile them, did supply a negative in the second verse; *Let not thy fountains be dispersed abroad, etc.* So the Roman edition of the Septuagint; and so Aquila his translation, which is also followed by ancient Origen, and by Clemens Alexandrinus; as is observed by Cornelius a Lapide, upon this place. Others, but to the same effect, have found a way (because they find it not in the Hebrew) to supply this negative, reading the words by way of interrogation: *should thy fountains be dispersed abroad, and rivers of waters in the streets?* There is no violence offered to the text by this; and the coherence is much better by it. But it does not give content: for I find it not followed by many. These considerations as they make me to have the better opinion of the first interpretation; so they give me the liberty to cast about and search, whether anything else may be found: as probable for the sense, as what has been said, and not liable to the same exceptions; that is, not involved with such obscurity, or ambiguity of expression. I cannot say that I have found what I would: but what I have thought of, I shall here impart to the reader. Why might not all this passage be taken literally, as well as allegorically? That this expression, for a man to drink of his own well, or cistern: (for both come to one, and the difference in the words is but little in the original:) is an expression of temporal peace and happiness, may appear by 2 Kings 18:31. *eat ye every man of his own vine, and every one of his fig tree, and drink ye every one the waters of his own cistern;* repeated Isaiah 36:16. And what a considerable thing a well, or a fountain, was in ancient times, as by many other evidences, so particularly may appear by the word frater, brother: which originally came from the Hebrew *באר*: but more immediately (as all learned linguists acknowledge) from the Greek *φρέαρ*, (*φρέατερ*, *φράτερ*) which signifies a well: as if community of the same well, or fountain (pagani came thence also, and divers other words:) had been the greatest bond of union among men. At such times therefore such advice could not be thought impertinent: That every man that was a housekeeper, and master of a considerable family: should be careful to provide himself of a conveniency of water, both for his own use, and to pleasure his poor neighbors: which no man, (to prevent strifes and contentions, which often did arise about wells, and common waters) might challenge an interest in, but himself. That this does very naturally arise from the words, I think there will be no question; and many such precepts, or admonitions there be in this book, Mercerus does often observe, that are merely economical: which they that do not observe, and would be wiser than Solomon, turn into impertinent allegories. All the objection will be, which was against the first interpretation, why Solomon should interpole this here, which is of a different argument from the rest of the chapter. This would be somewhat, if there were not more to be objected against the common interpretation. We may therefore answer, that this is no more, than what is done throughout the whole book, there being scarce any chapter, some one or two at the most excepted, where promiscuousness of matter, through the whole chapter, or some heterogeneous insertions, where the chapter runs most upon some one subject, may not be observed. Besides, these Proverbs, as most are of opinion, being for the most part but collections, it is impossible that the common name of well or fountain, though literal, in the one; and allegorical in the other, might occasion this conjunction of different precepts or instructions. And since the allegory or similitude is grounded upon some resemblance in the nature of the things, (which is particularly insisted upon and showed by some expositors: but more discreetly, and of purpose; as they profess themselves, passed over by others: *Plura dici possent physica & medica de analogia cisternae & putei cum foemina: sed parco castis auribus*, etc. says one:) it was not altogether improper, or inconsequent, from precepts concerning literal wells and waters, to pass to allegorical; and to put both in one chapter. Nay, though it be granted that what Solomon in this place, was chiefly, or only (if you will) to commend chastity; yet to make his words more graceful and rhetorical, he might use such an entrance, or prooemium as this, taken from the happiness (according to those days) of being sole master of one good fountain, from which plenty of water did issue, and spread itself abroad into many branches: such a fountain, unto which no other could claim any right, or title unto: and so pass very plausibly and naturally, as it were; (there being so much affinity in nature) to the commendation of chastity. There be divers things of the same nature, in chapter 30. see there upon verse 15. *The horseleech.* And upon chapter 6. verse 16. *These six things, etc.* But this I do but propose, in case it should fall out with any other, as it has done with me, concerning this passage, not to be satisfied with the common interpretation. If any can satisfy themselves with it, I shall advise them to seek no further.

**Verse 16.** *{Let thy fountains be dispersed abroad}* Although, according to that interpretation of the former verse, which we have proposed, these words may be interpreted to signify plenty of waters, that issue out of one fountain; which plenty being a great blessing, (especially in a hot country: to which purpose, besides many other places of Scripture, I think Numbers 24:7. *and his seed shall be in many waters;* will bear a good sense:) must needs make the benefit and propriety of a good fountain the more considerable: yet even in this sense, they seem to me not in the matter only, but in their very form and tenor, to have so much appearance of repugnancy to the former, and following verse; that I can hardly persuade myself that Solomon would speak so obscurely. Neither should I think it any great crime, or wrong done to the Scripture, if I should join with them, that think (whereof upon the former words) that a *לא* or an *אל* is wanting. But to avoid offense, I will rather say; that if the words be read with an interrogation, (as was showed there) they would be more current, so; and the sense, not worse. And there may be a good reason for it. For though in point of charity, to pleasure poor neighbors; it be commendable; and, if not community, yet communication generally plausible: yet in matter of wells and fountains (a thing so considerable in those days) community and communication (besides other inconveniences perchance:) might probably breed prescription; and prescription, quarrels and contentions; and in some cases violence and oppression. But I said before; if any can satisfy himself with the common interpretation, I do not desire to put him out of conceit with it.

**Verse 18.** *{Let thy fountain be blessed}* That is, love your wife. The proper blessing of marriage is children; which they that transfer their love from the proper fountain, and settle it elsewhere, do not regard, or propose to themselves. I take the aim of the words to be the same, as 1 Corinthians 7:3. *Let the husband, etc.*

*{rejoice with the wife of thy youth}* Which you did marry in your youth, and so may have lawful children by her to rejoice in, chapter 2:17. Malachi 2:14. See also upon chapter 2:17. *the guide of thy youth*.

**Verse 19.** *{Let her be as the loving hind}* Hebrew, *as the hind of loves*: that is, as the most beloved or amiable hind. So, *a man of reproofs*: that is, oft reproved, chapter 29:1. Now because it has been the fashion of some, at all times (and then was of more, perchance, in those countries:) to bring up such creatures by the hand; which being so brought up, they become very dear sometimes (as in Nathan's parable 2 Samuel 12:3. we read of an ewe lamb, brought up by a poor man, which *lay in his bosom, etc.*) to them that have brought them up; It is the opinion of some, that Solomon does here allude to such. Others, that he alludes to their nature; being of themselves both in their innocent carriage, and feature, very lovely unto others: to look upon: and very loving among themselves. I think he might have a respect unto both.

*{let her breasts satisfy thee}* Hebrew, *make thee drunk*. Let them give you abundance of content.

*{satisfy thee}* Hebrew, *water thee*.

*{at all times}* Not by fits, as men take content in strumpets.

*{be thou ravished always with her love}* Hebrew, *err thou always in her love*. As the hart wanders up and down when he misses his best beloved hind. It requires abundance of affection from the husband to the wife.

**Verse 20.** *{And why wilt thou}* Seeing there is so many inconveniences in adultery, and conveniences in marriage, Why should you be taken so with the adulteress.

*{a strange woman}* See notes on chapter 2:16.

**Verse 21.** *{For}* Or, *Seeing that*.

*{For the ways of man}* If the former reasons will not draw you from adultery, yet let the consideration of God's judgments do it. It is the argument that prevailed with Job, and kept him in awe, as he professes of himself, Job 31:1, 2, 3, 4. There is no question, but it would with most men, that believe it really, that the ways of men are before God, and that there will be a time of reckoning: But this faith is not in every man that is called a Christian.

*{before the eyes of the Lord}* Though men see not your adultery, yet God does, and will punish you for it, Hebrews 13:4.

*{For the ways of man are before the eyes of the Lord.}* Job 31:4. and 34:21. Chapter 15:3. Jeremiah 16:17. and 32:19. Hebrews 4:13.

**Verse 22.** *{His own iniquities shall take the wicked himself}* Having spoken of God's judgments, and privity, in the former verses, as the chief thing, that should keep men in awe, if all men have faith to believe it; or prudence to consider of it in time: he proceeds unto that which is more present and visible: and that is, the punishment which (besides the torments of an evil conscience common unto all sins; which by heathens themselves, upon good observation and experience, are set out as a very hell.) attends every sin, arising from the sin itself: as for example, drunkenness; such and such diseases; untimely deaths, etc. and most particularly, adultery. Besides bodily diseases innumerable, and a general consumption of the flesh and body, as we had it before in the eleventh verse: an adulterer does expose himself to many dangers. See chapter 6:30. *Men do not despise, etc.* And Horace, most appositely and fully: *Audire est operae pretium, procedere recte Qui moechis non vultis, ut omni parte laborent.—Hic se praecipitem tecto dedit: ille flagellis Ad mortem caesus: fugiens hic decidit acrem praedonis in turbam: dedit hic pro corpore nummos*: etc.

*{with the cords of his sins}* Chapter 29:6.

*{sins}* Hebrew, *sin*.

**Verse 23.** *{He shall die without instruction}* Hebrew, *because there is no instruction*. Or *because of no correction*: Or as Mercer: sine correctione: without correction. If because of no correction, the reason is given of his loss and destruction; because he was never disciplined by his parents, in his youth: nor visited and chastised by God in his riper years, but was suffered to go on without any control. See Hebrews 12:5, 6, 7. Or *if disciplined, and chastised*: yet his perverseness was such, that he profited not by fatherly chastisements: as it does often fall out. *Why should you be stricken any more? you will revolt more and more;* Isaiah 1:5. and Jeremiah 2:30. *In vain have I smitten your children, they received not instruction;* and elsewhere. The Greeks have a very proper word to this purpose. They call an intemperate riotous man, *ἀκόλαστος*: that is properly, an undisciplined man: an unchastised; or, one that was never punished: whether, never, actually, de facto, as we say: or, never, effectually, to his amendment.

*{he shall go astray}* That is; he shall miscarry: A very proper word. For all men naturally, seek one and the same thing: all, propose to themselves the same end, generally: to wit, happiness. But in the right way to happiness: and what it is, that leads a man to real, not conceited happiness: or, if you will, what true and real happiness is, and wherein it consists; there is all the difference: and in the mistake of this, is miscarrying of no less than life. Therefore all sins, are sometimes called in Scripture errors, or, ignorance: *ἀγνοήματα*: Hebrews 9:7. and the Greek word for sin, *ἁμαρτία*, or *ἁμάρτημα*, does properly signify, an erring from the scope: or, a shooting besides the mark. The Septuagint express it by the preterit, *καὶ ἀπώλετο δι᾽ ἀφροσύνην*: and he perished through his folly; or, want of wit, and advisedness: which is not amiss. See more upon chapter 14:6. *The wisdom of the prudent.* Now if any shall think thereby to excuse themselves, because what happens through error, seems excusable: let him see upon chapter 1:20. *Wisdom crieth without, etc.* out of Aristotle, etc. This going astray, if expressed by the preterit, (as is ordinary, to put preterits for futures; and vice versa:) might also be referred unto the cause of the miscarrying (or unfortunate death:) of the wicked: to wit, their unsettledness, in their judgments and resolutions; and continual wandering according to the propriety of the Hebrew word:) in their courses, and actions: of which somewhat has been said out of Seneca: upon chapter 2:15. *whose ways are crooked*, and much more might be added out of the same author, if need were. See also upon chapter 21:16. *The man that wandereth out of the way of understanding.*

## CHAPTER 6

**Verse 1.** *{Surety for thy friend, if thou hast stricken thy hand}, etc.* Chapter 17:18. and 20:16. and 22:26. Of the different translation of the words of this verse, see upon verse 3. *into the hand of thy friend*. As for the matter here handled from this verse to verse 6. to wit, suretyship: some observe a coherence of matter or sense between this, and that which was last spoken of, in the former chapter: to wit, riot, adultery, and whoredom: of which having said among other things, that by it the wealth and substance of a man is exhausted, verse 10. etc. from that he now passes to suretyship, as the likeliest after whoring, and the like, (whereof again verse 26. of this chapter. *For by means of a whorish woman, a man is brought to a piece of bread,*) to overthrow a man in his temporal estate. And as in the former chapter verse 10. strangers were mentioned; so here again. But since that in the greatest part of the book no such coherence of matter is observed, and that there is no need of it here, more than elsewhere, I think with Mercerus, that the observation is rather curious, than necessary. As for the thing itself, suretyship, both here, and in divers other places of this book; as chapter 20:16. *take his garment, etc.* much spoken against, Solomon's intention herein was not to deter men from doing offices of friendship to their friends: such as either giving, (all manner of liberality, and especially to poor, being much commended in this book:) or lending, according to ability; commended by David, Psalm 37. and 112. and nowhere spoken against by Solomon: but only to warn men against the danger, and abuse of pretended friendship: of which nature this kind of suretyship, which Solomon speaks against, seems to be; when a man is importuned, or solicited by his friend, (if he may be called or accounted a friend, who to ease himself, will put his friend upon such inconveniences;) to engage himself for him, beyond what he is able, either at present, out of his own store; or to bear hereafter, if he be put to it, as in common providence must always be presupposed, that he may probably: which at the best, (be the intentions on both sides never so sincere) is bad enough; and is besides subject to much juggling and imposture, whereby many plain honest men are undone by cunning foxes, under color of friendship. Neither is Solomon the only that has been of that mind. It was one of the three famous precepts, or cautions, ascribed unto the gods, and graven upon the doors of Apollo's temple at Delphos: and it seems that before Homer, (except he were the first author of it: it is in his IX. Odyssey,) there was a proverb among the Grecians to that purpose, importing, that suretyship was the character of baseness and wretchedness; whereas to give, or to lend, no man doubts, are rather of nobleness and generosity.

*{for thy friend}* Or, *to thy neighbour*, as verse 3.

*{if thou hast stricken thy hand}* Joined hands with a stranger, as a surety, to pay your friend's debts, if he do not.

*{with a stranger}* A man to whom you owe nothing, nor know how he will use you, if you fall into his hands. Some think that here, by this word stranger, is particularly intended an usurer, who must be a stranger indeed, taken in the propriety of the word; that is, some one of another nation; no Jew, or Israelite; to whom all kind of usury (among themselves) was forbidden, Deuteronomy 23:19, 20. but a stranger might be an usurer among them: as they also were allowed to lend upon use to them that were strangers. But there being no mention made of any use here, but of lending and suretyship only; there is no necessity, nor much probability, that an usurer should be intended. It may do as well, that stranger be here opposed to friend and acquaintance, as we said before. See also upon chapter 2:16. *from the strange woman*.

**Verse 2.** *{Thou art}* Or, *And art*.

*{Thou art snared}* He forbids us not to become sureties for our friends, according to the rule of charity; but wills, That we consider for whom, to whom, and upon what terms we do it; so that the creditor may not be defrauded, nor ourselves damnified.

*{with the words of thy mouth}* Many think words bring no hurt, if they part with no present money; but Solomon tells us, Our promises do ensnare us.

*{thou art}* Or, *and art*.

**Verse 3.** *{when}* Or, *seeing*.

*{into the hand of thy friend}* Hebrew, *בכף־רעך*. Which words are differently expounded. Some, as Mercerus for one, think that the same is intended, as was before in the first verse, called a stranger. For, says Mercerus, it is ordinary in the Hebrew tongue, to indite any man a friend, whom we have to do with, (and so in ordinary English too) whether before known, or unknown. See also upon chapter 11:15. *He that is surety for a stranger.* And so is the word neighbor, I am sure, not by the Hebrews only, but ancient Grecians also used. And the reason, says Mercer, why he was called a stranger before, might be to distinguish him (the creditor) from the friend, (for whom the money is borrowed, and the debt contracted) there also mentioned. But others by friend, understand a creditor indeed, but not the same as was before called a stranger; but one, who is a friend and acquaintance, properly. For they conceive, that two sorts of creditors are here spoken of by Solomon; a friend, and a stranger. And to that end, some translate in the first verse, *אם־ערבת לרעך*, *if thou be surety to thy friend*, not, *for thy friend*, as we have it there. There is no question but the words may be so translated; nay, no question, but that according to the more ordinary use of the particular, or prefix *ל*, that is the more literal translation of the two. Now, say they, what Solomon advises here, should be done by the surety, if the creditor be a friend; that he should lose no time, but speedily humble himself, entreat, get mediators, other friends, etc. much more necessity is there, that the same should be done, if the creditor be a stranger; whom no consideration of friendship, or acquaintance does oblige to use him kindly, or mercifully: and therefore *a minore ad majus*; under the name of friend, the stranger also is included in this advice. I like either of these better, than their interpretation, who by friend, would have the party, for whom this surety is in trouble, understood: as if it be intended to say; Since it is so, that you have convented, or contracted with your friend, to become his surety, etc.

*{and make sure thy friend}* Or, *so shalt thou prevail with thy friend*. The Hebrew word *רהב* is not elsewhere found in Scripture in the conjugation Kal, (as they term it) but in this place; and Ezra 3:5. where it is translated, to behave himself proudly. *The child shall behave himself proudly against the ancient, etc.* But in *Hiphil*; Canticles 6:5. there, to overcome; as here: and Psalm 138:3. to strengthen: as some would have it, have also, *confirmare*; or, *roborare*: *robora amicum tuum*: but not so well I think. To overcome, certainly, fits but in this place; and it must be understood, of an overcoming by importunity; such as Luke 11:8. is emphatically called *ἀναίδεια*: which otherwise does more properly signify impudence.

*{Thy friend}* Some would have *thy friends*, in the plural; and say it is so in the Hebrew. But they are mistaken. There is a jod in the Hebrew indeed; but not as a sign of the plural there; but a jod paragogicum; or, exuberant, as best expositors agree. Now the meaning to some, is; That if we be not able to pay, nor can find no favor with the creditor, we should be importunate with our friends to free us from him with all speed, as beasts or fowls, pursued, fly for their lives, verse 5. But others, with more probability, make this friend here, to be no other than he that was spoken of before, verse 1. that is, either the creditor himself, from whom the danger is; or the debtor, for whom you are engaged. I conceive but one and the same man to be intended. But the advice is good, that we should deal with both in time, what by humility, towards the creditor, as; Matthew 18:26. and 29. *fell down, etc.* (and indeed the word *רפס* here following, may very properly be turned; prostrate yourself:) what by importunity, toward him, for whom we are in danger.

**Verse 4.** *{Give not sleep to thine eyes, nor slumber to thine eyelids}* Psalm 132:4.

**Verse 6.** *{Go to the ant, thou sluggard}* Most observe a coherence and think it material: which is, that having in the former words and passages given counsel to him that is bound, to bestir himself to get himself free with all possible speed; he further encourages him (that he might not pretend disability,) by the example of the industrious and provident ant; so that this, *Go to the ant, etc.* is more particularly spoken to him, spoken of in the former verses; but yet so nevertheless, that it was intended also as a general invitation and exhortation, (whereof all stand in need,) to all men, to be industrious and provident.

*{consider her ways and be wise}* Concerning the ant, her industry, providence, and wisdom, (which Solomon would have us to learn from her,) there be many things written by divers, which, because I neither dare absolutely reject, as false, there being many things in this kind, to many others incredible, of the truth and certainty whereof I make no question: nor yet can upon my certain knowledge and experience, avouch myself for true; I shall therefore, though here exhorted by Solomon, to consider her ways, forbear particularly to insist upon. A certain bishop, a learned man, of Italy, professes to have seen a kind of little town (in length, four foot, and one foot broad;) erected by ants under a rafter, that lay on the ground, out of the way. He describes it very particularly, and their conversation in it; and speaks of it with marvelous confidence: and some others affirm to have seen the like elsewhere. But not to go beyond what all men know and acknowledge of them; here also, and again, chapter 30:25. avouched by Solomon himself, it will be worth our consideration, by what power the ants do this, and how they come by this providence, and this wisdom, which Solomon does here commend unto us. For there be some, not ancient heathen philosophers only, but even professed Christians, who grounding upon this, (not this passage, or testimony; but upon the thing itself here spoken of and confirmed by Solomon,) and other like instances, and experiments, have maintained, that dumb creatures were endowed with reason (yea and speech too, some have said,) as well as men: and others have further argued hence, that the souls of men and beasts, were of one nature, and essence; both the one, and the other, (as some have maintained) equally immortal; or, (as some others) equally mortal. But I will not digress so far; neither, I hope, are they many among us, that need confutation. Divers have written of it; yet not any that I should commend to the reader more, than Origen in that incomparable piece of his (which is extant in Greek, and but meanly translated in Latin) against Celsus the philosopher. All that I think necessary to say about it here, I shall contract into these few positions. First, that Solomon's sending men to the ant to learn wisdom, is no argument (though some would make one of it) that therefore they are naturally endowed with more wisdom, than men are. The argument is *a minore ad majus*; rather to shame us, than to aver anything positively concerning them.
