If they, that want reason, can for their own preservation, by mere instincts of nature, do some acts of reason; how much more unexcusable are we, who by God and nature are endowed with reason, of purpose to provide for the future: to forecast, and to foresee, both for those things that belong to the body; as for those that belong unto the soul: if we make not use of it to that end? So Christ bids us *to consider* (it is the same word) *the ravens*, Luke 12:24. and *to consider the lilies of the field*, verse 27. and so may we in some cases appeal to very fools and children; not that we would have them thought wise; but that they that are, or should be, may be ashamed, if in anything they come short of the wisdom of such. Secondly, we say, that such acts in brutes proceed not from reason, properly, or any ratiocinative faculty; but are instincts of nature, imprinted in the fancy: and that what we call their providence, is not theirs, properly to speak, but his providence, who is their Creator, and of nature, whose power and providence, when we see such things in dumb creatures, we should, as Origen well observes, so much the more admire, who can produce some effects of reason and wisdom, even from them, that are not rational, nor by nature, capable of any wisdom. So that wisdom in them, is not wisdom, properly, and truly; but analogically only. It may be that may be it, that is intended, chapter 30:24. *There be four things that are little upon the earth, but they are exceeding wise;* so there translated: but in the Hebrew it is, *sapientia, effecta sapientia*; that is, as in the margin there, *wise, made wise*. For therein is the difference: All men are naturally endowed with the faculty; that is, all men are capable of wisdom, by nature: but none become actually wise, but by study, and practice, and experience: whereas brutes; what wisdom they have, it is born with them: (we speak of such instincts, or acts of wisdom, in beasts, as are above the ordinary nature or capacity of beasts: such as are there spoken of by Solomon: the providence of the ants, for one particularly, verse 25. *The ants are a people not strong, etc.*) they are wise, made wise, that is, their wisdom is merely natural, or a pure instinct of nature in them, no act or effect of reason, or ratiocination. So I think the words may be understood. But to return where we began: we say lastly, from this and other like places of Scripture, we may learn, that the study and knowledge of nature, (if well used) is very profitable, and does not a little conduce to piety, and true wisdom: not therefore to be neglected, as by most, through idleness, and want of industry, it is; not to be condemned, as by some supercilious ignorant men, who think it makes atheists rather, than promotes piety; which the abuse of it indeed (as of the Scriptures, and all other good things) may do, but not the right use, such as we commend and wish. Having done with the ant, before we proceed to the other matter, I shall only warn the reader, that in some editions of the Septuagints, or Greek translations, after this of the ant, there follows immediately another like exhortation taken from the example of the bee; *Go to the bee, etc.* There is not any suspicion at all, that ever it was in the original Hebrew: neither do any expositors, that I have seen, (not Cornelius a Lapide; or Salazer,) propose it as authentic, but as an addition of the said Greek translators merely; who have many other such, in other places; as is generally acknowledged. The only reason that moves me so far to take notice of it, as to warn the reader of it, is, because most ancient Greek, and some Latin Fathers also, because they found it in that ancient translation (at least in some copies of it) which to them for want of the Hebrew, was in some manner authentic, they take notice of it, as part of Proverbs; whose allegations might trouble a man, that should not know it before, and put him to an endless labor of seeking, or breed in him some suspicion of a defect.

**Verse 7.** *{which having no guide}* The ant, that has none to go before her to show her how to do it, nor none to oversee whether she do it or no, nor no ruler to punish her for idleness, yet labors: much more should men, that have those motives she has not, chapter 30:25, 27.

*{overseer}* Hebrew, *שטר* of which word, if you desire further satisfaction, see Fulleri Miscell. book 3. chapter 19. As to this place, there is nothing that requires much illustration.

**Verse 8.** *{Provideth her meat in the summer}* When the weather is fair, the way smooth, and food to be had, she provides against winter: so should we against sickness and old age.

**Verse 9.** *{How long wilt thou sleep}, etc.* Chapter 24:33. He chides the sluggard for being so long slothful.

**Verse 10.** *{Yet a little sleep}* The sluggard answers, that he had need of more sleeps and slumber: for sluggards never have sleep enough, but ever seek new occasions. This is the most received, and, to me, most probable sense. Yet the words will bear another sense, followed by Mercer, and others: which is: *Sleep little*, (by way of precept or exhortation:) *slumber little, etc.* Or, *A little sleep, a little slumber; a little folding of the hands: to lie down:* (or, sleep). *Lest that thy poverty, etc.* as follows in the next verse. The sense comes all to one: but there is more emphasis in the former interpretation. Some observe a gradation in the words: First, sleeping, then slumbering: and (which uses to be the last usually before a man, a lazy man, does rise:) Thirdly, folding of the hands, and all this, *to sleep*: but better in the Hebrew, *לשכב*, to lie down, properly: which may be equivalent to, before you do rise: or if they be words of the sluggard himself: then; before I rise.

*{sleep}* Hebrew, *sleeps*.

*{slumber}* Hebrew, *slumbers*.

**Verse 11.** *{So shall thy poverty come}, etc.* Chapter 13:4. and 20:4. As if God should reply, Sleep on; but you shall be poor enough at length.

*{as one that travelleth}* Suddenly, and unlooked for, verse 15.

*{thy want as an armed man}* So as it cannot be resisted, Luke 11:21, 22.

**Verse 12.** *{A naughty person, a wicked man}, etc.* In this and the two next verses is set down the character, or description of a naughty man, or, a man of Belial, (of the uses, and composition, or etymology of which words, I doubt not but there has been enough said in former annotations, being so frequent in Scripture) as it is in the original Hebrew. Most of the marks or evidences here expressed, are taken from the outward carriage: which therefore must not be taken as infallible, or necessary; as if none were of that stamp inwardly, but those in whom these outward signs are found; or again all such in whom they are found; but, as all such descriptions must be understood, *ὡς ἐπὶ τὸ πολὺ*, that it is so for the most part; and that these in whom those outward signs or evidences do concur, are to be suspected, till better known. Such a description, from outward signs I mean, of a naughty man, we have in Martial; *crine ruber, niger ore*, etc. *Rem magnam praestas Zoile, si bonus es.* Some observe a coherence of matter between the former passage, and this: because it commonly so falls out, that idleness and sluggishness, breeds lewdness and wickedness: which is true enough; but that Solomon, in this connection of passages, aimed at it, I will not warrant.

*{walketh with a froward mouth}* Some by a froward mouth, here, understand one whose mouth or words, in his ordinary talk, and conversation, is divers from his heart: which in very deed, is a very essential mark of wickedness: contrary to that ingenious, and so much commended simplicity in them that are really good and upright. Others, saucy, bold, impudent language; spoken of before, chapter 4:24. which, as commonly, does issue, from a perverse evil mind.

**Verse 13.** *{He winketh with his eyes}* To wink often, chapter 10:10. seems to be a mark of folly; *He that winketh with his eyes, causeth sorrow*: but (rather, and; as Mercer there, and others) *a prating fool shall fall*; or, *shall be beaten*: (*Punietur*; Mercer) and accordingly, Philo Judaeus, *Stultorum palpebrae oberrant, quorum nec oculi quidem quiescere possunt prae stultitia*, as cited here by Salazar. But it is as often, and so here, a mark of an evil mind, and of a dangerous man; as is more fully expressed by Ecclesiasticus, chapter 27:22, 23. *He that winketh with the eyes, worketh evil, and he that knoweth him, will depart from him. When thou art present he will speak sweetly, etc.* But we must distinguish between a natural kind of winking, which may proceed from weakness of eyes, or some such inward bodily cause: (Pliny makes frequent winking, to be a sign of timorousness; *Plerisque naturale est ut nictare oculis non cessent, quos pavidiores esse accepimus*, book 11. 37. and has no dependency from the heart or mind; and that which is occasioned by the motions and suggestions of the mind, and inward thoughts. Certainly, when that there is much winking in a company, there is just cause to suspect it not right: and at the best, if there be no wickedness in the mind, yet there is incivility in the carriage; especially if it be in company as good, or better than ourselves. But now it must be remembered, which was said but very now, that these outward signs are probable, (a thing often inculcated by Aristotle in his Ethics) not necessary. To speak roughly, and to be full of gesticulations in company, are of themselves bad signs, and to be avoided; yet it is possible, that both may be sometimes, where the heart is well affected. And on the contrary, none more dangerous and pernicious often, than those that are most silent, or most smooth both in tongue and carriage, when in company. So that Cato (that Cato, who is taught to children, but of whom old men, and learned men may learn, without any disparagement,) his advice is not amiss, when he says, *Demissos animo, ac tacitos vitare memento; Qua flumen placidum est, forsan latet altius unda.*

*{he speaketh with the feet, he teacheth with his fingers}, etc.* Isidore does bring a passage out of old Ennius, which does very lively represent the many gesticulations, and busy deportment of a mischievous lewd woman in company; fit to be compared with this; *Quasi in choro pila ludens*, etc. *Alium tenet, alii adnutat, alibi manus est occupata, alii pervellit pedem, alii dat annulum spectandum*, etc. Cornelius a Lapide, has the whole passage.

**Verse 14.** *{Frowardness}* Hebrew, *Frowardnesses*.

*{he deviseth mischief continually}* Besides abundance of corruption in his heart, (Hebrew, frowardnesses) he adds more by devising mischief still, even in the night, when workmen sleep, chapter 3:16. Micah 2:1. Hosea 7:6.

*{he soweth discord}* As there is an extraordinary reward proposed unto them that make peace, James 3:18. and elsewhere; so there is not a more devilish practice in the world, than the sowing of discord, and the love of strife and contention. *But unto them that are contentious*, Romans 2:8. under which word all manner of wickedness is comprehended, as appears by the opposite member. The wise man therefore, among other effects of wickedness, reckons this particularly, that it causes discord: a thing not prejudicial to particular men only, but to human societies in general, therefore of all things to be shunned and detested by all men, that have not abjured humanity, and common nature. See more upon the 16th verse.

*{soweth}* Hebrew, *casteth forth*.

*{discord}* Hebrew, *discords*.

**Verse 15.** *{broken without remedy}* Jeremiah 19:11.

*{without remedy}* A mocker's misery will abide on him.

**Verse 16.** *{These six things doth the Lord hate; yea, seven}, etc.* This whole passage to verse 20. seems to be but a rhetorical amplification, or emphatical prosecution of that which was delivered before verse 14. to wit, that it is a most wicked thing, or the part of a very wicked man, to sow discord. Thus to set it out the more emphatically, and that it might make the greater impression, he reckons divers other things, which are commonly known to be very wicked, and in great detestation with God and man; after all which, he reckons that, as the last, or worst of all: as if that were the utmost of wickedness, beyond which nothing could go. Hereupon expositors take great pains to show why, and wherein sowing of discords, or whispering, or a slandering tongue (for all come to one) should be accounted the very worst of sins, and to God most abominable. There is no question, but a great deal may easily enough be found to that purpose; for it is a most grievous sin, and the cause of greatest mischiefs in the world. See Ecclesiasticus, chapter 28:13. *Curse the whisperer, etc.* and St. James speaks of the tongue with much bitterness, as the occasion of evils, chapter 3. it is the subject almost of all that chapter. However, I do not conceive that this was it that Solomon aimed at, nor that his words should be taken and pressed so precisely, as if he intended positively to maintain, that sowing of discord was a greater sin, than any other here mentioned. I take it rather, to be a rhetorical (as I said before) amplification, or aggravation; whereof there might be the more necessity, because many that offend in that kind, by whispering, carrying of tales; (some, to ingratiate themselves; others, out of an evil custom, and a pleasure they take in prating) and the like, do not apprehend what a sin they commit, nor to what judgments, because out of the reach of human laws and censures, they expose themselves and their families. Neither is it much material whether we translate, septem, seven; and so make all the vices, here recorded, equally hateful and abominable: or, *septimum*, the seventh, (taking *numerum cardinalem pro ordinali*; as, *una sabbathorum*, for, *prima sabbathorum*, and the like) and so make the six first reckoned sins, an object of God's hatred, simply; but the last, or seventh, of his abomination, which is more: whereupon is tacitly implied, that therefore it is the greater sin, because an object of greater hatred: either way, as I conceive, is not much material, because the end of either expression, is only to make us sensible, that whispering, etc. is no small sin, as many think; but of heinous consequence, and by God himself extremely detested. It seems this way of amplification, by six, and seven, as it is here, was ordinary among the Hebrews, as appears by Job 5:19. *He shall deliver thee in six troubles, yea, in seven, etc.* See also Hosea 6:2. and Micah 5:5. and Amos 1:3, 6. etc.

*{unto him}* Hebrew, *of his soul*.

**Verse 17.** *{A proud look}* Hebrew, *Haughty eyes*. It is observed by most, that as in the former passage, the naughty man is described by several members; so here also in this enumeration of several vices, they are most of them, reckoned by the members of the body, beginning from the highest parts, and so downwards in order; which does the more confirm the relation (which has been spoken of) of this passage to the former.

**Verse 18.** *{An heart that deviseth wicked imaginations: feet that be swift in running to mischief}* Solomon by these words seems to set out two sorts of men, (though it is possible that one and the same man may have both qualities:) which are often seen in the world. Some men are great plotters; very able and cunning to devise ways, whatsoever their own lust, or their necessity does cast them upon: either, to compass, what is desired: or to avoid, what is feared. But very cowards though in matter of action and execution; and no men, at their hands or weapons. Others, dull and heavy to find, or invent; but quick and stout, to execute. Now, in point of wickedness, and mischief; which of the two be the worst, I know not: but the plotter, (as before we concluded of counsels, upon chapter 1:5. *shall attain, etc.*) the more dangerous and pernicious of the two, certainly: both, abominable unto God, as we are here taught.

*{in running to mischief}* By mischief, here, seems chiefly to be intended shedding of blood: as may be gathered by chapter 1. verse 16. *For their feet run to evil, and make haste to shed blood;* and by Isaiah 59:7. (where both members of this verse are contained, and will serve for further illustration:) *Their feet run to evil, and they make haste to shed innocent blood: their thoughts are thoughts of iniquity; wasting and destruction are in their paths.* Blood, indeed (the shedding of blood) is the most crying sin; at least, in point of nature: which may be termed rightly *κατ᾽ ἐξοχὴν*, *ἄγος*: piaculum, mischief: and it is observable, that nature (or rather God, the author of nature:) has imprinted the horror of it unto the soul of man so deeply, that in ancientest times of the world, (nearer to the beginning of time, by some thousands of years;) if a man were known to have committed murder, all men, (without any other laws, or solemn excommunications, but the law, and voice of nature,) did shun his company, and thought themselves polluted, if by any chance they had come near him: so that for the most part, they that were guilty of it, proved miserable fugitives and vagabonds (as Cain, by sentence pronounced by God's own mouth:) all their lifetime. And such was the horror of the fact, among them commonly, that strange expiations and penances, almost as bad as death itself, were devised, and undergone by many, who thought they could never do enough, to reconcile themselves to God and the world. Of which, if you please, you may read more, in him, whose deserts will make him live, (though lately, to the great grief of all that love learning, corporally dead:) as long as learning, (so truly called; not that which some late sophisters, broachers of new doctrines, and anabaptists, would cry up for learning:) does live in any part of the world: even learned Mr. Selden, in his III. book de Synedriis: his last work, and left imperfect, because of his death. But now, who they be, that are guilty of this horrible and detestable sin, of shedding of human blood, would be a disquisition well worthy to be taken into consideration, by any Christian, that aspires unto heaven: a real I mean not imaginary, such as many (I doubt) that are called, and call themselves saints upon earth, are likely to inherit. But I may not digress so far. Learned Grotius has handled the matter very well, in his commentaries upon the Gospels.

**Verse 19.** *{speaketh lies}* Chapter 19:5.

*{discord}* Hebrew, *discords*.

**Verse 20.** *{My son, keep thy father's commandment, and forsake not the law of thy mother}* Chapter 1:8. See there.

**Verse 21.** *{neck}* See chapter 2:9.

**Verse 22.** *{it shall lead thee}* The commandment mentioned, verse 20. That is, in effect, the law of God; as is showed upon chapter 1:8. *My son, Hear the instruction of thy father, etc.* and appears also by the words following, *For the commandment is a lamp, etc.* compared with Psalm 19:8. etc. See upon the next verse.

*{keep thee}* Or, *watch over thee*.

*{it shall talk with thee}* Direct you what is to be done all day, as masters in the morning appoint their servants what to do the day following.

**Verse 23.** *{For the commandment is a lamp; and the law is a light}* Psalm 19:8. and 119:105.

*{the commandment is a lamp}* The commandment of your father, and the law of your mother, interpreted as before, may guide you, as a candle by night, and the light by day; and their reproofs are means to save you.

*{and the law is light}* The order of the words in the Hebrew is: *A lamp is the commandment, and the law is light*: which in the original runs very smoothly, and seems to imply a reciprocation of light, and law; law, and light. Now there is a double law, natural, and revealed: both these are lights, and so styled in the Scripture. The natural law is, *λόγος*, reason: and Christ, *Λόγος*, is said to be *the true light, that enlighteneth every one that cometh into the world*: which by Calvin and Beza, is interpreted of the natural light of reason, common to all men. However, I take Solomon here to speak of the law of God revealed; as David Psalm 19:7. etc. where also we may observe the reason of the coherence, between the first and second part of that Psalm; from this place: the former setting out the praises of light, in general; and the latter, the praises of the law of God; as being that to the soul of man, what the light of the sun is to the body.

*{lamp}* Or, *candle*.

**Verse 24.** *{To keep thee}* Chapter 2:16. and 5:3. and 7:5. The verse before may be included in a parenthesis, and this verse depends on the end of the two and twentieth.

*{from the evil woman}* Some men, very skillful in Scripture phrase, and generally much approved for their labors in that kind, are of opinion, that here, and Psalm 15:4. (where according to our late translation, we read it in the English, *who sweareth to his own hurt*: but according to the former, *who sweareth to his neighbour*:) the Hebrew word *רע* or *rangh*, may be taken for *רע* or *reangh*; that is, a neighbor. So the sense would be: *To keep thee from [thy] neighbour's wife.* And so indeed in this very chapter verse 29. we find it: *So he that goes in to his neighbour's wife:* whereas I do not remember that she is called evil elsewhere in this book. However, this is not to make her sin the less, if she be not called evil here. There is enough said of her besides, to make us know her. Nay, I think, your neighbor's wife, (that is, as has been often said already: another man's wife: (or, any one, that is not yours:) absolutely spoken, is more effectual to the aggravation of the crime. I would not be thought to seek jests (I abhor it.) in a sacred subject. I do believe seriously, there be men in the world, that think fornication and adultery no great sin, so it be among the godly; that is, such as they account so, by their outward garb, and manner of speech; men and women of their tribe and profession. To prevent such distinctions, which the devil may suggest, of an evil woman, which they will say perchance, is a common whore, that admits of all men; and a godly sister, as they call them: your neighbor, or, another man's wife; (or, any other, that is not yours:) I said is more absolute and convincing. But because there be so many other places of Scripture, in this very book, to convince their madness, that are so captivated to the spirit of error and delusion; we need not fly to this for an argument to make this interpretation of the word, more plausible. Either of the two, may do very well.

*{of the tongue of a strange woman}* Or, *of the strange tongue*.

**Verse 25.** *{Lust not}* Or, *That thou lust not*.

*{Lust not after her beauty in thine heart}* Matthew 5:28.

*{neither let her take thee with her eyelids}* Take heed of her fair looks as well as of her sweet words; for if she catch you with her eyes; when she has brought you to extreme poverty she will take away your life also.

**Verse 26.** *{For}* Or, *When*.

*{piece of bread}* 2 Samuel 2:36.

*{and}* Or, *then*.

*{the adulteress}* Hebrew, *the woman of a man, or a man's wife*.

*{the adulteress will hunt}* Hebrew, *the wife of another man*: that is, a whore. Or, *A woman given to men*.

*{for the precious life}* Which should be precious to you, Matthew 16:26. Or, *after precious souls*; that is, properest persons, and well qualified: well descended, well bred, wealthy, etc. There be also, who by precious souls, here; would have the soul, properly taken, to be meant. And this may be a good sense, if we take those creatures to be as the devil's bait, or instruments, by which he catches souls. For he it is, that hunts after souls, properly; though it may be ascribed unto them also, whom he sets on work, to compass his ends. However, I think this somewhat remote: and I rather believe, that by precious life, is understood, not life only, which though it be consumed and corrupted by such actions, yet is not the thing properly, that whores and strumpets, hunt after; but livelihood, or substance; which is sometimes called blood, and life: as has been noted upon chapter 1:11. *Let us hunt after blood.* This greediness, unsatiableness, yea unmercifulness and cruelty of such women, towards those that deal with them, is excellently well set out by ancient comics: who represent them as very harpies, or cormorants, in point of prey. See, if you please, but Plautus in his Truculenta, where Astaphius speaks alone. You cannot have a better comment upon these words. And therefore were comics anciently said, to teach; and they, among other titles, called teachers, because of the good they did, by such lively representations, to reclaim many young ignorants, from their idle courses. There be words in that comedy which I have named, that make to my purpose very much. One lays to their charge there, that by them young men did perire, perish: intending, that they were the destruction of men. Now because the word is more proper to destruction of the person, or life itself; than of substance: one of the company does answer: *Dicis contumeliam. Nemo hominum hic perire solet apud nos: res perdunt suas: ubi perdiderunt Res; abire hinc si volunt, uti jus, licet.* The meaning of which words is, (for they are to my purpose:) that it is a slander, to say, that men did perish there: No, their goods and their substance did indeed; but for their persons, when they had spent all, they might go whither they would: they would not hinder their liberty. So there. But withal it must be known, that in those days, though at all times, the frequent use did consume the body, and shorten life; yet those horrid diseases, that will make a more speedy end, and are of a more penetrative quality, were not known or heard of. More unexcusable are they therefore, who notwithstanding such visible destruction to their lives, livelihood, and very souls, (as Christ and his apostles so plainly teach:) cannot be reclaimed. Precious life, we may interpret, whatsoever he does possess; even that which is most precious; goods, lands, and houses: for even they are not spared, oftentimes; but all goes, to maintain this wicked trade. Or, precious life, may be, any man his livelihood, or substance, be it more or less; which is precious unto wise men; and none but mad men, or infatuated, would bring themselves to beggary; that is, to be a scorn unto all men; for such momentany pleasures, more fit for brutes, than men.

**Verse 27.** *{Can a man take fire in his bosom}* A very fit similitude, because a wife, or woman is according to the Hebrew idiom, said to be taken into the bosom, when entertained for carnal copulation. See Genesis 16:5. Sarai speaks unto Abraham: *I have given my maid into thy bosom.* And Deuteronomy 13:6. *the wife of thy bosom.* Besides, there may be some intimation of dangerous insinuation, when the word bosom, is applied unto lewd women. For then, and thereby chiefly, they have the opportunity to do mischief: that is, to obtain what they will: and man then in worse capacity to make use of his wit, and reason, against them, if he have any.

**Verse 28.** *{Can one go upon hot coals}* A man cannot use familiarity with such, or dalliance, but it will turn to his ruin, Judges 16.

**Verse 29.** *{So he that goeth in to his neighbour's wife}* Most ancient Fathers take these words not of adultery committed; but of outward familiar conversation by way of ordinary friendship and acquaintance, with another man's wife: as if Solomon here, and in the former verses, addressed his speech to them, who (as doubtless many do) frequent and love the company of some women, not out of any evil intention or purpose, either to corrupt, or to be corrupted, in that kind: but out of mere idleness, curiosity, want of better employments, or haply, some respect (at least, as they fancy to themselves) to their good and (for so is that good word in ordinary language prostituted and profaned) virtuous parts. If we shall so interpret this *goeth in*, here; then the sense of the former words must be, that as a man cannot take fire into his bosom, nor go upon hot coals, but he shall be burnt: so it is as impossible that a man (such a one especially, as Solomon speaks to young and lusty, etc.) should long converse (by way of company-keeping, must be understood,) with a woman, (especially, if wanton, and beautiful; and cunning withal,) but the fire of love will kindle in him at last, so that by degrees he shall be drawn to that which at first he would not have thought. This is the more probable, because it is very ordinary for all writers, poets especially, to compare beautiful women to fire: as he in that comic, *Accede ad ignem hunc*, etc. and divers others. However, I conceive that there is not less ground for the former interpretation; from the context, and otherwise.

**Verse 30.** *{Men do not despise a thief}, etc.* Some would have this to be the sense: *Men do not despise a thief*: to wit, to let him escape unpunished; though he be driven to it by some kind of necessity, against his will. And if not a thief; how much less an adulterer? But the authors of this interpretation contrary to the current of interpreters, might have done well, to have showed, that the original word *בוז* is ever taken for contempt in this kind; which, I take, is more than they could. The more safe therefore and more received interpretation is, that Solomon does here aggravate the crime of adultery above ordinary theft. The comparison is very proper, adultery itself being a kind of theft, and so called ordinarily by Latins and Grecians (*furtum*, *furtivae nuptiae*, *γαμεκλόπος*, etc.) but as a theft of a higher nature: so, subject (among the Hebrews at least) to much more danger and ignominy, than ordinary stealing. In most places, it was death: among the Athenians, though not death, yet was the punishment of it very shameful and ignominious, (*τιλμός*, *ῥαφανίδωσις*, etc.) described by the scholiast upon Aristophanes. But though theft was not properly punished by reproach; yet it was a reproach doubtless, and a great reproach too, to be convicted of it. This therefore must not be understood absolutely, but comparatively only; as if a man said, a thief is an honest man, to you; or the like. Besides, it does much qualify the case (in point of reproach at least:) where apparent necessity, to save one's life, is the ground. Solomon's mind is very well expressed (and it may be some confirmation to this interpretation we follow:) by an ancient poet, (though not so ancient as the title pretends,) commonly called Phocylides, in these words: *Ὅστις ἑκὼν ἀδικῇ*, *κακὸς ἀνήρ*, *ἀλλ᾽ ὑπ᾽ ἀνάγκης*, *Οὐκ ἐρέω τὸ τέλος*· *βουλὴν δ᾽ εὔθυνε ἑκάστου*.

*{Men do not despise}* The civil law does distinguish between fures, latrones, and grassatores: in their guilt, and in their punishment. Fur, properly, is a petty thief, that steals by advantage of the night, or privacy; not by any force, but sleight and cunning: sheds no blood nor does any other harm. Latro, is one, who whether in the night or day, sets upon men by force, and first bereaves them of their lives, and then of their goods: as the fashion is of most highway robbers, beyond the seas. Grassator, otherwise called Abigeus, is one; or to speak more properly, grassatores, (or abigei: For they go always by companies:) are they, that set upon men by force, whether in their houses or upon the ways: but openly, and without any intention to kill. Booty, is their aim, and that they live by. Now when Solomon says *that men do not despise a thief*, it would be most proper to understand it of the grassatores, (for latrones, they are to be looked upon rather as homicides, than thieves:) who indeed are more properly feared, than despised: neither is their punishment by the civil law so great, as of absolute thieves. But because of that which follows, of restitution, if he be taken; it must needs be taken of ordinary thieves, that deal by sleight, not of them that deal by open force. We say then, that Solomon's words may aim at the same thing, that is delivered by our Savior, where speaking of the unjust steward, who seeing that he should be turned out of his place, and considering with himself, that he was not brought up either to beg or to dig; found a way, how he might subsist, when out of his place. An unjust way it is true, and therefore called the unjust steward: he shamefully and unconscionably cozened his master by it: but what is conscience to a man, that has no further aim than this life? who knows of no immortality of the soul: no day of judgment: in a word, whose summum bonum, or principal end is, in the goods of this present world? Therefore, says Christ, his Lord commended him. Why? Because he did not sit down, like a fool; bemoaning his condition, and expecting that men should pity him: but as a wise man, according to his principles (wherein consists chiefest wisdom, as Aristotle shows, in all our deliberations, to have a respect to our principles:) he did stoutly bestir himself, lost no time; and by the help of his wits, found a way to live out of misery. So may we, upon this warrant conclude of a thief, that has no principle to oblige him to conscience; or to think, that it were better for a man to starve, (according to St. Paul's doctrine, Romans 8:35. *—shall tribulation, etc.*) or to suffer any kind of misery in this world; than to incur the wrath of God, by an evil life: that such a one, as a mere natural man, is more to be commended, than he who through want of wit or spirit, can make no shift for his living. And all this tends but to aggravate the more the folly and want of wit of any, that wastes himself, body and goods upon harlots. The one, being hardly put to it by unavoidable necessity, finds a way (though not without danger, because of the law:) to subsist: the other, having plenty of both, a vigorous body, and competency of wealth, for which he can never be too thankful unto God; suffers himself to be stripped of both, and becomes a prey to a lewd woman, who herself, (in her own thoughts:) judges him a fool, while he dotes upon her conceited beauty, at that rate. Who therefore of the two deserves to be more despised? This therefore does not properly tend, to compare the heinousness, or grievousness of the sins in point of law either divine, or human: but only in point of wit, and worldly wisdom, which more or less despicable. And Cicero does well teach us to distinguish between *contemnere*, (to despise:) and *male existimare*, (to have an ill opinion.) *Non enim (homines) omnes eos contemnunt, de quibus male existimeat. Nam quos improbos, maledicos, fraudulentos putant, et ad faciendam injuriam instructos; eos haud contemnunt quidem, sed de iis male existimant. Contemnuntur ii, qui nec sibi nec alteri prosunt; in quibus nullus labor, nulla industria, nulla cura est.* Which is very pertinent to Solomon’s words.

**Verse 31.** *{But if he be found, he shall restore sevenfold}* Of a sevenfold restoration, we read not anywhere in the law: but of double, and fourfold in some cases; and in others fivefold: Exod. 22:1, 4. &c. As therefore seven is often taken for many; so sevenfold here, for manifold: by which word, all those different restitutions appointed by the law, are comprehended. So most. Some by sevenfold, understand *septies*, or seven times: (as Ps. 12:6. *purified seven times*, *שבעתים* the word here:) that is, say they, as often as he is convicted, though it be seven times, or oftener; yet still he can be but put to restitution: his life is not in danger. Sevenfold may also be taken, for plentiful: restitution with good advantage, such as is prescribed by the law. Neither is their subtlety altogether to be slighted, who join in Exod. 22. the first and fourth verses, there together; that is, five, and two, there appointed for restitution, in several cases, would make up this sevenfold of Solomon’s words.

**Verse 32.** *{understanding}* Heb. *heart*.

*{all the substance of his house}* That is the worst that can happen to him; and that is, in case he be very poor, and that all that he has, will but serve to make restitution according to law, whether twofold, or sevenfold; Exod. 22:1, 4.

*{destroyeth his own soul}* Life, and livelihood. See before upon Chap. 5. v. 4, 5. *her steps take hold on hell*.

**Verse 33.** *{A wound and dishonour shall he get}* That is, death, appointed by the law, or be slain by the strumpet’s husband. See ver. 34. Or, *a wound in his fame*, which shall never be done away.

**Verse 34.** *{therefore he will not spare in the day of vengeance}* He shows, that a man commonly seeks his death that has abused his wife; and so concludes, That the husband will accept of no ransom, to save the adulterer.

**Verse 35.** *{He will not regard any ransom}* Heb. *He will not accept the face of any ransom*.

## CHAPTER 7

**Verse 2.** *{Keep my commandments, and live}* Chapter 4:4.

*{and my law as the apple of thine eye}* By this diversity of words, he means, That nothing ought to be so dear unto us, as the Word of God; nor we look on anything more, nor mind anything so much.

*{apple of thine eye}* Ps. 17:8.

*{eye}* Heb. *eyes*.

**Verse 3.** *{Bind them upon thy fingers, write them upon the table of thine heart}* Deut. 6:8. & 11. 18. Chapter 3:3.

**Verse 4.** *{kinswoman}* Heb. *kinsman*.

**Verse 5.** *{That they may keep thee from the strange woman}* Chapter 5:3. & 2. 16.

**Verse 6.** *{For at the window of my house I looked}* I speak not without reason, for I have seen the folly of those that have suffered themselves to be abused by harlots. Some make a question, whether Solomon does relate those things simply, and historically, or whether parabolically: so they call it: but figuratively, I think they would say. For here is nothing of a parable, or similitude, but a lively representation of the thing itself, which Solomon has so often insisted upon; the subtlety of harlots, to entice a young man, and to draw him into their nets: the dissimulation of the one, and the simplicity of the other; with the sad consequent of it. Such representations of things, which bring them (if artificial indeed; or rather, very natural and lively:) to the very sight; are called by rhetoricians, *ἐνάργειαι*: *evidentiae*: whereof Quintilian has divers choice examples out of Virgil, and Cicero. Some begin: *videre videbar*: as if we said, *Methinks I saw*, &c. which I think is all intended here by Solomon, when he says, *I looked out of a window*, &c. But Theognis, (often named, because of the same subject as this book is, and very ancient:) upon the like occasion, in the representation of a prodigal, and a miser; he is as peremptory in his expressions, as Solomon: *Ἐν τριόδῳ δ᾽ ἕστηκα*: *I stood*, says he, *in a place, where three ways meet*, &c. and then tells you what he saw.

**Verse 7.** *{the youths}* Heb. *the sons*.

**Verse 9.** *{in the evening}* Heb. *in the evening of the day*.

*{in the black and dark night}* He shows, that there was almost none so impudent, but they were afraid to be seen; and also their own consciences did accuse them, which caused them to seek the night, to cover their filthiness. Job 24:15. 1 Thess. 5:7.

*{in the dark and black night}* Not so dark, literally, but that he could see him through the window, vers. 6, 7. but he does insist the more upon it, the more effectually to set out to our apprehensions, the horror of the thing: all sins, of that kind especially, being rightly deemed *works of darkness*, (though committed in the light: and those as rightly said to *walk in darkness*, (1 John 1:16. *If we say that we have fellowship with him*, &c.) that commit them, or make a practice of them, though the sun be a witness to their lewdness.

*{with the attire of an harlot}* Whether a professed harlot, one that all men might at first sight know to be such, (as in some countries they are bound to a certain attire, of purpose to distinguish them,) or so attired (which may be said of many, who perchance, intend no further hurt, though by their attire they give just occasion of suspicion,) as might become an harlot better, than an honest virtuous woman, may be doubted. That she was (the woman here described) a very harlot by her deeds and actions, is no question: but whether a professed one, such a one as made a trade of it, not so much for her lust, as for her living; a common public harlot: that which follows afterwards, ver. 19, 20. concerning her husband, though not doubted by any expositors that I can find, makes it to me doubtful. My opinion is, (since I find nothing about it in others) that she was, if any such really, as some think; but if not really, yet that this description is intended of a common public harlot: and that what she says of her husband, as though she had one; or he that she had, were a stranger to her lewdness; it is to be understood, as cunningly and politicly spoken by her, the more strongly and powerfully to incense and engage the young fool, it being the manner of such (as Horace, and divers others observe) rather to desire to commit wickedness with one that has the reputation of an honest woman, than with a common professed strumpet. Somewhat she herself has to that purpose, afterwards, *Stolen waters are sweet, and bread eaten in secret, is pleasant*. Chapter 9:17.

**Verse 10.** *{subtil}* Heb. *reserved*, if *נצורת* here from *נצר*, *custodivit*, &c. But if as Mercer, and most others, from *צור*, *obsessit*, &c. then *cincta corde*, that is, *compassed about with heart*, or *subtlety*. And because the word *נצר* is supposed by most, sometimes also to signify, *to waste, to make desolate*: some think that *wasted* that is, *bereaved*, or *void of heart*, may do well in this place, as before v. 7. of the young man, we had it: *void of understanding*. And certainly that sense may fit such a woman very well: yea though she be granted (as most of that way:) subtle and crafty too. For there be none commonly, more unwise and foolish, as to the main end, to which all true wisdom, or folly is referred; (as we observed before out of Aristotle:) than those that are most subtle and crafty in their ordinary dealings and conversation; to bring some particular ends to pass, which they make their whole study. And to go no further than this very life, and this present world: How miserable is the end commonly, of such creatures? and how base and despicable, in the eyes of all men. How much abhorred and detested by them that have any sense of ingenuity, or generosity, are their courses? Besides daily affronts and scorn from them by whom they live; and in many places, legal punishments. We say therefore, the sense is good: but whether their supposition be true, is that which I more question. Two places are quoted for this sense, Es. 1. 8. *urbs devastata*: or, *desolata*. Indeed, the vulgar Latin has it there, *civitas quae vastatur*: but most other translations, with the Chaldee paraphrase; *obsessa*: or to that effect. So that we can build but little upon this place. The second place, is out of the same prophet, Es. 65. 4. where translations are very divers: some *speluncis*: some *delubris*: the Chaldee, *cadaveribus mortuorum*: Jun. *custodita*: our English, *monuments*. I find none, but Aben Ezra quoted for that sense. So that this signification of the word having no better ground; it will be much safer to stick to the known, and unquestionable. See also upon verse 11. *loud and stubborn*.

**Verse 11.** *{She is loud and stubborn}* He sets down some properties of an harlot, which agree very well with those descriptions we find of them in ancient comics; as that she is clamorous, impudent, unruly, never at home, and the like: some of which properties are repeated again, Chapter 9:13. but some there also which may seem contrary: as that she is *simple, and knoweth nothing*: contrary to what he says here, in the verse before, that she is *subtle of heart*. We might answer in general, as before, that all such marks and descriptions of manners must always be read with reservation, *ὡς ἐπὶ τὸ πολύ*; that is, *for the most part*: which supposes, that the contrary may be true sometimes. See also upon verse 10. *subtle*. But whether the same be intended there also, as is here spoken of, and described, is a great question: of which, see more there.

**Verse 12.** *{Now she is without}, &c.* He insists the more upon this, because in those times and countries, it was not the fashion for honest women to be much abroad, nor without great occasion: as by ancient writers, Menander, and divers others, does appear: And St. Paul, it seems, would have it the fashion, everywhere; who among other things, requires in virtuous and godly women, that they should be *οἰκουροί*, *Keepers at home*, Titus 2:5.

**Verse 13.** *{and kissed him}* It was very ordinary among men (in those days: among the Romans especially:) to kiss one another (being friends) when they met in the way. Martial makes mention of it, often in his Epigrams: yea and complains of it, because it was a great hindrance to men that went about their business, to be so often interrupted in the streets. Only in the extremity of winter they forbare: or at least, they were thought uncivil, (as by the same Martial does appear) that did not then forbear. But that the same was done by and towards women too, that they kissed, and were kissed in the streets; I mean civil women of honest reputation: I do not remember neither among the Romans, nor elsewhere. So then I take this for a certain sign, that this here spoken of was a public whore.

*{with an impudent face said}* Heb. *she strengthened her face, and said*.

**Verse 14.** *{I have peace-offerings with me}* Heb. *peace-offerings are upon me*. Some interpreters would have these peace-offerings, here mentioned to have reference *to the young man*, as though she intended to say, that she had made a vow to God concerning him, either for his good health, or her meeting with him, or both: and should now acknowledge, and congratulate herself, that she has obtained her wish; or to that effect. But certainly they are mistaken, that think so. In ancient times, when sacrifices were in use, both among the Jews, and elsewhere, almost all the world over, they seldom did make any feast; (not sober housekeepers, at least) but when they did sacrifice: whence it is, that as the word *feast* with us, which properly does signify *a holy day*, is taken for an invitation, and merry meeting; so *sacrifices* in the Scripture is sometimes (as Prov. 17:1.) taken for *good cheer*. Hence are those expressions in comics, *Rem divinam facio*; used as a preface to an invitation: and, *Sacrificant: dant inde partem majorem mihi*, &c. and the like. Now sacrifices, among the Hebrews, were of several kinds. For in some; (as in holocausts:) nothing at all was reserved; but all consumed to smoke and ashes. In others, the priest had all the flesh that was left: it was his due by the law. In some others again, (of which kind were those, that were called peace-offerings, properly: of which we read, Levit. 7. and elsewhere,) the greatest part went to the offerer himself, who was at the charge of the sacrifice: and those were the sacrifices that caused invitations and good cheer. Here therefore, *I have peace-offerings with me*, is, in effect, I have good provision at home: this day I make a feast, &c.

*{this day have I paid my vows}* Which declares that harlots outwardly will seem holy and religious, both because they may the better deceive others, and also flatter themselves; thinking, by observing of ceremonies and offerings, to make satisfaction for their sins. Which is the religion of many in these days; who think if they hear so many sermons in one week, they need no other convoy to heaven, though otherwise very vicious; as malicious, covetous, fraudulent, railers, whoremongers, drunkards, and the like: and some ministers are well enough content they should think so, who therefore make that a special mark of the godly; (and will not allow that title to far honester men:) because they reap no small profit by it.

**Verse 15.** *{to meet thee}* As if her affection were to him only, which was ready for the first comer.

**Verse 18.** *{our fill of love}* *be drunken with loves*, Chapter 5:19.

*{until the morning}* Which shows her unsatiable lust.

**Verse 20.** *{He hath taken a bag of money}* There is no fear of my husband’s sudden return; for he has taken money with him to supply the charge of a long journey. See before upon ver. 10. *with the attire of an harlot*. Certainly, if this woman had a husband really, and feared he should know what she did, she was (which nevertheless is most agreeable to the description here made of her,) no common professed harlot: it being one of the properties of such, to be unmarried; or if married, yet to have the consent and approbation of their husbands, in what they profess. Now if according to the description in the former verses, a common harlot; and that what she pretends here of a husband, be but a mere pretense, as we show there; it must be understood likewise, that she was a *stranger*, of another nation, and not a daughter of Israel: it being against the law, Deut. 23:17. that any such should be allowed to make profession of that infamous life. However, it must be added withal, that the word Deut. 23:17. is *קדשה*, which whether it be the same as *זונה* here, in these Proverbs, and in the next verse there, there is some question: and in case the words (as is most probable) be the same; yet from 1 Kings 3:16. and other places of Scripture, but especially in this book of Proverbs, it may as probably be conceived, that notwithstanding that law, Deut. 23:17. by a kind of connivency, divers that were Israelitish women born, were permitted: but separated though from the rest, not only by the common imputation of profaneness, but also by legal excommunications: so that they were either *strangers*, really, and by birth; or held as *strangers*, in the common estimation of men.

*{with him}* Heb. *in his hand*.

*{the day appointed}* Or, *the new moon*. We find the same word, Ps. 81:4. and not anywhere else in the Scripture. There translated, *the time appointed*; in our English, as here; *the day appointed*: but there also, by some, *New moon*. The common opinion is, that it comes from *כסס* which signifies, *to reckon, to cast up: to sum up*: and so the noun of it, to signify an appointed time, or season. But they that would have it to signify *New moon*, they fetch it from *כסה* *to cover*, because, say they, the moon is then covered by the sun. But there is yet another interpretation of the word, as it may be supposed to descend from that radix, and that is, *the feast of tabernacles*: more commonly called *סכות*, from *סכך* *to cover*, because of the covering of booths and tabernacles, wherein it was celebrated. This last interpretation may fit perchance, Ps. 81:4. but *New moon*, much better here: and better too I am of opinion, than *appointed day*. For new moons, were great festivities; as appears by the words of David; 1 Sam. 20:5. and if a man had occasion to travel, yet it is likely he would endeavor to be at home against such a festivity. And divers heathens in imitation of the Jews, that lived among them; observed those days, and forbare ordinary employments, as may appear by Horace, where the *New moon* festivity, is called, *trigesima sabbatha*.

**Verse 21.** *{With her much fair speech she causeth him to yield}* Fair words prevail more to draw men to looseness, than wanton gestures. Chapter 5:3.

*{with the flattering of her lips she forced him}* When once they begin to yield, they go on, as it were by force.

**Verse 22.** *{He goeth after her straightway}* If he had taken time to consider of the sin, and punishment, he might have escaped.

*{straightway}* Heb. *suddenly*.

*{as an ox goeth to the slaughter}* Which, thinking he goes to the pasture, goes to the shambles.

*{or as a fool to the correction of the stocks}* Who goes cheerfully, not knowing that he shall be punished. Or he may mean more generally, that the place whither this young man (though merrily and cheerfully, through his ignorance and folly,) is going, is in very deed no other, than as a place of slaughter to the ox; or public stocks, to one that is condemned to them: who probably (if he be not very desperate and shameless:) goes but heavily to them, to become a public spectacle, &c. and so would this young man go to a harlot’s house, as heavily, and more, did he rightly know whither he goes.

*{as a fool to the correction of the stocks}* Heb. *as the stocks are for the correction of the fool*. Or, *and* [to] *the stocks*, [which are] *for the correction of the fool*. This is the order of the words, in the Hebrew, which is somewhat intricate; and though our English does express the sense very well; yet that English, cannot, grammatically, be made of the words: because *מוסר* being *in regimine*, as Mercer well observes against Aben-Ezra; *מוסר אויל* *correction of a fool*; or, *the fool*; must go together; and cannot be parted in the sense. Therefore Buxtorfius has another interpretation of the words: *Et sicut [vir] compedis ad eruditionem stultorum.* It is very ordinary in the Hebrew, to leave out one of the substantives, whereof the said Buxtorfius has collected sundry examples in his grammar, and reduced them to certain rules. But this very book of Proverbs will afford us enough, for our turn in this place. Chapter 17:4. *falsitas*; for, *vir falsitatis*: *a liar*. Chapter 10:18. *lying lips*; for, a man of lying lips: as we shall see there. Chapter 14:1. *foolishness*, or *folly*: that is, a foolish woman. So here, *velut compes*: that is, *vir compedis*, or *compeditus*: and that is, (as I conceive; for I cannot make sense of it otherwise: though he does not express himself so clearly:) *compedibus implicandus*, which does imply; *as a man goeth to the stocks*, [which are] *the correction* [or punishment] *of fools*: Or, *as a man that is to be put in the stocks, to the correction of fools*.

**Verse 23.** *{Till a dart strike through his liver}* He shows the blockishness of the young man, who never thinks of his danger, till he have undone himself.

*{strike through his liver}* Job 28:24, 25.

*{snare}* Chapter 1:17. Eccles. 9:12.

**Verse 24.** *{Hearken unto me now therefore}* Being warned by this terrible example, hearken rather to my wholesome words, than to the alluring ones of harlots.

**Verse 26.** *{For she hath cast down many wounded}* That is, she has wounded many: Or, many have been cast down, by those wounds they received from her.

*{yea, many strong men have been slain by her}* Heb. *and all that are slain by her are strong men*. Or, *and they many; all that are slain by her*. And they that are slain by her are many. So Junius, and some others. And it is well observed by some, that the Hebrew word, does not only signify *strong*: but *many*, also. So Num. 31:1. and Ps. 35:18. according to most translations.

*{many strong men have been slain by her}* Many stout men have been overcome by harlots; yea, the most of them that she destroys are stout men; so that the strength of a man is oft overcome by the sleight of a woman: none has been so strong, but, being careless, they have been overcome by lust, Jud. 16.

**Verse 27.** *{Her house is the way to hell}* Chapter 2:18. & 5. 5. Heb. *the ways to hell are in her house*; that is, there are many ways to hell in the harlot’s house.

*{way}* Heb. *ways*.

## CHAPTER 8

**Verse 1.** *{Doth not wisdom}* This whole chapter contains nothing else, but the praises of wisdom; human and create, to verse 22. *By me*, &c. Divine and uncreate, to verse 32. *Now therefore hearken unto me*, &c. where begins, for conclusion, an exhortation unto men, to embrace so useful and heavenly a thing: Wisdom herself, by a *prosopopoeia*, under the person of a woman, being made to commend herself: which though among men be not allowable (Chapter 27:2. *Let another man*, &c.) because we have nothing that we have *not received*, 1 Cor. 4:7. and because the most perfect, in strict judgment, are but as *unprofitable servants*: Luke 17:10. in God, it is quite another thing; of whatsoever we can say by way of praise, is rather his nature, than what we call praise among men: and of whom we can say nothing properly, that may bring us unto some conceit of his nature, but but by way of praise and admiration. Besides, that the benefit of divine praises, does not redound unto God; but unto men, who by those praises come to the knowledge and love of God; wherein is their felicity. Some expositors would have Christ to be the only subject; but they give no reason at all, but that they think it not convenient, that these two should be confounded, and represented under one person. But when they come to expound the first part of the chapter, to verse 22. they swallow far greater inconveniences, and improbabilities, to apply all to Christ’s person: and whatsoever they bring, to prove that Christ must be understood, is taken from, or has reference to that which follows, from verse 22. *By me*, &c. which we also interpret of Christ. So that all in effect they do say, is, they do not think it probable, that Solomon would join divine substantial wisdom, with that which is communicated unto men. Neither indeed does he. For he speaks of them in order, by themselves. And why not of both, in one chapter; and under one wisdom; since all human wisdom, even that of artificers and tradesmen, is, in the Word of God, ascribed unto God? See before upon Chap. 1. ver. 2. *to know wisdom*, &c. the second annotation: and Chapter 3:19. *The Lord by wisdom*, &c.

*{Doth not wisdom cry, and understanding}, &c.* Solomon declares, that man is cause of his own perdition; and that he can pretend no ignorance, forasmuch as God calls to all men aloud by his ministers in his church, to walk in the ways of piety. See more upon Chapter 1:20. *wisdom cryeth without*, &c.

**Verse 2.** *{in the top of high places}* In the highest places, where she may best be heard. Teachers were wont to be placed higher than their auditors, Neh. 8:4. Acts 22:3.

*{by the way}* Where men commonly pass by, that she may be heard of all men. Thus wisdom speaks openly, in the light, not caring who hear her; whereas, the harlot speaks in darkness, and corners, Chapter 7:9, 12.

**Verse 3.** *{She crieth}* Heb. *They cry*, Chapter 1:20.

*{She crieth at the gates}* Where the people resorted for justice, and which they passed through for commerce, Ruth 4:1. Ps. 127:5. Jer. 17:19.

*{gates}* Heb. *hand*, or *side of the gates*.

*{entry}* Heb. *mouth*.

*{at the coming in at the doors}* Where they must needs go in, and out upon all occasions.

**Verse 4.** *{O men}* That is, great men, and mean, as Ps. 49:2. Wisdom cries to all sorts.

**Verse 6.** *{I will speak of excellent things}* Heb. *princes*: that is, words that do as much excel others, as princes do the people: or, words that all men ought to be guided by, as by princes. So we call the sentences of judges, in times past, *ruling cases*. Or, *[words] of princes*: (according to Buxtorfius in his *Thes. Gramm.*) that is, words that best become princes, both as teachers, (because of their authority:) and learners; because most necessary unto them, who are to be examples unto others. See more in the preface, concerning Proverbs in general: and upon Chapter 1:1. *Proverbs*: and the references there.

*{excellent things}* Chapter 22:20. See there.

**Verse 7.** *{my mouth shall speak truth}* There is nothing but truth in wisdom’s speech; whereas, in the best men’s sayings there may be errors, as water mixed with wine, and dross with gold, Psalm 12:6. & 19. 10.

*{mouth}* Heb. *palate*. Chapter 5:3.

*{wickedness is an abomination to my lips}* I will set out the abomination of wickedness by my lips. I will describe it, and expose it to the view of all men, that it shall be abhorred by all, that will read and consider. So most, or to the purpose. But because wickedness is oftener ascribed to the tongue, or lips in Scripture; partly because, *out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaketh*, usually, and partly, because, from words, men usually proceed to actions; (so Ps. 52:2, 3, 4. *thy tongue deviseth mischief*, &c. and Ps. 140:11. *an evil speaker, a wicked man*, &c. according to the margin there, and Prov. 12:13, 14. *The wicked is snared*, &c.) we may extend the words to actions also; as a protestation of perfect integrity and innocence.

*{an abomination to my lips}* Hebrew, *the abomination of my lips*.

**Verse 8.** *{froward}* Heb. *wreathed*.

**Verse. {They are all plain}** Heb. *right before him*; that is, obvious, and easy to be found. We may not infer from hence, that the Scripture is plain, and easy to be understood by all that will read it. The contrary is well known to them that have taken pains in a right way, that is, (humility and constant praying, and a godly life, always presupposed:) by the help of good literature and knowledge in the tongues: (not by indulging to groundless fancies, and enthusiasms,) to understand it. But this, That those things that are absolutely necessary to salvation, in point of faith; but especially, to direct us to a godly life, (the best means to a sound and saving faith:) are obvious enough to be understood, if men will read with diligence, and humility; and study a good conscience (without which no knowledge is profitable) at the same time. The words also may be translated: *They are all straight, to him that understandeth*, as some would have them. And the sense is good, so too: and more agreeable to the second member. All men are not satisfied concerning the justice and equity of all that they read in the Scripture. It has made many; some, professed; some, concealed atheists. Our Savior says in a place, *if any man will do his will*, he should understand his doctrine, John 7:17.) that is one condition: a man must study purity of heart, and integrity of life, that will be a proficient in the knowledge of Scriptures: and Solomon says here, he must be *מבין* *one that understandeth*: that is who does heartily desire knowledge, and understanding, and does apply himself to the means, with constancy and sedulity. The *deed* sometimes in Scripture, is put for the *endeavor*, so Genes. 37. 21. *and he delivered them out of their hands*, that is, he did his best to do it, and did think he should. And Exod. 8:18, *the Magicians did so*, that is, they did endeavor it, but *could not*, as follows there.

*{to them that find knowledge}* That is, that endeavor to find it, that are studious of it, and apply themselves to the means. See the former annotations: and before, Chapter 2:3, 4. *If thou criest after knowledge*, &c. *If thou seekest after silver*, &c.

**Verse 10.** *{and not silver}* That is, rather than silver, Hos. 6:6.

**Verse 11.** *{For wisdom is better than rubies}* Job 28:15. Ps. 19:10. Chapter 3:15. & 16. 16.

**Verse 12.** *{I wisdom dwell with prudence}* Heb. *subtlety*. See upon Chapter 1:4. *subtlety*. And here we may add, that it is ordinary in ordinary language; whether spoken; or written; when we are very earnest upon the commendation of a thing, to give it the names and titles of those things, (if there be any analogy, or ground in nature:) that we know to be in request with them to whom we address our speech. So we may call good water, (when, and where it may be drunk safely; and there be in some places of the world, men and women of quality, we know, that drink no other, all their life:) wine, as if one should say: this is my wine, my claret, and my sack: and of any wholesome ordinary meat, here I have my partridges, and my pheasants: which every man would easily understand. So here, when Solomon does ascribe *wit*, and *subtlety* to true wisdom, that is, to piety and religion; he has a respect unto the generality of men who affect wit and subtlety (whence is that of the poet: *Aurum et opes et rura frequens donabit amicus. Qui velit ingenio cedere rarus erit*, above all things. Such an expression has Arrianus (who of all philosophers, Greek or Latin, both in language and doctrine, comes nearest to Christian simplicity;) upon Epictetus, lib. 2. c. 2. *περὶ ἀταραξίας*: *Τοῦτο σοὶ προοίμιον*: *τοῦτο διήγησις*: *τοῦτο πίστις*: *τοῦτο νίκη*: *τοῦτο ἐπίλογος*. So the apostle: that *Godliness with content, is great gain*, 1 Tim. 6:6. not intending gain, as ordinarily taken, but that there is more happiness in godliness, accompanied (as always it is) with a contented mind, than in great wealth. In the same sense we may say that all wit, and craft, and subtlety, yea wisdom and eloquence: and all generally that is commendable and much desired among men, is included in religion and godliness, from which eternal bliss and happiness does descend. And yet, in all this come short of the stoics, (whose philosophy had much Christianity in it, though long before Christ, in great esteem and splendor:) who maintained of their *wise man*, that he was the only, not rich and valiant only; but fair and beautiful man of the world.

*{witty}* Or, *skillful*.

**Verse 13.** *{The fear of the Lord is to hate evil}* He then that does not hate evil, fears not the Lord, Chapter 16:6. Gen. 20:11. Job 1:1. Ps. 97:10. This verse does somewhat interrupt the context; neither does it properly contain, or concern the praises of wisdom, what some rabbins have devised, to bring it to that, may be read in Mercerus: but it gives me little content. But since we have many such interruptions, in this book, I know not why we should much stand upon it here. It may be thought that Solomon having spoken somewhat in commendation of wisdom, in the phrase and language of the world; ascribing to it *subtlety*, (which is often taken in a bad sense) and *the knowledge of witty inventions*: which is an ambiguous word too as well as the former: he might think it seasonable immediately to insert this of *the fear of the Lord*, &c. to prevent any either abuse of his words; or mistake of his intention. But this is not very material.

*{pride and arrogance, and the evil way}* Here be two words in the Hebrew, (*גאה* and *גאון*) which are but one word, both in sense and sound, as if this word *pride* we should make, *prideness*, and *prideship*. Neither can it be said, that it was for want of other words, whereby to express pride and arrogance. More true therefore, that it is an idiotism and elegance of the Hebrew tongues, thus sometimes by repeating the same word, to intimate and include all particulars, all divisions, I mean, and differences of the same kind. So for example, Isa. 3:1. *For behold the Lord doth take away from Jerusalem, the stay and staff*: in the Hebrew it is but one word with a little variation of the termination only. But then, what by that repetition of the same word is intended; is immediately after declared, in this, and the following verses; by enumeration of particulars; *the whole stay of bread, and the whole stay of water. The mighty man*, &c. Now we know there be divers kinds of pride; internal, external, spiritual, worldly: Some are proud even in their humility, because they are not proud. It is a large subject. All kind of pride is very abominable to God. See more upon Chapter 3:34. *He scorneth the scorner*, &c.

**Verse 14.** *{Counsel is mine, and sound wisdom}* Heb. *תושיה* *sound wisdom*, here in opposition to *human wisdom*, which oftentimes proves faulty, not in point of salvation only, but in regard of the world, and worldly businesses. Insomuch that some have doubted, whether there be any such thing as worldly wisdom, and prudence, really; because they see it so often disappointed, and contrary events to follow. Of the Hebrew word, see upon Chapter 2:7. *sound wisdom for the right*.

*{I have strength}* I have power to bring my counsels to effect, or, I give both policy and power.

**Verse 15.** *{By me kings reign}* Rulers cannot rule justly, nor establish their authority without wisdom: for from Christ they have their power, and wisdom to use it aright. See more upon Chapter 10:11, 16. *A divine sentence*, &c. Or, if Christ, the eternal and uncreated Wisdom, as afterward, be here properly intended; it agrees with Colos. 1. 16. *For by him all things were created*, &c. and Rom. 13:1. *There is no power but of God*.

*{and nobles}* Heb. *נדיבים*: that is, according to the propriety of the word, *voluntarii, spontanei*. And because nothing better beseems a prince or great man, than to be *free*, and to do all things *freely*: Princes, and governors also are so called. Both significations are very usual and obvious in Scripture. Princes and governors, I say, whether good, or bad: free, or exactors, without (as in many words it falls out:) any respect to the etymology. See Ps. 83:11. *make their Nobles*, &c. However, I cannot like so well of the Greek, that expresses it here by *τύραννοι*, though I know that even by later authors, the word is sometimes taken in the better sense, & that it is true of *Tyrants*, as well as of kings, (though in a different respect:) that they are made, not vicious, or tyrannical, but great and prosperous, not in favor to them, but in just indignation, and for a punishment, to others: by God, the supreme Ruler, and disposer of all things.

**Verse 17.** *{those that seek me early shall find me}* That is, they that seek me in my word diligently, with a desire to profit by it, as students do, that rise betimes to get knowledge.

**Verse 18.** *{Riches and honour are with me}* Chapter 3:16. See there.

*{durable riches and righteousness}* By these words Solomon does seem clearly enough to interpret himself, what he principally, (if not only) intended by these words before, *Riches and Honour*, &c. For who is such a stranger in the things of the world, that knows not that worldly wealth and riches are the most uncertain and unconstant things of the world? Who has not said it, and observed it; that has any occasion to speak of riches? And who better, more pregnantly, and elegantly, than wise Solomon? Witness those words of his. Chapter 23:4, 5. *Labour not to be rich, cease from thine own wisdom*: (for that indeed is the chief wisdom of the world, and according to which men among worldlings are styled wise:) *Wilt thou set thine eyes upon that which is not? For riches certainly*, &c. So that if Solomon had said no more than *durable riches*, we might have understood him: But adding *righteousness*, we are the more confirmed in this sense. For *the riches and justice*, (which some would have translated, *riches of justice*; as if there were a *regimen* in the words; but as I conceive not so rightly:) I take to be opposed to that which is called by Christ, (and I believe was so commonly) *The Mammon of unrighteousness, and the unrighteous Mammon*: (that is, deceitful, transitory, inconstant:) Luke 16:9, 11. which words, they that understand of ill gotten wealth and riches; besides that, it is very inconvenient and improbable, that Christ should bid us to be liberal or charitable out of ill gotten goods, without mention of any satisfaction to be made; may be refuted by the opposite member; in the 11. verse, *if you have not been faithful in the unrighteous Mammon, who will commit to your trust the true riches?* as in the 10. verse *ἐλάχιστον*, *that which is least*, (to wit, worldly wealth, so inconstant, and so perishable:) to that which is *much*, or eternal. I like of Beza’s translation here very well, who renders it, *Mammonâ fallace*, and I wish the English (since there is so much ground for it,) had been so too: *deceitful Mammon*, instead of *unrighteous Mammon*, or *Mammon of unrighteousness*. See also upon Chapter 11:18. *a deceitful work, and sure reward*. I will not say that *ἡ ἀπάτη τοῦ πλούτου*, *the deceitfulness of riches*, Matt. 4:19. must be so understood, neither is it the same word in the Greek; though it may bear that sense too in that place. Besides, that justice, (whether by it we understand *godliness*, or general uprightness or *justice* precisely, as the crown or foundation of the other virtues, it matters not here) has a more peculiar relation to eternal life in the Scripture phrase, may appear by that speech, *the resurrection of the just*, Luke 14:13. for which John 5:29. we have *the resurrection of life*; that is, to eternal life; as by the opposite *resurrection of damnation* does appear. Now though we do not so well approve that a *regimen* should be made of the word here, in point of grammatical construction, as we said before; yet the sense by the figure called *ἓν διὰ δυοῖν*, (whereof many examples be in Scripture, as, *thy sorrow and thy conception*; Gen. 3:16. for the sorrows or pains of this conception, &c.) the sense I say, may be all one: *riches and righteousness*: for riches of righteousness. Though this too is more than needs: since *righteousness* alone, without any such, whether grammatical or figurative construction after *durable riches*, may well stand for a further explication of eternal life. We may observe besides, that in the following verses, *justice* is not joined with *silver* or *gold*, but in some manner opposed unto them; so that Solomon having as it were by way of condescension to the weakness of man, begun with promise of silver and gold; he does now seem to recall or rather to interpret himself; proposing unto us justice (that is, eternal life) as so much better, and which does virtually comprehend in itself whatsoever can be desired. See also v. 21. *to inherit substance*, and the reference there.

**Verse 19.** *{my fruit is better than gold, yea, than fine gold}* Chapter 3:14.

**Verse 20.** *{lead}* Or, *walk*: or: *make to walk*.

*{in the midst of the paths of judgment}* For there can be no true justice or judgment, which is not directed by this wisdom.

**Verse 21.** *{to inherit substance}* Heb. *להנחיל יש*. See before upon Chapter 2:7. *He layeth up sound wisdom for the righteous*, &c. I think *true being*, (as the word is expounded by most here) would have been a truer and more plain translation. It is true that substance as it is interpreted by Platonists, who deny (as we said in the reference,) that anything had reality, of being, that was not eternal; or at least, that was corruptible and temporary, may do as well. But otherwise, the word is ambiguous in ordinary English, and as I conceive, apt to be mistaken by the vulgar.

*{and I will fill}* Or, *and fill*.

**Verse 22.** *{The Lord possessed me in the beginning of his way}* Much ado there has been of old about these words, as unto them that are not altogether unacquainted with ecclesiastical histories and heresies, cannot be unknown. For what is here in the Hebrew *קנני*, well rendered *possessed me*: was by the Septuagint translated *ἔκτισέ με*, that is, *created me*: which the Arrians, who denied the eternal deity of Christ, took hold of, and made great boast of this authority: and the fathers, most of them for want of knowledge of the Hebrew tongue, were much put to it to answer them: yet many answers were devised; but among them all, not many, very solid and satisfactory. Since the Hebrew text has been better understood, most men have been of opinion, that the Greek translation was right enough at first; but since that, either of purpose, or casually by reason of the affinity of the words, depraved: that is, that whereas they had rendered it, *ἔκτησέ με* (with an *η* from *κτάω* *possideo*:) that is, *possessed me*: it had been made afterward, *ἔκτισέ με* (with an *ι* from *κτίζω*: (that is, *created me*. Certain it is, that Aquila, and some other later Greek interpreters had rendered it by *ἐκτήσατό με*, which is the same, but more usual, as *ἔκτησέ με*. This though it be, at first hearing, very probable, and by most received; yet upon further search it will appear, as I conceive not true. For first, the author of Ecclesiasticus, who lived about that time when the Septuagint did, and often, (as was observed in the preface) expresses Solomon, he has the word *κτίζειν* often; and varied by several tenses, Ecclesiasticus 24:8, 9. (*ὁ κτίστης ἁπάντων καὶ ὁ κτίσας με*: — *πρὸ τοῦ αἰῶνος ἀπ᾽ ἀρχῆς ἔκτισέ με*:) and though the book be now extant in Greek only, yet it was first (as all acknowledge) written in Hebrew: so that no question at all can be made but that he intended, and so understood by Solomon, *created*; and not *possessed*. To him agrees the Chaldee paraphrase, where Solomon is expressed: *אלהא בראני בריש בריתיה*, &c. And not here only, but also Gen. 14:19. and 22. where the word *קנה* is found, of God; both the said Chaldee paraphrase, and the Septuagint interpreters, use the same words; the one, their *ברא* and the other, their *κτίζειν*. No question therefore can be made, but they intended *created*: but whether rightly is another thing. For my part I could be content to believe, and I think I see ground enough for it, that the Hebrew word *קנה* had anciently both significations *to possess* and *to create*: (and so it may be in the fore-quoted place, Gen. 14:19, 22. *Possessor of heaven and earth, Creator of heaven and earth*, with the LXX. and Chaldee would not be much amiss: and though I make no question at all, but *to possess*, is the more proper signification in this place, yet if it were granted that the word might be *created* also, I think we should grant no more, but what might conveniently enough be interpreted according to St. Paul’s expression of Christ, Col. 1:15. *the Image of the living God, the firstborn*, (*πρωτότοκος πάσης κτίσεως*) *of every creature*. Neither is the word *ברא* so proper to things that are made, or produced of nothing; but that sometimes (as *procreare*, in Latin:) it is used of generation also; as Ps. 102:18. *And the people shall be created, or born*: according to the most interpreters.

*{possessed me}* A very proper word to express the eternal generation of Christ the Son of God: Of a temporal, but mystical generation it is used, Deut. 32:6. where Moses speaks to the Israelites: *Is not he* (God) *thy father*; *קנך* *possessor tuus*; as Junius his margin there: our English; *that hath bought thee*: so it may be too: but *possessor* is as literal and proper to the word; not improper to the place, and coherence; which is: *Hath he not made thee, and established thee?*

*{in the beginning of his ways}* That is, of creation: then possessed by him, and therefore before any creation: that is, in effect eternal, just. So S. John, *In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with* *God*; here *possessed*:) *and the word was God*, &c. *ὁ πρωτότοκος πάσης κτίσεως* *the firstborn of every creature*. Col. 1:15. and *ἀρχὴ κτίσεως τοῦ θεοῦ*: *the beginning of the creation of God*: (that is, of all that was created by God,) Apoc. 3. 14. And here indeed as we noted before, begins another person to speak; or if you will the same person; but in different relations: and so to verse 32. where begins the exhortation grounded upon these several respective praises of wisdom: see at the beginning of the chapter, upon v. 1. *Doth not wisdom*.

*{in the beginning of his ways: before his works of old}* The Hebrews, because (as is here observed by Mercer,) they have no proper word to express eternity, (for *עולם* is properly, *saeculum*: *קדם* *antiquity*; and so of the rest:) they are wont to set it out by expressions of times and things before the creation: which though improper of itself, yet is most suitable!
