(Eternity being a thing incomprehensible to man) to men’s apprehensions and capacities. See upon vers. 25. concerning the mountains.

*{of old}* Ps. 93:2. Heb. *from then*: that is, before his works were made in the creation.

**Verse 23.** *{I was set up from everlasting}* Heb. *נסכתי* which may also be translated, *I was anointed*: and is the same word (there actively; here passively:) used of Christ, Ps. 2:6. *Yet have I set my King*, &c. Christ was anointed King over the church by God’s decree, before the world was.

**Verse 24.** *{I was brought forth}* He was begotten by the Father from eternity.

**Verse 25.** *{Before the mountains were settled}* Some translate *mersi essent*, or *immersi*: that is, *were sunk*: with reference to the waters, which are supposed lower than the earth: to express a deep foundation, which such a vast bulk (as if it were a building: as mountains are, need to stand firm: Others, *fixi essent; were fixed*; that is, in effect, *settled*. Such an expression of God’s eternity we have Ps. 90:2. *Before the mountains were brought forth, or that thou hadst formed the earth and the world: even from everlasting to everlasting thou art God*. So that the Arrians had no cause from such expressions as these, fitted to the capacity of man, to argue against the eternity of Christ, the Son of God. See before upon the 22. verse.

**Verse 26.** *{fields}* Or, *open places*.

*{nor the highest place of the dust of the world}* Heb. *the beginning of the dusts of the world*: that is, before the matter of the earth was made. *וראש עפרות תבל* which may also be translated, *et caput pulverum terrae: id est, potiorem partem*: as Mercer expresses it: that is, *the head*, or *principal parts of the earth*.

**Verse 27.** *{When he prepared the heavens}* Christ was with the Father in the creation of the world, when he made the heavens, girded in the sea, set the clouds fast above, and the fountains beneath.

*{a compass}* Or, *a circle*.

**Verse 29.** *{When he gave to the sea his decree}* Gen. 2:9, 10. Job 38:10, 11. Psalm 104:9. The decree of the sea, to keep it always in its bounds.

**Verse 30.** *{as one brought up with him}* As a child with the father. *Brought up by him*, I think would have been more proper to that signification of the original word, (*alumnus*, in Latin) which most embrace in this place. *Brought up with him*, should rather (if I mistake not in the propriety of the English phrase) be *ὁμοτρόφιμος*; than *alumnus*, Rabbi Solomon indeed does use that expression in the exposition of the word, (the original word I mean; *אמון*) *educata cum eo*; but Mercerus does interpret him, *apud illum*: and *ואצלו* I believe will bear *by him* (or as the Chaldee) *in latere ejus*; as well as *with him*. But there be divers other expositions of the word, followed by several commentators. And this very interpretation of it by *alumnus*, is ambiguous; because *alumnus* is both active and passive: either *educatur*, or *educans*: which is Junius his word and expression in this place. See his notes there upon it; which is the sense of many besides him. Next unto him, the most received is *artifex*: which both the Septuagint, and the vulgar Latin; with some other of the eastern translations (the Chaldee is for *alumnus*: (seem to aim at; so it would be in English *I was coworker with him*: or more literally; *I was a worker* (or *at work*:) *by him*; or *with him*.) And this, does very well agree with the expressions of the evangelist; (S. John chapter 1:3.) and of Saint Paul, Col. 1:16. &c. And some that have taken the pains to examine the places, where the preposition *אצל* is used in Scripture (above sixty in all) observe upon it, the distinct personality of this *Wisdom* from the *Father*: because never used in the Scriptures, but of different subjects, and *supposition*.

*{I was daily his delight}* Heb. *delights*: that is, his greatest delight: for even when the world was in making, God the Father took more pleasure in his Son, than in his works. Agreeable to this, is that voice that came down, from heaven concerning Christ when he was baptized, *This is my well beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased*: Matt. 3:17.

*{rejoicing}* Or, *sporting*. So vers. 31.

*{rejoicing always before him}* Heb. *sporting greatly*, as little ones do before their parents. Or, rejoices with him in the works of the creation. So the psalmist of God the Father, Ps. 104:31. *The Lord shall rejoice in his works*.

**Verse 31.** *{Rejoicing in the habitable part of his earth}* Heb. *sporting greatly*: so that the work of creation was no pain or labor, but a pleasure to the Son of God.

*{my delights were with the sons of men}* Though he delighted in all the creatures, yet most of all in men; and came afterwards to dwell among them, John 1:14. And of his wonderful love to the sons of men; himself speaks, John 15:13. *Greater love hath no man than this, that he lay down his life for his friends*. And here ends this excellent character of Christ, as God, the eternal Son of God the Father, the Savior and Redeemer of men. Christians, (sincere Christians) should make much of such passages, since that Christ to this day continues, *a stumbling block unto the Jews, and unto the Greeks* (Heathens, Mahometans, &c. the far greater part of the world) *foolishness*: Yea, and too many Christians too, as Socinians, and others. I wish that even among them that are sound enough in this point, there were not many that have found another way, of their own devising, without any ground of Scripture, to expose Christianity to more contempt and scorn, and obloquy, than the greatest mysteries of it, grounded upon assured revelation, could ever bring it to among infidels. They that shall read Popish commentators upon this chapter: will easily understand what I mean, though I cannot without some horror, yet that the reader may the better know how to value sound doctrine, grounded upon the Word of God; I will here give him some account of their extravagances, that I say not blasphemies: detested by none more, I verily believe than by the blessed virgin herself, whom they would seem to honor. *Beata Virgo*, (says Corn. à Lap.) *fuit estque idea Sapientiae: mystice Dominus creavit et procedit Virgine, utpote Christi matrem, quasi principium vivum, i.e. operum suorum, &c. Beata virgo ab aeterno praedestinata fuit, ut esset principium, i.e. prima princeps, et Domina omnium operum Dei: ut esset idea Sanctitatis*; This may seem somewhat: but is little or nothing in comparison of what Salazar has done, and yet he I know, not the first author neither. He disputes it, and concludes it absolutely, that *omnia bona temporalia et spiritualia* are in the power, and disposing of the blessed virgin: and that we may obtain *velociorem salutem*, (more quick and speedier salvation or deliverance:) *per matrem quam per Filium*. And that the whole Trinity, Father, Son, and Holy Ghost, have passed in heaven an absolute decree, *ne quid unquam gratiae, et auxilii ab ipsis in homines proficisceretur, quod Virginis Oratio non impetraret*: That is, (for I would have all men to take notice,) *that no grace, favor, or assistance, should issue from them to any*, (a man or woman) *that were not obtained* (or *granted*,) *at the entreaty of the Virgin*. So he, and much more he has there to the same purpose; so that I believe they that shall peruse him, himself, will not find that I have done him any wrong. See also what has been noted of Bonaventure’s Psalter, in the preface upon the Psalms.

**Verse 32.** *{Now therefore hearken unto me}* Seeing I am eternal, and so dear unto God, and count you as my children, hearken to my counsel, and you shall be happy.

*{blessed are they that keep my ways}* Psalm 119:1, 2. and 128. 2. Luke 11:28.

**Verse 34.** *{Blessed is the man that heareth me}* Chapter 3:13, 18.

*{waiting daily at my gates}* No pains is too much to get wisdom.

*{watching at the posts of my doors}* Men must take instruction when God offers it; and like scholars attend their masters’ hours, vers. 17.

*{obtain}* Heb. *bring forth*.

**Verse 35.** *{Shall obtain favour of the Lord}* Chapter 3:4. *So shalt thou find favour*, &c. See there. Or, *thou shalt obtain thy desire whatsoever it is, of the Lord,* Psalm 145:19. *He will fulfill the desire*, &c. and John 9:13. *Now we know*, &c. See upon Psalm 21:2. *his heart’s desire*.

**Verse 36.** *{all they that hate me love death}* Very emphatical. Hence we may learn, when it is in our power to prevent a mischief; if we do not, we may be charged (before God) as the cause of it, and to have desired it. Of life and death, how to be understood here. See upon Chapter 3:19. *She is a tree of life*.

## CHAPTER 9

**Verse 1.** *{Wisdom}* Heb. *wisdoms*. See upon Chapter 1:20.

*{Wisdom hath builded}* That is, Christ the supreme Wisdom, Chapter 8:1. Or rather wisdom in general, opposed unto folly: a heavenly thing, even so, as heathens themselves speak of it, that knew nothing of Christ, either as God, or man. See also upon 13. v. *A foolish woman*.

*{her house}* He has prepared him a church. Or if wisdom in general be understood, the building of a house, and hewing of pillars, may serve to set out the firmness and stability, yea, and the glory of true wisdom, in its own nature; though it does not always fall out to wise men, in this world, according to their wisdom, observed by Solomon, Eccles. 9. vers. *I returned, and saw under the sun, that the race is not unto the swift*, &c.

*{seven pillars}* Many chief stays and principal parts of his church, as were the patriarchs, prophets, apostles, pastors, and doctors. Some rabbins by these seven pillars, understand the seven liberal arts. Others, the seven gifts of the Holy Ghost, Isa. 11:2. Others, the seven first days of the creation of the world. Many such allegories may be devised; which may have some ground of application. But the most literal interpretation is, to take *seven*, here, as is ordinary in the Scripture, *pro numero perfectionis*, for a perfect number: so that by seven pillars, be understood many pillars; so many, both for number and variety, as might be sufficient both to uphold, and to adorn the building.

**Verse 2.** *{her beasts}* Heb. *her killing*.

*{she hath mingled her wine}* Heb. *mingled it: or poured it out*. So they did, in those hot countries, mix their wine with water, to allay the heat of it. So does Christ fit his heavenly instruction for our earthly capacity.

*{she hath also furnished her table}* He compares wisdom to a great queen, who keeps open house for all comers. So does the King of heaven, Matt. 22:2. This is opposed to the harlot’s feast, Chapter 7:14.

**Verse 3.** *{She hath sent forth}* Salvation wrought by Christ will not profit us, except it be published to us.

*{her maidens}* The incorrupt teachers and ministers of Christ, who are his servants; 2 Cor. 4:2. Or more generally and literally, we may understand all them, whom God raised in several ages, and nations of the world, to teach righteousness, and to reclaim men, whether by speaking, or by writing from their vicious courses. To this end tended all moral philosophy among the heathen, as appears by divers of their writings yet extant, from which, even Christians may reap great benefit.

*{the highest places}* Heb. *the wings of the high places*. See before upon Chapter 1:20, 24. *in the chief place of concourse*.

**Verse 4.** *{Whoso is simple}* He that knows his own ignorance, and is void of malice. See more upon Chapter 1:4. *to the simple*.

*{in hither}* Or, *aside*, as ver. 15, 16.

**Verse 5.** *{eat of my bread}* By meat and drink, is meant the Word of God, and the ministration of the sacraments, whereby God nourishes his servants in his house, which is, the church, John 6:27. Or more generally, and probably, by *bread* here, we may understand any variety of fish or flesh, that may probably be supposed in a sumptuous feast, and plentiful entertainment. So Genesis 31:54. *Then Jacob killed beasts*. (So our English margin there, which I think is more right than the English of the text, in that place; as Junius his notes will tell you:) *upon the mount, and called his brethren to eat bread, and they did eat bread*, &c. and so elsewhere, frequently.

*{mingled}* Or, *poured out*, as ver. 2.

**Verse 6.** *{Forsake the foolish}* Leave the company of ignorant and wicked men: or leave your foolish and sinful ways. So Prov. 1:22. See upon Psalm 14:1. *The fool hath said*, &c.

**Verse 7.** *{He that reproveth a scorner}* The 7, 8, 9, 10. verses may be read included in a parenthesis; for in the 11. verse, is a reason given of the exhortation given in the fifth and sixth verses.

*{getteth himself a blot}* For the wicked will condemn the reprover, and labor to defame him, Matthew 7:6.

**Verse 8.** *{Reprove not a scorner}* Meaning them which are incorrigible, which Christ calls dogs and swine: or, he speaks in comparison; Rather rebuke a wise man, than a fool: not that scorners should not be rebuked simply, or of none; but advisedly and discreetly, considering their malice, and the small hope of doing them good. So Chapter 23:9. *Speak not in the ears of a fool, for he will despise the wisdom of thy words*. See also Chapter 26:4, 5. *Answer not a fool*, &c.

*{Reprove not a scorner, lest he hate thee}* We may understand this as spoken according to the rules of human wisdom, and by way of divine condescension rather, as many things of the law: (whereof, see Matthew 5. from verse 21. *Ye have heard*, &c. to the end,) than absolute approbation, or command. Though even among Christians, who are tied to much more strictness, we deny not but good use may be made of this admonition. For though charity does oblige us, where we think we may do good: yet upon this general warrant of charity without any other obligation; to take upon us to reprove all men promiscuously, is rather a breach of charity, and common peace; than a fulfilling of it. If therefore by our indiscreet boldness (which commonly is an effect of pride) we incur hatred, and expose ourselves to dangers and persecutions, we may thank ourselves: and it is to be feared, that we do it rather to please our own humor (as naturally perchance, rigid and censorious:) though we pretend zeal, than to serve God. But if we have a particular calling, or commission from God, to reprove, as either magistrates, masters, parents, ministers, and preachers especially; there the case is altered; and we must be so far from fearing hatred in the discharge of our duties, that we have more reason to fear, that we do not discharge our duties as we ought to do, if we be not hated; either hated, generally; or at least by some. Then must we remember the words of Christ; *Ye shall be hated of all men*: Matthew 10:22. and *Blessed are you, when men shall hate you*: Luke 6:22.

**Verse 10.** *{The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom}* Job 28:28. Ps. 111:10. Chapter 1:7. They then that fear not God, have not one spark of true and saving knowledge. See more upon chapter 1:7.

*{and the knowledge of the holy is understanding}* A very plausible sense, and agreeable to some former expressions, if it can be made out of the original words: which we shall examine by and by. In the meantime for the sense and matter of the words, besides the former reference upon chapter 1:7. *The fear of the Lord*: so upon chapter 2:5. *Then shalt thou understand*. And because, I find the words so pregnant and pertinent to the place; to what you have there out of Plato; add this divine passage of one of the Roman emperors, (inferior unto none whether emperor or other that we have heard of, but Solomon, for wisdom:) out of the 5. book of his *Meditations*: *Why should imprudent unlearned fools* [mere worldly wise men, he means: great artists, great politicians, and the like:] *trouble that which is learned and knowing*, [or, understanding:] *And which is the* [truly] *learned and knowing* [or, *understanding* *ψυχὴ ἔντεχνος καὶ ἐπιστήμων*, are his words:] *soul? She that understandeth the beginning and the end*: [or, *him that is the beginning and the end*:] *and hath true knowledge of that Rational Essence, that passeth through all things subsisting* [Greek *τὸν διὰ τῆς οὐσίας διήκοντα λόγον*.] *and through all ages, disposing and dispensing* (as it were) *this universe by certain periods of time*. What does he say by all these words, but what is said by Solomon here; and by Job and David in the references? But by the prophet Jeremy, chapter 9:23, 24. *Thus saith the Lord, Let not the wise man*, &c. more emphatically, than by any other. Yet this wise Roman had it not out of Job, or David, or any other inspired author, but from very ancient philosophers (as we have showed elsewhere:) who had no other light to guide them unto it, than the light of nature. This would be considered by the atheists of our times, as many of them as pretend to learning: as also by those (great promoters of atheism and Mahometism:) who cry down human learning as unprofitable, or contrary to true godliness. But now to the original words: *ודעת קדשים בינה*. All the question is about the word *קדשים* which may either be taken *Neutraliter*, for *holy things*, as it is found in many places: or *masculine*, for *holy men*: and in that sense common enough: so that according to the first sense of the word, the translation would be: *and the knowledge of holy things*: (that is, of things that belong to piety and godliness:) according to the second, *The knowledge of holy men*; that is, that knowledge which holy men do profess, and that makes men holy) *is understanding*. Neither of these is to be rejected; and most interpreters are for one of the two. But according to our English here, the word *קדשים* is not only taken *masculine*, but also *κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν*, or *per excellentiam*, for the *Holy of Holies*, or *the Holy One*; absolutely even God himself. Indeed, we know that God is often styled, *The Holy one of Israel*, (or Jacob,) and *the Holy One*: absolutely, yea, Esa. 57. 15. *Holy is his Name*. But the original word here is not *Holy* in the singular, but *Holies* in the plural: so that to make this translation good, it must be further supposed, that as *אלהים* and *אדונים* being plurals, (by an idiotism or civility rather of the Hebrew language; which divers others have imitated therein: for to make a mystery of it as some do; I see but little ground of reason for it, and it does give the Jews great advantage against us:) are spoken of one God: so in this *קדשים* in this place, though plural yet to be understood and expressed singularly. But for an example of the like I know none, (Chapter 30:3. of this book excepted: where the words in effect, being the same, we can conclude nothing from them to help us here:) in any other place of Scripture; and if there be none; (for I have not books by me at this time to determine it absolutely:) then may we say of this plural in this sense and place, that it is a singular in more respects than one. All that I can find towards it is, that *קדשים* is joined with *אלהים*, and both construed singularly, Josh. 24:19. and that Hos. 11. last verse, where the text renders it *and is faithful with the Saints*: the margin has it, *with the most holy*, (which if allowed) does agree with our English here; but that instead of *holy*, which is a positive; the plural is there expressed by a superlative singular *most holy*. The Greek translation of these words, that goes under the name of the Septuagint, in the Roman edition, is double: *καὶ βουλὴ ἁγίων σύνεσις* and *τὸ γνῶναι νόμον*, *διανοίας ἐστὶν ἀγαθῆς* which to reduce into one, a *γὰρ* has been added since *τὸ γὰρ γνῶναι*, &c. as in divers other places we find, sometimes, two; sometimes three, several translations, huddled and confounded into one. As for the latter of the two, *καὶ τὸ γνῶναι νόμον*, we might make a very perfect and elegant translation of it, if we might take *νόμον* in a philosophical sense, for God himself; as is observed by Cicero, out of some ancient Greek philosophers, in his books of philosophy. But I do not believe that. These Septuagint (whoever they were) were such philosophers; and I fancy not groundless subtleties.

**Verse 11.** *{For by me thy days shall be multiplied}* Chapter 10:27. See upon Chapter 3:18. *She is a tree of life*.

**Verse 12.** *{thou shalt be wise for thyself}* I speak not for my profit, but for yours: if you hearken to me, the good will be yours; and if you scoff at my instruction, the hurt will be your own. Job 35:6, 7. Gal. 6:5. The Grecians have an excellent saying to this purpose: *Μισῶ σοφιστὴν ὅστις οὐχ αὑτῷ σοφός*: That is, *I hate that wise man, that is not wise unto*; (Or, for:) *himself*.

**Verse 13.** *{A foolish woman is clamorous: she is simple}, &c.* See before, Chapter 7:11. *She is loud and stubborn*, &c. But whether the same, (as was there intimated) be intended here and there, is a great question. Most and best interpreters; (as Mercer, and others) because this woman here is opposed to wisdom, before described and set out at large in this very chapter; some of wisdom’s words being also ascribed to this woman, as verse 16. *who so is simple*, &c. compared with verse 4. before: and further moved perchance, because the description, Chap. 7. does not altogether agree, as is there showed: for these, and what other reasons, I know not; but their opinion is, that the same are not intended; and that what is spoken of the woman here, must be understood allegorically, making this (the allegory) to be the literal and immediate meaning of the words. Of all these passages and descriptions concerning harlots and adulteresses in general, it was said before, upon Chapter 2:16. *from the strange woman*, &c. that they might very well bear an allegorical sense and application; and that it is very likely, that Solomon himself had a further aim, than the literal and immediate. Now there, as also Chapter 5:3. &c. Mercerus is altogether for the literal sense; *Literae sensus maxime placet* (upon Chapter 1:16.) and, *Nos aliis relinquamus allegorias*, &c. (upon Chapter 5:3.) and Chapter 7:1. no question at all is made of the literal sense: all the question is, whether that description be a history, (so far he is from suspecting it an allegory) or a parable. Yet now the same Mercer is so much for the allegorical sense, here, by this woman, understanding heresy, false doctrine, and whatsoever opposes itself to sound wisdom, and the true church and faith of Christ; that rather than the literal sense should be followed, he is content those former passages also should be taken allegorically of the same, so that the literal sense be not utterly excluded. I would not conceal anything from the reader, where I think there is a liberty left, without any wrong to the Scriptures, that he may freely make his choice. As for mine own opinion, I rather incline to believe that Solomon did intend it of an harlot, according to the literal sense; as in former chapters, yet so, that we exclude not the allegorical sense, which I think as proper and true, as the other, though not so immediate and literal. See more upon Chapter 2:16. *from the strange woman*. As for that seeming contradiction in the descriptions, *subtle of heart*, Chapter 7:10. and *foolish*, &c. here: how one and the same, in different respects, may be both, both *subtle*, and yet *foolish*, &c. see before upon Chapter 7:10. *subtle*: and upon Ps. 14:1. *The fool hath said in his heart*, &c. We may also with no less probability say that *folly* in general, (including all kind of sin: whereof see upon Ps. 14:1. *The fool*, &c.) is here represented by Solomon under the type of a woman, and opposed unto *wisdom*: so that two contrary parties are here brought upon the stage to act their parts, and to speak for themselves. So *virtue*, and *pleasure*, under the figure of two women, were feigned by some very ancient heathens, to have appeared unto Hercules, being yet young, and unsettled in his resolutions, what course of life to take; and each of them by turns have used their best rhetoric to him, to draw him: so wisdom, power, and pleasure, under the shape of three goddesses, to Paris: and so in that excellent Map of the World, commonly called *Cebetis tabula* (a piece of very great antiquity) Error and Deceit, (as women there too:) are opposed to *Παιδεία*, that is true knowledge or learning: by which the same is intended, as here by wisdom. The reading of all which, joined with this book of Proverbs would be of very good use, and make Solomon’s meaning more clear in many places.

**Verse 15.** *{Who go right on their ways}* That would hearken to wisdom’s lessons, if they were not seduced.

**Verse 16.** *{Who so is simple}* Wicked men, for the most part, (for all are not of one mind, nor equally desperate) think themselves the only wise men of the world, because they enjoy the world freely indulging to their senses and fleshly lusts with all liberty; and make much of this life, whilst they live: and those that do not, they account, fools and idiots: and upon this account, they draw many unto them. Which indeed, upon this ground, that the soul is mortal, nor any account to be given after this life, would not be without some ground of reason: but if otherwise, that the soul (whereof few heathens ever made any question) be immortal, &c. then is their wisdom apparently mere brutishness; it being the propriety of brutes, to look no further than the present. See somewhat to this purpose upon Ps. 35:4. *Let them be confounded*, &c. where the Epicureans are spoken of.

*{Who so is simple let him turn in hither and as for him that wanteth understanding}* Folly (or Vice) uses here the very same words, which were before ascribed unto wisdom verse 4. *Who so is simple*, &c. whereby we are given to understand, that it is the property of vice to appear under the shape of virtue: and of devils, under the habit and disguise of angels of light. It was ever so; we need not make a wonder of it in our days. Who are they, that have God, and godliness, and Christ Jesus, in their mouths, and writings, most? Let their deeds speak. But so are simple people caught, and they that have more judgment; yet, when once engaged by worldly interests, (if men addicted that way:) their judgments are swayed by little and little by their affections, until (by a just judgment of God upon them, for loving the world more than the truth) they be quite perverted in their very judgments, and past feeling. So delusion prevails upon men, as we are taught by Scripture, and right philosophy.

*{in hither}* Or, *aside*, verse 4.

**Verse 17.** *{Stolen waters are sweet}* Sinful delights taken by stealth, are the more pleasant to persons sensually affected; contrary to Chapter 5:15. So he, whom the poet speaks of: *Si interdicta petes; si vallo circumdata. Nam te Hoc facit insanum.* Neither is it the case of sensual men only, but of most men, (even the best) generally, to covet after those things most which are forbidden them. And as it is natural unto men so to do, so is it an argument of the corruption of man’s nature, the fruit and effect of original sin. It has been observed by many: *Nitimur in vetitum semper, cupimusque negata: Sic interdictis imminet aeger aquis.* Known verses, but the more observable to us, because of waters, the instance; as in Solomon, so here: though it be not altogether the same case, because the poet’s instance is of sick folks particularly, who are apt to covet, and catch (in a feverish fit, or the like: and in those hot countries, where water is the ordinary drink: to those especially that cannot go to the charge of wine:) at water, when it is forbidden them; who at other times perchance when they can come at it freely, would not make such reckoning of it. But some stick at Solomon’s words, and because water is so obvious and common a thing; they would have Solomon by *stolen waters*, to intend unlawful lusts and pleasures, and that to be the literal sense of the words. But they do not consider, that in those eastern countries water sometimes is a very rare and precious commodity; where wells were wont to be sealed, and an object of strife and envy as much as anything. See more upon Chapter 5:15. *Drink waters out of thine own cistern*. However, we deny not but unlawful lusts and pleasures, of what kind soever, were intended by Solomon in general by this particular instance of *stolen waters*: and sins of incontinency, very properly as in the former reference is showed. And so it is with most proverbs, or proverbial speeches. One thing is said, and another is intended. But the general sense, does always presuppose the truth, and reality of the literal and immediate, upon which it is grounded; else it has not the propriety of a proverb; of this nature, used for confirmation, &c. which is from matter of common sense and experience, to insinuate somewhat that is not so vulgarly known, or acknowledged. As if I should say; *It is a good horse that never stumbleth*; my intention may be, to excuse some mistake; yet it must be supposed, if I speak pertinently, that really best horses may stumble sometimes. But the ignorance of matters of fact, in process of time; and the alteration of customs in the same countries; or diversity, in different countries and climates; make many proverbs to become very obscure, or to seem impertinent, to some; which at first, and where first used, were most clear and evident. This may be the case of many proverbs in this book; and therefore I have been the longer in vindication of the literal sense: confirmed also by the following words, *and bread eaten in secret*: which have not the same analogy with unlawful lusts, as waters (as is showed in the former reference) have: and therefore more undoubtedly to be taken in the literal sense; the literal sense nevertheless (according to the nature, and common use of a proverb) extending to whatsoever is unlawfully got and enjoyed.

*{and bread eaten in secret}* Heb. *of secrecies*: that is, eaten most secretly. Chapter 20:17. *Bread of deceit*. &c.

**Verse 18.** *{dead are there}* Prov. 2:18. & 7. 26. Heb. *רפאים* of which see upon Chapter 2:18. *unto the dead*.

*{her guests are in the depths of hell}* That is they are in extremity of danger, in the very jaws of death. The Hebrews often express death by variety of words; when either they would set out the horror, or the certainty of it; or both. See Ps. 88:12. *the grave: destruction: the dark: and the land of forgetfulness*: all, for one thing. But the most common word, by which they use to vary it, is *שאול*: *grave*: which is the word here: translated sometimes *hell*, sometimes the *grave*: of which see more on Chapter 1:12. *as the grave*, and Chapter 5:5. *take hold on hell*. *The depths of hell*, here; as *the belly of hell*. Jonas 2:2. *out of the belly of hell cried I*. So that there is no necessity of interpreting the words here, of the torments of hell; or to make that the literal sense. There was not so much knowledge of hell, (as we take it now:) and paradise in those days. Though Solomon himself might know more than others; yet it is likely, that he would speak as other did. This therefore I conceive to be the most literal immediate sense; not excluding the other. Nay, since that *φωτισμὸς*, revelation, or *bringing to light, of life and immortality*, which the apostle tells us of, 2 Tim. 1:10. I conceive it most proper for us Christians to understand all promises of life, and denunciations of death, of that which is eternal, whether life or death; which is called by the ancients (and most followed in all their expositions:) the *anagogical* sense.

## CHAPTER 10

**Verse 1.** *{The proverbs of Solomon}* Here indeed begin those that we may more properly call *Proverbs*; or *Sentences*; most of that which has gone before in the nine foregoing chapters, containing rather matter of exhortation, in general, to wisdom and virtue; and dehortation from vice, and the love of pleasure, under the representation of a lewd woman; because women, and the love of bodily pleasure, is that which draws away most men from the right way: such general matter I say, fit for an introduction, or general preface, to particular moral precepts and sentences, rather than proverbs, or sentences. So that the name of Solomon may be here prefixed anew, as to a second book, or different head of collection, taking this word *proverbs*, in a more proper signification, than before. It is Aristotle’s observation in the first of his Ethics, that young men are not so capable of moral philosophy, because, *πάθεσιν ἀκολουθητικοί*. What he means by *πάθεσιν ἀκολουθητικοὶ*, may be gathered from his 8. book, where he makes it the propriety of young men (*quatenus* young; for himself well distinguishes of young men, in the first) *κατὰ πάθος ζῆν καὶ μάλιστα διώκειν τὸ ἡδὺ αὐτοῖς*, *καὶ τὸ παρόν*; that is, *to live according to lust*; or, *sense; and above all things to seek after pleasure, and to regard that which is present only*. It is not therefore without great cause, that Solomon before he begin with particular instructions, did judge this large introduction needful, where he does so seriously exhort to the study of wisdom, and as effectually dehort all young men (as most subject to it) from the love of pleasure and sensuality. Somewhat to this purpose, see before in the preface, and upon Chap. 1. verse 1. *The Proverbs*.

*{A wise son maketh a glad father}* See before upon Chapter 1:8. *the instruction of thy father*. To Plutarch’s words there, add these, which we reserved for this place as most proper to be read here: *Διὸ τοὺς μὲν ἄλλους κακῶς ποιεῖν ἀπείρηται*, &c. that is; *We are forbidden to do other men any wrong: but to mother and father, not evermore to be doing and saying somewhat, (though we do nothing to grieve them): whereby to glad their hearts, is generally accounted wicked and ungodly.*

*{but a foolish son is the heaviness of his mother}* Because I see how apt some men are to impose their own fancies upon Scripture, presuming they shall find readers that will gladly entertain anything that relishes of wit and curiosity, though otherwise never so groundless and impertinent, whereof we have a notable example in the expositions of some chief expositors (as shall appear afterwards) upon this place: it will not be amiss to observe somewhat here in general concerning the form, or character of most of these sentences, or *Proverbs*; but more properly, (for the most of them) sentences: which will afford a good and seasonable caution or *περίαπτον* against such extravagances, and groundless curiosities. Observe we then in the first place, that most of these *Proverbs* or *Sentences* (which you will:) consist of two members, whereof the latter, is either opposite, or *exegetical*, that is explanatory and confirmatory of the former. Opposites by *antithets*, (as they call them) or otherwise, as it is accounted generally the most gracious, yea and powerful kind of language (witness Demosthenes his so much admired *enthimemata*; whereof we have some examples in Saint Paul’s epistles:) so they have this advantage also to illustrate one another, (according to the vulgar axiom, *contraria simul posita elucescunt*:) and to make the whole more clear as we find in divers of these proverbs, the sense whereof, were it not for the opposite member, would be very obscure, and if not altogether inextricable, yet liable to much more debate. Where the second member is merely *exegetical*, that is, coincident in sense with the former; the end of it is not only to make the sense the plainer, which must needs be, when the same thing is repeated in other words: but also it adds weight to the sentence, and gives it the more advantage (by this *ἐπιμονὴ*, as they call it:) to make impression both upon the mind and memory. Which is far from *tautology*; a repeating of the same thing in ordinary discourse without any end or occasion; through mere garrulity, or want of wit. Where Solomon does intend an *[exegesis]*, or *exegisis*:) or *ἐπιμονὴ*, he has many ways to vary the words, with much concinnity and elegance, not so easily to be discerned in translations, as in the original words: so that though the same thing, in effect, be repeated; yet the understanding fixed upon the weight of the matter is well satisfied, and the ear at the same time well pleased. But to find out the right meaning, where there is any doubt, we must not confine ourselves to the members of one verse, whether opposite, or exegetical; but consider well, whether there be not some parallel verse to be found either in the same, or some other chapter: and not to fasten upon any, though plausible conceit or interpretation of some one verse, until we see how it will agree with the parallels, if any be found. Now to make application of what has been said to this particular place, that by this president the reader may be warned, and withal, directed how to help himself, when need is; *A wise son* with Solomon, *maketh a glad father: but a foolish son, is the heaviness of his mother*. When we find a foolish son in the second member, opposed to a *wise*, in the first, and *heaviness*, to *gladness*: who would not expect a perfect opposition of sense between the second members, as if he had said, *A wise son maketh a glad father, but a foolish son, is the heaviness of his father*, and in father, who would not imagine *mother* also, though not expressed at all, (there being the same reason) to be included? But Solomon instead of repeating the word *father*, in the second member, he varies it by *mother*; being, in this case, equivalent to father; so that his words so ordered, without any fastidiousness of reiteration, amount to this: *A wise son maketh glad parents*; or if you will, *a glad father and mother, but a foolish son is the heaviness of father and mother*. He might have said, *of both*, I confess; as afterwards, upon another occasion, Chapter 17:15. but it pleased him better so to divide them to each member one; that one, comprehending both. So far we might have gone with much probability. But to put it beyond all dispute, look we upon the parallel places, and the business is done. First Chapter 1:8. *My son hear the instruction of thy father, and forsake not the law of thy mother*. Chapter 17:25. *A foolish son is a grief to his father, and bitterness to her that bare him*. See also 23. 22. *Hearken*, &c. and Chapter 30:17. *The eye that mocketh*, &c. Might not then Salazar, and Cornel. à Lapide have saved their labor, when they did put themselves to it, to devise reasons, why Solomon should ascribe *gladness*, to the *father*: and *heaviness* to the *mother* particularly, why not *gladness*, and *heaviness* to both rather: why most proper to the father, to rejoice, when children do well; and most proper to the mother to grieve, when otherwise? I know they had some rabbins to lead them, but when Solomon himself does so clearly expound himself, why should we seek any further? I would not have been so long upon this place; but that what is here said, may concern many other places; and the understanding reader may make good use of it. I would not have the inventions of men, to pass for divine wisdom; if I could help it. It is enough that preachers take that liberty. Expositors should be more accurate.

**Verse 2.** *{Treasures of wickedness profit nothing}* It may be taken of riches wickedly got, in general; or by unlawful means, which though enjoyed for a while, yet afterwards prove the destruction of many families. But comparing with Chapter 11:4. *Riches profit not in the day of wrath but righteousness delivereth from death*: it may more particularly be expounded of capital causes and judgments; when a man is publicly and legally questioned for his life or the like. Then integrity may do much, and more than any money, or bribes: but that must be understood, under a just government, or judge; whether king or other. For otherwise, that may be as true that wicked unrighteous men may with money and bribes break through all laws, as cobwebs, and pacify their judge; be their cause never so odious, or horrid: when poorer men, for want of money, and ransom, perish for lesser crimes and occasions. And in Solomon himself in this very chapter; *The rich man’s wealth is his strong city*: (not as the poet, *— hic murus aheneus esto, Nil conscire sibi, nulla pallescere culpa*: which expresses well the former saying and observation) *the destruction of the poor is their poverty*. And Chapter 13:8. *The ransom of a man’s life, are his riches; but a poor man* (here again the case is altered; and it is true, according to variety of circumstances; that many poor whether guilty or not guilty, whether questioned or not so much as questioned; escape *eo nomine*, merely, because poor, and that they have nothing to lose; and that many perish, or are cast sometimes justly, sometimes unjustly, because of their poverty, who might have escaped, if they had had anything to give) *heareth not rebuke*. Somewhat to this purpose, see also in the preface, before; and upon Ps. 6:13. *He winketh*, &c. Thus must every wise & intelligent reader ponder and examine many (if not most) proverbs, according to variety of circumstances; & accordingly make application, of them for want of which consideration many are deceived and by false application do themselves and others more hurt, than good. And this is rather to pervert (as many illiterate, or factious, out of the pulpits daily;) the Scriptures, than to use them to edification. But if we take the words with reference to God, and his just judgments: (which by wrath also, Chapter 11:4. according to Rom. 2:5. *wrath*, and, *the day of wrath*, and elsewhere the New Testament, may be understood) then it is absolutely true, that riches cannot profit any man, to save either soul or body from the just hand of God, which only righteousness and innocence (such as man is capable of, and God in his mercy does accept) can do.

*{but righteousness}* It was observed before upon Chapter 3:27. *them to whom it is due*; that the Hebrew word *צדקה* is by the rabbins used, for *alms*: certain it is, that both the Hebrew word in the Old, as also the Greek *δικαιοσύνη*, in the New, are often taken for *mercy*, yea and *δίκαιος* sometimes, (as Matt. 1:27. *δίκαιος ὤν*: *being a merciful man* not as we have it there *just* which is very improper there) for merciful. Upon this sense of the word *צדקה*, is grounded by some, another interpretation of Solomon’s words, agreeable to our Savior’s words, Luke 16:9. *Make to yourselves friends of the Mammon of unrighteousness*, &c. and to Dan. 4:24. *Break off thy sins by righteousness, and thine iniquities by showing mercy to the poor*: where if we take *mercy*, in the second member, to be an interpretation of *righteousness* as in the first is very usual, then *righteousness* must be taken in that rabbinical sense we have spoken of. We may add the words of our Savior, Luke 11:41. *Give alms of such things as you have, and behold all things are clean unto you*. This sense therefore being confirmed by so many pregnant passages, and the words affording it without any violence, we may not reject it. As to the thing itself we say briefly; though true religion does not consist in giving of alms, properly, which is an external thing, whereas religion essentially is spiritual and internal; and that we are taught by St. Paul, that a man may sell all that he has, and give to the poor, and yet reap no benefit from them to salvation; yet alms are so great an evidence of religion, and that which in God is most glorified & wherewith of all outward acts, most pleased, that we have warrant enough from reason and Scripture, to conclude, that that man’s religion is not sound and sincere before God, who is not (according to his power) charitable, and bountiful to the poor. And especially in matter of repentance, that that repentance is not true and real, nor effectual to remission of sins, (still supposing some ability) that does not manifest itself to God and the world, by such fruits.

**Verse 3.** *{The Lord will not suffer the soul of the righteous to famish}* Ps. 37:25. *I have been young*. See there. Though he suffer the just to want for a time, yet he will send him comfort in due season. Of this and other like temporal promises, annexed to godliness and piety, see upon Ps. 37:1. *Fret not thyself* &c. and other verses of that psalm.

*{the substance of the wicked}* Or: *the wicked for their wickedness* according to Mercerus and others. Or: again according to others, which also seems most literal, *the wickedness of the wicked* that is, wicked men. See also Chapter 14:32.

**Verse 4.** *{He becometh poor that dealeth with a slack hand}* Chapter 12:24. The Hebrew words *ראש עשה כף רמיה* may also (and are by Mercerus) be translated, *Pauper facit lingulam bilancium* (*כף* often, *a hand* sometimes, *the needle*, or *tongue of a balance*) *fraudulentam*: that is *A poor man makes a deceitful weight*. Or, *Lingula fraudulenta facit pauperem*: that is. *A deceitful weight makes a poor man*. So there would be an elegant allusion between *כף* in the first and *יד* in the second member. And certain it is, that as poverty makes many a one fraudulent, (which made Agur, Chapter 30:10. to pray against it) that otherwise would be honest: so fraudulency or fraudulent dealing, is the undoing of many, who by truth and honesty might have thrived better. However this translation we have here, is as good and warrantable, and more generally embraced.

*{with a slack hand}* Or *a deceitful hand*, Jer. 48:10. for slothful men very frequently live by deceiving others. See Chapter 19:15. *an idle soul*.

*{but the hand of the diligent maketh rich}* So it is commonly which is enough to justify the truth of this saying. However though diligence & industry be the ordinary means which God himself has appointed to that end, yet it must always be understood, that God’s blessing does go along without which no diligence can prevail. When I say God’s blessing, I intend it in a general sense; when God is pleased according to his general order, and appointment, that such and such means being used, they shall be effectual to such an end. So many men though void otherwise of piety and religion, come to be rich, by using the means to which God in his ordinary providence has appointed and promised a blessing, that is success: and many, otherwise godly and religious, come to poverty, because they do not use the means that God has appointed. Yet lest men, if it should always and infallibly be true, that by diligence and industry riches may be got, should cast off all thought of God, and wholly depend of the means and secondary causes: therefore God is pleased sometimes to cross his ordinary providence or general order, in this particular: so that wealth shall come unto some, they know not how and some others, though never so diligent and industrious, shall do no good. And this has given occasion to some other proverbs and sentences, which may seem to be repugnant and contrary to what is there said: as in this very chapter v. 22. *The blessing of the Lord, it maketh rich and he addeth no sorrow with it*, where see the references. But they may easily be reconciled, if all things be taken into consideration that ought to be considered, and all circumstances well weighed: for want of which, ignorant men are apt to make foolish applications, whereby they both deceive themselves, and others; and shamefully abuse the Scriptures: as was said before upon the second verse, *Treasures of wickedness*.

**Verse 5.** *{He that gathereth in summer is a wise son}* He that takes his opportunity for his business, credits himself and his: he that neglects it, discredits both.

**Verse 6.** *{violence covereth the mouth of the wicked}* When their wickedness shall be discovered, they shall be as dumb and not know what to say: or, other men’s violence shall stop their mouths.

**Verse 7.** *{The memory of the just is blessed}* Ps. 112:6. Men shall speak of a just man after his death with praise, and of the wicked with disdain and disgrace. Or, the just shall leave a sweet scent behind him, and the wicked, a stinking savor.

**Verse 8.** *{a prating fool}* Heb. *a fool of lips*. See upon Chapter 11:12. *despiseth his name*.

*{a prating fool shall fall}* He that shows the folly of his heart, by irregular speech shall fall, though he look to rise by his talk. See also upon Chapter 1:5. *A wise man*, &c.

*{shall fall}* Or, *shall be beaten*.

**Verse 9.** *{He that walketh uprightly, walketh surely}* Ps. 23:4. Upright ways bring peace, though wicked men think them exposed to danger: but crafty plots, to prevent danger, prove the ruin of the devisers. See upon Ps. 22:9. *Thou didst make me hope*, &c.

*{but he that perverteth his ways shall be known}* Heb. *יודע* *shall be known*, is by some rabbins, both here and elsewhere expounded, *shall be broken*. So Judg. 8:16. *and with them he taught*, (*ויודע*) *the men of Succoth*. *He taught*, (or, as in the margin there, *he made to know*) that is, (and so translated by some) *contrivit, he bruised*.

**Verse 10.** *{He that winketh with the eye causeth sorrow}* He that mocks others with his eyes. See notes on Chapter 6:13. *He that winketh with his eyes*, &c.

*{a prating fool shall fall}* **Verse 8.**

*{shall fall}* Or, *shall be beaten*. See the reference before, to Chapter 6:13.

**Verse 11.** *{The mouth of a righteous man is a well of life}* Chapter 13:14. It always refreshes others and cannot be stopped.

*{but violence covereth the mouth of the wicked}* The same word, in the original, we had before, verse 6. but whether to be interpreted here, as there, the different coherence, makes it doubtful. There, *violence*, as a just judgment, is denounced to the wicked: as *blessings*, in the first member of the verse, the reward of the just. Here *violence*, as proceeding from the mouth of the wicked, is opposed to *life*, that proceeds from the mouth of the righteous: here therefore, *violence*, active; before, *violence*, passive: but as we have it in our English, we make but one sense (without any respect had of the coherence, or context) of both places. *But the mouth of the wicked covereth violence*, as I conceive, is the truer translation. All the difference is in the word *חמס* violence, whether we make a nominative, or an accusative of it. The original word may be either: and why then may not we take that which will fit the context best? Now because *חמס* may signify *iniquity* also, some make the sense of the words to be, that it is the propriety of a wicked man to conceal his faults, or sins, and this is drawn to confession by the Papists. I cannot say but this may be without any great violence to the words. Neither is it very clear in what sense the mouth of the wicked is said *to cover violence*. But it may be understood of the wickedness of his heart, which he covers by fair speeches, when he does intend mischief: or figuratively his mouth may be said *to cover violence*, that is, to be full of it: full of oaths, and curses, and railings, and the like. Psalm 109:18. the wicked is said, *to clothe himself himself with cursing as with a garment*: which comes somewhat near, though it be not altogether the same. We have had occasion before to observe it more than once, that the same word, words, or phrase, sometimes in one and the same chapter, yea verse, is taken differently.

**Verse 12.** *{Hatred stirreth up strifes}* By discovering and upbraiding men’s faults to them, which love covers, & so extinguishes strife.

*{love covereth all sins}* 1 Pet. 4:8. James 5:20. in which places by *sins*, some understand the sins of him that loves, or teaches: but more probable, as by this place does appear, that offenses and wrongs, which use to cause strife and contention, are to be understood.

**Verse 13.** *{In the lips of him that hath understanding, wisdom is found}* Whereby he knows how to speak without danger; which fools do not.

*{a rod is for the back of him that is void of understanding}* Chapter 26:3.

*{understanding}* Heb. *heart*.

**Verse 14.** *{Wise men lay up knowledge, but the mouth of the foolish is near destruction}* Comparing with Chapter 13:3. *He that keepeth his mouth, keepeth his life, but he that openeth wide his lips shall have destruction*: I think it not improbable, that by *knowledge*, anything, that comes to our knowledge concerning other men, neighbors or others, should be understood. Except we make this to be the sense; that whilst wise men are busy, and in a manner wholly taken up with it, to store themselves with profitable knowledge, which may stand them in stead, in time of need; the fool, that has nothing to busy himself with, but to prate of other men’s businesses, meets with speedy destruction. See also Chapter 18:6, 7. If we stick to the former sense, then *to lay up*, here, is as much as, *to conceal*, See also upon Chapter 12:23. *A prudent man concealeth knowledge*.

**Verse 15.** *{The rich man’s wealth is his strong city}* A rich man trusts in his riches: a poor man fears destruction, for want of wealth. See before upon verse 2. and Chapter 18:10, 11, 12.

**Verse 16.** *{The labour of the righteous tendeth to life}* That which a righteous man gets, he uses to preserve his own life, and other men’s; but wicked men use their wealth to undo themselves and others.

**Verse 17.** *{erreth}* Or, *causeth to err*. The original word will bear either; but the last more proper here, as I conceive. See before upon Chapter 5:23. *shall go astray*.

**Verse 18.** *{with}* Or, *hath*. But rather, as in many places *איש* to be supplied: *He that hideth hatred is* [a man] *of lying lips; and he*, &c. See before upon Chapter 7:22. *as a fool to the correction of the stocks*.

**Verse 19.** *{In the multitude of words there wanteth not sin}* Eccl. 5:3. James 3:2. Not of pondered words, but of rash ones, which wisdom teaches men to forbear: or, men that are full of talk, cannot but talk amiss sometimes.

**Verse 20.** *{The tongue of the just is as choice silver}* Good men’s words are of great price; wicked men’s thoughts are of small value.

*{the heart of the wicked is little [worth]}* First, I would have the reader by comparing verses 8, 13, 14, 20, 21. of this chapter, (not to seek further) to observe that the *heart, tongue,* and *lips*, are used promiscuously for one and the same; the one sometimes ascribed to the just, or wise; the other, to the foolish; and contrarily: lest men should trouble themselves with groundless speculations, and impose reasons upon the Scripture (a very common thing) of their own devising. See also upon Chapter 15:14. *the heart of fools*. Secondly, the original word *מעט* *little, small*, properly may perchance as well, if not better, with his prefix *as*, which is in the original, be here explained, as *nothing*, or if you will, more literally yet, (this very word *momentum*, by reduplication of the first radical, to supply the guttural, of harsh pronunciation, coming as I take it, from the Hebrew, *megnat*,) as *a moment*: moment taken mathematically, for the least and lowest of divisions; that is in effect, as nothing.

**Verse 21.** *{The lips of the righteous feed many}* They are able to admonish, exhort, counsel and comfort others, when wicked men cannot preserve themselves. The Hebrew word signifies also to rule, to govern, to lead.

*{but fools die for want of wisdom}* Fools, that is sinners as often already has been observed, and references made to proper places. The reader, I hope, will not expect that we should make upon every verse, for explication of words often used. If he do not think fit to read from the beginning: yet his concordance will easily direct him to former passages, either in this book of Proverbs or in that of the Psalms where probably he may find what he does look for. See also somewhat to this purpose, upon Ch. 5. last verse, *he shall go astray*. So that in effect Solomon by these words intends no other than what by the apostle is expressed in other words. *That the wages of sin is death*: Rom. 6. last verse.

*{of wisdom}* Heb. *of heart*.

**Verse 22.** *{The blessing of the Lord it maketh rich}* God’s blessing makes men rich, without those careful toils that covetous men are oppressed withal, Ps. 127:2, 3. See also Chapter 3:32, 33. and Chapter 11:24. and upon Ps. 25:14. *The secret of the Lord*, &c. and before upon ver. 4. *but the hand of the diligent*.

**Verse 23.** *{It is a sport to a fool to do mischief}* Chapter 14:9.

*{but a man of understanding hath wisdom}* Or, *and so is wisdom* (or, *to do wisdom*) *to a man of understanding*. That is, the study of wisdom, is the delight and pleasure of a man of understanding. See upon Chapter 18:1. *Through desire*, &c. Or, according to the former translation, which seems to be most literal: comparing with Chapter 14:9. and 13. 15. *Good understanding*, &c. by *wisdom*, here we must understand (as Chapter 11:2. *with the lowly is wisdom*. See there:) the fruits of wisdom, that is, grace and favor, with God, and man. So ordinarily, and *ἐπὶ τὸ πολύ*: upon which proverbs are grounded, extraordinarily sometimes, and by a special appointment, a providence of God, a man may perish (as to the world) through his wisdom; when fools and wicked men flourish. According to some; *a man of understanding hath wisdom*: that is, say they, to avoid doing mischief. But this seems somewhat frigid.

**Verse 24.** *{but the desire of the righteous, it shall be granted}* See upon Ps. 21:2. *his heart’s desire*.

*{be granted}* Or, *yield fruit*, as Chapter 12:12. Or, *[God] granteth*: according to some, both here, and there.

**Verse 25.** *{As}* Or, *When*.

*{As the whirlwind passeth}* As soon as a whirlwind passes away: or, when the whirlwind of God’s wrath passes over them.

*{so}* Or, *then*.

*{the righteous}* Or, *to the righteous*.

*{as vinegar to the teeth}* Very contrary to the teeth, by reason of its coldness and acidity, as out of divers ancients by Leteneurus, in his *Adagia sacra*, and some others, is observed. Yet verjuice is much commended: but there may be reason for that.

*{so is the sluggard to them that send him}* He is but a trouble and grief to them that employ him. You may compare with Chapter 25:13. *As the cold of snow*, and Chapter 26:6. *He that sendeth*.

**Verse 27.** *{The fear of the Lord prolongeth days}* Chapter 9:11. See more upon Chapter 3:18. *She is a tree of life*.

*{prolongeth}* Heb. *addeth*.

*{years of the wicked shall be shortened}* Job 22:16. Ps. 55:23. See the former reference.

**Verse 28.** *{The hope of the righteous shall be gladness, but the expectation}, &c.* This hope of the righteous; and this expectation of the wicked, here intended, though it may be extended to things temporal also, yet to relate chiefly to another world, and things eternal, appears first by Chapter 14:32. *the wicked is driven away in his wickedness, but the righteous hath hope in his death*: there is for the hope of the righteous. Now for the *expectation* of the wicked, *When a wicked man dyeth, his expectation shall perish; and the hope of unjust men perisheth*, Chapter 11:7. See also upon Ps. 39:7. *And now, Lord, what wait I for? my hope*, &c.

*{the expectation of the wicked shall perish}* Job 8:13. and 11. 20. Ps. 112:10. They that fear not God are accounted wicked men, in Scripture phrase, though they be not openly profane.

*{The way of the Lord}* That is, godliness, righteousness, see Rom. 2:9, 10. Or, *The way of the Lord*: that is, his secret dispensations and providences, for the salvation of them that fear him. Hos. 14:10. *for the ways of the Lord are right, and the just shall walk in them: but the transgressors*, &c.

**Verse 30.** *{The righteous shall never be removed}* Ps. 37:22. and 125. 1. Out of his habitation: his children shall dwell there after him, Ps. 37:29. See upon Ps. 37:2. *For they shall soon be cut down*, &c.

**Verse 31.** *{The mouth of the just}, &c.* Plentifully, as a fruitful field does corn.

*{bringeth forth wisdom}* To avoid cross words, that bring destruction.

*{the froward tongue}* Heb. *tongue of frowardness*; that is, accustomed to speak frowardly.

*{shall be cut out}* Like a bad tree, that brings forth evil fruit, Ps. 4:52.

**Verse 32.** *{The lips of the righteous know what is acceptable}* That is, a godly wise man will take care both what he speaks, that it may be acceptable to God; and that it may be in an acceptable time: and God does direct him, to make his speech seasonable and acceptable (*the answer of the tongue is from the Lord*, Chapter 16:1.) among men. Compare with 11. 27. *procureth favour*, and 12. 2. *obtaineth favour*. It is the same word (*רצון*) in both places, as is here, *bene placitum*, properly, *lips, tongue,* and *heart*, as promiscuously used, was observed before, in this very chapter.

*{the mouth of the wicked speaketh frowardness}* Things justly displeasing to others.

*{frowardness}* Heb. *frowardnesses*.

## CHAPTER 11

**Verse 1.** *{A false balance}* Heb. *balances of deceit*. Under this word, he condemns all false weights, measures and deceit.

*{abomination to the Lord}* Though some men make light of it, yet God hates it.

*{A false balance is abomination to the Lord}* Lev. 19:36. Deut. 25:15. Chapter 16:11. & 20. 10, 23.

*{a just weight}* Heb. *a perfect stone*.

**Verse 2.** *{When pride cometh, then cometh shame}* Chapter 15:33. & 16. 18. & 18. 12. When man forgets himself, and exalts himself above his vocation, then God, by some sudden accident, brings him to confusion.

*{but with the lowly is wisdom}* Chapter 3:34. *Surely he scorneth the scorners*, (by St. James and St. Peter expressed, *God resisteth the proud*, &c. James 4:6. and 1 Pet. 5:5.) *but he giveth grace unto the lowly*. The *wisdom* therefore here intended, is divine wisdom, such as makes one *wise to salvation*; and so throughout the book, the word (though sometimes also more generally, for worldly wisdom, and prudence:) must be taken of such wisdom especially. But this, upon a supposition, that by *grace* as most understand) spiritual saving grace is intended by Solomon. But there be also who in that place expound the word otherwise: to wit, of temporal temporary grace and favor (and that too, from God:) with men. Truly, this place does seem to favor much this interpretation. For, as by *shame* in the first member, temporal worldly shame is intended, so it stands with reason, that by *wisdom* in the second, the temporal worldly fruits of wisdom; grace and favor: (of which see more upon Chapter 10:23. *a man of understanding hath wisdom*) should be meant.

*{with the lowly is wisdom}* And, by consequence, honor, from them that rightly weigh things.

**Verse 3.** *{integrity of the upright shall guide them}* Chapter 13:6.

**Verse 4.** *{Riches profit not in the day of wrath}* Chapter 10:2. Ezek. 7:19. Zeph. 1:18. Though well gotten, and not by fraud, as verse 1.

**Verse 5.** *{direct}* Heb. *rectify*.

**Verse 6.** *{The righteousness of the upright shall deliver them}* It shall not only lead him in safe paths, but also free him out of dangers.

*{transgressors shall be taken in their own naughtiness}* Chapter 5:22.

*{in their naughtiness}* His plots to ruin others, shall ruin himself, Chapter 5:22.

**Verse 7.** *{hope}* Or, (taking *און* as for *הון* as elsewhere, more than once:) *expectation of strength*; that is, the most strong hope.

**Verse 8.** *{The righteous is delivered out of trouble}* Chapter 21:18.

*{the wicked cometh in his stead}* It shows the great love of God to his, who brings them out of the snare, and their enemies into it, as in Mordecai’s case, Esth. 7:9.

**Verse 9.** *{An hypocrite with his mouth destroyeth his neighbour}* Job 8:13. A man that dissembles with his neighbor, by fair words, leads him to destruction, unless he be a wise man, that by knowledge finds out his drifts, and prevents them: or, a man that dissembles with God, draws others to do so: but good men that know God’s will, will not be drawn by him.

*{hypocrite}* Or, *profane man*, Ps. 35:16.

**Verse 10.** *{The city rejoiceth}* Because of the good they get by them: whereas, by wicked men exalted they get hurt, verse 11.

**Verse 11.** *{By the blessing of the upright the city is exalted}* By the prayers of righteous men, a city is exalted. That is the proper signification of the word *blessing*, in this place, though some intend it to good counsels also, but not so properly. Now if the prayer of any just man may do much with God for the public good: how much more theirs, that have a particular commission from God, to pray, and to bless? Especially, if not only lawfully called, but such also, as approve themselves by their holy conversation, not unworthy of their calling? Truly, in ancient times the public set prayers of the church were thought so much to conduce to the public welfare, that even heathen emperors, as may appear by some edicts of theirs have made great reckoning of them: and it is a bad sign, at any time, when those mouths are stopped, by human authority, which God has appointed to bless.

*{by the mouth of the wicked}* Not bad counsels only, which is not so proper here; (though *mouth* sometimes be taken for deeds also: as has been observed:) but abominable oaths, and curses, by which God is extremely dishonored and provoked. They might do well to think of it, that pretend to a good cause. There is no cause so good, but may be lost by such impieties.

**Verse 12.** *{void of wisdom}* Heb. *destitute of heart*.

*{despiseth his neighbour}* Speaks ill of him, or to him; but he that is thoroughly wise (Heb. *a man of understandings*) will not therefore despise others, or reproach him, that reproves him. Or the meaning may be, that a self-conceited fool (as none commonly more self-conceited, than those that are more foolish) because he thinks nobody wise but himself, will not hear others when they speak, either to deliver their opinions in a matter, or to give their advice; nobody shall speak (by his good will) but himself: but a wiser man will be content to hear others, and to be silent himself. See upon Chapter 1:5. *A wise man will hear, and increase learning*, &c. Theognis. (of whom, see before upon Chapter 1:32. *For the turning away of the simple*, &c.) has the very same, and almost in the very same words, but more fully expressed: *Ὅστις* (says he) *τοι δοκέει τὸν πλησίον ἴδμεναι οὐδέν*, *Ἀλλ᾽ αὐτὸς μοῦνος ποικίλα δήνε᾽ ἔχειν*: *Κεῖνός γ᾽ ἄφρων ἐστὶ*, *νόος βεβλαμμένος ἐσθλοῦ*: *Ἴσως* (not *aeque*, as some there translate: but either *fortasse*; or altogether vacant: as often: his meaning is, that there is no man, but is wise sometimes, and may speak to the purpose as well as another;) *γὰρ πάντες ποικίλ᾽ ἐπιστάμεθα*: that is, *Whoever thinks his neighbor to know nothing*, &c. I would wish them that are able to read this author in his own language, (for by translations they will hardly understand him,) even for Solomon’s sake to read him accurately, whose sayings will give more light often to divers of these proverbs, than large commentaries. See also 12. 15. *The way of a fool*.

*{understanding}* Heb. *understandings*.

**Verse 13.** *{A talebearer}* Heb. *he that walketh*, being *a talebearer*, Lev. 19:16. and Chapter 20:19. *He that goeth about*. See there.

*{A talebearer revealeth secrets}* Things committed to him concerning the speaker, or some other, upon condition of secrecy.

*{a talebearer}* According to the propriety of the original word, *a peddler of tales*.

**Verse 14.** *{Where no counsel is, the people fall}* 1 Kings 12:1. &c. Chapter 15:22. Heb. *counsels*. Such as shipmasters use to guide the ship well: for so the word signifies. Of the original word *תחבלות* See upon Chapter 1:5. *shall attain*.

**Verse 15.** *{He that is surety for a stranger}* Whose condition he knows not: or, for one whom he is no way obliged to, and therefore is not tied to be bound for him. So a strange woman is she, that is not a man’s own wife, Chapter 2:16. and 6. 1. But comparing with Chapter 6:1. *My son, if thou be surety for thy friend*, &c. and because it is no common case, for men to bind themselves (in any considerable sum:) for strangers, that it should much need this caution: it is more likely that by *stranger* here, is to be understood *another*, simply, whoever he be, whether friend or other. See before upon Chapter 6:3. *into the hand of thy friend*.

*{shall smart for it}* Heb. *shall be sore broken*. Heb. *be broken by breaking*: That is, to shivers, as an earthen pot with a fall: and we call it breaking when men are undone.

*{he that hateth suretyship, is sure}* Loves it not, and will not do it without just cause. The *hated* wife, Deut. 21:15. that is, less beloved.

*{suretyship}* Heb. *those that strike hands*.

**Verse 16.** *{A gracious woman retaineth honour, and strong men retain riches}* Some, because *עריץ*, is commonly taken in a bad sense, would have this the meaning, that as violence and oppression is the way for men to get wealth: so meekness and gentleness, for women to get honor, their chiefest wealth. This might do, if Solomon might not seem so to approve violence in men, as he does meekness in women: or if there were no way, at least no surer way, (which Solomon himself elsewhere contradicts) for men to get wealth, than by violence and oppression. More probable therefore it is, that *עריץ* is here used in a good sense, for an industrious, active, stout man: and that Solomon by these words intends to set down the commendation and properties as well of the man, that is master; as of the woman, that is mistress of a house and family: of the woman, that she must be kind, affable, meek, gracious: this, her chiefest care, and credit: of the man, that he must be painful, laborious, resolute, undaunted, and the like: as also to provide for the safe keeping of his goods against any violence. There was a proverbial saying among the Hebrews, used Isa. 49:24. *היקח מגבור מלקוח* *Shall the prey be taken from the mighty?* or, *strong man?* To which our Savior alluding, grounds his parable upon it, Luke 11:21, 22. *When a strong man armed, keepeth his palace, his goods are in peace. But when a stronger than he, shall come upon him, and overcome him, he taketh from him all his armor wherein he trusted, and divideth his spoils*. I believe, if this had been thought of, this interpretation of Solomon’s words would have given all men good content; and that they had forborne to seek further.

**Verse 17.** *{The merciful man doeth good to his own soul}* He that is of a bountiful disposition affords himself things needful, which a cruel man does not: or, he that is good to others, shall receive a reward for it.

*{but he that is cruel, troubleth his own flesh}* According to the interpretation of the first member; so this second also does afford a double sense, which may easily be gathered. And so we take *flesh* here, to be the same as *soul*, before: by both, the body, or whole person (as often:) being understood. Some by *שאר* would have the kindred, or nearest relations, as wife and children, to be intended. And so the word is taken too, as in Genesis often. So the sense would be, that the cruel or extremely miserable, *rationem nullam habet propinquitatis*; has no regard of them that are near to him, as by Grotius the words are paraphrased. This also may be allowed. Some rabbins upon the first, and indeed most literal sense, take occasion to reprove them that macerate their bodies by penances which the law does not require. Mercerus as a Christian, not ignorant of what St. Paul professes of himself, and the practice of the primitive times is more moderate: yet he blames that excess, which does rather dull the soul, than quicken it, to other more necessary duties & performances.

**Verse 18.** *{The wicked worketh a deceitful work}* That promises a reward, but brings none, Rom. 6:21. Eph. 5:11. His reward, or that reward which he promised himself, and aimed at, even in this world, often fails: but in case he speed, to the utmost of his desires; yet is it no reward properly, but a bait rather, to lead him to eternal destruction.

*{to him that soweth}* Ps. 112:9, 10. It is like good seed: it will certainly bring profit, Gal. 6:8. 2 Cor. 9:6.

*{righteousness}* Sometimes it is taken for alms.

*{a sure reward}* Hebrew, *שכר*, *Secher*, for *שכר* *Schar*; *a reward*. But because Isa. 19:10. this word *שכר* *Secher* is there also found, (*כל עשה שכר אגמי נפש*) there translated in our English, *all that make sluices and ponds for fish*, or, *of living things*,) and by most; (among others, by learned Mercer,) conceived of necessity to signify there *obstructionem, clausuram*, or somewhat (Junius renders it, *excipulum*,) to that purpose; which granted, it follows, that *שכר* is there for *סכר*: a *Sin*, for a *Samech*: upon this, some rabbins infer, that it is not without some allusion to that word and signification there, that *שכר* is here put for *שכר*, the more emphatically, say they, to set out the certainty of the reward, as a fisherman thinks himself sure, when he has the fishes enclosed in his net, or drag. Many such secret allusions there be in the Scripture, which makes me, after Mercerus, to take notice of this observation. Yet I will not bind any man to believe it: if it may go for a probable conjecture, it is the utmost that can be said of it.

*{a deceitful work — a sure reward}* Heb. *a reward of truth*: So Luke 16:9, 11. *the Mammon of unrighteousness*, that is, (as upon Chapter 8:18. is showed) deceitful; is opposed to *true riches*.

**Verse 19.** *{As}* *So*.

*{As righteousness tendeth to life}* He unfolds what he meant in the last verse, expounding the last part first.

*{so}* Heb. *and*.

**Verse 20.** *{of a froward heart}* Heb. *עקש לב* perverse, crooked: full of dissimulation, and hypocrisy, unstable, inconstant, &c. See upon Chapter 2:15. *whose ways are crooked*, &c.

**Verse 21.** *{Though hand join in hand, the wicked shall not be unpunished}* There be no less (as I find them reckoned by some, though some of them seem coincident) than 14. several interpretations of these words, which are not elsewhere in Scripture, but here and Chapter 16:5. They may be found in Velerio his *Adagies*. I do not desire to multiply words, where I see no greater cause. The words seem to me, both of themselves, and by the reason of the context, plain enough. Though wicked men plot and project never so much, and strengthen themselves by mutual counsels and confederacies, to secure themselves; yet when the time for their punishment, which God has appointed is come; all their designs, plots, and confederacies, in which they trust, as though they had made a covenant with hell, and the grave, shall stand them in little stead. The scope of the words is, not, to deny, but that wicked men by their cunning and policy may prevail a long time; (as we call a long time: though the prophet Habakkuk does teach otherwise: *though it tarry, wait for it; because it will surely come: it will not tarry*, Chapter 2:3.) God is willing they should; and has some use for them; but to set out the certainty, and unavoidableness of their destruction; which the longer it is put off, the more terrible and remediless it will be. This to me seems best. Yet I will give the reader an account of Mercerus his interpretation of the words also; not only because of his authority, but also because it agrees very well with the context. *Hand in hand*, he takes for, *From hand to hand*; and by that, he would have a succession of judgments and punishments from father to son, to many generations, to be intended. We know the same is threatened in divers places of Scripture: and so it will be more opposite (according to the usual tenor of these proverbs) to the next member; *but the seed of the righteous shall be delivered*. But we cannot say so of Chapter 16:5. where the former sense seems to agree better. These (among so many) be the two chief interpretations.

*{the wicked shall not be unpunished}* A wicked man cannot free himself from mischief; but a good man may free his posterity also.

**Verse 22.** *{As a jewel of gold}* What kind of jewel, or ornament this *נזם* (the original word) was, there be many conjectures; but conjectures only, no certainty: neither does it much concern the sense, or matter itself. There be people at this day in some parts of the world, that wear rings at their nose, or nostrils, from which hang jewels, pearls, &c. It is possible such a fashion, or not unlike, might be among the Jews of those days. However, it is more likely that Solomon’s words allude to the ringing of swine, as they call it, to keep them from digging; a thing used in all places.

*{so is a fair woman which is without discretion}* Heb. *that hath departed from taste*: That is, that wants reason to guide herself. Or; *that hath lost her savor*: That is, her credit, by light courses.

*{the expectation}* Or, *to the expectation*.

**Verse 23.** *{The desire}* Or, *to the desire*.

*{The desire of the righteous is only good}* The good shall have that happiness they desire; but the wicked can expect for nothing but God’s anger. See more upon the next words.

*{but the expectation of the wicked}* As here *expectation* is not properly taken, but for event, or reward, which use to be the object of expectation; so *desire* in the former member too, I take for no other than event, or reward, for the same reason. So that the effect of the words seems no other than this, that all things *work together* (though seemingly otherwise for a time:) *for good to them that love God*: that nothing can happen to them but by a special providence for their good in the end; which ought to be our chief aim to refer all things to the end. As contrariwise, nothing to the wicked, &c. So that we shall not need to trouble ourselves to reconcile these words, *the desire of the righteous is only good*: with divers other places of Scripture, importing that the best of men, are prone enough to covet and desire things that they should not; yea and to pursue sometimes their desires unto accomplishment.

**Verse 24.** *{scattereth}* Ps. 112:9, 10.

*{and yet increaseth}* He that gives liberally, gets by giving; and the covetous lose by not giving. See also upon Ps. 25:14. *The secret of the Lord*. And upon Chapter 10:4. *The hand of the diligent*.

**Verse 25.** *{The liberal soul shall be made fat}* 2 Cor. 8:8, 7, 10. That is, rich.

*{The liberal soul}* Heb. *The soul of blessing*.

*{he that watereth, shall be watered also himself}* He shall be made richer, that he may still do good. Hebr. *הוא יורא*, which if from *ירא*, as some think, must signify, *he shall cast*, (*jacere, profundere*,) that is, *he shall abound*; as Junius expresses it. But it is more probable, that *יורא* is here for *יורה*, from *רוה*, *irrigare*, that is, *to water*, as it is here.

**Verse 26.** *{He that withholdeth corn}* He that keeps corn till it be dear, or faulty.

*{the people shall curse him}* Heb. *strike him through*. For curses are like darts.

*{but blessing shall be upon the head of him that selleth it}* They will pray God to bless him.

**Verse 27.** *{He that diligently seeketh good, procureth favour}* He that rises betimes to seek good to himself, or others.

*{but he that seeketh mischief, it shall come unto him}* Ps. 7:15, 16. & 9. 15, 16. & 10. 2. & 57. 6.
