**Verse 28.** *{He that trusteth in his riches, shall fall}* Like a dry leaf, or as one that trusts to an unsure stay, Es. 36. 6. 1 Tim. 6:17. but the righteous that trust in God, shall flourish like a green leaf or branch.

*{the righteous shall flourish as a branch}* Ps. 1:3. & 92. 12. &c. Jer. 17:8.

*{branch}* Or, *a leaf*: That is, a green leaf.

**Verse 29.** *{He that troubleth his own house, shall inherit the wind}* Chapter 15:27. By imposing too much labor, sparing too much, or raising quarrels; he shall not mend his estate, but impair it.

*{the fool shall be servant to the wise of heart}* He that has not wit to keep what his friends left him, must in his old days serve them, that are wiser than himself.

**Verse 30.** *{and he that winneth}* That they learn of some, they teach others.

*{he that winneth souls, is wise}* Subdues them to the knowledge and will of God, 2 Cor. 10:4, 5.

*{winneth}* Heb. *taketh*. Or, because the noun that comes from that verb (one of them,) signifies *discipline, doctrine*, some make that to be the signification of the verb also in this place; and translate, *qui disciplina imbuit*; that is, *he that doth instruct*, or *teach*: which points at the ordinary way of winning of souls, by teaching; whether by public preaching, or by private admonitions and instructions at home. But it is done often by a godly life, and good example, as effectually; and more effectually too, sometimes.

**Verse 31.** *{Behold, the righteous shall be recompensed in the earth}* 1 Pet. 4:18. Punished for his sins.

## CHAPTER 12

**Verse 1.** *{Whoso loveth instruction, loveth knowledge}* Rather, *correction*. To wit, by word of mouth, called reproof afterwards. It is a sign he loves knowledge, that can bear his master’s reproof: but he that cannot bear it, loves it no more than a beast.

**Verse 3.** *{the root of the righteous shall not be moved}* Chapter 10:25. The state of the righteous, though it shake sometimes, yet is like a deep rooted tree, it will not be blown down, whereas the wicked, when they seem strongest.

**Verse 4.** *{virtuous}* Heb. *Of virtue*, Chapter 31:10.

*{A virtuous woman is a crown to her husband}* 1 Cor. 11:7. Not only an excellent helper, but also a chief ornament in the eyes of others, Chapter 31:23.

**Verse 5.** *{The thoughts of the righteous are right}* Heb. *judgment*. That is, right as judgments ought to be.

*{the counsels of the wicked are deceit}* Not only their rash thoughts, but also their deliberate ones.

**Verse 6.** *{The words of the wicked are to lie in wait for blood}* Chapter 1:11, 18. Or, *words*, for works and actions, as often.

*{the mouth of the upright shall deliver them}* Those whose blood the wicked seek to spoil, 1 Sam. 19:4. or, themselves. As Chapter 11:9. *An hypocrite with his mouth destroyeth his neighbour: but through knowledge shall the just be delivered*.

**Verse 7.** *{The wicked are overthrown, and are not}* Ps. 37:37. Chapter 11:21.

**Verse 8.** *{A man shall be commended according to his wisdom}* Not always so; and that Solomon knew very well: Eccles. 9:11. *I returned*, &c. See upon Chapter 10:23. *a man of understanding*.

*{of a perverse heart}* Heb. *perverse of heart*.

**Verse 9.** *{He that is despised, and hath a servant}* In some English Bibles that I have seen, the word *hath*, is printed in another letter; as though it were not in the original text: which may occasion a mistake, because *despised and a servant* might seem to do very well together, without any such supply. But the truth is, there is a *לו* (*illi*) in the Hebrew, which does amount to the verb *hath*, in English. Except we make of it a reciprocal pronoun; as some do: and then the English must be: *and is a servant to himself*. Neither will the sense be amiss so. For though a man may bring himself into contempt by it with the vulgar; yet wiser men will commend, and think him happy too, that can be his own servant, and suffice himself, without other help. Philosophers much commend it, (*τὴν αὐτουργίαν*: *self-ministry*:) and it is observed, that Homer (who knew what is commendable and virtuous better than many philosophers, as Horace thought:) does set out his *heroes*, as not disdaining sometimes some very mean services. Hence the vulgar: *esse sufficientem sibi*: which, among men of understanding, is the highest of commendations and happiness, and brings a man nearest unto God, the only true *αὐτάρκης*.

**Verse 10.** *{A righteous man regardeth the life of his beast}* Is merciful even to the very beast that does him service. He does not overwork them, and affords them good keeping.

*{the tender mercies of the wicked are cruel}* If they be cruel, when they pretend to show mercy, what are they, when they come with a drawn sword to shed blood?

*{tender}* Or, *bowels*.

**Verse 11.** *{He that tilleth his land, shall be satisfied with bread}* Chapter 28:19.

*{he that followeth vain persons, is void of understanding}* And, so neglects his business, whereby he should live. The Hebr. word signifies *vain things* (and so used often:) as well as *persons*, which gives us occasion to consider, whether this proverb may not have some affinity with that in Ecclesiastes, *He that observeth the wind, shall not sow; and he that regardeth the clouds, shall not reap*, chapter 11:4. There may be very good sense made of it: and it puts me in mind of many vain precepts and observations that Hesiod has concerning husbandry, in choosing proper days, &c.

**Verse 12.** *{The wicked desireth the net of evil men; but the root of the righteous yield}, &c.* Men who have no grounds in them of piety and godliness, and are for the present only; which when they see the prosperity of the wicked, and how they thrive in their bloody designs, who *take up men, as fishes with the angle: they catch them in their net, and gather them in their drag*, Hab. 1:15. *they envy them* (chapter 23:17.) and covet extremely to be of their society, that they may share with them: but *the root of the righteous*, which has its growth, underground, not seen by fleshly eyes; by God’s constant and secret blessing yields to abundance, without any such violent indirect means. So we may expound it of temporal blessings also. But it is more likely that the fruits here intended, are the mystical fruits of eternal life, to the righteous himself; and the spiritual fruits of good instruction, and example, to the conversion, and spiritual improvement of many others; according to that we had before, *The fruit of the righteous is a tree of life; and he that winneth souls, is wise*, chapter 11:30. In the first words, why he that desires the society of bloody violent men, should be called *רשע* (which commonly imports *wickedness* in the highest degree:) and the bloody men themselves *רעים* only, which is a more general word; first I answer, that those distinctions of words do not always hold: as for example, in the first psalm, first verse, three sorts of evil men by way (as many are of opinion,) of gradation being mentioned, *רשעים* are the first of the three: *חטאים* *sinners*, the next: *לצים* *scorners* the last. Secondly, as there is great affinity of letters, so frequent permutation of significations also between *רוע* *to be evil* *רעה* *to have fellowship* and *רעע* *to bruise in pieces*: or *to destroy*; (as the word is translated chapter 13:20.) it is not improbable that Solomon might make choice of that word in this place with allusion (as there is a manifest allusion in the very words chapter 13:20.) to some of those other significations. Lastly, the Hebrew word *מצוד* here translated *the net*, signifies *hunting* also, and (if the *מ* be radical) *a castle* or *fortress*: which significations are here followed by some. But for the first, whether *net*, or *hunting*, there will be no great difference of sense. But if we translate, *fortress*, (as Junius does: *Desiderat improbus praesidium malorum; sed radix justorum dat illud*:) then the best sense (whether *malorum*, be taken for a neuter, or a masculine: either of which the Hebrew *רעים* may be:) will be, that wicked men are very careful to provide for their security: but in vain; because it is the prerogative, and as it were, the natural fruit and effect of integrity, and godliness: which is often inculcated in these Proverbs: and how to be understood, see upon Psalm 91:5. *Thou shalt not be afraid for the terror by night*, &c.

*{the net}* Or, *the fortress*.

*{the root of the righteous yieldeth fruit}* Meaning, their heart within, which is upright, and does good to all.

*{yieldeth fruit}* Heb. *יתן* (*dabit*:) as Chapter 10:24. where the English is: *shall be granted*.

**Verse 13.** *{The wicked is snared by the transgression of his lips}* Heb. *The snare of the wicked is in the transgression of his lips*, Chapter 18:7. An evil man’s tongue brings himself into trouble, and a good man helps himself out, 1 Sam. 25.

**Verse 14.** *{A man shall be satisfied with good by the fruit of his mouth}* Chapter 13:2. Psalm 34:12, 13, 14.

*{and the recompense of a man’s hands shall be rendered unto him}* A good man shall be rewarded, both for his good words and works.

**Verse 15.** *{The way of a fool is right in his own eyes}* Chapter 3:7. His own device: for he thinks himself wisest, and hearkens to no man’s counsel. See upon Chapter 1:5. *A wise man will hear*, &c. and Chapter 11:12. *despiseth his neighbours*.

*{he that hearkeneth unto counsel is wise}* Hesiod (who is thought by some to have been before Homer: but of great antiquity certainly, and in many economical precepts and admonitions, well agreeing with Solomon:) makes two degrees of wise men: *Οὗτος μὲν πανάριστος*, &c. The first, of them, that are wise of themselves, and can see what is expedient, both present and future, without taking counsel of others: the second of them, that will entertain good counsel given them by others.

**Verse 16.** *{A fool’s wrath is presently known}* Or, *A fool is known in the day of his wrath*. For then he vents his folly.

*{presently}* Heb. *in that day*.

*{a prudent man covereth shame}* Bridles his affections, that he may not speak, or do anything that may shame him: or, hides the shame of another, by taking no notice of an injury.

**Verse 17.** *{He that speaketh truth, sheweth forth righteousness}* Chapter 14:5. Though *truth* and *righteousness*, are different in themselves; (and the apostle speaks of some that held *the truth in unrighteousness*, Rom. 1:18.) yet they have so much affinity, that a man may probably presume, that he that loves and uses to speak the truth, is a right honest man; just and righteous in his dealings: and besides it is a great means to preserve justice and righteousness among men, to speak the truth at all times.

*{a false witness}* Heb. *of lies*. The language of a false witness, or perfidious man, is full of guile, dissimulation, and hypocrisy.

**Verse 18.** *{There is that speaketh like the piercings of a sword}* Ps. 57:4. & 59. 7. It is not every man that does so, but there are some so bad, that their tongues wound like swords.

**Verse 19.** *{The lip of truth shall be established for ever}* A true speaker can stand it out to the end; but a liar can shift but a while. Or it may be understood as properly with reference to another world, that eternal life shall be the reward of truth; whereas all that is got by lying, and cheating, and such indirect courses is but for a moment, and shall soon vanish away.

**Verse 20.** *{Deceit is in the heart of them that imagine evil}* Or, *Deceit returns into the heart*. That is, they that seek to deceive others, hurt themselves: but they that give good counsel, fare the better for it themselves.

**Verse 21.** *{There shall no evil happen to the just}* Because all things that happen unto them in this world, whether in the nature of cross, or prosperous events, *work together for their good*, Rom. 8:28. See the former reference upon Ps. 91:5. *Thou shalt not be afraid*, &c. And of temporal promises in general, see upon Ps. 37:1. *Fret not thyself*, &c. and other verses of that psalm.

**Verse 22.** *{Lying lips are abomination to the Lord}* Chapter 6:17. Though God hate all manner of evil; yet lying is most hateful to him, being a God of truth, Rev. 22:15.

*{they that deal truly are his delight}* That do as they speak, which liars do not.

**Verse 23.** *{A prudent man concealeth knowledge}* Chapter 13:16. & 15. 2. He that is wise reveals his knowledge, only when he may do good with it: but fools by prating show their folly. Some of the ancients have excellent sayings to that purpose, that we should not conceal knowledge, but freely and ingenuously impart it unto others. Their meaning is, that we should not conceal it out of envy; not that we should make brags of it, or prostitute it to the contempt of them, who are not likely to be benefited by it: which is to *conceal* it, in Solomon’s sense. See also upon Chapter 10:14. *Wise men lay up knowledge*.

**Verse 24.** *{The hand of the diligent shall bear rule}* Chapter 10:4.

*{The slothful}* Or, *deceitful*: For such men live by their wits, not by their hands. See before upon Chapter 10:4. *with a slack hand*.

**Verse 25.** *{Heaviness in the heart of man maketh it stoop}* Chapter 15:13. As a heavy burden weighs down the body.

*{a good word maketh it glad}* A word of comfort from a friend.

*{word}* Or, *matter*, Chapter 15:20.

**Verse 26.** *{The righteous is more excellent than his neighbour, but the way of the wicked seduceth them}* *יתר* *more excellent*, here; is by some, (as Mercer and others: and so by our margin here,) rendered *abundant*: and some that follow this translation, make this sense of the whole, (which is agreeable enough to divers other places of Scripture:) that the just and godly shall abound in all good things, when the wicked, through their way, or wickedness, shall err, or wander for want; that is, in effect, shall beg. I do not see, but this may do well enough: certain it is, that the Hebrew *תתעם* here, and by most interpreters, expressed *seduceth them*, may as well be translated, *causeth them to err*; or *to wander*. Yet *seduceth them* as it is more generally received, so will afford a more general and warrantable sense; to wit, that the way of the wicked first or last, leads them to destruction: or that their wicked life, leads them to wicked opinions, and seduces them from the truth, though right before in their faith. And this may be the sense of this latter part, whether we translate *excellent*, or *abundant* in the first. But if *excellent*, Rabbi Kimki makes the sense of the whole (and his too, not improbable:) this: That righteousness is of itself, a more noble thing, than unrighteousness, and more commendable, few or none make any question: and by consequent, that a righteous man is more excellent than his neighbor, or any other that is not righteous. What then is the cause, that so few apply themselves that way, to follow his example? Because they see, though (*Virtus laudatur et alget*: as one of the poets has it:) justice be commended and commendable, yet most men thrive in the world by injustice. To this purpose see more upon Chapter 1:32. *For the turning away of the simple, — and the prosperity of fools shall destroy them*. The vulgar Latin (which some take upon them to justify as the most genuine sense: *profunde et germane*, says Cornelius à Lapide, which I wonder at:) expresses it: *Qui negligit damnum propter amicum, justus est.* It does afford them that follow it, sufficient matter of discourse (and so their volumes come to swell:) how a man is bound to neglect his own profit, or advantage, for his friend, or neighbor’s sake. There may be good use enough of that speculation; but as to Solomon’s words, I conceive it altogether impertinent.

**Verse 27.** *{The slothful man roasteth not that which he took in hunting}* As if a man should say; I am not the richer for anything you ever gave me; the meaning may be, either not the richer, because it did not thrive with me; or, because you never gave anything: so here, *roasteth not*; either when he has it, lets it spoil, or be lost through neglect and laziness: or, *roasteth not*, because through laziness he will not take the pains to hunt, or to hunt diligently; and so gets nothing: which, of the two, is the more probable interpretation. In ancient times, before men were incorporated into towns and cities, hunting was a principal object of man’s industry; and it is very likely that the Latin word *studium*, came originally from the Hebrew *צוד* which signifies *to hunt*. Most likely nevertheless, (as most are of opinion) that the words are to be taken proverbially, to this effect; that as it often falls out, that they that have taken great pains in hunting, and have got somewhat; yet through some either sudden chance happening to their own person, as either death or sickness; or some other casualty, through which they are bereaved of their prey, they eat not of it: so it is with the wicked man’s wealth, which though he scrapes and heaps together with much labor, yet he does not at all, or, not long, enjoy. This to be the right meaning of the words, most interpreters agree: but for the truth of the sentence, it must be remembered (as has been often told) that it is enough, if it be so for the most part; or that there be now and then, in every age, some notorious examples of it: which if there were not, it is not likely it would have become so common an observation among the wise heathen too, who also have divers sayings and sentences to this purpose. If it be objected, that it so falls out with the righteous too sometimes, (or say, as often, or oftener, if you will; though I believe it not) and therefore, not the proper case of the wicked; I answer, that the difference between the case of the righteous and the wicked, upon which the observation is grounded, is in their different condition at the time of their death: *The wicked is driven away in his wickedness, but the righteous hath hope in his death*, Chapter 14:32. See upon Chapter 10:28. *The hope of the righteous shall be gladness*, &c. See the reference there, and upon Ps. 37:1, 2. &c. *Fret not thyself*, &c.

*{slothful man}* Heb. *man of slackness*, as Chapter 10:4. Mic. 6:9. Or, *fraudulent man*. See before upon 10. 4. *with a slack hand*.

*{roasteth not}* Learned Mr. Fuller, *Miscellan.* l. 6. c. 13. would have the words translated, *non clathrabit*, that is, *doth not hang upon the rack*; to preserve, &c. He has a great deal to prove that to be the proper signification of the word: but it does not concern the sense, in general; nor us much, in this place.

*{but the substance of a diligent man is precious}* Heb. *but the substance which is precious of a diligent man*. Such trajections (as they call them) are frequent in Scripture. Yet the words might be otherwise ordered too, but to the same effect; and this is the most probable construction.

**Verse 28.** *{In the way of righteousness is life — no death}* See upon 3. 18. *a tree of life*. And 10. 2. *delivereth from death*.

## CHAPTER 13

**Verse 1.** *{Instruction}* Or, *reproof*.

**Verse 2.** *{A man shall eat good by the fruit of his mouth}* Chapter 12:14. He that uses his tongue aright, shall get good by it: he that does otherwise, shall get hurt.

**Verse 3.** *{He that keepeth his mouth, keepeth his life}* Chapter 21:23. From rash and hasty speeches. See before 10. 14. *wise men*.

**Verse 4.** *{The soul of the sluggard, desireth, and hath nothing}* He ever desires, but takes no pains to get any living: and therefore must lose his longing.

**Verse 5.** *{A righteous man hateth lying}* A man is not righteous that abstains from lying, unless he do it out of the hatred of that sin. See before upon Chapter 12:17. *He that speaketh truth*.

*{lying}* Heb. *a word of a lie*.

*{and cometh to shame}* By his lying.

**Verse 6.** *{Righteousness keepeth him that is upright in the way}* Chapter 11:3, 5, 6.

*{the sinner}* Heb. *sin*.

**Verse 7.** *{There is that maketh himself rich, yet hath nothing: there is that maketh himself poor, yet hath great riches}* These words will afford a double sense; the one economical; the other, philosophical: so I speak, taking philosophy here, for a speculative science: though I know otherwise, that *oeconomia*: is a part of moral philosophy. The first, economical, and most literal, tending to warn us, that the world is full of dissimulation: and that we should not judge of men’s estates, in our mutual dealings, and commerces; by what themselves make show: which is of dangerous consequence: because, for example, some will make themselves poor of purpose, that they may receive support from them, whom they are better able to relieve, or gratify. Others, very beggars, or in a sinking condition, make themselves rich, that they may, by their supposed wealth and ability, have credit to take up where they will, and to borrow: through whose dissimulation many are undone. The philosophical, this; That some men, (truly virtuous and godly:) though outwardly they appear poor, (*that maketh himself*; that is, that has the port, & resemblance: *prae se fert*, as the Latins speak; or, *maketh himself poor*, because he does not apply himself to the world, and contemns the wealth and pomp of it: and so on the other side, *makes himself rich*, because it is his only study: see upon 10. 4. *but the hand of the diligent*) yet in very truth are rich: rich in their souls, the best riches. Others who appear rich (vicious ungodly men:) who in very truth, are very poor; naked and starved in their souls. Somewhat to this purpose upon Chapter 12:9. *He that is despised*, &c. And Rev. 2:9. *I know thy works, and tribulation, and poverty; but thou art rich*. Or, if we will take this *maketh himself*, of purposed dissimulation, or deluded imagination: then Rev. 3:17. *Because thou sayest, I am rich, and increased with goods, and have need of nothing; and knowest not, that thou art wretched, and miserable, and poor, and blind and naked*. And on the contrary, *that maketh himself poor*: to wit, through humility of spirit, abasing himself, as the greatest of sinners; wherein consists perfection of virtue and godliness.

**Verse 8.** *{but the poor heareth not rebuke}* A rich man questioned and troubled, may make his peace by his riches: but a poor man is seldom questioned, or quarreled with, having nothing to lose. See upon Chapter 10:15. *the rich man’s wealth* &c.

**Verse 9.** *{The light of the righteous rejoiceth}* Chapter 4:18, 19. The prosperity of good men is like the light of the sun, which, increasing, brings more joy still: but the prosperity of the wicked is like a candle, that lessens till it goes out.

*{rejoiceth}* Or, *shall rejoice*.

*{but the lamp of the wicked shall be put out}* Job 18:6. and 21. 17.

*{lamp}* Or, *candle*.

**Verse 10.** *{Only by pride cometh contention}* Angry men are provoked by wrongs; but proud men contend for preeminence: or, the main cause of contention generally is pride. See James 3:13. &c. *Who is a wise man*, &c.

*{with the well-advised is wisdom}* Proud men quarrel, because they hearken not to counsel: they that hearken to it, are too wise to quarrel.

**Verse 11.** *{Wealth gotten by vanity, shall be diminished}* Chapter 10:2. & 20. 21.

*{by vanity}* By wickedness, as 10. 2. *Treasures of wickedness*, Or, more probably (that being more agreeable to the ordinary notion of the original word:) by vain means, or courses: as gaming, acting upon a stage, and the like.

*{by labour}* Heb. *with the hand*.

**Verse 12.** *{when the desire cometh, it is a tree of life}* Chapter 13:19. See upon Chapter 3:18. *a tree of life*.

**Verse 13.** *{Whoso despiseth the word, shall be destroyed}* Meaning, the Word of God, whereby he is admonished of his duty.

*{shall be rewarded}* Or, *shall be in peace*.

**Verse 14.** *{The law of the wise is a fountain of life, to depart from the snares of death}* Chapter 14:27.

**Verse 15.** *{the way of transgressors is hard}* Or, *harsh*. And therefore displeasing to others.

*{hard}* Or, *rough*.

**Verse 16.** *{Every prudent man dealeth with knowledge}* Chapter 12:23. & 15. 2.

*{layeth}* Heb. *spreadeth*.

**Verse 17.** *{A wicked messenger falleth into mischief}* An unfaithful messenger shall be punished, because he has wounded his master’s estate by neglect of his business: but a faithful one, which has healed it, shall be rewarded.

*{a faithful ambassador is health}* Heb. *an ambassador of faithfulness*; that is, a most faithful one, Chapter 25:13.

**Verse 18.** *{instruction}* Or, *rebuke*.

*{but he that regardeth reproof, shall be honoured}* Chapter 15:5, 31. With riches and dignity.

**Verse 19.** *{the desire accomplished is sweet to the soul}* **Verse 12.** God does accomplish the desire of good men to their joy, Ps. 145:19. but not of the wicked, because they cannot be drawn to leave their sins. So some. But the most obvious and more immediate exposition is; That all men (it being the nature of man, still to be in the pursuit of something or other) have their desires, in the accomplishment of which is the chief comfort of their lives: whence it is, that fools or wicked men can so hardly be reclaimed, because, being full of desires, (such as they are) they have found so much sweetness (not considering that it will be bitterness in the end) in the accomplishment, as they cannot promise themselves from honest ends and desires, to the sweetness of which, for want of experience, they are altogether strangers: and therefore not easily persuaded to forsake their wonted courses. According to this interpretation, it should rather be, (which is the most proper and usual signification of the Hebrew particle *ו*) *and it is abomination*, &c.

**Verse 20.** *{He that walketh with wise men, shall be wise}* And so preserved, whereas a companion of fools, learning folly of them, shall perish.

*{shall be destroyed}* Heb. *shall be broken*. Or *shall be worse*; (*pejor erit*; Junius;) from *רוע*, *to be naught*. The affinity of letters between these words *רעע* and *רוע* makes (as in many other Hebrew words of that kind) that they sometimes exchange significations. See upon Chapter 12:12. *The wicked desireth*, &c.

**Verse 21.** *{pursueth}* Heb. *eagerly pursueth*.

**Verse 22.** *{a good man leaveth an inheritance to his children’s children}* He leaves it to his children, and they theirs, (as if you should say; *De bene quaesitis laetatur tertius haeres*: according to the poet’s expression; *De male quaesitis vix gaudet tertius haeres*, which comes to the same effect:) whereas the wealth of wicked men many times comes not to their own children, but to good men, that have no relation to them, Psalm 37:26.

*{the wealth of the sinner is laid up for the just}* Job 27:17.

*{laid}* Heb. *hidden*.

**Verse 23.** *{Much food is in the tillage of the poor}* Chapter 12:11. If he follow his business with *judgment*, without which, rich men may want. This interpretation is much confirmed by a passage in Esay, where this very word *משפט* (Isa. 28:26.) is used concerning the mystery of husbandry: there translated *discretion*: *For his God doth instruct him to discretion*, (or, *judgment*) *and doth teach him*. However, the words may be otherwise, and warrantably too translated to this effect: That a poor man may thrive and do well, through his labor and industry, (and God’s blessing upon it; as must be supplied from other places) when rich men are consumed; *בלא משפט*, *quod non fit ei jus: or, citra judicium*: (both, Mercerus his expressions) that is, *through unrighteousness*. Which may also be confirmed by other like passages. This interpretation Mercer does prefer before any other: but since the original words will afford either, let the reader take his choice. Junius is somewhat different; *Sed est qui consumit se inertia*; (and in his margin, *in non officio*) that is *but there is who destroys himself through his slothfulness*: which sense is, of itself, as warrantable, by comparing with other like passages, as any other; but not so proper, as I conceive, to the original words of this place.

**Verse 24.** *{He that spareth his rod, hateth his son}* Chapter 22:13. Does as one that hates him: lets him be corrupted, and so undone.

*{he that loveth him chasteneth him betimes}* Ere his sin grow to a head.

**Verse 25.** *{The righteous eateth to the satisfying of his soul}* Ps. 34:10. & 37. 3. Has food to fill his belly. Of all earthly promises, as life, wealth and the like, in the Old Testament, how to be understood by Christians, has often been said, and references made almost in every chapter, hitherto to those places. I hope the reader will not still expect it. We will only take notice of a seeming contradiction between the words of David, and these of Solomon. David says, Ps. 17:14. *Thou God fillest the bellies of the wicked with his hid treasure*. And Solomon here, that *the belly of the wicked shall want*. Both true literally, with some limitations of times and circumstances: as most proverbs are to be understood, as has been observed more than once. But most true, and most considerable indeed, that even when the bellies of the wicked are most full, and they in sight, most at ease; then may they be said, to be in most miserable case, because by prosperity, they are fatted and culled against the day of slaughter: whereas by misery, temporal, and bodily visitations, (had God been so merciful unto them) they might have come to repentance. Whereas, the grace of God is sufficient to the godly to make them happy at any time, St. Paul assuring us, that no *tribulation, distress, persecution, famine, nakedness, peril, or sword*, can separate true Christians *from the love of God*, Rom. 8:35.

## CHAPTER 14

**Verse 1.** *{Every wise woman buildeth her house}* Increases the welfare of it, by looking well to her children and servants: but foolish women ruin the family. Pride then must not make a man despise the counsel of a prudent wife, nor affection force him to give way to a foolish. There may be a further meaning also (if not the literal and immediate) in these words, God is said to have *made* the godly *midwives houses*, Exod. 1:21. but these words are otherwise both translated, and interpreted by some; as Junius, particularly. But there is no question of Ruth 4:11. where we find a solemn form of blessing women, when married, then in use among the Hebrews: *The Lord make the woman that is come into thine house like Rachel and like Leah: which two did build the house of Israel*, &c. Children also are called *בנים*, from *בנה*, which signifies *to build*. Why then should not a wife, that is, a religious godly woman, be said to build her house, not by her prudence and industry only, but also by her fertility; being made fertile by God’s special blessing, as is often promised in the Scripture? As on the other side sterility is also threatened, or at least mentioned, as a curse. So that as the godly woman may be said to build her house in both those respects; so also the foolish or ungodly, to pluck it down, not only by her sterility, but also by her bad carriage and administration.

**Verse 2.** *{He that walketh in his uprightness, feareth the Lord}* Men’s courses show, whether they fear God, or despise him.

*{but he that is perverse in his ways, despiseth him}* Job 12:4.

**Verse 3.** *{In the mouth of the foolish is a rod of pride}* Some by *a rod of pride*, would have that rod understood wherewith the fool himself is punished, often mentioned, as Chapter 10:13. & 26. 3. *A rod for the fool’s back*, &c. and this *rod*, here called a *rod of pride*, because many fools by proud talk (as much as anything) expose themselves to the lash. Others more probably, by *a rod of pride*, understand proud scornful words, words of reproach and contumely, very frequent in the mouth of fools, whereby they strike at other men, as it were with a staff. The Latins use to say, *virgula Censoria*, of rigid and over-severe judgments and censures. Solomon might aim at both, that whilst proud fools strike at others with their contumelious tongues, they expose themselves to the stroke, which haply they might have avoided: according to that of the poet, *caedimur, inque vicem, praebemus crura sagittis.* There be also, who think that fools’ talk might be called here, *a rod of pride*, because fools oftentimes (as children) speak more freely of great men’s faults, than wiser men dare do. Which is true enough: the more is the pity. Even a wise man shall be accounted a fool, if he speak freely, though it lie upon him as a duty. However, I do not think this to have been Solomon’s meaning.

**Verse 4.** *{Where no oxen are, the crib is clean}* By the ox, is meant labor; and by the crib, the barn: meaning, without labor, there is no food for cattle, much less for men:

*{much increase by the strength of the ox}* Solomon’s aim is not to commend oxen, for their strength; but the care, and labor of men by which oxen become serviceable for so necessary a work, plowing, &c. (according to the custom of those places) by which men’s lives are chiefly maintained. It is a good sign, of a careful and provident husbandman, when either oxen, or horses, employed in that work, are well kept.

**Verse 5.** *{A faithful witness will not lie}* Exod. 20:16. & 23. 1. 1 Chr. 6. 19. & 12. 17. Many hate the imputation of false witness, which make little or no conscience of lying; especially if it be upon an extraordinary occasion, or for a friend, or in a good cause, or the like. But as he is a faithful witness in very deed, who will not lie; so he that tells lies, let his pretense be what it will, he is a false witness. So many, upon a persuasion grounded upon some outward performances and appearances, (as long prayers, diligent hearing of the Word, and the like,) that they are godly and religious, stick at no manner of unrighteousness in their daily practice and dealings, and never think the worse of themselves for it: to whom St. John says, *Little children; Let no man deceive you: He that doth righteousness, is righteous, (even as he is righteous,) He that committeth sin, is of the Devil*. &c. 1 John 3:7, 8. Or, *A faithful witness*, &c. That is, He really deserves the title and commendation of a faithful witness, who not for his credit only, or any other worldly consideration is faithful; but because he makes a conscience (before God) to tell a lie; and not only in bearing of witness, but even in his ordinary life and conversation does avoid to tell a lie: whereas others, who upon ordinary occasions make no conscience of lying, through custom of lying, may be drawn in time to bear false witness also. So some rabbins, who are followed by some other expositors. Both good; but I think the former the more genuine interpretation.

*{A faithful}* Heb. *Of faithfulnesses*.

**Verse 6.** *{A scorner seeketh wisdom, and findeth it not}* That is, When a scorner seeks. For it is not the propriety of a scorner to seek after wisdom: but when he does, it is for his own ambition, or vain glory, (as Simon Magus did, Acts 8:18, 19.) or some other worldly end, not in simplicity, and sincerity of heart, to glorify God with it, or out of love of godliness: in which case, no wonder if he seek, and find not. For he *scorneth the scorners; but he giveth grace unto the lowly*, Chapter 3:34.

*{knowledge is easy unto him that understandeth}* Chapter 8:9. See there.

**Verse 8.** *{The wisdom of the prudent, is to understand his way: but the folly of fools, is deceit}* Heb. *חכמת ערום*, translated by Mercerus, *Sapientia callidi*: by Junius, *Sapientia astuti*. The matter of this saying, is excellent; and the expression is pithy. There is not anything, that men ordinarily are more ambitious of, and more heartily (some secretly, some openly) applaud themselves in, than to be *subtle*, and *crafty*: or as we translate, *prudent*, and worldly wise. Now Solomon tells us here, what that craft, or prudence is, and wherein it chiefly consists, that makes men wise indeed; and should be desired: and what again is the proper craft of fools. A wise man’s craft says he, is to look into his own actions, and to observe himself: and the craft of fools, (or worldly wise men) wherein they boast and glory, as great wisdom; it is their folly: it is mere folly. Or, *Their folly, is their craft*: which comes all to one. *Stulti stultitia sua hoc unum spectant*, &c. as Mercerus here: that is, Fools through their folly mind nothing else, but how they may cheat, or overreach, and overplot others: this is their wisdom: this is their wisdom, and that wisdom, (as they deem it,) is their folly. By the antithesis it clearly appears that the words must thus be supplied and interpreted. And so Junius too; *Sapientia astuti, est advertere ad viam suam: stultitia autem stolidorum, ad dolum.* So that we have here in these words, that by ancient heathen philosophers, and others, so much admired wisdom, by them consecrated in some of their most holy temple, expressed in those words, *Know thyself*: and by Socrates afterwards (whose ordinary speech, and motto, which became very famous in after ages, included in one verse, *Ὅττι κεν ἐν μεγάροισι*, &c. to this purpose; was, *That he that is wise, should be wise at home*, to observe his own actions, *what just, what unjust; what right, what wrong*, &c.) further advanced, for which he was himself among the learned gentiles; both Romans and Grecians, little less than adored; in these words, I say, most elegantly and pithily comprehended. It is a common subject in the writing of ancient philosophers; and they have done so excellently well upon it, that Christians (as many as are able) may be sent to them to learn, and edify. But especially to what they have written concerning Anacharsis the Scythian philosopher, his long search for wisdom among the Grecians; and his determination upon the matter, and their large commentations upon it. There be other passages (see ver. 14. *Their backslider*, &c.) in this chapter, that would make a man suspect, that Solomon was not unacquainted with ancient philosophy; or rather, that ancient philosophy has been beholding to Solomon. Some expound the latter part of the verse otherwise too, which is as agreeable to the Hebrew, and not less agreeable to ancient philosophy: *But the folly of fools is deceit*; that is, say they, the wickedness of wicked men proceeds from the error of their judgment. For all men, doubtless, propose to themselves the same end, happiness; yea true happiness: so that what they do contrary to that end, proceeds from want of judgment and knowledge, in discerning between that which is truly good, and that which is truly evil. Or to express it in the words of an ancient philosopher; *Whosoever sinneth, doth in that decline from his purposed end, and is certainly deceived*. And this is rational Aristotle’s doctrine also, who disputes at large and maintains, that he is a good man, who *ἕκαστα κρίνει ὀρθῶς*, *judgeth aright of all things*: and *to whom* (*ἐν ἑκάστοις τ᾽ ἀληθὲς φαίνεται*:) *the truth of all things doth appear*: and again, that *ἀγνοεῖ πᾶς ὁ μοχθηρὸς ἃ δεῖ πράττειν*; *that every naughty man is ignorant*. Ethic Nicom. l. 3. c. 4. &c. Upon this ground they make excellent inferences against anger: but we will not digress so far. Diodate is the man, who commends this sense unto us. See also upon chapter 5:23. *he shall go astray*.

**Verse 9.** *{Fools make a mock at sin: but among the righteous, there is favour}* This passage is very differently expressed by interpreters. What we have here, *Fools make a mock*; is answerable to the most usual signification of the Hebrew word *יליץ*, confirmed also by chapter 10:23. *It is a sport to a fool, to do mischief*. And as for the second member, there will be found in it a very plausible (observable in most of these sentences) antithesis, if with some we expound the whole verse to this purpose; That as fools being past all sense of goodness, find some kind of pleasure (such as it is,) in sin; so, and much more, there arises *רצון*, an inward complacency, peace, and pleasure to the just from righteous actions, and a virtuous life. But because the same word signifies also *to speak*, (properly, *to speak as a mediator*, or *interpreter*,) some following that sense, translate, *Stulti loquuntur delictum, et inter rectos est benevolentia*: (so Mercer,) and expound, That as the talk of fools tends to give offense, to make bate, &c. or to excuse and palliate offenses; so the discourse of the just, to maintain good will and friendship. Rabbi Ezra, cited by Mercerus, has a very different interpretation of the word *אשם*, and thereupon grounds a very different sense of the whole verse: but I see so little probability in it, that I pass by it as impertinent. I dare not say so of Junius his translation, and exposition, which is not less different, but more justifiable, if any shall like of it. It is this; *Stultos conciliat reatus*, &c. *Guiltiness joins fools*, (or, *wicked men*) *together: But among the just, that which is acceptable*, (is the means of their conjunction.)

**Verse 10.** *{The heart knoweth his own bitterness}* Great is the liberty (and for which, as ancient philosophers teach, we owe great thanks to our Creator) wherewith God has endowed every man, the meanest of men, as well as the greatest; in leaving the motions of his heart (in things civil, at least) not only free, but secret also: so that it is in our power, (of which good use may be made often: as against our enemies, when we are foiled and the like,) as to conceal our joy, so our sorrow too. But all men have not equal power in that kind. They are accounted wisest, who least betray themselves: but most dangerous withal. For they are most capable to do mischief, if they be so minded. But it is the part of a wise man, never to trust (in matters of concernment) any other man’s countenance; till he know him very well: and even to remember, that the face of a man is one thing, and his heart another: and that at the same time when the face is composed to sorrow, the heart (though invisibly) may be full of joy: and so on the contrary: It is not unlikely, that Solomon by these words, might aim at some such caution; that men might be wary. It is possible also that these words may have some coherence of sense with verses 12, 13. and then the chief scope will be to let us know, that many whom we think very happy (in this world, at least:) and sometimes are apt to envy their happiness, because they live in great plenty of worldly goods, are richly clad, fare well, and seem to us always merry; may nevertheless, notwithstanding all this outward appearance, be sad enough, (especially when by themselves, and out of sight) at the heart: and on the other side, not a few, whom we either pity, or despise as wretched and miserable, full of comfort inwardly: and more happy in effect, even in this present life, than the former.

*{his own bitterness}* Heb. *the bitterness of his soul*.

**Verse 12.** *{There is a way which seemeth right unto a man}* Chapter 12:15.

**Verse 13.** *{Even in laughter the heart is sorrowful, and the end of that mirth is heaviness}* *Ipsae voluptates in tormenta vertuntur.* And again. *Sed non ista hilaritas longa. Videbis eosdem intra exiguum tempus acerrime ridere, et acerrime rabere.* And again, *Ipsae voluptates eorum trepidae, et variis terroribus inquietae: subitque cum maxime exultantes sollicita cogitatio; Haec quam diu?* They are Seneca’s words, an ancient Latin philosopher: the words otherwise might as well be taken for some expositor’s gloss: for none can be more to the purpose. *Their very pleasures*, says he, *are mixed with fear, and disquieted with many terrors: and even when they are at the height of their mirth and joviality, this sudden anxiety pierceth them through; Alas, how long will this last?* All this of worldly men’s joys; of which we think Solomon’s words properly to be intended: But if extended further, to all worldly joys and pleasures in general, as divers expositors apprehend it; see upon Ps. 126:5. *They that sow in tears, shall reap in joy*, &c.

**Verse 14.** *{The backslider in heart}* Heb. *סוג לב* by most translated, *aversus corde*: by Junius, *vir averso animo*; that is, *He that is averse in his heart*: or, *that turns his heart back*, or *away*: to wit, from wisdom, and her summons, or invitations: as Chapter 1:24. &c. is more fully expressed. See there also, upon verse 32. *For the turning away of the simple*, &c. It is the more likely that this here, has relation to those expressions there, because there also, (as here, *shall be filled with his own ways*:) verse 31. follows, *Therefore shall they eat of the fruit of their own way, and be filled with their own devices*. He then that is *averse in heart*: that is, whose heart is averse from good counsel, &c. that is in effect, *a fool*: by which expression is tacitly implied, that no man is naturally so foolish, or wickedly bent, but might be made wise enough to salvation, would he but apply himself to good counsel, and abide reproof: according to that of the poet, *Nemo adeo ferus est, qui non mansuescere possit, Si modo culturae patientem praebeat aurem.* But the same may be called *a backslider* too, in another respect: to wit, in reference to those first principles and notions of nature; of which see upon Chapter 1:20. *Wisdom crieth without*, &c. And so ancient philosophers expound the word *fugitive* too, of one who does not in all things conform himself to the will of God.

*{and a good man shall be satisfied from himself}* Heb. *מעליו* *ex sese*, as Mercerus expresses it: that is from himself, as we have it here, in effect the same, as before *מדרכיו* *with his own ways*: in sum, that whether good, or bad, every one shall reap the fruit of his own doings; as both in this book, and elsewhere often, This I take to be the clear meaning, though there be sundry other expositions of these words. There be that make this to be the sense of the whole verse: That sinners indeed shall be punished proportionably to their demerits, according to the rule of justice; but the just, through the superabounding goodness of God, shall be rewarded beyond measure. Somewhat to that purpose the apostle may seem to say, Rom. 6. last verse. *For the wages of sin is death*, &c. But this interpretation is chiefly grounded upon the *מעליו* we have spoken of; which they would have to signify, *more than he*; or, above him: which I think is more nice, than solid. What is (as I apprehend it) the most genuine meaning of the words, I have showed: which may concern ordinary readers. For their sakes that are scholars, I shall further observe, the consonancy of ancient philosophy, if not in the matter altogether, yet with the expressions of the divine Scriptures. For that is a main point of ancient philosophy, that *αὐτάρκης ἡ ἀρετή*: that virtue, or the practice of virtue is sufficient to happiness: and that he only is to be termed wise and happy, *cui ex seipso* (Solomon’s word here *מעליו*) *apta sunt omnia quae ad beate vivendum fuerunt*: as Tully has it in a place. But *ex sese*, or *seipso*, (*ἐξ ἑαυτοῦ*, in the Greek:) is the word by them all used upon this subject. The matter is thus briefly expressed by Epictetus; *Ἰδιώτου στάσις*, *ἢ χαρακτὴρ οὐδέποτε ἐξ ἑαυτοῦ προσδοκᾶν ὠφέλειαν ἢ βλάβην*, &c. *the state and character of an idiot* (that is, of one that is a mere worldling;) *is, never to expect either good or evil from himself; but from things external only. The state and character of a philosopher is, to expect all good or evil from himself only*. Epict. Ench. Cap. 71.

**Verse 15.** *{but a prudent man looketh well to his going}* That is, with most: will not easily be led by talk or applause of flatterers, or others, who speak without ground; for their own ends. A wise man will take more care of his actions, than so. But may not this *his*, be referred to the talker as well, or seducer, included in, *every word?* Many such figurative expressions there be in Scripture: whereof we shall have one manifest example in ver. 26. as also chapter 19:23. And accordingly here the words may be supplied: *The simple believeth every man that speaketh: but the prudent*, &c. that is; but he that is wise, will observe the actions, the life and conversation of those that seek to persuade him to such and such things. According to our Savior’s rule: *by their fruit ye shall know them*, Matt. 7:16.

**Verse 16.** *{but the fool rageth}* The Hebrew verb is here translated by divers others: *transgresseth*: or, *passeth by*: or, *passeth on*: as chapter 22:3. (repeated also chapter 27:12.) where we have the same sense in effect: *A prudent man foreseeth the evil, and hideth himself: but the simple pass on, and are punished*. It is the same verb in both places, but different conjugations. Either of these translations is warrantable enough, and makes no great difference in the sense.

**Verse 17.** *{a man of wicked devices is hated}* Men can bear with him that is rashly angry, but they hate him that devises mischief to others in cold blood: or, a man that conceals the mischief he contrives, is hated, 2 Sam. 13:22.

**Verse 18.** *{but the prudent are crowned with knowledge}* Knowledge is their crown: they get credit by it, and wealth too, under a just government. See before upon 10. 23. *but a man of understanding*.

**Verse 19.** *{The evil bow before the good}* Upon the former verse; and the reference there.

**Verse 20.** *{The poor is hated even of his own neighbour}* Chapter 19:7.

*{neighbour}* Or, *friend*.

*{the rich hath many friends}* Heb. *many are the lovers of the rich*.

**Verse 21.** *{He that despiseth his neighbour, sinneth}* He that will not give to the poor, shall be punished as a sinner, although he take nothing from them, Or because it is the propriety and privilege of charity, *to cover a multitude of sins*, Jam. 5. last verse: and 1 Pet. 4:8. *he that despiseth his neighbour sinneth*, may be also (and is by some) interpreted, shall be found a sinner: all his sins shall be reckoned to him, and he shall be punished without any mitigation. So 1 Kings 1:21. *I and my sons shall be sinners*, that is, shall be proceeded against as offenders: sin will be imputed unto us, &c.

*{but he that hath mercy on the poor, happy is he}* Ps. 112:9. See also on Ps. 41:1. *Blessed is he*.

**Verse 22.** *{Do they not err that devise evil}* They that seek to hurt others, go out of the way of safety themselves, and perish miserably.

*{mercy and truth shall be to them that devise good}* God’s merciful promise shall be made good to them.

**Verse 23.** *{the talk of lips tendeth only to penury}* Idle prating, to which they are addicted, are seldom good for anything else, and bring themselves (besides other inconveniences) to much contempt. But of serious discourse, it was said before, *The lips of the righteous feed many*: and there is not anything more beneficial than speech, if rightly used.

**Verse 24.** *{The crown of the wise is their riches}* Rather, *A crown to the wise*, (that is, an ornament, because of the opportunity they have by them to make themselves known, and to do good:) than, *their crown* absolutely; which may be mistaken, as though riches were the thing, wherein their excellency, and chiefest commendation lies: which is not the meaning. In this sense, wisdom itself is the true crown of men; (Chapter 4:9.) whether rich or poor: but as riches, so a good wife, *a crown*: Chapter 12:4. and so many other things, that are outward, and accessory.

*{but the foolishness of fools is folly}* So Solomon says; A fool is a fool, though he be never so rich. Riches of themselves, can never cure, nor cover his folly; but rather increase, and reveal it. It stands with reason it should be so; since *ἀνόητον ὁ πλοῦτος ἐστὶ*; (as Aristotle observes in his Rhetorics) wealth of itself, is more apt to impair wit, than to increase it: and, *bonae mentis soror paupertas*; prudence and poverty, are reputed of a kind. However, through vulgar men’s baseness, whose hearts and affections are set wholly to the world, and think nothing worthy to be looked after, but wealth and riches; let a man be never so foolish, or so wicked, he shall be told that he is wise and religious. The world is full of such dunghill worshippers: which made the poet say, and in his sense, truly enough; *Stultitiam patiuntur opes*; that it is a rich man’s privilege to be a fool, and yet to be reputed wise, Yet Solomon here in the right, nevertheless, that however men alter their names, they cannot alter the nature of things: a fool will be found a fool, and a wicked man, a wicked man; when that time shall come, prophesied of by the prophet Esay, that *the vile person shall be no more called liberal, nor the churl said to be bountiful*, Isa. 32:5.

**Verse 25.** *{A true witness delivereth souls, but a deceitful witness speaketh lies}* **Verse 5.**

*{A true witness delivereth souls}* Saves men by witnessing the truth; whereas false witnesses destroy themselves and others.

*{souls}* Or, *lives*.

**Verse 26.** *{and his children shall have a place of refuge}* God’s children, or the children of him that fears God, Ps. 115:13, 14. Though the sense it be all one, whether we read it, *his children*, that is, God’s: or, his, that fears God: yet according to the best learned in the language, the tenor of the original Hebrew words does import the latter: so that the words fully set out, will be: *God* or, *The Lord is a strong confidence to him that feareth him, and his children*, (or the children of such) &c. See also upon ver. 15. *but a prudent man*, &c.

**Verse 27.** *{The fear of the Lord is a fountain of life, to depart from the snares of death}* Chapter 13:14.

**Verse 28.** *{In the multitude of people is the King’s honour}* That is, strength; as appears by the opposition: for a king without strength has neither honor nor safety.

*{is the destruction of a Prince}* Hebr. *מחית רזון* which (because *רזון* in the singular, is not found elsewhere, to signify *a prince*; but *leanness* only, as Ps. 106:15. and elsewhere) is by some, (as by Mercer, for one) translated *attritio maciei*; that is, *consumption of leanness*; by others, *timor tenuitatis*; or to that purpose. It is most likely that Solomon intended by that word *a Prince*, (according to the exposition of most rabbins) and chose rather to say *rason*; than *rosen*, (the plural whereof *rosenim*, for *principes*, is ordinary) of purpose, to allude to *rason*, as it signifies leanness; as intimating thereby that that prince which is without a people, is, (in outward estimation) more like to the carcass, or the *exuviae* of a prince, than the reality. See upon Ps. 106:15. *but sent leanness into their souls*, of another allusion to the same word: and upon Ps. 58:1. *O congregation*.

**Verse 29.** *{He that is slow to wrath, is of great understanding}, &c.* To overcome wrath and passion, with the spirit of meekness and patience, is true wisdom indeed, and a great conquest: whereof see more upon Chapter 16:32. But to be able to repress wrath, or rather the effects of wrath for a while, and to lay it up against an opportunity, as it gives men great advantage to do mischief: so it argues a most devilish temper and disposition. Such anger the Grecians call *κότος* properly, and they make it the worst kind of anger. Whereas choleric angry men, though they show more weakness, and expose themselves to more danger; yet they are commonly the best natures, that are so, who, as soon angry, so soon pacified. *bonorum animi irritabiles*; is a common observation. Seneca goes further; *alia animi mala ad pessimos quosque pertinere; iracundiam eruditis hominibus, et in alia sanis, irripere: adeo ut quidam simplicitatis* (of an ingenuous mind, void of dissimulation or hypocrisy) *indicium iracundiam dicant; et vulgo credatur, facillimus quisque huic obnoxius.*

*{he that is hasty of spirit exalteth folly}* Shows it to all, even as things set on high are seen far.

*{hasty of spirit}* Heb. *short of spirit*.

**Verse 30.** *{A sound heart is the life of the flesh}* A heart free from passions, preserves the health of the body.

*{envy the rottenness of the bones}* Ps. 111:10.

**Verse 31.** *{He that oppresseth the poor, reproacheth his maker}* Chapter 17:5. Matt. 25:40.

*{reproacheth his maker}* His own maker, or the maker of the poor: for God made him, and in that state, not to be despised, but to be relieved.

*{his maker}* God is *the maker of the rich*; and of *the poor*: chapter 22:2. Nor creator of their persons only, which we know he is; but not indeed here; but of their different fortunes: as he has appointed the one, so the other also: *The Lord maketh poor, and maketh rich: bringeth low and lifteth up*. 1 Sam. 2:7. Therefore, says Homer in a place, it is not lawful for me, to despise you: (*ὦ ξεῖνε*:) *πρὸς γὰρ Διός εἰσιν ἅπαντες Ξεῖνοί τε πτωχοί τε*: *because all strangers and beggars*, (or *poor*:) *are from God*. But Hesiod yet more pertinently and expressly to Solomon’s words *Μηδέ ποτ᾽ οὐλομένην πενίην θυμοφθόρον ἀνδρὶ Τέτλαθ᾽ ὀνειδίζειν*, *μακάρων δόσιν αἰὲν ἐόντων*: that is: *Take heed of ever upbraiding any man for his* (I leave out some poetical epithets, that are not to the purpose:) *poverty; because it is the gift of the Gods*.

*{but he that honoureth him, hath}* Or, *but he that honoureth him hath*.

**Verse 32.** *{driven away in}* Or, *for*. See upon Ps. 1:4. *like the chaff*. And chapter 10:3. *the substance of the wicked*.

*{hath hope in his death}* Job 13:15. & 19. 26. Ps. 23:4. 2 Cor. 1:9. See upon Chapter 10:28. *The hope of the righteous*, &c.

**Verse 33.** *{Wisdom resteth in the heart of him that hath understanding; but that which is in the midst}, &c.* Laying aside other interpretations, that which both Mercer, and Buxtorfe, from some learned rabbins commend unto us, (and the only that I can think probable,) is this; One that is truly wise and learned, can conceal his wisdom, but where he sees occasion to use it: he is not ambitious to make show of it. Whereas a fool that has a smack of learning and knowledge, in all places, and upon all occasions, out with it he must, or he is ready to burst. See before upon chapter 12:23. *A prudent man concealeth knowledge*, &c. and upon chapter 18:2. *A fool hath no delight in understanding, but that his heart may discover itself*. Again, *Wisdom resteth*, &c. that is, A wise man will well consider of a matter, before he deliver his opinion; and rather hold his peace in company, though among many inferior to him in learning and wisdom, than to utter anything rashly: But a fool’s bolt is soon shot. The more ignorant every man is, the more bold commonly and confident. *Ἀμαθία θράσος*, *παιδεία δ᾽ ὄκνον φέρει*; as the wise Grecians of old. *The heart of the righteous studieth to answer; but the mouth of the wicked poureth out evil things*, Chapter 15:28.

**Verse 34.** *{Righteousness exalteth a nation}* A mean people grows great and honorable by justice, whereas a great nation is ruined by injustice, and suffering sin to be unpunished.

*{but sin is a reproach to any people}* There be two words in the original text, of different significations; which cause great difference of interpretations. The first, *חסד* which ordinarily does signify *mercy*; and sometimes (though it be denied by some) *reproach*. The second, *חטאת* which as it signifies *sin*; so also, *sacrifice for sin*, very frequently. As therefore from the second notion of the first word, and the first, of the second, this interpretation we have here, does arise; so from the first, and second, this; as naturally: *Et misericordia populorum [est velut] sacrificium pro peccato.* That is: *And the mercy of people* (or *nations*:) *is as it were a sacrifice for sin*. And for the probability of this sense in reference to the context, or parallel place; it has as much as can be required. Chapter 16:6. *By mercy (חסד) and truth, iniquity is purged*. Chapter 20:28. *Mercy and truth preserve the King: and the throne is upheld by mercy*. Chapter 21:21. *He that followeth after righteousness and mercy* (the very words of this verse) *findeth life, righteousness and honour*. See also upon Chapter 10:2. *Treasures of wickedness*, at the end of the observation there, out of Daniel, &c. And that which seems to commend this interpretation unto us not a little; is that (as before intimated) it is not very certain, that *חסד* does signify *reproach* properly, anywhere in the Scripture, if this one place be controverted, or excepted. Lev. 20:17. *a wicked thing* in matter of lust, indeed; but upon another consideration; as *קדש* a holy word of itself, transferred to persons most abominable. However, it is certain that the verb is so used, not only by rabbins, but even in the Scripture, in this very book, chapter 25:10. so that I would not rely much upon this. Let them both pass, (as well they may) for probable interpretations, and let the reader make his choice. See also upon chapter 16:6. *By mercy and truth*.

*{to any people}* Heb. *to nations*.

**Verse 35.** *{his wrath is against him that causeth shame}* By doing the King’s business foolishly, whereas he that does it wisely, gets the King credit, Isa. 22:18.

## CHAPTER 15

**Verse 1.** *{A soft answer turneth away wrath}* Chapter 25:15.

**Verse 2.** *{but the mouth of fools poureth out foolishness}* **Verse 28.** Chapter 12:23. & 13. 16.

*{poureth}* Heb. *belcheth*, or *bubbleth*.

**Verse 3.** *{The eyes of the Lord are in every place, beholding the evil and the good}* Job 34:21. Chapter 5:21. Jer. 16:17. & 32. 19. Heb. 4:13.

**Verse 4.** *{A wholesome tongue}* Heb. *the healing of the tongue*.

*{a tree of life}* See before upon Chapter 3:18. *a tree of life*.

*{a breach in the spirit}* The original word, here translated *spirit*, signifies also *the wind*: as Ps. 48:7. *Thou breakest the ships of Tarshish with an East wind*: where also we have the same word for *breaking*, as is here. Hereupon some rabbins and Christians, (as Junius, Piscator, and others:) express the sense of the words to this effect; *is a breach by wind*: understanding the eastern wind, proverbially used to express a vehement sudden breach, or scattering, as Jer. 18:17. *I will scatter them*: &c. But I conceive the former translation, the more emphatical of the two, and *breach in the spirit*, more directly to be opposed to *a tree of life*, in the former member; than, *a breach by wind*. If a man would indulge to his fancy, he might (embracing that translation, approved by many, *a breach by wind*:) think that Solomon might allude to the nature of words, which physically are but wind; as we say, commonly: but I will not give myself so much liberty.

**Verse 5.** *{A fool despiseth his father’s instruction}* Chapter 10:1.

*{instruction}* Or, *rebuke*.

*{but he that regardeth reproof, is prudent}* Chapter 13:18.

**Verse 6.** *{In the house of the righteous is much treasure}* *Godliness with contentment is great gain*, 1 Tim. 6:6.

*{but in the revenues of the wicked is trouble}* A wicked man may get much wealth, but he has much care and trouble with it, because God’s blessing goes not with it, Ps. 127:2. whereas a good man’s treasure may be his strength, and without trouble, through God’s blessing. See 1 Tim. 6:10. *and pierced themselves through with many sorrows*. It is not always so; some wicked wealthy men enjoy themselves (as to the world) and their plenty, quietly enough, even to the last. But it has often been said, that no moral sentence, or observation, can be absolute among men, by reason of the variety of circumstances, the perfect knowledge whereof is above the reach of any man. We shall only say: 1. Some may be accounted wicked among men, (especially when men of different parts do judge) which are not so in the eyes of God: as on the contrary. Which made the apostle, to refer all judgments, concerning men, to the great day, when the hidden counsels of every heart shall be made manifest: 1 Cor. 4:5. Secondly, God does sometimes reward wicked men, whom he has used to accomplish his will, in some things, with temporal blessings: Ps. 17:13, 14. But of temporal blessings, how to be understood by Christians, &c. see upon Ps. 37:1. *Fret not thyself*, &c. and before, upon chapter 3:18. *She is a tree*, and other places, to which reference has often been made.

**Verse 7.** *{disperse knowledge}* Scatter it abroad in hope of increase, as husbandmen do their seed. Ps. 112:9.

*{doth not so}* Or, *is not right*. So Junius, and divers others, making a noun, of that which, more usually is an adverb.

**Verse 8.** *{The sacrifice of the wicked is an abomination to the Lord}* Chapter 21:27. Isa. 1:11. and 66. 3. Jer. 6. 20. 7. and 22. Amos 5:22. That thing is abominable before God, which the wicked think to be most excellent, and whereby they think to be most accepted: For they pray solemnly with sacrifice, God hates it: but he accepts the bare prayers of the godly, without sacrifice.

**Verse 9.** *{that followeth after righteousness}* That follows eagerly after it, Chapter 21:21. 1 Tim. 6:11. Heb. 12:14.

**Verse 10.** *{Correction}* Or, *Instruction*.

*{correction is grievous unto him}* Or, *A grievous correction shall befall him*.

*{Correction is grievous unto him that forsaketh the way}* He that swerves in his whole course from God’s Word, cannot endure to be reproved.

*{he that hateth reproof shall die}* Chapter 5:12. &c. & 10. 17.

**Verse 11.** *{Hell and destruction are before the Lord}* Job 26:6. Ps. 139:8. There is nothing so deep, or secret, that can be hid from the eyes of God: he knows the souls in hell, and the bodies in the grave, and much more men’s thoughts here. Of the word *שאול* how taken commonly among the Jews of those days. See before upon chapter 9:18. *the depths of hell*: and the reference there, and upon verse 24. *hell beneath*.

**Verse 12.** *{one that reproveth him}* Or, *that one should reprove him*.

**Verse 13.** *{A merry heart maketh a cheerful countenance}* Chapter 17:22.

*{but by sorrow of the heart, the spirit is broken}* Chapter 12:25.

*{the spirit is broken}* So much, that a man’s grief appears in his face.

**Verse 14.** *{the mouth of fools feedeth on foolishness}* Their heart receives foolishness, as their mouth receives meat; for all things are nourished by their like.

*{the heart of fools}* Here, of fools; *the mouth* of the wise, *the heart*: and contrarily, verse 7. *the lips of the wise*; and, *the heart of fools*: promiscuously used. See before, upon chapter 20:20. *the heart of the wicked*. And therefore the speculations of some men here, and upon the seventh verse, must needs fall to the ground.

**Verse 15.** *{of the afflicted}* That vexes himself needlessly through his own peevishness, or other untowardness. We must not understand it of godly sorrow, or of the temporal afflictions of the godly: *For godly sorrow worketh repentance to salvation, but the sorrow of the world worketh death*. 2 Cor. 7:10. See also Ecclesiastes 7:2, 3, 4, 5. &c. Isa. 57:15.

**Verse 16.** *{Better is little with the fear of the Lord, than great treasure, and trouble therewith}* Ps. 37:16. Chapter 16:8. 1 Tim. 6:6.

*{Better is little with the fear of the Lord}* Which usually is without trouble, as on the contrary great troubles attend the treasures of them that fear not God.

**Verse 17.** *{Better is a dinner of herbs where love is, than a stalled ox, and hatred therewith}* Chapter 17:1.

*{a dinner of herbs}* The word signifies, a traveler’s dinner, a short and sudden one without preparation.

**Verse 18.** *{A wrathful man stirreth up strifes}* Chapter 26:21. and 29. 22.

**Verse 19.** *{The way of the slothful man is as an hedge of thorns}* He ever finds some let or stay, and dares not go forward: *There is a Lion in his way*, Chapter 26:13. But if we compare with chapter 22:5. *Thorns and snares*, &c. & Hos. 2:6. *I will hedge up thy ways as thorns*, &c. it may be thought as probably, that this *hedge of thorns*, is not intended by Solomon of imaginary thorns, (as the *Lion*, chapter 22:13. and 26. 13.) in the fancy of the slothful only, but of real difficulties and perplexities, wherewith the slothful use to be entangled, and which they bring upon themselves through slothfulness and delay: I am much confirmed in this sense, by a saying of ancient Hesiod, to this very purpose: *Αἰεὶ δ᾽ ἀμβολιεργὸς ἀνὴρ ἄτῃσι παλαίει*. and so *made plain*; or, *raised up*, &c. in the second member, will better agree with this sense, than the former.

*{the slothful man}* To whom is opposed in the second member, *the righteous*. Most likely therefore, that the same is intended here, who chapter 22:5. is called *froward*. That all wickedness does proceed from slothfulness, ancient philosophers and fathers do maintain; and Matt. 25:26. *ὀκνηρὸς* and *πονηρός*: *wicked and slothful*, are joined together. But of this we have treated elsewhere more at large.

*{hedge of thorns}* Hos. 2:6. and Mic. 7:4.

*{but the way of the righteous}* Then a slothful man is not righteous, nor a righteous man slothful.

*{is made plain}* Heb. *is raised up as a causey*.

**Verse 20.** *{A wise son maketh a glad father}* Chapter 10:1. See there.

*{a foolish man despiseth his mother}* And so grieves her: as on the contrary, the father is made glad by honoring him.

**Verse 21.** *{Folly is joy to him that is destitute of wisdom}* Chapter 10:23. As fools rejoicing in evil live wickedly: so wise men rejoicing in goodness live uprightly.

*{destitute of wisdom}* Heb. *void of heart*.

**Verse 22.** *{Without counsel, purposes are disappointed: but in the multitude of counsellors they are established}* Or, *Break purposes without counsel: for in the multitude of counsellors thou shalt be established*.

*{Without counsel, purposes are disappointed}* Things rashly undertaken, are blown away like chaff. Chapter 11:14. See there. However, the word here is *סוד* more properly *secret counsel*: and such should state-counsel be. Want of secrecy, has been the overthrow of many brave purposes.

*{counsellors they are established}* Heb. *it is established, purposes is established*, that is, according to the Hebrew idiom, every one of them, and so, frequently.

**Verse 23.** *{by the answer of his mouth}* Answer is put for speech, though no man speak before, as it is often in the Old and New Testament. See more upon verse 28.

*{a word spoken in due season, how good is it}* Chapter 25:11. If we will that our talk be comfortable, we must wait for a seasonable time.

*{in due season}* Heb. *in his season*.

**Verse 24.** *{The way of life is above to the wise, that he may depart from hell beneath}* Phil. 3:20. Col. 3:1, 2. Those sentences, and sayings are accounted most witty and elegant, (which Quintilian calls *ἐξ ἀπροσδοκήτου*,) that are grounded upon some common speech, but taken in a contrary sense; or contrarily applied to vulgar apprehension: as, *Shut thine eyes, that thou mayest see*, (the Arabs have a proverb to that purpose,) to wit, the eyes of your body, that you may see with the eyes of your soul; which is the true light of man. *Cast thy bread upon the waters*, Ecclesiastes 11:1.) *for thou shalt find it*, &c. a thing contrary to ordinary sense. And therefore the Grecians, of cost ill bestowed, and as good as lost, (as when a man is bountiful to unthankful men,) used to say, *σπείρειν ἐν πόντῳ*: *to sow upon the water*, or *sea*. But the meaning is, Lose it, (your seed, or bread,) as to men; that you may receive it back from God, who is best able to repay you. So is it here in this divine sentence, wherein the acuteness of the expression seems to strive with the excellency of the matter. The Grecians, of one that did not look to his way in going, were wont to say, that he did *ἄνω βλέπειν*: that is, *look up*, as indeed, if a man be in his way, alone, and keep his eyes upwards, he will hardly avoid to fall into some ditch, or stumble dangerously at some block, or the like. And there is a common story, by Plato, and divers others recorded at large, of one Thales, a philosopher, who being in his way, and keeping his eyes fixed (for observation, being a good astronomer) upon the heavens, as he went along, fell into a well; for which he was prettily handsomely flouted by a malapert bondslave of his own, which came to draw him out. *The way of life*, says Solomon; that is, The way that leads unto life, or happiness; *is above to the wise*: whilst he does not only lift up his eyes often to heaven, but also and chiefly, whilst he fixes his thoughts and his heart there: by which he may be thought so much the more to expose his body to dangers that are from below. There may be an *ἀπροσδόκητον* in the words, in another respect too: if he should (in the literal immediate sense) say, that the best way to avoid precipices, is to go aloft, which is contrary to common sense; the surest way to avoid a fall, whereby our lives may be in danger, being to keep the lowest way: according to that of the poet, *— tolluntur in altum, ut lapsu graviore ruant*: and, *Qui jacet in terra non habet unde cadat.* Now for the mystical, or allegorical sense, we are referred to Phil. 3:20. Col. 3:1, 2. which certainly was Solomon’s aim. I make no question but that by *the way of life*, he intended, as present, so future happiness too: but whether by *שאול*, which sometimes signifies a pit, or the grave, or the inferior parts of the earth, he intended that which we now call *hell*; or only destruction in general; may be doubted. It is more likely that he intended destruction in general. Learned Grotius his paraphrase upon the words, is; *Homo sapiens in omnibus actionibus suis Deum respicit; ut a periculo servetur.* Of the word *שאול* see more upon chap. 9. last verse: *the depths of hell*. It is well known, that many Jews and rabbins have been of opinion, that the godly only should arise, and live: and that the souls of other men, infidels and sinners, did utterly perish, as brutes: opposed by Christ, John 5:28, 29. And was not this a thing, by all sober men (though some that live like brutes, would be glad of the end of brutes, and that there were nothing beyond death:) to be dreaded, to be utterly extinct and annihilated; when others should enjoy life and immortality? It does not follow therefore, that if *שאול* were dreaded after death; it was therefore known, as a place of torment, by them that dreaded it. What therefore is grounded by some men upon these words, I do not apprehend to be of much validity.

**Verse 25.** *{The Lord will destroy the house of the proud}* Chapter 12:7. & 14. 11.

*{he will establish the border of the widow}* Who has no cause to be proud. And therefore the word widow is used proverbially sometimes, to denote men of very low condition, and dejected spirits. Ps. 107:40, 41. *He poureth contempt upon princes*: etc. where the poor is opposed to princes (lawless and tyrannical) as the widow here to the proud in general.

**Verse 26.** *{The thoughts of the wicked are an abomination to the Lord}* Chapter 6:18. Wicked men's thoughts and words displease God; good men's please him.

*{but [the words] of the pure}* Or, *but to the pure*.

*{pleasant words}* Heb. *words of pleasantness*.

**Verse 27.** *{He that is greedy of gain, troubleth his own house}* Hinders the comfort of himself and his: whereas, he that hates bribes, so that he will not receive them offered, much less ask them, he and his shall live comfortably.

*{troubleth his own house}* Chapter 11:29.

**Verse 28.** *{The heart of the righteous studieth to answer:}* Every man that has some knowledge of the Hebrew, or is acquainted with Scripture phrase of either Old, or New Testament, must needs know, that *to answer*, is as much, often, as to speak: and might have done in the text, (I think) better, than, *to answer*: except those that put it there, had thought themselves bound to keep all Hebraisms: which we know they do not. *Studieth then to answer*, is as much as, will premeditate when he is to speak. It seems that, that inspiration (as they call it: that is, *quicquid in buccam*: whether sense, or nonsense:) was not in use, or not well thought of, in those days: especially upon sacred occasions: Eccles. 5:2. *Be not rash with thy mouth*, etc. what would become of many a man, in these days, that lives in good fashion, if nonsense, and vain prating were banished out of pulpits? But, *the people love to have it so*: Jer. 5:31. and so it is like to be, until God mends it. Yet we deny not, but there be some, who, by the advantage of some natural dispositions and long practice, are able to speak very well without premeditation if need be, which ability of theirs, deserves great commendation: but their modesty more, if in reverence to God, they avoid to put themselves to it, (whether in preaching, or praying) but when there is great need. See also chapter 16:23. *the heart of the wise.*

*{the mouth of the wicked poureth out evil things}* Rashly and abundantly, for lack of premeditation. See upon chapter 14:23. *Wisdom resteth*, etc.

**Verse 29.** *{The Lord is far from the wicked}* Ps. 34:16. God is far off from wicked men, and will not hear their prayers: but he is nigh to good men, and hears them quickly: therefore the former must not presume, nor the latter despair of the good success of their prayers. Eccles. 5:2.

*{he heareth the prayer of the righteous}* Ps. 145:18.

**Verse 30.** *{The light of the eyes rejoiceth the heart}* As light delights the eyes; so good news; or a good name joys the heart.

*{a good report}* See upon 25. 25.

*{report}* Heb. *hearing*. Chapter 12:15.

**Verse 31.** *{The ear that heareth the reproof of life, abideth among the wise}* **Verse 5.** That suffers himself to be admonished by God's word, that brings life, and so amends.

**Verse 32.** *{instruction}* Or, *correction*.

*{heareth}* Or, *obeyeth*.

*{getteth understanding}* Heb. *possesseth an heart*.

**Verse 33.** *{The fear of the Lord is the instruction of wisdom}* Job 28:28. See the references, upon the next words.

*{before honour is humility}* Meaning, that God uses to exalt them that are truly humble. See upon chapter 3:34. *For God res.* and chapter 11:2. *with the lowly*.

*{before honour is humility}* Chapter 18:12.

## CHAPTER 16

**Verse 1.** *{The preparations of the heart in man, and the answer of the tongue, is from the Lord}* Chapter 19:21. & 20. 24. Jer. 10:23. He derides the presumption of man who dare attribute to himself any thing, and yet can neither prepare his heart to speak, nor speak what he has prepared, without God's assistance: or, though man have never so well prepared himself, yet without God he cannot utter his conceptions: as verse 9. In divers of those sentences, the first part serves only to usher in the second, and to make the expression the more emphatical: and ought not therefore to be pressed: as a thing positively, or dogmatically, and absolutely delivered. So 19. 14. *House and riches are the inheritance of fathers, and* (or, *but*) *a prudent wife is from the Lord*. The intent is not, to deny that riches come from God, which is elsewhere often affirmed; but only to set out a good wife, as a special providence and gift of God, more considerable to man than riches are. See also upon Ps. 10:17. *thou wilt prepare their hearts*, etc.

*{preparations}* Or, *disposings*.

*{in man, and}* Or, *are of man, but*.
