**Verse 2.** *{All the ways of a man are clean in his own eyes}* Chapter 21:2. He shows hereby, that man flatters himself in his doings, calling that virtue which God calls vice, Ps. 36:2. Isa. 5:20.

*{the Lord weigheth the spirits}* God weighs men's thoughts in an even balance, and finds them too light, and their actions also, Dan. 5:27. *For I know nothing by myself, yet am I not hereby justified: but he that judgeth me, is the Lord*, 1 Cor. 4:4.

**Verse 3.** *{Commit thy works unto the Lord, and thy thoughts shall be established}* Ps. 37:5. & 55. 22. Matt. 6:25. Luke 12:22. 1 Pet. 5:7.

*{Commit}* Heb. *Roll*.

*{Commit thy works unto the Lord}* Those things that are too hard for you, trust God with them; as men use to lay their burdens on other men's shoulders, when they cannot bear them, and he will effect them according to your intention.

**Verse 4.** *{The Lord hath made all things for himself}* For his own glory. This is (has been I am sure) the most received interpretation: Junius, omnes, all men; not, all things; restraining it, (the sense of the words here; I mean:) to men only. The difference is not great. But exceptions be made against this translation of the text; and consequently, against the exposition, as grounded upon it. For the thing is not denied, that God has made all things for himself: but this denied to be the sense of the words in this place: Geierus, the last I have seen, that has written upon these Proverbs, (a man of great diligence, and good judgment, for the most part: a Lutheran:) is much against it. Two things are objected: first, the accent under *למענהו*: and secondly, the irregularity of the affix pronoun, if a preposition, according to our translation, be made of the words. These are niceties of grammar, which might be answered well enough. There be examples of far greater irregularities in many places, which no man sticks at. But the question will be: if the words will afford another more literal interpretation, (that is, without irregularities:) and that interpretation of the words, as good and warrantable sense, or matter: why not to be preferred? This we are now to consider of. The translation commended unto us as most literal, is: *Omne operatur Deus ad responsum ipsius*: that is, *God hath created all things to answer him*: or, to answer one another: for both are in the Hebrew affix: or, to speak more properly; the Hebrew affix pronoun is indifferent to either. The Chaldee paraphrase is for the first; *ut obtemperent ipsi*: that is, to obey him. There is no difficulty in this. But from the other translation, the sense that is made of it, is not so obvious: by Junius in his Scholia, (as I find him quoted: for I have them not:) it is so set out: *sapientissima vi & ordinatione Dei sic disponi omnia, ut singula antecedentia facta consequentibus*, etc. that is: *That all things by the great power and wisdom of God, are so ordered, that every antecedent deed* (or action) *and thought, doth answer to the consequent; and every consequent, to the antecedent.* I do not well know, what he intended: but I am sure that the Stoics anciently used the same words in the exposition of their fate, or destiny. Others more plainly, draw the words to that mutual harmony and consent of all natural things among themselves to their mutual preservation: observed by many, with admiration. But what then for the coherence? How comes in the wicked? *Malo culpæ permisso, respondet malum pœnæ immissum*: says Geierus, with some other words to that purpose. I must profess myself not much satisfied with any thing he has to this purpose, either of his own; or from others. I shall speak what may seem strange; yet no more, I am sure, than what will be made good, (if taken) in the most literal sense. Ancient Greek philosophers have written much more to the illustration of this sense, than has been done by any commentator: this answering of things to one another, they call most appositely, *τὴν τῶν πραγμάτων ἀντιστοιχίαν*. But that which is most strange, is, that they have upon this subject; that very instance Solomon has, of the wicked: which they say, (and take upon themselves to prove, though I will not undertake for the validity of their arguments:) was necessary to the world: yea so necessary, some say, that the world could not subsist without such. This is not a place to dilate upon philosophical speculations; neither have I the books by me. For indeed if we take this; (with some rabbins, and many Christians) to have been Solomon's meaning; why should it be thought more philosophical, than theological? But I have them not; at this time: neither, if I had, would I so much presume upon the reader's patience. Grotius is for this sense too. *Singula Deus facit (ordinat:) ad id quod singulis convenit. Etiam impium ad diem calamitosum, [ordinatur a Deo]* as exhibited by Geierus, where also his note is: *Nam ענה, ut & vox respondendi Latina; significat τὸ συνηχεῖν: ut Coh. 10:19.* So he. I think *ἀντιστοιχεῖν* had been better, and more suitable to the style of ancient philosophers: though *συστοιχεῖν* I know be the word used in the New Testament. So much may serve in this place.

*{yea even the wicked for the day of evil}* In whose condemnation he intends to glorify his justice, Job 21:30. Or rather, to be executioners of his wrath for the punishment of others: who therefore, Ps. 17:13, 14. are called *the sword*: and, *the hand of God*. See also Ps. 80:17. *Let thy hand be upon the man of thy right hand: upon the son of man, whom thou madest strong for thyself*: out of Esay and Hab. passages there to this purpose. See also upon Psalm 105:25. *He turned*, etc. the same author being as earnest upon this place also, to vindicate God from being the author of sin, or true cause of any evil, which some ignorantly from such places would infer. Thus, if we stick to the first and most received, I said, translation of the words of the first member. But if we like best of the second, as most literal, and approved of by eminent men; see what has been said there,

**Verse 5.** *{Every one that is proud in heart, is an abomination to the Lord}* Chapter 6:17. & 8. 13.

*{proud in heart}* Chapter 11:21. *wicked*. So, superbia, is used by the Latins for any particular vice; and for vices in general. Hence came the denomination of Tarquinius Superbus; by which they intended, the wicked and tyrannical, or unjust; as by many has been observed.

*{though hand join in hand}* See upon chapter 11:21. *Though hand*, etc.

*{unpunished}* Heb. *held innocent*.

**Verse 6.** *{By mercy and truth iniquity is purged;} etc.* Or; *Where mercy and truth is, iniquity is purged; and where the fear of God* shows itself by *departing from evil*: that is, piety is a sure token of remission of sins, and not sacrifices, without it. See also Matt. 23:23. *the weightier matters of the law, judgment, mercy, and faith*, etc. This I conceive to be the aim of Solomon in these words, tacitly to reprove their error, who did think, (which was the opinion of many both Jews and Gentiles:) that they could purge or expiate any sin by sacrifices, and oblations: and whilst they acquitted themselves of the duty, (and perchance above duty) with care and conscience; were careless otherwise, of their lives and conversations. Some would have the immediate aim of the words to be, to commend bounty, and charity to the poor; and would have chapter 10:12. *Hatred stirreth up strifes: but love covereth all sins*: (as they interpret it: see there:) to be parallel to this. Of that sense and subject see upon the second verse of that chapter, *Treasures of wickedness*, etc. But I do not believe either those words there, or yet the sense they would make of the words, to belong to these. I am glad I have Mercerus on my side. And I am much confirmed in this opinion by the second member, the following words: *and by the fear*, etc. See there.

*{and by the fear of the Lord men depart from evil}* By *the fear of the Lord*, according to the Hebrew phrase, religion in general, and God's worship, is understood. See upon Psalm 130:4. *but there is forgiveness with thee*, etc. And, *to fear*, is as much as, *to worship*, in many places. So Jer. 5:24. *Let us now fear the Lord, that giveth us rain*: and, *Fear the Lord, and his goodness.* Hos. 3. last verse. And I remember, that Steuchus Eubinus (a very learned man) does somewhere commend, (and deservedly) the Sept. (as we call them) for expressing (in some place) the Hebrew words, by *θεοσέβεια* in Greek, that is: *the worship of God*. Whereas therefore some men did take upon themselves to be very religious: great worshippers of God, because of their sacrifices, wherein they were not only punctual, but free: Solomon would have them know here, that true religion, (or, *fear of the Lord*) did not so properly consist in sacrifices (so the prophets, and David in many places we know:) as in *departing from evil*. Just as St. James, *Pure religion, and undefiled before God and the Father is this, To visit the fatherless and the widows in their affliction*; (there we have the effect of the first member:) *and to keep himself unspotted from the world*: that is, *to depart from evil*. James 1:27. So St. Paul (as to this second member) *Nevertheless the foundation standeth sure*, etc. *and let every one that nameth the name of the Lord*, (that is, professes to be religious; to fear God,) *depart from iniquity*. 2 Tim. 2:19.

**Verse 7.** *{When a man's ways please the Lord}* He that will enjoy the favor of men for good, must get God's first, Luke 2:54. See upon chapter 3:4. *So shalt thou find favour.*

**Verse 8.** *{Better is a little with righteousness}, etc.* Psalm 37:16. chapter 15:16. A little well gotten, is better than a great deal ill gotten, Psalm 37:16.

**Verse 9.** *{A man's heart deviseth his way}* **Verse 1.**

*{but the Lord directeth his steps}* See notes on verse 1.

**Verse 10.** *{A divine sentence}* Heb. *divination*.

*{A divine sentence is in the lips of the king}* Divine sentences and sayings become them best that are in greatest authority. See before in the preface, and upon chapter 1:1. *The proverbs.* Or, *divination*, as the word does properly import: of which sense, how *divination* may be ascribed to kings and princes more particularly than to other men, see learned Grotius his notes upon John 11:54. *And this spake he not of himself, but being high priest that year, he prophesied*, etc. Add this pregnant passage of Cicero, omitted (I wonder) by Grotius. *Veteres qui rerum potiebantur, iidem auguria tenebant: ut enim sapere, sic divinare regale ducebant*, etc.

Or if we understand it rather, of a certain discerning power and faculty, wherewith God does endow many kings and princes, in matters of judgment, and the like, above other men; we have an example of it in King Solomon himself, 1 Kings 3:24. etc. and other like examples of other kings also, are there produced by the same Grotius in his annotations upon that place: to which divers others might be added out of ancient and later histories. So of David, 2 Sam. 14:17. *for as an angel of God, so is my lord the king, to discern good and bad*: and again, verse 20. *and my lord is wise, according to the wisdom of an angel of God, to know all things that are in the earth*. *קסם* properly, is such *divination*, as is forbidden by the law, Deut. 18:10, etc. but here it seems to be taken more generally; as divers other words of the like kind are. So that here is no warrant for any kind of divination, or astrology, which is unlawful and forbidden. See upon Psalm 58:5. *to the voice of charmers, charming never so wisely.*

**Verse 11.** *{A just weight and balance are the Lord's}* Lev. 19:36. chapter 11:1.

*{all the weights}* Heb. *all the stones*.

*{all the weights of the bag are his work}* If they be true and just, they are God's work, and he delights therein: but if they be false, they are the work of the devil, and serve to their condemnation that use them.

**Verse 12.** *{It is an abomination to kings to commit wickedness}* Wise kings hate injustice, knowing it will overthrow their thrones.

*{for}* Or, *but*.

**Verse 13.** *{Righteous lips are the delight of kings}* Chapter 14:35. & 22. 11. Many things are absolutely delivered in the Scripture, which must be understood, rather, of what should be, or usually happens, than what is actually, or necessarily must be. So 1 Cor. 6:19. *Know ye not that your body is the temple of the Holy Ghost*: and 7. 32. etc. *He that is unmarried careth for the things that belong to the Lord, how he may please the Lord*; and the like.

**Verse 14.** *{The wrath of a king is as messengers of death}* Chapter 20:2. That is, he finds out many means to execute his wrath.

*{a wise man will pacify it}* For there is no resisting it.

**Verse 15.** *{In the light of the king's countenance is life}* The king's cheerful countenance rejoices the subjects, like sunshine; and enriches them like the latter rain.

*{his favour as a cloud of the latter rain}* Chapter 19:12.

**Verse 16.** *{How much better is it to get wisdom, than gold}* Chapter 8:11.

**Verse 17.** *{The high way of the upright, is to depart from evil: he that keepeth his way}* It may be Solomon does allude to travelers, who are safest, when they keep the road. So literally, *to keep his way*, may be understood; but otherwise, to keep his way; that is: to take care: *to walk circumspectly*, to make a conscience, etc.

**Verse 18.** *{Pride goeth before destruction}* Chapter 11:2. & 18. 12. Men's estates are like earthenware, the higher they are lifted, the greater is their fall and breaking. Pride makes many break in our days, chapter 17:19.

**Verse 19.** *{Better is it to be of an humble spirit with the lowly}* Humility, the best of gifts; as pride, the worst of vices, and most incompatible with true religion. *For God giveth grace to the humble*, etc. James 4:6. and *every one that exalteth himself*, etc. Luke 18:14. and *God dwelleth with the humble*, Isa. 57:15.

*{than to divide the spoil with the proud}* A proverbial expression of highest worldly joy and exultation. *I rejoice at thy word, as one that findeth great spoil*, Psalm 119:162. See upon Psalm 76:4. *than the mountains of prey.*

**Verse 20.** *{He that handleth a matter}* Or, *he that understandeth a matter*.

*{He that handleth a matter wisely shall find good}* He means, that he that will bring his affairs to a good end, must have understanding in his business, and trust in God. Or thus; it is good and commendable for a man when he goes about any business, to use deliberation, and to make use of his best discretion concerning the carriage of it: there is the better hopes of a good issue, when that is done. But nevertheless, let no man trust to his own wisdom and discretion, but put his trust in God, and depend of him for the event: which for a man to do in all things, is chiefest happiness.

*{trusteth in the Lord, happy is he}* Psalm 2:12. & 34. 8. & 125. 1. Isaiah 30:18. Jer. 17:7.

**Verse 21.** *{shall be called prudent}* Shall attain to the reputation of a prudent wise man: and by the effects, he shall approve himself so. *Shall be called*, may be also interpreted, shall be, or become: as often, in the Scripture. He that affects wisdom in his heart, and applies himself to the study of it, shall in time attain to it: as before, often.

*{the sweetness of the lips increaseth learning}* Wisdom joined with eloquence, does much good.

**Verse 22.** *{Understanding is a wellspring of life unto him that hath it}* Chapter 13:14.

*{him that hath it}* Heb. *the owners of it*.

*{the instruction of fools is folly}* Either that which the wicked teach others; or else, it is folly to teach willful fools.

**Verse 23.** *{teacheth}* Heb. *maketh wise*. A wise man will consider of his words: nothing shall come out of his mouth, but what has first been weighed in his heart. See before, 15. 28. and upon 14. 33. *Wisdom resteth*, etc. and upon chapter 15:28. *The heart of the right*, *the heart of fools is in their mouth: but the mouth of the wise is in their heart*, Ecclesiasticus 21:26. Or, if any does affect to speak well, (as anciently it was the readiest way, to wealth and honor) let him first take the care to store his heart with good literature. For, *scribendi recte*, (there is the same reason for *dicendi* too:) *sapere est principium, & fons*; as a learned poet tells us: but a better master, (to the same purpose) that, *Out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaketh.* Matt. 12:34.

*{and addeth learning to his lips}* To himself or others.

*{to}* Or, *by*.

**Verse 24.** *{Pleasant words are as an honeycomb.}* Chapter 12:25.

*{sweet to the soul}* Pleasant and profitable.

**Verse 25.** *{There is a way that seemeth right unto a man}* Chapter 14:12.

**Verse 26.** *{He that laboureth}* Heb. *the soul of him that laboureth*.

*{craveth it of him}* Heb. *boweth unto him*. *All the labour of a man is for his mouth; and yet the appetite is not filled*, Eccles. 6:7. We say commonly (and somewhat answerable to what all languages have) take pains whilst you can, (or, are young:) and it will be your own one day. And if we go no further than so, it is a very good encouragement to industry, that ourselves shall reap the benefit of our labors; (if it fall out otherwise sometimes, that is nothing to the purpose, as has been often observed:) nay, that we cannot maintain life otherwise, for which we think no ransom too great. *All that a man hath will he give for his life.* Job 2:4. They are Satan's words there: but we have better authority for it, if need were. Though this may serve, yet we may extend it further; that as we refuse no labor to maintain this life; that is in effect, for the body: so should we take pains as willingly for a better life, the proper life of the soul: *Labour not for the meat which perisheth*, etc. John 6:27. that is: if for that, much more for the other. And, *If thou be wise, thou shalt be wise for thyself*: chapter 9:12. We shall reap the fruits (though not externally visible:) of those labors too, that we take for our souls: *Our labour is not in vain*: 1 Cor. 15:58. Yet there be, who make a far distinct sense of this whole verse, *Qui molestus est, molestus est sibi: nam reflectitur in ipsum os ejus.* So Junius: not he alone, I know, some rabbins go that way too: and most Lutherans. The sense is very good so too: very well expressed by old Hesiod. *Οἷ αὐτῷ κακὰ τεύχει ἀνὴρ ἄλλῳ κακὰ τεύχων*: *Ἡ δὲ κακὴ βουλὴ τῷ βουλεύσαντι κακίστη*. We need no other paraphrase to Solomon's words. If he had intended it, I know not how he could have done it better. And I should sooner be induced to believe that Solomon's words are to be taken in this sense; by this parallel place of that ancient poet; (*He that contriveth mischief to another, doth contrive it to himself: Evil counsel will light upon the head of the counsellor*; what can be more verbative?) than by that reason which is given by some, because say they, by the former, *non reconditum quid videtur proferri*; etc. no very hidden, or mysterious thing seems to be spoken, but what every body knows, etc. Indeed, by this rule, every man taking to himself the liberty of his own judgment; we shall not leave perchance to Solomon many proverbs; or shall make him say what we would have him; not, what he intended.

**Verse 27.** *{An ungodly man}* Heb. *A man of Belial*, 2 Sam. 20:1.

*{in his lips there is a burning fire}* For he consumes himself and others. See James 3:6. *And the tongue is a fire*, etc.

**Verse 28.** *{A froward man soweth strife}* Chapter 6:14, 19. & 15. 18. & 26. 21. & 29. 22.

*{soweth}* Heb. *sendeth forth*.

*{a whisperer separateth chief friends}* Heb. *a guide*. One that is of our intimate counsel, Psalm 55:13. Mic. 7:5. See more of the original word, upon chapter 2:17. *the guide of her youth*.

**Verse 29.** *{leadeth him into the way that is not good}* Either to undo him, or to make him join with him in undoing others. See chapter 1:10, 11, etc.

**Verse 30.** *{He shutteth his eyes to devise froward things}* The Hebrew word may be translated also, *He winketh*: as by Junius and Buxtorfius. But however translated, that winking, and such like gesticulations are intended, is very probable: of which see before upon chapter 6:13. *He winketh with his eyes*. Some would have this *shutting of the eyes* to be referred to the intention of the mind, which then is most itself, when not distracted by external objects; to which the shutting of the eyes does not a little conduce; as if he should say, with his whole endeavor he labors to bring his wickedness to pass.

**Verse 31.** *{if it be found in the way of righteousness}* Old men that fear God ought to be reverenced, Lev. 19:32. Or, *it is found*: that is, God, for the most part, gives old age to righteous men, as a reward, Psalm 91:16.

**Verse 32.** *{He that is slow to anger, is better than the mighty:}* It is the opinion of many that are not very wise, that anger, is an effect of a great spirit, and of magnanimity; whereas there is no anger (we speak not of moderate anger, upon a just provocation; an effect of nature:) but proceeds from weakness; and to indulge to passions, is always an argument of a base and cowardly disposition: to which therefore, as children, so women, (as the weaker sex,) are naturally more subject than men. The Latins therefore very properly, and naturally, express all passions, (anger especially,) by the word impotentia; and Solomon here uses these similitudes, to let us know how much more true generosity there is in meekness, and clemency, than in passion and revenge. Seneca also in one of his epistles, uses the same similitude; *Multa inveniuntur, qui ignem inferant urbibus, qui inexpugnabilia prostrent, & muros in miram altitudinem adductos, arietibus ac machinis quassent, etc. Nemo illis venientibus restitit, sed nec ipsi ambitioni, crudelitatique restiterunt.* But Cicero's words come yet nearer, *In omnibus sæculis pauciores viri reperti sunt, qui suas cupiditates, quam qui hostium copias vincerent.*

**Verse 33.** *{The lot is cast into the lap: but the whole disposing thereof is of the Lord}* See before verse 1. & 9. This is not appliable to trivial light occasions nor to such lots, as are not warranted by the word of God: nor inferences to be made, from such, of God's approbation, or rejection. The devil, by God's permission, and just judgment, may have the managing of some lots, for his own ends: yet God still said to have the disposing, because of his overruling power, when he pleases; and that nothing can be without his permission.

*{whole disposing}* Or, *every judgment* (or, *sentence*).

## CHAPTER 17

**Verse 1.** *{A dry morsel, and quietness therewith, than a house full of sacrifices with strife:}* Chapter 15:17.

*{than a house full of sacrifices with strife}* For where were many sacrifices, there were many portions given to the people, wherewith they feasted. Or, the word may signify cattle slain to eat, not to sacrifice. See before upon chapter 7:14. *I have peace offerings*, etc.

*{sacrifices}* Or, *good cheer*.

**Verse 2.** *{A wise servant shall have rule over a son that causeth shame}* Sometimes a wise and faithful servant is by the father set over slothful children, and has part of the inheritance with them.

**Verse 3.** *{The fining pot is for silver, and the furnace for gold}* Psalm 26:2. chapter 27:21. Jer. 17:10. Mal. 3:3.

*{but the Lord trieth the hearts}* Men can find out other things, but not other men's thoughts. Yea, it is a work for a man to know his own heart. Nothing more deceitful, and wherein men are more subject to be beguiled, than in judging of themselves. Without great diligence, and humility, and much seeking unto God by prayers, it is not to be done. Hence it is, that most men live so secure, because so insensible and ignorant of their condition: as has been observed before more than once. The words may be understood of trials by afflictions, by which God usually does use to cleanse and purify those, whom he has destinated to glory: *That the trial of your faith, being much more precious than of gold that perisheth*, etc. 1 Pet. 1:7. *that we may be partakers of his holiness*, Heb. 12:10.

**Verse 4.** *{a liar giveth ear to a naughty tongue}* No tongue so bad, but a liar will hearken to it.

*{naughty tongue}* Heb. *of naughtiness*; that is most naughty.

**Verse 5.** *{Whoso mocketh the poor, reproacheth his Maker}* Chapter 14:31. See there.

*{unpunished}* Heb. *held innocent*.

*{and he that is glad at calamities}* Whom the Grecians very properly call *ἐπιχαιρεκάκους*; and the vice itself, (rejoicing at the calamities of others,) in one word, *ἐπιχαιρεκακία*.

**Verse 6.** *{Children's children are the crown of old men}* Psalm 127:3. & 128. 3.

**Verse 7.** *{Excellent speech}* Heb. *a lip of excellency*.

*{Excellent speech becometh not a fool}* As eloquent speech shows a fool speaks not like himself; so a prince speaks much less like himself, if he speak falsely. See upon chapter 1:1. *The proverbs.*

*{excellent}* Or, *high, magnificent, lofty language*: such as fools, that think themselves wise, (especially if in some authority:) do principally affect.

*{lying lips}* Heb. *a lip of lying*.

**Verse 8.** *{A gift is as a precious stone in the eyes of him that hath it}* Chapter 18:16. Heb. *of the masters of it*. Which some refer to the giver; as if he intended, that rich men, with their money, presume they may do what they will. Others, to the taker, more properly. However, the sense is the same; and for the truth, we have daily experience.

*{a precious stone}* Heb. *a stone of grace*.

**Verse 9.** *{He that covereth a transgression, seeketh love}* Chapter 10:12. Concealing injuries done us, breeds peace; ripping them up, breeds discord among nearest friends.

*{separateth very friends}* See upon chapter 2:17. *the guide of her youth*.

*{seeketh}* Or, *procureth*.

**Verse 10.** *{A reproof entereth more into a wise man}, etc.* A wise man is more affected with, and mends sooner after a reproof, than a foolish person after many stripes.

*{A reproof entereth more into a wise man, than an hundred stripes into a fool}* Or, *a reproof aweth more a wise man, than to strike a fool an hundred times*.

**Verse 11.** *{therefore a cruel messenger shall be sent against him}* An hangman or executioner. *The wrath of a king is as messengers of death*, chapter 16:14. The Hebrew word *מלאך* may likewise be translated angelum; an angel: and so some rabbins understand it here; and think the same to be intended both here, and Psalm 78:49. Of which see more there.

**Verse 12.** *{Let a bear robbed of her whelps meet a man}* It is better to meet a she-bear, that falls on every one she meets, as if he had stolen her whelps, than to meet a wicked man in a violent passion.

*{a fool in his folly}* *A fool*, for, a wicked man: observed many times before. But by a fool here, we may also understand a great talker: for such, most fools are: so good an opinion they have of themselves usually. So Horace, Serm. lib. 1. sat. 9. Ibam forte via, etc. He does there very wittily and elegantly set it out, as death, yea a cruel violent death: (sub cultro, etc.) to fall into the hands of a great talker.

**Verse 13.** *{rewardeth evil for good, evil shall not depart from his house}* Rom. 12:17. 1 Thess. 5:15. 1 Pet. 3:9.

**Verse 14.** *{The beginning of strife is as when one letteth out water}* He that raises strife, knows not how to stay it, no more than he that opens a breach to let out waters to drown a country; therefore begin not to contend at any time.

**Verse 15.** *{He that justifieth the wicked, and he that condemneth the just}, etc.* Exod. 23:7. Chapter 24:24. Isa. 5:23.

*{even they both are abomination to the Lord}* Men think it mercy to favor wicked man in judgment, but God accounts it as bad as condemning an innocent.

**Verse 16.** *{Wherefore is there a price in the hand of a fool to get wisdom}* If a man have never so much opportunity and means, yet he will not use them, unless he have an heart desirous to learn. See upon chapter 18:1. *Through desire*,

*{no heart}* That is, *no desire*: as before. Or, no natural ability, without which all labor is lost. *Non ex quolibet ligno fit Mercurius.* Hence it is that Aristotle does ascribe so much to *εὐφυΐᾳ* (a good natural disposition) in his Ethics; see also chapter 27:22. *Though thou shouldest bray.*

**Verse 17.** *{A friend loveth at all times, and a brother is born for adversity}* Chapter 18:24.

*{a brother is born for adversity}* They are true friends, and kindred, that forsake us not in adversity.

**Verse 18.** *{A man void of understanding striketh hands}* Chapter 6:1. & 11. 15. See there.

*{understanding}* Heb. *heart*.

*{in the presence of his friend}* Openly, in the presence of the creditor, chapter 6:3.

*{friend}* Or, *neighbour*.

**Verse 19.** *{he that exalteth his gate, seeketh destruction}* He that makes him fairer gates than his neighbor, seeking to exalt himself above his degree, gives others occasion to seek his ruin. Broad gates break men many times. By high gates, we may also (with some rabbins) understand sumptuous houses and edifices, the very cost whereof undoes many, before they be up and finished; and the envy, more, when finished. Which made the poet say; *Auream quisquis mediocritatem Diligit, tutus caret obsoleti sordibus tecti: caret invidenda sobrius aula.* But the Hebrew word *פתחו* may also be expounded (as Mic. 7:5.) of the mouth; *He that maketh wide his mouth*: or, *He that speaketh big*. See Psalm 12:3. *to speak proud things: the Lord shall cut off—and the tongue that speaketh proud things*. And so among the Latins, Verba superba; used for an argument of great and dangerous pride: and *ἔπος μέγα*, among the Grecians, Solomon, by using this word common to words and edifices, might have a respect to both, to condemn pride, or affected greatness in both.

**Verse 20.** *{he that hath a froward heart}* Heb. *the froward of heart*.

**Verse 21.** *{He that begetteth a fool, doth it to his sorrow}* Chapter 10:1. See there.

*{that begetteth a fool}* *He that begetteth a fool*, and, *the father of a fool*; in the next member seem to be all one. Because of the certainty of the thing, they say. I know it was so in Pharaoh's dream: and there was great reason for it. But does not hold so in words, of things present and obvious. I should rather say, to make the speech more pathetical, and thereby, more operative. However, best writers, have some tautologies; so apparent in some, that they may be thought to have affected them. Neither were it altogether absurd, or improbable, if we said that by *ילד* here, though masculine, and joined with a masculine verb, the mother is principally understood. I could say more to that: but I would not be too grammatical.

**Verse 22.** *{A merry heart doth good like a medicine}* Chapter 15:13. & 12. 25. A cheerful heart helps well to recover health lost: but a sad one breeds deadly diseases.

*{like a medicine}* Or, *to a medicine*. Which sense seems to be most literal, because without any supplement. A cheerful heart helps the operation of physic, that it may do good to the body, which heaviness (so the rabbins, and Mercer out of them) does hinder. But it comes all to one.

**Verse 23.** *{A wicked man taketh a gift out of the bosom}* That is secretly, as it were out of the bosom of the rich.

**Verse 24.** *{Wisdom is before him that hath understanding, but the eyes of a fool are in the ends of the earth}* He seems to intend the same by this verse, as before chapter 14:6. *A scorner seeketh wisdom, and findeth it not: but knowledge is easy unto him that understandeth*, besides what he has to that purpose elsewhere. Yet some, by *before him*, understand by him, and conspicuous in his very countenance and carriage: and refer to Eccles. 2:14. & 8. 1. where the face is apparently spoken of. But I think the first interpretation is the most genuine. See upon the next words.

*{but the eyes of a fool are in the ends of the earth}* As one that has lost his way, (says one here) and is besides himself, nor knows where he is, nor which way to turn, etc. I am very confident (though I find it not observed by others, which I wonder at) that Solomon by these words, does allude to the words of Moses, Deut. 30:11, etc. *For this commandment which I command thee this day, it is not hidden from thee, neither is it far off. It is not in heaven, that thou shouldest say, Who shall go up for us to heaven, and bring it unto us, that we may hear it and do it? Neither is it beyond the sea, that thou shouldest say, Who shall go over the sea for us, and bring it unto us, that we may hear it, and do it? But the word is very nigh unto thee, in thy mouth, and in thy heart, that thou mayest do it.* Solomon therefore says, that a man of understanding, need not go very far, to find true wisdom, having the law of God before him, to instruct and direct him; which is able to *make any man wise unto salvation*: 2 Tim. 3:15. Whereas *a fool*, that seeks wisdom for credit, ostentation, profit, or any other worldly ends; he keeps a great deal ado; seeks far and near: and when he has done all, is never the nearer.

*{Wisdom is before him that hath understanding}* Easy to be gotten; or ready to guide him, or, appears in his face by his modest carriage: in fools, all is contrary.

**Verse 25.** *{A foolish son is a grief to his father, and bitterness to her that bare him}* Chapter 10:1. & 15. 20. & 19. 13.

**Verse 26.** *{Also to punish the just is not good}* Some refer this also, to verse 23. *A wicked man*, etc. Others, (and so Mercer) interpret, *To punish the just also, is not good*. But we need not be solicitous about the coherence, in such collections: as was said before.

*{nor to strike princes for equity}* Or; *that princes should strike for equity*: which is the part of a right tyrant, to punish them that do well; not of a king. See before, chapter 16:13. and the note there. According to the first translation, (which is ours here) by princes, must be understood inferior magistrates, who are often punished by professed tyrants, for doing their duties; that is, for being just.

**Verse 27.** *{He that hath knowledge spareth his words}* James 1:19. It is a sign of excellent wisdom, for a man to moderate his speech at any time, but especially in his anger.

*{an excellent spirit}* Or, *a cool spirit*. But the first, as more generally received, so more agreeable to the context: as does appear by the following verse. And to this sense, *sparing of breath*, or wind; (as chapter 27:11. *A fool uttereth all his mind*. It is the same word:) had been more proper, I think; than as we have it here, *excellent in spirit*. See also upon 12. 23. *A prudent man.*

**Verse 28.** *{Even a fool when he holdeth his peace, is counted wise}* Job 13:5. The sense of this sentence has been many ways expressed by the Grecians: as by Dion, Laertius, Plutarch, and others may appear, and divers apothegms made of it; but by all to one purpose: yet by an ancient poet, a man would think (though he did not think of him perchance) in Solomon's very words: *Πᾶς τις ἀπαίδευτος φρονιμώτατός ἐστι σιωπῶν*.

## CHAPTER 18

**Verse 1.** *{Through desire a man having separated himself, seeketh and intermeddleth with all wisdom}* Or, *He that separateth himself seeketh according to his desire, and intermeddleth in every business.* The sense of this, and the next verse, is generally thought very obscure; and there is great variety of expositions. The main question is whether this separation here mentioned, be spoken by way of commendation, or discommendation. Of one side it is objected, that the word *התגלע*, is never found in a good sense: but on the other side, that *תושיה*, never in a bad. Most rabbins interpret the words of a laudable separation, when a man, through an earnest desire of wisdom, or any good literature, does separate and sequester himself from the world, and all other impediments, that he may apply himself to his business; of which kind of separation, philosophers treat at large, (Plato especially) and show the necessity of it to all that will speed in the pursuit of true wisdom, or learning. And that the ground of this separation must be a strong desire, or no good to be done, the same teach also. Mercer, and some others, with the Vulgar Latin, would have contentious men here intended, who out of private ends and desires separate themselves, that is, make quarrels, and breach of friendship, and are apt to meddle in every matter; upon a pretense of reason, whatsoever they do, though, really, they will hear no reason. Others again will have the words to be meant of such as out of a humor at first, separate themselves from the church, and afterwards proceed to such a contempt of God, and his word, as that they openly scorn and deride both. Lastly, some think fantastical fools, and such as affect singularity, and to be cross to all the world, and yet to meddle in all things, to be the men here intended. Of all these expositions, the first best pleases me; and besides other reasons, I am induced to this judgment by the observation of the coherence of sense between this and the next verse; this verse (as I apprehend it) containing a lively character of one that is truly studious of wisdom, and goes the right way to attain unto it: the next, of one, that is an ambitious ostentator of it; who does not study to benefit himself, or any other, by it; but only to make a show, and that he may make himself known, and get some reputation abroad, and by the observation of two words; of *יתגלע* in the first verse; to which, (opposite in sense, apposite in sound,) to make an allusion *בהתגלות* does answer in the next. But let the reader make his choice.

**Verse 2.** *{but that his heart may discover itself}* *Nisi cum revelat cor suum*; as some in Mercer, that is, but that he may discover his heart: or, wisdom; such as he has, according to that of the poet: Scire tuum nihil est, etc. See before upon chapter 14:33. *Wisdom resteth*, etc.

**Verse 3.** *{then cometh also contempt}* Either contempt and shame from God lights upon wicked men; or else, he means, they contemn all others: both true, but the former more like to be the sense here.

*{and with ignominy reproach}* Some translate, *& cum viro ignominioso, opprobrium*: the Hebrew will bear it, and it answers the first member better. Yet this, as we have it here, is the more literal of the two; and to the same purpose, so that there needs no alteration. *Contempt: ignominy: reproach*: a wicked man's most ordinary livery: and accordingly, they are called *ὑβρισταὶ*, in Greek: and *ὕβρις* (contumely, reproach, properly:) any kind of sin, or outrage.

**Verse 4.** *{The words of a man's mouth are as deep waters, and the wellspring of wisdom as a flowing brook}* Chapter 20:5.

*{as deep waters}* Which can never be drawn empty, but ever bring profit. Chapter 10:11.

*{and the wellspring of wisdom, as a flowing brook}* *He that believeth on me, as the Scripture hath said, out of his belly shall flow rivers of living water*, John 7:38. It is not certain what place of Scripture Christ did especially intend. It is conceived, that he alluded unto divers; of which this also might be one. This very similitude of wisdom compared to a spring, is found in the writings of ancient philosophers also. *Ἔνδον βλέπε*, says one: *ἔνδον ἡ πηγὴ τοῦ ἀγαθοῦ*, *ἣ ἀεὶ ἀναβλύειν δυναμένη*, *ἐὰν ἀεὶ σκάπτῃ*.

*{wisdom}* By wisdom, may also be understood here eloquence; or fluent speech; as many do *Σοφία*, wisdom, in the N. T. often for eloquent speech, as we have showed elsewhere, *στόμα καὶ σοφίαν*, Luke 24:15. Nestor's eloquence is set out by Homer, in words equivalent to these.

**V. 5.** *{It is not good to accept the person of the wicked, to overthrow the righteous in judgment}* Lev. 19:15. Deut. 1:17. & 16.9. Chapter 24:23.

*{It is not good to accept the person of the wicked}* To favor and support him, to the injury of him that has the right on his side.

**V. 6.** *{his mouth calleth for strokes}* Encourages others to fight, or causes himself to be beaten, verse 7.

**V. 7.** *{A fools mouth is his destruction, and his lips are the snare of his soul}* Chapter 10:14. & 12.13. & 13.3.

**V. 8.** *{The words of a tale-bearer are as wounds, and they go down into the innermost parts of the belly}* Chapter 12:18. & 26.22.

*{tale-bearer}* Or, *whisperer*.

*{are as wounds}* They are firmly believed by some, and wound men's reputation irrecoverably. The Hebrew word *כמתלהמים* is not found elsewhere, but here and Chapter 26:22, where the same verse is repeated. It is by some Rabbins, interpreted, *blanda*; that is, *soft*: as Ps. 55:21. *the words of his mouth were smoother*. &c. and by others, *hidden*, or *close*. But this is the most likely signification (approved by Mercer) that we have here: grounded upon the affinity of the word with *הלם* *percussit*, &c.

*{as wounds}* Or, *like as when men are wounded*.

*{innermost parts}* Heb. *chambers*.

**V. 9.** *{is brother to him that is a great waster}* Will be as poor as he; for one gets nothing, and the other spends all.

**V. 10.** *{The name of the Lord is a strong tower}* Ps. 18:2. & 27.1. & 144.2. God rightly known, and speedily called upon, keeps him safe against trouble.

*{is safe}* Heb. *is set aloft*.

**V. 11.** *{The rich mans wealth is his strong city, and as an high wall in his own conceit}* Chapter 10:15. See upon Chapter 10:2. *Treasures of wickedness*, &c.

*{in his own conceit}* The same word in the original signifies a picture, image, idol: and, *conceit, imaginations*. There is no question but rich men's conceits and imaginations, (that trust to their wealth as most do,) are mere idolatry: and so described and taxed by Job: *If I have made gold my hope*, &c. Chapter 31:24, 25, 26. &c. How much better might many men employ their zeal in destroying these idols of their hearts; than in persecuting poor stones, that served only to adorn the houses of God?

**V. 12.** *{Before destruction the heart of man is haughty, and before honour is humility}* Chapter 11:2 and 15.33. and 16.18.

**V. 13.** *{He that answereth a matter}* He that answers one word, before he hear the whole matter, that he may understand it thoroughly, shows his own folly, and brings disgrace upon himself.

*{answereth a matter}* Heb. *returneth a word*.

**V. 14.** *{The spirit of a man will sustain his infirmities}* That is, he that is of a cheerful temper naturally, and enjoys peace of conscience, he may bear much; though his body be cast down, yet he may hold up (and his body will be the better for it too) in his soul and spirit. Some ancient philosophers have gone very far that way, maintaining that the pains of the body were nothing to the soul of man, because of a different nature from the body: and it cannot be denied but their doctrine and discipline did bring forth some marvelous examples of patience, and resolution, in greatest bodily pains and torments. But generally, their arguments have more of subtlety, than solidity; and failed them when they came to the trial. As for the torments of a wounded spirit, they have been acknowledged by all men and both pithily and pathetically set out by divers poets and orators: those of a bad conscience, especially: but by none better, or more fully, than by Aristotle, *Ethic. Nicom.* l. 9. c. 4.

*{who can bear?}* Some think that Solomon's aim by these words is, to give a check to pusillanimity, and want of courage: *who can bear*; that is, *who can endure?* Or, *quis elevabit, eriget attollet?* that is, *who shall lift*, or, *raise up?* Indeed, it is a hard thing to apply comfort to a man that is sunk in his spirit. Especially if it proceed from a natural distemper of the body, as often; though neither the patient himself sometimes, nor they that are employed to administer comfort unto him, through ignorance, suspect any such thing: who therefore do more hurt commonly, in such a mistaken case, than good. It were much more expedient, in such a case, to repair to a learned physician; than to men utterly ignorant of all natural causes and effects. But if a man be very pusillanimous naturally, it is almost impossible to make a good Christian of such a one. *But the fearful and unbelieving*, &c. Rev. 21:8.

**V. 15.** *{and the ear of the wise seeketh knowledge}* A man must use both heart and ear to get wisdom: that is, both meditation of his own, and instruction from others.

**V. 16.** *{A mans gift maketh room for him, and bringeth him before great men}* Chapter 17:8.

*{A mans gift maketh room for him}* Gets him liberty to speak; and favorable audience of great men: or, brings him out of prison, and exalts him to honor.

**V. 17.** *{He that is first in his own cause}* The accuser seems to be in the right, till the defendant shows the weakness of his plea. But there is another sense made of these words, of excellent use. *The just is first in his own cause*, or, *trial*: that is, he begins with himself first, to examine and search his own conscience, that he may judge himself, and condemn, if he see cause, (as most likely) before he meddle with others. Justus, prior est accusator sui; as the vulgar Latin here, very well. *Then cometh his neighbour and* (after he has done with himself; strictly and impartially:) *he searcheth him.* We are not forbidden to reprove our neighbor, or those whom we have to do with, except we find ourselves altogether innocent in the same kind (licentious wicked men would be glad of that: but Seneca has met with them, and their objections long ago:) No; but instructed to begin with ourselves, and not to spare ourselves; and so shall we be in a better disposition to deal with others. An excellent sentence: anybody will say so much, but few will be persuaded, to put it in practice effectually.

**V. 18.** *{The lot causeth contentions to cease}* A lot is a good way to end contentions, for the avoiding of part-takings.

*{and parteth}* Ir *parteth*, literally, while it assigns unto every one his portion: and *parteth*, figuratively, (but very truly too) while it keeps them from falling one upon another, through strife and contention.

**V. 19.** *{A brother offended is harder to be won than a strong city}* Because he finds hurt where he had reason to look for help. Fratrum contentiones: an ancient proverb mentioned by Aristotle, and divers others. Yet some of the Rabbins make a quite different sense of the words, that a brother, though offended, will nevertheless upon occasion be as a strong city, &c. The Greek and vulgar Latin go further from the Hebrew; but their sense is more warrantable; to wit, that the conjunction of brothers, is as a fenced city, &c. These I mention only: but the Hebrew words are clear, and the sense of them unquestionable.

*{their contentions are like the bars of a castle}* Which being strong, will neither bow nor yield.

**V. 20.** *{A mans belly shall be satisfied with the fruit of his mouth}* Chapter 12:14. & 13.2 and 14.23.

**V. 21.** *{Death and life are in the power of the tongue}* A man's tongue well used, brings life: ill used, brings death. Of the power of the tongue, besides former references, see also James 3:2, 3. &c. and Matt. 12:37. *For by thy words*, &c.

**V. 22.** *{Whoso findeth a wife, findeth a good thing, and obtaineth favour of the Lord}* Chapter 19:14.

*{Whoso findeth a wife, findeth a good thing}* Marriage is good in itself, for a wife is a helper. However by what follows, *and obtaineth favour of the Lord*, (which words upon another occasion we had before, Chapter 8:35.) compared with Chapter 19:14. *a prudent wife is from the Lord*: it is very probable, that *good*, or *prudent*, must be supplied. And so most expositors think.

**V. 23.** *{roughly}* James 2:3. Psalm 31:18. *grievous things*. The same word in the original.

**V. 24.** *{A man that hath friends, must shew himself friendly}* Some make this to be the sense, *Unusquisque diligit amicos, ut amicitias contrahant secum: Quia amicus*, &c. that is, *Every man is covetous of friends, to contract friendship with them; Because a friend will stick*, &c. But some others very differently, *Vir qui multos habet amicos, [finis est] ut conteratur*; from another root, which is usual enough; and is warrantable enough by frequent experience. *A man of friends*, [that is, who has many friends: his end will be,] *that he shall be broken in pieces*; or, *undone*. But this does not suit so well with that which follows.

*{and there is a friend that sticketh closer than a brother}* Chapter 17:17. Ofttimes friends are found that are more ready to do us pleasure, than they that are near to us in the flesh, and so more obliged to us. And in trouble, a friend sticks close to us sometimes, when a brother forsakes us.

## CHAPTER 19

**Verse 1.** *{Better is the poor that walketh in his integrity, than he that is perverse in his lips, and is a fool}* Though he be rich, as must be supplied from Chapter 28:6. *Better is the poor that walketh in his uprightness, than he that is perverse in his ways, though he be rich.* But it seems also to be implied in these words, *perverse in his lips*: because it is the property of many rich men, (and some affect it, as well becoming, they think, their greatness) to speak roughly; (which is a kind of perverse speaking) as was observed before by Solomon himself, Chapter 18:23. *The poor useth entreaties; but the rich answereth roughly.*

*{Also that the soul be without knowledge it is not good}* Some make this sense of the words: *Etiam absque scientiâ desiderium non est bonum*: that is, Appetite, [or, *desire*:] *without knowledge, is not good*; or a dangerous thing. An excellent saying. I have read it in some comics very well expressed. There is nothing to be objected against this translation, but the accent; which I do not conceive to be of much weight, except it be granted, that vowels and accents were anciently: which I do not believe. And then the coherence too, will be much better.

*{he that hasteth with his feet, sinneth}* He that does things rashly without premeditation, or forecast: *sinneth*; that is, (as the word is often used) shall suffer, or smart for it; according to the ancient Greek proverb, *Προπέτεια πολλοῖς ἐστὶν αἰτία κακῶν*: *Haste*, (or precipitancy) *is to many the cause of evils*.

**V. 3.** *{and his heart fretteth against the Lord}* Though man's foolish carriage makes his business not succeed well, yet he lays the fault upon God. Gen. 3:12. Homer in his first Odyssey, says the very same, that Solomon does here; to wit, That men, what evils they incur through their own folly and perverseness, they lay the fault of them upon the gods: *Ὦ πόποι*, *οἷον δή νυ θεούς*, &c.

**V. 4.** *{Wealth maketh many friends; but the poor is separated from his neighbour}* Chapter 14:20.

**V. 5.** *{A false witness shall not be unpunished, and he that speaketh lies shall not escape}* Exod. 23:1. Deut. 19:16. Chapter 6:19. & 21.28.

*{A false witness shall not be unpunished}* Heb. *a witness of falsehood*: That is, a most false witness. Let him be as cunning as he can to invent lies, all his tricks cannot keep him from punishment.

*{unpunished}* Heb. *held innocent*.

**V. 6.** *{Many will entreat the favour of the prince}* Heb. *the face*. Because they that sue for favor to a great man, fasten their eyes on his face, Ps. 123:2.

*{a friend to him that giveth gifts}* Chapter 17:8.

*{him that giveth gifts}* Heb. *a man of gifts*.

**V. 7.** *{All the brethren of the poor do hate him}* Chapter 14:20. Neglect him, as if they hated him, Matt. 6:24.

*{how much more do his friends go far from him}* Are far off in affection, and help him no more, than if they were far off in place.

*{he pursueth them with words}* To have comfort from them, or help. Or, puts them in mind of their promises.

*{yet they are wanting to him}* Heb. *לא־המה*, that is, *they* (or, *but they*) *not*: or, *they are not*. So in the text: but in the margin instead of *לא* it is noted *לו* as the more correct reading. In this English, both the one and the other, (the reading of the text, and that of the margin) seem to be expressed: as in many other places besides. What sense some Rabbins make of the *לו* alone, may be seen in Mercer: he likes it not, neither can I. The *לא* alone, may serve to make this sense we have here; *but they not*: that is, they are wanting to him. Such expressions are ordinary in the Hebrew.

**V. 8.** *{He that getteth wisdom, loveth his own soul}* Shall preserve his life, and thrive, though all men fail him.

*{wisdom}* Heb. *an heart*.

*{he that keepeth understanding, shall find good}* Heb. *he keepeth understanding to find good*.

**V. 9.** *{A false witness shall not be unpunished, and he that speaketh lies shall perish}* **Verse 5.**

**V. 10.** *{Delight is not seemly for a fool}* The free use of things is not to be permitted to him that cannot use them aright. Or, *delight* may be taken here for dainties and pleasures; the usual effects and evidences of wealth and greatness. As if he said, Wealth and abundance does not become a fool. See Chapter 30:22. *For a servant*, &c.

*{for a servant to have rule over princes}* Chapter 30:22. Eccl. 10:6, 7.

**V. 11.** *{The discretion of a man deferreth his anger, and it is his glory to pass over a transgression}* Chapter 14:29.

*{discretion}* Or, *prudence*.

*{it is his glory to pass over a transgression}* To take no notice of it, or to forgive, and forget it, Lev. 19:18. The heathens themselves could say, (and they have many sayings to that purpose) that in nothing man came nearer to the glory and perfection of God himself, than in goodness and clemency. See also upon Chapter 16:32. *He that is slow to anger*, &c.

**V. 12.** *{The Kings wrath is as the roaring of a lion: but his favour is as dew upon the grass}* Chapter 16:14, 15. & 20.2. & 28.15. *Where the word of a King is, there is power*: says Solomon, in Ecclesiastes: *and who may say unto him, What dost thou?* Eccles. 8:4. And where power is, (without which the name of a king may be, but not the reality:) there *the Kings wrath is as the roaring of a lion*: a thing much to be dreaded; of sad consequence, commonly: *as a messenger of death*, Chapter 16:14. But, if a king study goodness, more than majesty; or bring himself to want and penury by bad counsel, and ill husbandry: himself suffer his people to grow upon him, and thereby (as the nature of people is:) to contemn him: Or, if God, (for the sins of either king, or people, or both:) *doth pour contempt upon princes*, Job 12:21. a king may have occasion enough to roar perchance, (as David speaks of himself) but all occasion of terror will be from them, where the power is: who, the more vile, originally; the greater will their terror be; because, *Corpora magnanimo satis est prostrasse leoni*; but (every schoolboy can tell) *Asperius nihil est humili cùm surgit in altum.* However, there be examples of strange judgments. But this is enough to Solomon's words; and less could not be said, to make him understood.

**V. 13.** *{A foolish son is the calamity of his father}* Chapter 10:1. & 15.20. & 17.21, 25. Heb. *calamities*. That is, the greatest calamity.

*{and the contentions of a wife are a continual dropping}* Chapter 21:9. & 27.15. As rain that drops in, and rots the house at length.

**V. 14.** *{House and riches are the inheritance of fathers}* Parents can leave their children wealth, but not good wives. See before upon Chapter 16:1. *The preparations of the heart*, &c.

*{a prudent wife is from the Lord}* Chapter 18:22.

**V. 15.** *{an idle soul shall suffer hunger}* Chapter 10:4. and 20.13. Or, *deceitful*. For such persons as live idly, necessity for the most part, makes them deceitful.

**V. 16.** *{He that keepeth the commandment, keepeth his own soul}* That is, shall live: as appears by the antithesis, *shall die*; in the next words, Of eternal life and death; as Luke 10:25. &c. *this do and thou shalt live.* Of temporal; He that kept the law, avoided those punishments, (whereof some were capital) which the breakers of it did incur. And in reference to this, some translate *supplicio afficietur*: that is, *he shall suffer*; to wit, by judgment of law.

**V. 17.** *{He that hath pity upon the poor, lendeth unto the Lord}* Matt. 10:42. & 25.40. 2 Cor. 9:6, 7.

*{that which he hath given}* Or, *his deed*.

**V. 18.** *{Chasten thy son while there is hope}* Chapter 13:24. & 23.13.

*{and let not thy soul spare him for his crying}* Some, *Et ad interficiendum eum ne eleves animam tuam*; that is, *And do not lift thy soul* (*to lift his soul unto* any thing; that is, to covet, to desire, to attempt: a known Hebraism: as Jer. 22:27. and elsewhere:) *to kill him*. This pleases some, as the most literal, who except against that other, as forced, or not sufficiently warrantable. But it is not so, as will appear by examining of particulars. First, the same verb that signifies *to lift*, signifies also *to spare*, or *to forgive*; as Num. 14:19. according to all translations; and Gen. 4:6. & 13. according to some. So that taking the substantive *soul*, (which they make an accusative) to be the nominative to the verb; *thy soul spare him*, will be the literal sense of the words. This cannot be denied. What then? *המות* they say, is not found elsewhere for *noise*, or *crying*. What if it be not? How many words besides, that are found but once in the Bible? or twice? Besides, though the word, as here pointed be not elsewhere, in this sense; yet with other points it is: Es. 14.11. *and the noise of thy viols*. And why may not that be the right pointing and reading of this place also? But that will trouble them more, that would have the pointing to be of the same antiquity and authority, as the consonants. Let them choose, therefore; for either of those two ways we have spoken of, the word is justifiable in this sense. And truly it is much more likely, that Solomon should warn us *not to spare*, which is done so generally, and the undoing of so many children; than not to kill, which though it happens sometimes; yet rather as a prodigy, when it does, or a mischance, (so contrary to nature it is:) than as a thing purposed, or intended: nor a thing certainly, that any father should be suspected *to lift his soul unto*; which is an expression of a great longing, or eager pursuance after a thing. But lastly, let us quit that controverted (though there be little cause) notion of the word and stick to that which is more common, of killing: yet even so shall we come to the sense that we would have, & avoid that, which they embrace; but we think it not so convenient. And this difference comes from the different exposition of the verb *נשא* which we first spoke of, and different ordering of the substantive. *Let not thy soul spare him to his destruction*; or, *to kill him*. This needs no further explication. Only we may observe, that in this case, as in many others, noted by many; too great indulgence, proves greatest cruelty.

*{for his crying}* Or, *to his destruction*: or, *to cause him to die*. So Mercer. See also upon Chapter 23:13.

**V. 19.** *{yet thou must do it again}* Though he be brought out of danger, yet his passion will bring him in again. Some make this verse to cohere with the former, translating; *Magnus est excandescentiâ, condonans mulctam: dicens te, si eripias, deinde amplius mulctaturum*: That is, *Though thou forgive him, yet make thyself very angry to him, saying, If he escape now, that his punishment shall be the greater hereafter*: So Junius; but he only: neither do I see any reason, why any man should follow him.

*{do}* Heb. *add*.

**V. 20.** *{Hear counsel and receive instruction, that thou mayest be wise in thy latter end}* Some (as Junius) of this *thy latter end*, make but a *tandem* of it: as if he meant, *that thou mayest be wise at the last*, (tandem aliquando) and no more. But I rather take it, that Solomon had a further aim than so: *That thou mayest be wise in thy latter end*; that is, Provide wisdom against the time of death, when most needful, that you may not die a fool. Much to this purpose is the prayer of the Psalmist, *So teach us to number our days that we may apply our hearts unto wisdom*, Ps. 90:12. He that has wisdom enough to die well, need to care for no more. But it is a great chance, if they that have been strangers to it all their lives, find it at their death: if a wicked life, beget a godly death. So that he that prays for wisdom at his death; and he, that the consideration of his death, may make him live wisely, though they seem to differ in their words, in effect pray but the same.

**V. 21.** *{There are many devices in a mans heart}, &c.* Job 23:13. Ps. 33:10, 11. Chapter 16:1, 9. Isa. 46:10.

*{nevertheless the counsel of the Lord, that shall stand}* Man's device shall not have success, except God govern it, whose purpose is unchangeable, Ps. 33:10, 11. Isa. 14:26, 27.

**V. 22.** *{The desire of a man is his}* Or, *That which is to be desired of a man is his*.

*{The desire of a man is his kindness}* A man's will is to be accepted for the deed, if he have nothing to give, 2 Cor. 8:12. This is a good sense; but much different from that which is made of these words by other expositors. The truth is, the words are ambiguous; and admit of more senses than one. *The desire of a man*, &c. that is, says Mercer, with the most Rabbins; It is the greatest and choicest ornament of a man, to be kind and bountiful; if so really. But many, because they covet the praise and reputation of it, are very forward in promises, but fail in performance. Better a poor man, that really performs what he is able, than such gaudy undertakers. Such men are very lively described by the comic; *id est genus hominum pessimum, in Denegando modo queis pudor paullulum adest* &c. he sets them out at large. Others make this sense; The kindness of many (of the richer sort especially) is mere covetousness: they give, but it is, that they may receive more. A poor man, that has little or nothing to give, is far better than such benefactors. Such men there be, there is no question; too many: comics and poets, if not experience, will tell us. The different exposition of the word *תאות* is the chief ground of all this difference.

**V. 23.** *{and he that hath it shall abide satisfied: he shall}* Or, *and he that is filled with it shall abide: he shall*. Or rather thus to be supplied: *The fear of the Lord*, &c. *and* [they that fear him,] *shall abide satisfied*. See upon Chapter 14:15, 26.

*{he shall not be visited with evil}* Besides former references to divers places in this book, see also upon Ps. 91:5. *Thou shalt not be afraid.*

**V. 24.** *{A slothful man hideth his hand in his bosom}* Chapter 14:19. & 26.13, 15. *It grieveth him to bring it again to his mouth*: which does somewhat abate of the hyperbole. Yet St. Chrysostom tells us of some in his time, who for mere wantonness and daintiness, would say, that it was a labor unto them to eat, and could have found in their hearts, to have their meat chewed unto them, to spare their pains. There is some controversy about the word *צלחת* which some translate, *lebetem*; others, *manticam*; not *bosom*, as here. But since it makes no difference of sense, (as they expound it) we need not trouble ourselves with it.

**V. 25.** *{Smite a scorner, and the simple will beware}* Chapter 21:11. Men that do ill out of ignorance, will amend by the punishments and reproofs of scorners, though they themselves will not: or, scorners must be smitten that offend out of malice, that others may amend; but he that sins out of frailty, will amend if he be but reproved, though he be not smitten, when he duly considers what he had done.

*{will beware}* Heb. *will be cunning*.

**V. 26.** *{He that wasteth his father, and chaseth away his mother, is a son that causeth}* The words may be taken reciprocally also and that perchance the most literal sense: *A son that causeth shame, and bringeth reproach, is* [as] *he that wasteth his father, and chaseth away his mother.* Which may be an aggravation of children's riotous courses, (as, *Rebellion, is as the sin of witchcraft, and stubbornness, as idolatry*, 1 Sam. 15:23.) which may cause as much grief to parents, as loss of goods, or life itself: or, a prognostic intimation, that they that take such courses, which cause shame and reproach; may in time also, &c. See also Chapter 28:24. And so *משדד* may be translated more properly, *destroyeth*: or, *spoileth*. For it imports properly, a violent and sudden destruction.

**V. 27.** *{Cease my son, to hear the instruction}, &c.* Some, because *מוסר* *instruction*, or, *discipline*, is commonly taken in a good sense, inverting (as sometimes it must be) the order of the words, translate, *Cease my son, to err from the words of knowledge, that thou mayest hear instruction.* So Junius; and Mercer likes it well.

**V. 28.** *{An ungodly witness}* Heb. *A witness of Belial*.

*{the mouth of the wicked devoureth iniquity}* Takes as much pleasure in speaking falsely, as gluttons and drunkards do in swallowing down abundance of delicate meats and drinks, Job 15:16. Eph. 4:19. Or, sticks no more at it, than at the eating of bread, or any other daily and ordinary business.

*{devoureth}* Or, *hideth*: covers, as Num. 14:20. *when the holy things are covered*. But the other, the usual signification of the word, and better answering to *scorneth*, or *derideth*, in the first member.

## CHAPTER 20

**Verse 1.** *{Wine is a mocker}* Wine, immoderately taken, makes men scoffers and furious: or, it overtakes men before they are aware, Gen. 9:21.

**V. 2.** *{The fear of a King is as the roaring of a Lion}* Chapter 10:14. & 19.12. See there.

*{sinneth against his own soul}* Puts his life in danger.

**V. 4.** *{The sluggard will not plow by reason of the cold}* Chapter 10:5. & 15.19. 22.13.

*{cold}* Or, *winter*.

**V. 5.** *{Counsel in the heart of a man is like deep water}* It is hard to find; for it is as deep waters, whose bottom cannot be found, yet a wise man will fetch it out of him, by observing his words and manners. But if *deep water* here, be meant as *deep waters*, Chapter 18:4. as most think; then the meaning will be, that though wisdom be very profound, and as it were concealed in a wise man's breast, yet a studious understanding man will find a way to draw out of it, for his need, and for his imitation.

**V. 6.** *{Most men will proclaim every one his own bounty}* Some translate, not, *his own goodness*: but, unusquisque misericordiam; that is, the bounty, or liberality of another. The sense; Many are prone enough, say they, to proclaim, or publicly to acknowledge their benefactors: but few to be found, that are really thankful, in point of requital, if occasion be.

*{but a faithful man who can find?}* Heb. *a man of faithfulnesses*: that is, a man faithful in every thing.

**V. 7.** *{his children are blessed after him}* Ps. 112:2. See there.

**V. 8.** *{scattereth away all evil with his eyes}* Where righteous judgment is executed, there sin ceases, and vice dares not appear, ver. 26.

*{all evil}* Or, *every evil man*, as ver. 26.

**V. 9.** *{Who can say, I have made my heart clean}, &c.* 1 Kings 8:46. 2 Chron. 6:36. Job 14:4. Ps. 51:5. Eccles. 7:20. 1 John 1:8.

**V. 10.** *{Divers weights}* Heb. *a stone and a stone*. An heavy one to buy withal, and a light one to sell withal.

*{Divers weights, and divers measures, both of them}, &c.* Deut. 25:13. &c. chapter 11:1. & 16.11.

*{divers measures}* Heb. *an ephah and an ephah*.

**V. 11.** *{Even a child is known by his doings}* Children show betimes, whether they be inclined to good ways or bad; and must be dealt withal accordingly. This is its common exposition. Another is proposed by Geierus, grounded chiefly upon the most usual signification of the Hebrew word *יתנכר* which in this sense, *to be known*, or *to make himself known*, (though most less proper to the original verb) is not found anywhere else; but, *for, to carry himself as a stranger; to conceal himself*, &c. in divers places. It is this: *Even a child can so disguise his doings, That whether his work be pure and right, [thou can not easily discern.]* And if a child; how much more a man? How careful then ought we to be whom we trust? This indeed is a caution of so much consequence, as that it can never be too often repeated, or varied in words. Whence it is, that the heathens made an oracle of Epicharmus his words tending to that purpose: *Νῆφε*, &c. However if we like best of our translation, as more commonly received; yet I am not satisfied, that Solomon's aim was only to tell us, that even children can dissemble. I rather conceive, that his words might drive to this; to tell us, that a probable judgment may be made of a child, or youth, what he will be, when a man; by his education, and by what he is accustomed unto in those flexible years: because, *Quo semel est imbuta recens*, &c. The consideration whereof would oblige parents (one main subject of this book) to all possible care of their children's education. See also upon Chapter 22:6. *in the way he should go*.

**V. 12.** *{The hearing ear, and the seeing eye, the Lord hath made even both of them}* Exod. 4:11. It is not possible then, that he from whom we have received these faculties, should want them himself. Which if he do not, and is privy to the actions, even the most secret, of men; as little doubt is to be made, but he will in due time reward all men according to their deeds. This to be the drift of these words, may be collected from Ps. 94:7, 8, 9, 10. *Yet they say, The Lord shall not see*, &c. See there.

**V. 13.** *{Love not sleep, lest thou come to poverty}* Chapter 12:15. & 19.15.

*{open thine eyes}, &c.* That is, Be vigilant, watchful; take pains, and shake off drowsiness.

**V. 14.** *{It is naught}* He often dispraises it, that he may buy it the cheaper. But what the principal aim is, is not certainly known. Whether Solomon, by these words, intended only to reprove the covetousness of many buyers, who care not what they say, though never so much against their own consciences, so they compass their desires. Or whether the inconstancy, or partiality, of men's judgments; who now commend, because their own; what they but now dispraised and slighted, while it was another's. Or whether he would intimate thereby, that we should not regard bare words and shows, because most men can, and will dissemble for their own ends and advantage. Many such things may be thought of; but we cannot be very confident of anything, more than what the bare letter does offer.

**V. 15.** *{but the lips of knowledge are a precious jewel}* He is richer that can speak wisely, than he that has much treasure. See also upon Chapter 18:4. *Wisdom.*

**V. 16.** *{Take his garment that is surety for a stranger}* Chapter 27:13. Trust him not without a pawn, that cares not whom he be surety for. But this must not be precisely understood according to the letter: (for so, it would be against the law, Exod. 22:26, 27.) but rather proverbially, importing no more, than if he said, Trust him not by no means: make no account of him, as of a sober man; or of credit, &c. Of suretyship in general, see upon Chapter 6:1. *Surety for thy friend.*

**V. 17.** *{Bread of deceit}* Heb. *bread of lying, or falsehood*.

*{Bread of deceit is sweet to a man}* Chapter 9:17.

*{his mouth shall be filled with gravel}* Fabius Verrucosus, an ancient Roman, was wont to call a kindness unkindly done, that is, with the abuse of language, and some show of reluctancy, *panem lapidosum*; that is, gravelly bread: (*quem esurienti accipere necessarium sit; esse acerbum*) which a hungry man must needs accept, but cannot eat without much displeasure.

**Verse 18.** *{Every purpose is established by counsel}* Chapter 15:22. & 24.6.

*{with good advice make war}* Heb. *advices*: that is, by store of good counsel. Luke 14:31.

*{make war}* That is, make not war; take heed of it, without good advice, and mature deliberation. So the words must be understood; not as a command, but as a warning; rather dissuading from it, than encouraging to it.

**Verse 19.** *{He that goeth about as a tale-bearer, revealeth secrets}* Chapter 11:13. See there, of the original word. To which may be added, that some make the proper notion of it, to be, *fraudulentia* (in the abstract;) and thence, *fraudulenter*: which is an adverb: and both here and elsewhere, *Qui ambulat fraudulenter*. Whatever it may do elsewhere, (which makes me to take notice of it) here certainly tale-bearer, will do much better. The meaning of this sentence is well expressed by an ancient poet: *Percontatorem fugito, nam garrulus idem est*; that is, in effect: Take heed of a man, that cunningly insinuates himself, with questions and quæres, where he is not concerned: for such a one, if not a traitor, is a prattler, and will do you mischief with his tongue.

*{flattereth}* Or, *enticeth*. The most literal signification of the original (here also followed by some) seems to be, *qui deceptus est*: So Junius: *simplex fatuus*: so Buxtorfius. And so the sense will be, meddle not with him that has no command, no government of his tongue: which to manage well, as it is accounted great wisdom; so, to have no power of it, must needs be great folly and simplicity; and of dangerous consequence. Others again, qui dilatat: that is, *who openeth wide*: which is justifiable too. See Gen. 9:27. God shall enlarge Japhet: and that is the more common use of the word in the Syriac tongue.

**Verse 20.** *{Whoso curseth his father or his mother}, &c.* Exod. 21:17. Lev. 20:9. Matt. 15:4.

*{his lamp shall be put out}* Or, *candle*. See upon Ps. 132:17. a lamp for mine anointed: and Chapter 24:20. *the candle of the wicked shall be put out*.

*{in obscure darkness}* Ps. 112:4. Isa. 58:10. *then shall thy light*, &c. It does much aggravate the case then, to lose the benefit of the light, when there is most want of it: as on the other side, we are most sensible of the benefit of it, when it affords itself in time of extremity.

**Verse 21.** *{An inheritance may be gotten hastily}* It has been observed by them that never heard of Solomon's name; and it is a great chance, but in the compass of 20. or 30. years a man (if he take heed:) may meet with examples enough, to persuade him, that it is so indeed. See also upon Chapter 21:5. of every one that is hasty. The reader may take notice, that the reading of the margin, not that of the text, as in divers other places, is here expressed. Yet a man with little labor, might make somewhat of that which is in the text: but when all is done, it must be granted, that the sacred Scripture (neither was there any promise past, that it should:) has not been altogether exempted from the fate of other books.

**Verse 22.** *{Say not thou, I will recompense evil}* Deut. 32:35. Chapter 17:13. & 24.29. Rom. 12:17. 1 Thess. 5:15. 1 Pet. 3:9. See more upon Ps. 35:4. Let them be confounded, &c.

**Verse 23.** *{Divers weights are an abomination unto the LORD}* **Verse 10.**

*{a false balance}* Heb. *balances of deceit*.

**Verse 24.** *{Mans goings are of the Lord}* Ps. 37:23. Chapter 16:9. Jer. 10:23.

*{how can a man then understand his own way?}* To understand his own way, in this place, seems to imply; to foresee the event of things, in the course of one's life; what is expedient, and what not: and the like. It was taken in another sense, Chap. 14. The wisdom of the prudent is, to understand his way, &c. But some translate here; *yet doth not he understand his way*; that is, as they expound it, Though God's providence shows itself towards every man in the course of his life, many ways; yet who is it, that takes notice of it, as he should? Or to that purpose. The Hebrew will bear this too: neither is there want of parallel places, to make this sense plausible. See upon Ps. 28:5. Because they regard not the works of the Lord, &c.

**Verse 25.** *{It is a snare to the man who devoureth that which is holy}* That is, to apply it or to take it to his own use, which was appointed to God's; yea, to go about to do it, by inquiring how the vow might be made void. Oh, that the godly men (as they persuade themselves) of these times, would consider of this in cold blood; and how wretchedly they expose their understandings to the Devil's illusions in all that they do; when they study evasions against such manifest light!

**Verse 26.** *{A wise King scattereth the wicked}* Ps. 101:5. &c. ver. 8.

*{and bringeth the wheel over them}* Which was a kind of punishment then in use; and in many places is yet.

**Verse 27.** *{The spirit of man is the candle of the Lord, searching all the inward parts of the belly}* There be sundry kinds of knowledge, (as philosophers are wont to distinguish) in man, though proceeding all from one single and simple fountain: but of all knowledge, that which they call reflexam cognitionem, whereby man does reflect upon himself, and is able to reason concerning himself, both of his nature, and actions; whether natural, or moral; is the most admirable, and a great evidence of the soul's immortality. It is called here the candle, or lamp of the Lord, because of the divine origin of the soul, (whereof this is a principal faculty,) which is as it were a sparkle of the Divine Nature; and because of the office it has, under God, as it were, in man; to see and oversee the whole man; to search into the most inmost and secret thoughts and actions; to guide and govern: and in sum, to be a light, both in matters of practice, and speculation. If a man might suspect, that ancient heathens had anything out of Solomon, I should suspect this as soon as anything. They tell us, (the writings of the Stoics and Platonists are full of it:) of a certain *δαίμων*, demon, or spirit, so they call it, *ὃν ἑκάστῳ προστάτην καὶ ἡγεμόνα ὁ Θεὸς ἔδωκεν*, *ἀπόσπασμα ἑαυτοῦ*: whom God (being part of himself,) has given to every man, to be a guide and overseer of his life; *οὗτος δὲ ἐστιν ὁ ἑκάστου νοῦς καὶ λόγος*: and this, say they, is every man's reason, and understanding.

*{searching all the inward parts of the belly}* Man's soul can dive into those things, which his eyes cannot. Belly, for heart; is ordinary in Scripture: and so *κοιλία* too in Greek sometimes.

**Verse 28.** *{Mercy and truth preserve the King}* Ps. 101:2. Chapter 29:14. See upon 14.34. but sin is a reproach.

**Verse 29.** *{the beauty of old men is the gray head}* Chapter 16:31.

**Verse 30.** *{The blueness of a wound cleanseth away evil; so}, &c.* Others with more probability, translate; The blueness of a wound, is a medicine (or cleansing) to an evil (man:) and stripes (that penetrate) to the inward of the belly. So that there is no similitude in the words, but the same thing (as often in this book) twice expressed. The sum is, that some be of that nature, that without perpetual basting, and stripes, they will not be good or do their duty.

*{so do stripes the inward parts of the belly}* Sharp punishments make wicked men to bewail their secret sins.

## CHAPTER 21
