**Verse 1.** *{The kings heart is in the hand of the Lord}* Though kings seem to have all things at command, and to do what they will; yet are not their very wills altogether of their own disposing; no further than God is pleased to leave them to themselves: who, if he see good, can overrule their very thoughts and wills, that themselves (against their own wills, in a manner) shall desire and endeavor, not what is most suitable to their own proper temper and disposition; but what God will have. Neither are the hearts of kings only, in the hand of God; but (according to that of the Psalmist, *He fashioneth their heart alike*, &c. Ps. 33:15. and before, Chapter 16:7. *when a mans wayes please the Lord, he maketh even his enemies to be at peace with him*: and divers other places to the same purpose) of all men equally: but of kings and princes eminently, as they are more eminent; in whose wills also, by reason of their power and authority, more men are concerned.

*{the Kings heart is in the hand of the Lord}* This has been thought so wise and notable a saying, that some wise heathens of the later times have made use of it, by whom it is called *Ἀσσυρίων λόγος*: a speech, or sentence of the Assyrians.

*{as the rivers of water}* As water is drawn in trenches this way or that way: or, as a boat in the water is easily turned this way or that way.

**Verse 2.** *{Every way of a man is right in his own eyes}* Chapter 16:2. See there: and upon 15.6. in the revenues.

**Verse 3.** *{To do justice and judgment, is more acceptable}, &c.* 1 Sam. 15:22. Chapter 15:8. Isa. 1:11. Hos. 6:6. Mic. 6:7, 8. Matt. 23:12.

**Verse 4.** *{An high look}* Heb. *haughtiness of eyes*.

*{An high look, and a proud heart, and}, &c.* Chapter 6:17. *רחב־לב* here, and Chapter 28:25. translated a proud heart: does properly signify largeness of heart, as it is rendered 1 Kings 4:29. And God gave Solomon wisdom and understanding exceeding much, and largeness of heart, (*ורחב לב*) even as the sand that is on the sea shore. It is not therefore always used in one sense. Some take it here, and Chapter 29:25. for covetousness: or for excess of joy. But by the tenor of the words in the original, (*רום עינים ורחב־לב*) which run with some kind of allusion, it is more probable, that external and internal; that is, all kind of pride is intended: and so Mercer, and most others understand it. There is more difficulty in the next word, *נר רשעים* by most, as here, translated, aratio impiorum: which some refer to the former words, High looks, &c. the plowing; that is, say they, the practice and usual carriage; the marks, or evidences of wicked men: or the field, as it were, wherein they daily exercise themselves. Others, take it by itself, and by it understand, the deep plots and projects: as *חרש* which properly signifies to plow, does also signify to plot, or invent. But the same word *נר* may also, (and is by divers,) be translated the lamp, or light: (as it is in our margin;) by which some understand the cunning and worldly wisdom: others, the goodly shows and outward appearance; that is in effect, the dissimulation and hypocrisy. Others, the pomp, stateliness, and worldly felicity: and upon it, their pride and insolency. That which makes this sense most probable, is, that in two other places of this book, (Chapter 13:9. and Chapter 24:20.) we find these two words so joined; the lamp (*נר* the word here; though not altogether pointed here, as it uses to be in that sense; which may be thought somewhat; but indeed not much considerable) of the wicked. Besides the word *נר* singly used in divers places, of some worldly enjoyment and felicity, whatever it be. But oratio is the more received interpretation.

*{the plowing of the wicked}* Or, *the light of the wicked*.

*{the plowing of the wicked, is sin}* A bad crop follows their plots, Job 4:8. Or, *is sinne*: that is, forerunner of their destruction, which is the fruit and effect of sin. For, When the wicked spring as the grass, and all the workers of iniquity do flourish, it is, that they shall be destroyed for ever, Ps. 92:7. and *before destruction, the heart of man is haughty: and before honour, is humility*, Chapter 18:12.

**Verse 5.** *{The thoughts of the diligent tend only to plenteousness}* They that do their business wisely and carefully, shall thrive: they that do it rashly, shall not prosper.

**Verse 6.** *{The getting of treasures by a lying tongue, is a vanity}, &c.* Chapter 10:2. & 13.11.

*{of every one that is hasty, only I want.}* Before, *An inheritance may be gotten hastily* at the beginning, Chapter 20:21. Hastily there then in respect to the time, chiefly: as if he had said, in a little time: here hasty, that is, rash, precipitate in his actions, and carriage of his businesses. Neither is that the same word in the Hebrew in both places.

*{tossed to and fro}* Like dust, or chaff, by the wind: not constant: or abiding, as wealth that is well gotten.

**Verse 7.** *{The robbery of the wicked}* Their spoiling and destroying of others, shall turn upon themselves: it will be their own undoing, and destruction.

*{destroy them}* Heb. *saw them*. A fit word to express, the torments of an evil conscience, Acts 5:33. *διεπρίοντο*, they were cut to the heart: ad verbum; they were sawn: or the grievousness of their punishment: Heb. 11:37. *ἐπρίσθησαν* they were sawn asunder) or, dwell with them they shall have no rest, no respite, (when their time, the time of vengeance and just judgment, is once come;) from their misery.

*{because they refuse to do judgment}* Unjust judges are robbers then, who poll others, to maintain their lusts.

**Verse 8.** *{The way of man}* The wicked: or, a man left to himself, Gen. 8:21. & Job 15:14. Other, (and I think better) *Perversus via vir etiam alienus.* Junius also to the same purpose: *Quisquis via sua volubilis est, alienus est.* That is, in effect; that a man of a perverse conversation, cannot be religious.

**Verse 9.** *{It is better to dwell in a corner of the house top than with}, &c.* Chapter 19:13. & 25.24. & 27.15.

*{a brawling woman}* Heb. *a woman of contentions*: that is, a very contentious woman.

*{a wide house}* Heb. *an house of society*.

**Verse 10.** *{The soul of the wicked desireth evil}* James 4:5. It may be Solomon by these words, aims at those men, whom the Greek properly call *ἐπιχαιρεκάκους*; who rejoice at calamities, Chapter 17:5. who are so far from relieving a poor neighbor in his need, that they rejoice at his misery.

*{findeth no favour}* Heb. *is not favoured*.

**Verse 11.** *{When the scorner is punished the simple is made wise}* Chapter 19:25.

**Verse 12.** *{The righteous man wisely considereth the house of the wicked}* Observing God's judgments on wicked men's families, he takes heed of their wicked ways.

*{but}* Or, *how*. See upon Psalm 28:5. Because they regard not, &c.

**Verse 13.** *{Whoso stoppeth his ears at the cry of the poor he also}, &c.* Matt. 18:30. Isa. 58:7, 9.

*{he also shall cry himself but shall not be heard}* He shall cry as loud to God, in vain as the poor did to him.

**Verse 14.** *{A gift in secret pacifieth anger}* Chapter 17:8. and 18.16. To do a pleasure to an angry man, in secret, pacifies him.

**Verse 16.** *{The man that wandereth out of the way of understanding shall remain}, &c.* Heb. *ינוח* *quiescet, requiescet*; properly: that is, shall rest; and is opposed (wherein the elegancy of the sentence does chiefly consist) to his wandering in his lifetime. The Grecians also use to express proverbially the life of a worldly minded man, by *ῥέμβεσθαι*, to wander: and *ῥεμβὰς* a wanderer. See also upon Chapter 5. last verse, *he shall go astray*. Shall remain, &c. that is, he shall one day have rest enough in his grave, from whence he shall never rise to life. See upon Psalm 49 last verse; is like the beasts that perish.

*{Chapter} ***21.*** {verse} ***16.*** {shall remain in the congregation of the dead.}* Somewhat to that purpose we had before, Chapter 2:18. and Chapter 9:18. where we conceive *רפאים* the dead, (with most interpreters) to be but a synonymon of *שאול* the grave, or hell, of which word (*שאול*) there has enough been said upon Chap. 5. verse 5. and elsewhere. But the LXX. & that is, after them, the vulgar Latin: *in cœtu Gigantum*) translate it, *ἐν συναγωγῇ γιγάντων*: in the congregation (or company) of the Giants. And a very learned man, in a particular Diatriba, upon this very place, and text does endeavor to prove that *cœtus gigantum* in Scripture, is used for the place of the damned which we commonly call hell. He does examine many places of Scripture, and does make it very probable; as all things else, that he does undertake; such was his learning; and the strength of his brain and ratiocination: but among all these places of Scripture, that he does examine; I do not observe that he does take notice of those places, that make most against him, where the same word or words are used: besides what else might be objected. But I shall not enquire into it further at this time. It is enough that I give notice of it to my reader, who may make use of it as he shall see cause. All I shall further say, is that I have read him (which may be some degree of satisfaction to some perchance) and am not yet convinced, that it is otherwise than as I have delivered it, concerning the word *שאול* in the Scripture.

**Verse 17.** *{pleasure}* Or *sport*.

*{that loveth wine and oil}* Of oil, see upon Ps. 104:15. Oil to make his face to shine.

**Verse 18.** *{The wicked shall be a ransom for the righteous}* Chapter 11:8. God shall cause that to fall on the wicked, which was intended against the just, by delivering the just, and putting the wicked in their places.

**Verse 19.** *{It is better}, &c.* verse 9.

*{in the wilderness}* Heb. *in the land of the desert*.

**Verse 20.** *{and oil in the dwelling of the wise}* Meaning abundance of all things. Some translate *pinguedinem*, which the word does also signify. But it comes to one. Oil, anciently was much used, inwardly, and outwardly: and was not only a treasure itself; but a sign of plenty of all other goods, where it was plentiful.

*{but a foolish man spendeth it up}* His own treasure, not the godly man's.

**Verse 21.** *{followeth after righteousness and mercy findeth life}, &c.* Chapter 15:9.

**Verse 22.** *{A wise man scaleth the city of the mighty}* Eccles. 9:14. &c.

*{and casteth down the strength of the confidence thereof}* Wisdom overcomes strength, and stratagems in war win strong cities.

**Verse 23.** *{Whoso keepeth his mouth and his tongue keepeth his soul from troubles}* Chapter 12:13. & 18.21. See also Ps. 34:12, 13.

*{his tongue, keepeth his soul from trouble}* Which bring others into such straits, that like a man in a narrow room, they know not which way to turn themselves.

**Verse 24.** *{Proud and haughty scorner is his name, who dealeth in proud wrath.}* A man, though otherwise of a good nature, and plausible conversation, may be choleric, and subject to anger: but he that gives way to his anger; that cannot moderate himself in such a case, cannot avoid the doing and speaking of such things for which he shall be accounted proud, &c. he shall suffer in his reputation very much. The scorner is an abomination unto men, Chapter 24:9.

*{in proud wrath}* Heb. *in the wrath of pride*.

**Verse 25.** *{The desire of the slothful killeth him}* Because he cannot attain desired food, he vexes himself to death; and yet he will not work for it.

**Verse 26.** *{but the righteous giveth, and spareth not}* Psalm 112:9. Has enough for himself, and to relieve others daily, while the slothful man pines.

**Verse 27.** *{The sacrifice of the wicked is abomination}* Ps. 50:9. Chapter 15:8. Isa. 66:3. Jer. 6:20. Amos 5:22.

*{with a wicked mind?}* Heb. *in wickedness*. Either thinking to satisfy God thereby for his sin or that God will accept of it, notwithstanding his sin. Jer. 7:9, 10. Mic. 6:6, 7, 8. Or it may be, does offer (or pray:) that he may compass somewhat that is wicked and unlawful, himself does not think so, perchance: but it is his wickedness that does blind him; else, he would judge more rightly. Some make a contrary sense of the words: *etiam quando solerter illud offerunt*, that is, yea though they offer with great art, or skill. There be wicked men in the world, that know how to do it; to set out their devotions to the best advantage, in the eyes of men, that they may be thought religious and holy: but God will not be mocked, though he wink for a time. Others again; yea though he offer with a good will or mind; That too, is done sometimes. There be that are liberal enough of their goods to the poor, or to pious uses: but otherwise very vicious in several kinds. But they cannot honor God with their gifts, that dishonor him in their conversation. All this variety proceeds from the Hebrew word *זמה* which has different significations, and uses. But none so probable here, as the first, wickedness, or, a wicked mind:) nor so generally received.

**Verse 28.** *{A false witness}* Heb. *a witness of lies*.

*{A false witness shall perish}* Chapter 19:5, 9.

*{but the man that heareth speaketh constantly}* The true witness: that speaks no more than he hears himself, shall be left alive to bear witness again, when God has destroyed false witnesses, and shall be heard with credit.

**Verse 29.** *{directeth}* Heb. *יכין*: but some copies have it *יבין* (with a Beth) which signifies: *he considereth*: which seems to be more proper. It is the propriety of an ingenuous modest man, and one that makes a conscience of his ways, (opposed here to him that is hardened) to pause and consider.

**Verse 30.** *{There is no wisdom, nor understanding, nor counsel against the Lord}* Jer. 9:23.

**Verse 31.** *{The horse is prepared against the day of battle}* Psalm 33:17.

*{safety}* Or, *victory*.

*{but safety is of the Lord}* Psalm 3:8. There was no trusting in human policy, verse 30. nor strength, verse 31. Isay 30.1. & 31.1. See upon Psalm 33:16. There is no king saved by, &c.

## CHAPTER 22

**Verse 1.** *{A good name is rather to be chosen than great riches}* Eccles. 7:1. Credit and friendship is better than riches.

*{loving favour rather than}, &c.* Or, *favour is better than, &c*.

**Verse 2.** *{The rich and the poor meet together}* Chapter 29:13. Rich and poor must love one another, because they live together, have need one of another, lie together in the earth, and have the same Maker: for God made the rich to help the poor, and the poor to prove the rich, Deut. 15:11. Matt. 26:11.

*{The Lord is the maker of them all}* See upon Chapter 14:31. his Maker.

**Verse 3.** *{A prudent man foreseeth the evil}, &c.* Chapter 14:16. (See there:) and 27.12. The evil of punishment, and withdraws himself; but fools run desperately into danger.

**Verse 4.** *{By humility}, &c.* Or, *the reward of humility*, &c.

*{By humility and the fear of the Lord, are riches, and honour, and life}* Psalm 112:3.

**Verse 5.** *{Thorns and snares are in the way of the froward}* Chapter 15:19. See there.

**Verse 6.** *{Train up}* Or, *catechize*.

*{in the way he should go}* Heb. *in his way*. Bring him up virtuously, and he shall so continue. *Quo semel est imbuta recens*, &c. It is a common observation: yet it fails sometimes, which is the case of most of these moral observations; as has already been observed, more than once.

**Verse 7.** *{The rich ruleth over the poor}* Especially, if he lend him money; as follows in this verse.

*{and the borrower is a servant}* Greek, *δανειζόμενος*, &c. in many respects a debtor; (if a debtor in necessity) becomes a servant, (if not a slave) to his creditor. It needs no further illustration, than common experience: especially when the creditor himself (as ordinarily seen) is a slave unto Mammon, and says (as Job intended it; that is, by his actions;) unto his gold: *Thou art my confidence*. But in another respect also (besides which is ordinary;) Rabbi, Maimon, in his *Halachoth* (which is a subdivision of the books of his *Jad*, divided into fourteen books, and every book subdivided into halach. and every halach. into chapters) Sanhedr. cap. 6. would have the words of Solomon verified: because says he, it was in the power of the creditor to draw, or to compel (if he saw occasion) his creditor unto the Synedrium or judgment of the LXXII. judges, the power whereof see more in learned Master Selden, his III. de Synedr. cap. 11. p. 38.

*{to the lender}* Heb. *to the man that lendeth*.

**Verse 8.** *{He that soweth iniquity, shall reap vanity}* Chapter 21:4. Job 4:8. Hos. 10:13.

*{and the rod of his anger shall fail}* Or, *and with the rod of his anger he shall be consumed*. His wealth and authority, whereby he oppressed others, shall be taken from him, Isa. 14:5, 6. See also upon chapter 21:4. *The plowing of the wicked*.

**Verse 9.** *{He that hath a bountiful eye}* Heb. *good of eye*.

*{He that hath a bountiful eye shall be blessed}* 2 Cor. 9:6.

**Verse 10.** *{Cast out the scorner, and contention shall go out}* Ps. 101:5.

**Verse 11.** *{for the grace of his lips}* Or, *and hath grace in his lips*. Some with no less probability, He that continues pure of heart, his lips shall be grace, (or very gracious:) and *the King shall be his friend*: according to which, 2 fruits are here proposed of an upright conversation, and simplicity of heart: acceptable vein of words, and with it, (as very consequent:) the King's favor. This seems to be the plainer of the two.

*{the King shall be his friend}* Good princes use their familiarity, whose conscience is good, and their talk wise and godly. *Righteous lips, are the delight of Kings*, &c. Chapter 16:13. See there.

**Verse 12.** *{The eyes of the Lord preserve knowledge}* Favor them that love knowledge.

*{the words}* Or, *the matters*.

**Verse 13.** *{The slothful man saith, There is a lion without}* Chapter 26:13. See there.

*{There is a lion without}* He derides them that invent vain excuses, because they would not do their duty. Chapter 15:19.

**Verse 14.** *{The mouth of strange women is a deep pit}* Chapter 2:16; 5:3. and 7.5. and 23.27. See upon chapter 5:15. *Drink waters out of thine own cistern*, &c.

*{he that is abhorred of the Lord, shall fall therein}* So God punishes one sin by another, when he suffers the wicked to fall into the acquaintance of a harlot, Eccles. 7:26.

**Verse 15.** *{Foolishness is bound in the heart of a child}* He is naturally given to it, and can hardly be parted from it, Job 11:12. Hence *σύνδεσμος ἀδικίας*, the bond of iniquity, Acts 8:23.

*{the rod of correction shall drive it far from him}* Chapter 13:24. & 19.18. & 23.13. & 29.15,17.

**Verse 16.** *{and he that giveth to the rich, shall surely come to want.}* To increase his riches, as before. But most interpreters understand the word, as spoken of one man. He that oppresses the poor, and to that end bribes the rich, that he may do it securely, or to redeem himself, if he be called in question; a thing among worldly men, that have no conscience, ordinarily practiced: he that does so, shall not thrive long by it; except it be for his further confusion and condemnation in another world: to which other world, most of these sentences concerning temporal rewards and judgments have reference. See before. Chapter 2:7. *He layeth up sound wisdom*, &c. and upon Psalm 37:1. Fret not thyself, &c. Or, *He that giveth to the rich*: having met with his match at last, one more powerful than himself, who strips him of all that he has unjustly got from others whom he could master: as the greater fishes eat the less. So worldly men devour one another oftentimes, and through their unjust covetousness, become executioners of God's just judgments. Here Mercerus makes the Monostick sentences (that is, those that consist every one of one single verse,) to the end in this sentence. After this verse indeed we may observe some alteration of style and method. Yet there be many Monostick sentences in the following chapters too; especially, from Chapter 25. where Solomon's name is again prefixed; to Chapter 30. where the words of Agur begin.

**Verse 18.** *{if thou keep them within thee, they shall withal be fitted in thy lips}* For, *Out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaketh*, Matt. 12:24. and (as once before already;) *Bene dicendi, sapere principium & fons*: as both orators and philosophers teach. Others make it a double precept: *If thou keep them within thee*; *and canst make use of them skillfully, upon occasion, with thy lips*, &c.

*{within thee}* Heb. *in thy belly*.

*{they shall be fitted}* Or, *prepared*. See upon Chapter 15:28. The heart of the righteous.

**Verse 19.** *{That thy trust may be in the Lord}* He shows what the end of instruction is; to wit, To direct us to trust in the Lord.

*{even to thee}* Or, *trust thou also*.

**Verse 20.** *{excellent things in counsels and knowledge}* Hebrew, *שלישים*, that is, ducalia, or principalia, properly. See before upon Chapter 1:1. Others, retain the more original signification of the word and turn, terna or triplicia: (some, ter, or, tripliciter,) which is variously expounded by them. Some think that he alludes to that threefold division of the Old Testament among the Hebrews: The Law, the Prophets, and the Hagiographa, as they call them. Others, to the threefold division of wisdom, moral, natural, and theological: by them called, *מוסרים*, *בראשית*, and *מרכבה*. Others, that he intends his three books, to wit, Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, and Canticles. Many other such conjectures there be; but so weakly grounded all, that we stick to the first interpretation: much confirmed by Chapter 8:6. where there is a word equivalent to this. See as before: upon Chapter 1:1.

**Verse 21.** *{to them that send unto thee}* Or, *to those that send thee*. *That send unto thee*; to be resolved in doubtful cases, or hard questions. *That send thee*; that is, *whose instruction is committed unto thee*; or *those, to whom thou art sent*. To send, is a proper word; in this argument: the word Apostle, comes from it.

**Verse 22.** *{Rob not the poor}* Oppress not the poor by might, nor by false judgments.

*{neither oppress the afflicted in the gate}* Zech. 7:10. See also upon Psalm 127. last verse.

**Verse 23.** *{For the Lord will plead their cause}* Job 31:21. Chapter 23:11.

**Verse 24.** *{Make no friendship with an angry man}* Choose not him for your friend that is not able to rule his affections; for he would hurt you by his evil conversation: and if he be very angry by nature, (Heb. *a man of wraths*) come not in his company at all.

*{thou shalt not go}* Of the danger of evil company; See upon Psalm 26:4. I have not sat; and upon Chapter 4:16. unless they cause some to fall.

**Verse 26.** *{Be not thou one of them that strike hands}* Chapter 6:1; 11:15. Which rashly put themselves in danger, by suretyship for others.

**Verse 28.** *{Remove not the ancient landmark}* Deut. 19:14. & 27.17. Chapter 23:10.

*{landmark}* Or, *bound*.

**Verse 29.** *{he shall not stand before mean men}* Men that live as it were in the dark, and observed but by few; whereas princes are the light, and all men's eyes are upon them.

*{mean men}* Or, *obscure men*.

## CHAPTER 23

**Verse 1.** *{Consider diligently what is before thee}* Eat with sobriety and discretion.

*{what}* Or, *who*.

**Verse 2.** *{And put}* Or, *else thou puttest*.

*{And put a knife to thy throat}* Bridle your appetite, as it were, by force and violence. Or, whatsoever you say or do, be as careful, and circumspect, as if a sharp knife were set against your throat. Every man knows when he is under the barber's hand, and the razor is about the throat, it concerns him to be careful how to behave himself: Or, if with some others we translate, *Impones cultellum gutturi tuo*; that is, *Thou dost as good as put a knife to thy throat*, if, &c. the sense will be, You may endanger your life, if you give way to your greedy appetite.

*{if thou be a man given to appetite}* Heb. *אם בעל נפש* *Si dominus animæ*; that is, *If thou be master of a soul*. If we supply appetite, the meaning will be, as it is here expressed, *if thou be a man*, &c. But some supply, rational or intellective: and then the meaning will be, *If thou be wise*. But with Mercer, I rather incline to the first.

**Verse 3.** *{for they are deceitful meat}* For oftentimes, the rich, when they bid their inferiors to their tables, it is not for any love they bear to them, but for their own secret purposes. A court compliment.

*{deceitful meat}* Heb. *meat of deceitfulnesses*, that is, most deceitful meat.

**Verse 4.** *{Labour not to be rich}* 1 Tim. 6:9, 10. Weary not yourself to get riches, nor take more pains than your strength can bear.

*{cease from thine own wisdom}* Chapter 3:5. and lean not. Men that are covetous, and have the art to gather wealth, think themselves the only wise men of the world. All other wisdom that tends not to that end, they despise, Luke 16:14. but the Pharisees, &c.

**Verse 5.** *{Wilt thou set thine eyes upon}* Heb. *Wilt thou cause thine eyes to flee upon*.

*{upon that which is not? for riches certainly} &c.* See before upon chapter 2:7. *He layeth up sound wisdom*, &c. and upon c. 8.18. durable riches.

**Verse 6.** *{of him that hath an evil eye}* That is, of a covetous man, who looks sourly on his guests; as on the contrary, a man of a good eye, is a liberal man, chapter 22:9. for he looks cheerfully on his guests.

**Verse 7.** *{For as he thinketh in his heart so is he}* He is to be esteemed according to his heart, not according to his words, now he thinks ill towards you, though he speak kindly to you.

**Verse 8.** *{shalt thou vomit up}* He will do you some mischief for his good cheer.

*{and lose thy sweet words}* Which he has spoken to you; or your cheerful words at his table. Or, thus many studied compliments, wherein vain men please themselves very much, and think to please others.

**Verse 10.** *{Remove not the old landmark}* Deut. 19:14. & 27.17. Chapter 22:28.

*{landmark}* Or, *bound*.

*{enter not into the fields of the fatherless}* To take away their corn, or do them harm.

**Verse 11.** *{For their Redeemer is mighty}* Job 31:21. Chapter 22:23.

**Verse 13.** *{Withhold not correction from the child}* Chapter 13:14. and 19.18. & 22.15.

*{for if thou beatest him with the rod, he shall not die}* The rod used with reason, will not kill him; and it may save him from eternal death; or from death: which he may incur by law, if he continue in his wickedness. So before Chapter 19:18. *let not thy soul spare, to cause him to die*; that is, let not your indulgence be the cause of his death.

**Verse 14.** *{and shalt deliver his soul from hell}* From destruction both of body and soul. But of hell, how commonly taken in those days; See chapter 5:5. Take hold on hell: and Chapter 9:18. in the depth of hell: and elsewhere.

**Verse 15.** *{my heart shall rejoice}* Chapter 10:1. See there out of Plutarch.

*{even mine}* Or, *even I will rejoice*.

**Verse 17.** *{Let not thine heart envy sinners}* Ps. 37:1. & 73.3. Chapter 3:31. & 21. & 24.1.

*{fear of the Lord all the day long}* Chapter 28:14.

**Verse 18.** *{For surely there is an end}* Chapter 24:14. The prosperity of the wicked shall not continue long: or there is a reward for your piety in the end, Ps. 37:37, 38. & 58.11. See also before upon Chapter 10:28. The hope of the righteous——but the expectation, &c. and upon Chapter 19:20. *that thou mayest be wise in thy latter end*.

*{end}* Or, *reward*.

**Verse 19.** *{guide thine heart in the way}* In the observation of God's commandments: or in all your courses.

**Verse 20.** *{Be not amongst winebibbers}* Rom. 13:13. Eph. 5:8.

*{of flesh}* Heb. *of their flesh*. Or *rather, of flesh to themselves*: which to themselves, according to the Hebrew idiotism, does redundare, or abound: and it is accounted an elegancy in the Greek and Latin, when the pronoun does so. Of their flesh, therefore would be but an improper translation.

**Verse 22.** *{Hearken unto thy father that begat thee}, &c.* Chapter 1:8.

**Verse 23.** *{Buy the truth and sell it not}* Spare no cost for truth's sake, neither depart from it for any gain: be a merchant in buying it, but sell it not again for any pretended or promised gain, as they do their best wares.

**Verse 24.** *{The father of the righteous shall greatly rejoice}* Chapter 10:1. and 15.20. See upon v. 15.

**Verse 26.** *{Give me thine heart}* Give yourself wholly to wisdom, and to walk cheerfully in the ways that I prescribe unto you.

**Verse 27.** *{For an whore is a deep ditch}* Chapter 22:14. See upon chapter 5:15. Drink waters, &c.

*{a strange woman is a narrow pit}* So that a man cannot get out again.

**Verse 28.** *{She also lieth in wait as for a prey}* Chapter 7:12.

*{as for a prey}* Or, *as a robber*.

*{and increaseth the transgressors among men}* She seduces many, and causes them to offend God.

*{Who hath woe, who hath sorrow}* Some conceive these words *אוי* and *אבוי* To have the original acclamations in the Bacchica sacra; expressed by the Grecians, by *εὐοῖ*, and *σαβοῖ*; or at least to have given occasion unto them: very plausible I confess at first hearing. But when we shall consider that these (the Greeks:) were acclamations of great joy; these, (the Hebrew:) words of sorrow and lamentation; I doubt whether it will hold so plausible.

**Verse 29.** *{Who hath woe?}* Isa. 5:11.

**Verse 30.** *{mixt wine}* Ps. 75:8. Chapter 9:2.

**Verse 31.** *{When it moveth itself aright}* Heb. *when it goeth up and down in uprightnesses*: that is, when it sparkles, as the most generous wine uses to do.

**Verse 32.** *{an adder}* Or, *a cockatrice*.

**Verse 33.** *{Thine eyes shall behold strange women}* That is, drunkenness shall bring you to whoredom: or, make you see strange sights, by disturbing your brain. Hebr. *זרות*, may be translated (and is so by Mercerus, and others) strange things, as well as strange women. Strange things, say they; which has reference to that which follows, ver. 34. *Yea, thou shalt be as he that lieth down*, &c. See there.

*{women}* Or, *things*.

**Verse 34.** *{in the midst of the sea}* Heb. *in the heart of the sea,* Ezek. 27:4. Jonah 2:3. In such great danger will you be: or, roll up and down as ships upon the sea, Ps. 107:26, 27. Or, as some would have it, with reference to some former words; You shall fancy strange things, and think yourself to be *in the midst of the sea*, &c. And there is an old story to that purpose, mentioned by divers ancients, of some that were drunk in a room, and fancying themselves to be in a boat, in a great tempest, threw out of the windows whatsoever was in the room, to save themselves. But since the Psalmist compares them that are tossed in a tempest, to drunken men, Psalm 107:27. I should think it more likely, that drunkards are here compared to them that are tossed in a ship, by Solomon.

**Verse 35.** *{and I was not sick}* Drunkenness makes men senseless, so that they feel no blows.

*{I felt it not}* Heb. *I knew it not*.

*{when I shall awake, I will seek it yet again}* By these words Solomon does most emphatically set out the conditions and misery likewise, of practiced drunkards: who though many times they find the inconvenience of drunkenness, and are so sensible of it, for a while, that they purpose with themselves to leave it: yet their resolution commonly does not hold: they are no sooner out of the smart of their excess, but they begin to long again after their beloved trade and company. So Horace the poet, having represented one Alphius, an old usurer, highly extolling and admiring the happiness of a quiet country life, after a long discourse to that end, by which he does most sweetly set out the several pleasures of it, according to the several seasons of the year: in the end he concludes, *Hæc ubi locutus fœnerator Alphius, Jamjam futurus rusticus; Omnem relegit Idibus pecuniam: Quærit Kalendis ponere.* As soon as he had done speaking, (says he) as one fully resolved, with all possible speed, to turn countryman; he did earnestly bestir himself to get in all his money: but no sooner had he got it in, but his mind was changed, and he was as much, or more troubled, with all possible speed how to put it out again. It is also partly from a natural cause; because drunkenness they say, will make a man very thirsty: whence came the proverb, to add drunkenness to thirst, Deut. 29:19. of obstinate perseverance in any evil thing. See also Isa. 56:12. The words will bear another interpretation; or exposition rather, keeping the same translation. But I shall only propose it, because I have not met with it elsewhere. When I awake, were words in those days among the Hebrews proverbially used, of things that could not be understood, though carefully sought into, and considered of. Of which, see upon Psalm 139:18. *when I awake, I am still with thee*. See Vatablus his notes there. See also Mark 4:27. should sleep, &c. *and grow up, he knoweth not how*. And would not this be very proper to that which goes before, of the strange effects of wine: To make this stupified ecstatical man conclude with it: That his understanding is lost in the consideration of those strange things that have happened unto him in his excess of wine: That he must rest in astonishment, and pass the rest in silence. The words for ought I know, are as proper to this sense, as to the former. However, I dislike not the form, nor command this to be received, but only to be considered of.

## CHAPTER 24

**Verse 1.** *{Envious against evil men}* Ps. 37:1, &c. and 73.3, &c. See there. Chapter 23:17. verse 19.

**Verse 2.** *{For their heart studieth destruction}* Though to others immediately, yet to themselves also in effect; yea, worse to themselves, than they can bring upon others. See upon Chapter 16:26. *craveth it of him*. The second interpretation, and Hesiod's words there; of mischief intended to others. See upon chapter 4:16. unless, they cause some to fall.

**Verse 3.** *{Through wisdom is an house builded}* The building of a house, is a thing that requires good deliberation and judgment; for want of which, many by such attempts have been ruined, Luke 14:28. For which of you intending to build a house, &c. But by a house here, with best expositors may also be understood, whatsoever does properly belong to a house: and housekeeping, as competent wealth a good wife &c.

**Verse 4.** *{And by knowledge shall the chambers be filled}* *חדרים* which some translate *interiora*, or, *conclavia*: that is, secret places, that are for store and provision; as granaries, barns, &c. though outwardly, (and therefore called outhouses:) seated.

**Verse 5.** *{A wise man is strong}* His wisdom is to him instead of strength, and he can do that by wisdom, which cannot be done by strength, Chapter 21:22. Eccl. 9:16.

*{is strong}* Heb. *is in strength*.

*{increaseth strength}* *strengtheneth might*.

**Verse 6.** *{For by wise counsel thou shalt make thy war}* Chapter 20:18. See there: and 11.14. and 15.22.

**Verse 7.** *{Wisdom is too high for a fool}* Heb. *wisdoms*: that is, the most excellent wisdom, as Chapter 9:1.

*{too high for a fool}* Chapter 14:16. *A scorner seeketh wisdom, and findeth it not*: whereby it appears, that a fool, and a scorn, are put for the same in this book: as a fool, and a wicked man; as has been showed, and why.

*{too high}* There is an Aleph in the word more than should be: which makes some expositors to interpret it, coral; as Job 28:18. and Ezek. 27:16. But there be many examples of such superfluous supernumerary letters: and the sense however will come to one.

*{he openeth not his mouth in the gate}* In place of judicature, where wisdom should be showed: Ruth 4:1. See Chap. 8. verse 3.

**Verse 8.** *{shall be called a mischievous person}* Heb. *a master of mischievous devices*: that is, the most mischievous person. Let him plot never so cunningly, and secretly, in hope to keep his reputation among men (which is the endeavor of most wicked men;) yet he shall be known, and men will speak of him, if not to his face, (because of his power) yet among themselves and behind his back.

**Verse 9.** *{The thought of foolishness is sin}* Taking foolishness, for wickedness, (as usually.) The meaning will be, that the very thought of wickedness though it proceed no further than bare thought, is sin. *Nam scelus intra se tacitus qui cogitat ullum, facti crimen habet*, and divers heathens have said the same. Or, if we take thought here for deed, (as is ordinary:) then the meaning may be that every wickedness is a sin; that is, hated by God, and liable to his wrath. For so is sin taken sometimes; whereof we have had examples formerly. And to this sense, the opposite member will answer very appositely: so that the meaning of the whole verse will be, that wickedness is abominable both to God and man, or (which comes to one) that wicked men expose themselves to the hatred both of God and man.

**Verse 10.** *{If thou faint in the day of adversity}* Man has no trial of his strength, till he be in trouble: faintness then discovers weakness. Some, (so Mercerus for one) join the words thus; *If thou beest remiss*, (to wit, in time of prosperity; that you do not then by applying your heart unto wisdom, provide against adversity) *thy strength will be but small in time of adversity*. The sense is good both ways; and the writings of ancient philosophers are full of admonitions and instructions to that purpose. But there be also, who make this verse to cohere with the next; *If thou beest slack, or remiss*, towards others, to their relief in their need) *when thou thyself shalt be in distress thou shalt find thy strength but small*. *For whoso stoppeth his ear at the cry of the poor*, &c. Chapter 21:13. See there.

*{small}* Heb. *narrow*.

**Verse 11.** *{If thou forbear to deliver them that are drawn unto death}* Psalm 82:4. Isa. 58:6, 7. See also upon Psalm 70. Those that are appointed to die.

**Verse 12.** *{doth not he that pondereth the heart consider it?}* None can be excused, if he help not the innocent when he is in danger, having ability and opportunity so to do, Chapter 31:8, 9. for God observes his neglect, to punish him. See Job 7:20. Jer. 17:9, 10.

*{keepeth}* Or *observeth*.

*{according to his works?}* Job 34:11. Ps. 62:12. Jer. 32:19. Rom. 2:6. Rev. 22:12.

**Verse 13.** *{eat thou honey}* This must not be taken as a command, but as a similitude, only grounded upon common practice; as if he said; as honey is sweet and pleasant to the taste of most men; so, &c. as follows in the next verse. Comparisons taken from the sweetness of honey, are very frequent in all authors.

*{to thy taste}* Or, *upon thy palate*.

**Verse 14.** *{So shall the knowledge of wisdom be unto thy soul}* Ps. 19:10. and 119.103.

*{then there shall be a reward}* Chapter 22:18. See there, and upon Chapter 2:7. *He layeth up sound wisdom*, &c.

**Verse 16.** *{For a just}* Or, *For though a just*.

*{and riseth}* Or, *yet he riseth*.

**Verse 16.** *{For a just man falleth seven times}* These words have been understood by many both Papists and Protestants, of falling into sin, or by sin; (which we may call a spiritual fall:) according to that of the Apostle, *Let him that thinketh he standeth, take heed lest he fall*, 1 Cor. 10:12. and accordingly applications, have been made in dispute of the words by many; but by as many or more since of all sides so opposed, that it is in some manner forsaken. *Cave de peccatis hæc accipias*, says Mr. Grotius here. The objections against it, are: First that the original word *נפל* to fall, is not taken so, in this whole book; some say not in the whole Scripture, (written in Hebrew:) but of temporal losses and calamities only. Secondly, that that sense does not agree with the context; either antecedent, or following: which indeed (as I apprehend it) is the most material objection: which makes me to forbear the further examination of the former. See also the next observation.

*{falleth seven times}* That is, often; as is ordinary in Scripture. So that it may very well be thought, that Solomon by these words intended the same as David, where he says, *Many are the afflictions of the righteous, but the Lord delivereth him out of them all*, Ps. 34:20. and by Job (a great confirmation to that interpretation commended unto us by later expositors,) speaking of the righteous: *He shall deliver thee in six troubles, yea in seven*. (which is as much as if he had said seven only: as appears by Proverbs, Chapter 30:15, 18, 21. compared with verse 24.) *there shall no evil touch thee*. *Touch thee*; that is, (as I understand it) take such hold of you, that you shall not be delivered. And this word seven times here; puts me in mind of Luke 17:4. *And if he* (thy brother,) *trespass against thee seven times in a day, and seven times in a day turn again unto thee*, &c. It is likely that these words might induce some to understand Solomon's words of spiritual falling, the rather; which we have the more reason to believe, because it is very probably conceived by some, that from them, those words in a day were intruded into some copies of the Latin vulgar, in this place, which are not in the original, nor in any other translation that I know of.

*{and riseth up again}* Though you bring him down often by your wicked devices, yet God will raise him up again still, but you shall fall for good and all.

*{For a just man falleth seven times, and riseth up again}, &c.* Job 5:19. Ps. 34:19. and 37.24.

**Verse 17.** *{Rejoice not when thine enemy falleth}* Job 31:29. Ps. 35:15. chapter 17:5. See upon Ps. 35:4. Let them be confounded, &c. concerning these very words. But divers other places of Scripture may be objected against this precept or counsel: as for example, Ps. 58:10. The righteous shall rejoice, when he seeth the vengeance; &c. and many other like. To this we answer, that there be private enemies, upon private quarrels and differences; and public enemies in a public cause, whether of state or religion or both. Upon the falling of a public enemy, there is no question but we may rejoice; nay, must, in some measure, in thankfulness to God that has delivered us: It is no small part of that thankfulness, which he does expect at our hands. But yet even then, our joy should rather be in reference to God's glory, (often not a little interested in our deliverance) and the public good; than out of any pleasure we take at the miseries of others, who though our enemies, must still be remembered as God's creatures, of the same flesh and blood, as we; descended of the same first-father Adam, and in that respect, (even whilst we rejoice at their overthrow, because of the consequence) an object of compassion, in their particular persons. As for particular enemies, though we must be thankful to God for them too, if they were our enemies without cause, and such as might do us hurt; yet our joy must be very moderate and wary; and if we have any generosity at all, their fall will make us to forget our wrongs, (if they have done us any) and rather dispose us to help them in their misery, than (as it does some base natures) provoke revenge.

**Verse 18.** *{it displease him}* Heb. *it be evil in his eyes*.

*{and he turn away his wrath from him}* To be avenged on you. Some conceive that *upon thee*, is sufficiently included in the original *מעליו* which is not without some reason: and therefore as I conceive ought to have been supplied in the very context, *from him upon thee*: there being many such supplements in translations, upon less ground of words, and less need as to the sense. For without this supplement, the words may seem to allow, though not outward rejoicing, and exultation, yet inward contentment of mind for the sufferings of our enemies: and a desire (which is most uncharitable) of their continuation.

**Verse 19.** *{Fret not thyself because of evil men}* Ps. 37:1. Chapter 23:17. Or, *keep not company with the wicked*.

**Verse 20.** *{For there shall be no reward to the evil man}* No good reward: for reward: in Hebrew it is end, because the reward is not given till the work be ended, and the general reward will be in the end of the world.

*{the candle of the wicked shall be put out}* Job 21:17. Chapter 13:9. See upon Chapter 21:4. *the plowing of the wicked*.

*{candle}* Or, *lamp*.

**Verse 21.** *{My son, fear thou the Lord, and the king}* First, the Lord, and then the king: so that if kings command that which God has forbidden, God must be obeyed before men, though kings.

*{meddle not with}* Or, *mix not thyself with*.

*{them that are given to change}* Heb. *changers*. *Cum rebellibus & seditiosis*; as Mercer here; with rebels, and seditious persons; whose proper work has always been, rebus novis studere, (as the common Latin phrase expresses it) to endeavor changes and alterations in a commonwealth, the benefit whereof they reap.

**Verse 22.** *{For their calamity shall rise suddenly}* Though sometimes put off for a long time; yet when it comes, it usually comes very suddenly.

*{who knoweth the ruin of them both?}* Of them that fear not God, and of them that fear not the king: or proceeding both from God and the king. There may be also some allusion in the original words between *שונים* and *שניהם* which although it add nothing to the sense; yet it makes the words to run more sweetly in their natural channel.

**Verse 23.** *{These things also belong to the wise}* Some would have here a new division, and make an inscription of these words: These also are [sayings, or sentences] of some that have been wise: or to that effect. By this, Solomon's proverbs should end here. But how can that be, when the very next chapter does begin with a new inscription bearing Solomon's name? Except they would distinguish of proverbs, which Solomon himself left, compiled into a book: and these, being his also, which were gathered after his death. But there is no need, the words may tend unto this, to make that which follows the more considerable, as indeed nothing can be more, than what belongs to the administration of justice.

*{It is not good to have respect of persons in judgment}* Lev. 19:15. Deut. 1:17. & 16.19. Chapter 18:5. & 28.21. John 7:24.

**Verse 24.** *{He that saith unto the wicked, Thou art righteous, him shall the people curse}* Chapter 17:15. Isaiah 5:23.

**Verse 25.** *{a good blessing}* Heb. *a blessing of good*.

**Verse 26.** *{Every man shall kiss his lips that giveth a right answer}* Or, *he that giveth a right answer, kisseth the lips*: that is, shows himself a friend. See upon Chapter 7:13. and kissed him.

*{that giveth a right answer}* Hebrew, *that answereth right words*.

**Verse 27.** *{and afterwards build thine house}* Be sure of the means how to compass it, before you take any enterprise in hand, Luke 14:28, &c.

*{thy work}* We may understand of the materials of a house, as stone: timber, &c. which if provided in time, and ready, before workmen be entertained; will save both money, and trouble, not a little. Others, by *thy work without*, understand matters of husbandry; which should indeed, (as the most necessary of the two) be well provided for, and settled; before we think of building of houses.

**Verse 28.** *{against thy neighbour without cause}* Heb. *חנם*, that is, for nothing; or without a cause. The sense may be; Do not offer yourself, and show yourself forward; rather avoid it, if you can. Or, *without cause*; that is, where there is no just ground; against your own conscience: that is, in effect; be not a false witness: and so is the word here translated by some of the best interpreters.

*{and deceive not with thy lips}* Or rather, (with Mercer, and other) *and entice not with thy lips*: that is, Neither be you yourself; nor suborn any other to be a false witness: which many will do when themselves are loath to appear. Some Rabbins would have the original word *והפתית* to come from *פתת*, not *פתה*: and so the sense will be; *Bruise not thy neighbor with thy lips, by bearing false witness against him*.

**Verse 29.** *{Say not, I will do so to him as he hath done to me}* Chapter 20:22. See the reference there.

*{I will render to the man according to his work}* He shows what is the nature of the wicked, To revenge wrong with wrong, Math. 5.39. Rom. 12:17.

**Verse 32.** *{considered it well}* Heb. *set my heart*.

*{and received instruction}* I learned by another man's damage, to look well to mine own affairs.

**Verse 33.** *{Yet a little sleep}, &c.* Chapter 6:9. &c. See there.

**Verse 34.** *{poverty}* Heb. *poverties*.

*{want}* Heb. *wants*.

*{an armed man}* Heb. *a man of shield*.

## CHAPTER 25

**Verse 1.** *{These are also Proverbs of Solomon, which the men of Hezekiah}* Here begins a new collection of Solomon's proverbs, made by some, appointed by the king Hezekiah; whether those mentioned, 2 Kings 18:18. (as some are of opinion) or any others. Divers studious princes, we know, among the later emperors of Greece especially, have had their Eclogarii, whose office was, by certain heads and commonplaces, to gather out of several books, what was thought most useful and profitable. It is likely that these proverbs were collected out of some writings of Solomon, then extant, (as doubtless there were many) which he had written since his own collection of proverbs.

*{copied out}* Heb. *העתיקו*, by most interpreted, transcripserunt; that is, copied out; as we find it here. The word does properly signify the removing of a thing, from one place to another: as Job 14:18. & 18.4. and the rock is removed out of his place: *וצור יעתק ממקומו* And so here the vulgar Latin expresses it, transtulerunt. Upon which and Chapter 9:1, 2, &c. *Wisdom hath builded her house*, &c. *She hath killed her beasts*, &c. is grounded Salazar, (one that has taken great pains upon this book) his opinion, which though perchance more acute, than probable, yet because it has no absurdity, and may please many, I will impart unto the reader. His conceit is, that Solomon wrote this book of proverbs in the manner and method of a *Συμπόσιον*, or banquet-meeting; of which kind of writing we have some examples in some ancient books, as Plato, Plutarch, and some others: where some learned men meeting together, speak by turns, upon some learned subject: nothing differing from ordinary dialogues, (a way of writing then much in use) but that it was at table, when they did both eat and speak. The collocutors here represented, he would have to be, Solomon, Agur and Lemuel: and this to be the place, according to Solomon's own order and method, where they should come in: to wit, immediately after the 24. chapter; and then after them, Solomon the chief speaker, and master of the feast, to come in again, and conclude all, from Chap. 25. (as now digested, but Chap. 27. according to that order they would have) It is the glory of God, &c. to the end of Chapter 29. An unjust man is an abomination to the just, &c. This to have been the order, till Hezekiah's time, at which time, to the end that all that was Solomon's might be together, Agur and Lemuel, their parts, to have been cast at the end, as now they are; and this to be the proper meaning of this *העתיקו* transtulerunt, that is, they did remove. But how this will stand with the interpretation of that word Lemuel, which by most is received, I must leave to Salazar himself to answer: It cannot be denied but there is somewhat plausible in this conceit: the reader may be pleased to consider further of it, there is no heresy in it if he like it.

**Verse 2.** *{It is the glory of God to conceal a thing}* God does not reveal the cause of his judgments to man: neither does he need any counselors, to advise him. Isa. 40:13. Jer. 23:18. Rom. 11:34. His judgments are unsearchable: to be adored by men, though not understood.

*{the honour of kings is to search out a matter}* Though kings oftentimes by a special providence, are endowed with extraordinary parts of wit and judgment, (whereof see upon Chapter 16:10. A divine sentence, &c.) above ordinary men; yet it is an honor unto a king, not to affect to be like unto God, in giving no account unto any of his actions, nor using any counsel: but the contrary; to advise with them that are wise; and to enquire diligently, before he resolve. Besides, an honor to a king too, not to trust others altogether, but himself to be present and assistant, at the search and examination of things; to judge himself and deliver his opinion, according to the quality of the cause.

**Verse 3.** *{and the heart of kings is unsearchable}* He shows that it is too hard a task for a subject to attain to a reason of all the secret doings of the king, even when he is upright, and does his duty.

*{is unsearchable}* Heb. *there is no searching*.

**Verse 4.** *{Take away the dross from the silver}* When vicious persons are removed from the king, he is a meet vessel for the Lord's use.

**Verse 5.** *{Take away the wicked from before the king}, &c.* Chapter 20:8. It is not enough that a king be upright himself, but he must also put away from him bad counselors and servants, if he desire to have his throne firm.

**Verse 6.** *{Put not forth thyself}* Heb. *set not out thy glory*.

*{and stand not in the place of great men}* If you are none of them.

**Verse 7.** *{For it is better that it be said unto thee, Come up hither}, &c.* Luke 14:10.

*{whom thine eyes have seen}* For as it was an honor to you to be admitted into the king's presence; so your dishonor will be the greater, because it is in his sight.

*{whom thine eyes have seen}* An expression (among Eastern people,) of great favor and honor done to a subject and inferior, from a king or prince. And hence the *Satrapæ* (or noble Persians) had their denomination from this privilege, of beholding their king's face at all times, as learned men in those languages do suppose. To this also is alluded by Christ, Math. 18.10. their Angels always behold the face of my Father which is in heaven.

**Verse 8.** *{Debate thy cause with thy neighbour himself}* Matt. 5:25. & 18.15.

*{discover not a secret to another}* Or, *discover not the secret of another*. Let not others, through your contention, become privy to your secrets; which may afterwards prove prejudicial to you both. But especially take heed, that through heat of strife and contention you do not discover your friend's secret; which when known, will be an eternal blot unto you; all wise men will abhor such a one.

**Verse 10.** *{Lest he that heareth it, put}* Or, *Lest he hearing*, put.

*{put thee to shame}* By passing sentence against you, and so you are ashamed forever.

**Verse 11.** *{A word fitly spoken is like apples of gold in pictures of silver}* Chapter 15:23. Some kind of ornament used in those days; whether about beds, tables, or houses; or somewhat by itself is not known; nor much material. The conjunction of gold and silver together, is also mentioned, Cant. 1:11. *borders of gold, with studs of silver*: which must needs be very beautiful to the eye: but the curious inlaying, and artificious interlacing, is the thing here chiefly aimed at, in this similitude. Some translate, *in reticulis argenteis*; that is, in network coverings made of silver: others, *in theca*: the Hebrew word *משכיות* may bear it.

*{fitly spoken}* Heb. *spoken upon his wheels*.

**Verse 12.** *{wise reprover upon an obedient ear}* Chapter 9:9.

**Verse 13.** *{As the cold of snow in the time of harvest, so is a faithful messenger}, &c.* Chapter 13:17.

*{in the time of harvest}* In the time of great heat, when men have need of cooling.

**Verse 14.** *{of a false gift}* Heb. *in a gift of falsehood*.

*{is like clouds and wind without rain}* Which have an outward appearance, and are nothing within: such are covetous men, that would be thought to be liberal. Or vain men that promise much, but perform little. It may be they have it not; but their ambition to be thought somebody, makes many to undertake freely, beyond either power, or purpose.

*{clouds and wind without rain}* Jude verse 12.

**Verse 15.** *{By long forbearing is a prince persuaded}* Gen. 32:4. &c. 1 Sam. 25:24. &c. Chapter 15:1. & 16.14. By not ministering occasion to provoke him further.

*{a soft tongue breaketh the bone}* That is, the heart that is bent to anger, and hardest to be pacified.

**Verse 16.** *{eat so much as is sufficient for thee}* Use moderately the pleasures of this world: or, go not too often to your neighbor's house, as verse 17.

**Verse 17.** *{Withdraw thy foot from thy neighbour's house}* Or, *let thy foot be seldom in thy neighbour's house*.

*{weary of thee}* Heb. *full of thee*.

**Verse 18.** *{A man that beareth false witness against his neighbour, is a maul, and a sword and a sharp arrow}* Ps. 120:4. Chapter 12:18. See the note upon Chapter 24:28. and deceive not, &c.

*{Confidence in an unfaithful man}* Or, *The confidence of an unfaithful man* (that is, a transgressor, a wicked man;) in time of trouble, &c. to which purpose we have divers proverbs before. Or, *A false, (or, deceitful) confidence in time of trouble*. The Hebrew will bear either of these, as well as the first; and the sense will not be amiss, let us take which we will. If this last please, we must suppose the words to have reference to riches especially, which are properly styled deceitful (the deceitfulness of riches, Mark 4:9. and the Mammon of unrighteousness, Luke 16:9, 11. that is, deceitful, as before noted) and of which it was said before, *riches profit not in the day of wrath*. Chapter 11:4. besides other proverbs to the same purpose.

*{like a broken tooth and a foot out of joint}* That makes a show, but is of no use at all.

**Verse 20.** *{As he that taketh away a garment in cold weather}* This sentence does admit of two contrary senses and interpretations; of which, which is the truest, when both the words, and the nature of the thing will admit of either, is hard to determine. We shall therefore propose both, and leave the choice to the reader. But first of niter and vinegar it must be known; that anciently they were often, and upon several occasions (as by the testimonies of several ancients, diligently collected by Salazar, and some others, does appear:) joined together: and in some cases, vinegar adds virtue; in others, it takes away from the niter. Now the Hebrew word here used *מעדה* as it does signify to put on, or adorn: so also, to take away. The sense therefore may be: As he that puts on a garment in cold weather, and tempers niter (as in divers cases it must, to make it operative, and physical: or tempers it, to take away the force of it, which Aristotle says it does, being of a contrary nature:) with vinegar; *so is he that singeth songs to a heavy heart*; that is, in effect: as those things, are most proper, and seasonable; so is music, or songs, &c. That music, in general; and songs, particularly, that are fitted to that end, to comfort and to revive, may do much with many (for all are not of one temper) to allay sorrow; besides what we read in the Scripture to that purpose, as of Saul, 1 Sam. 16:24. and of others elsewhere; daily experience may sufficiently satisfy. This sense is followed by Junius, and some Rabbins: approved also by Cornelius a Lapide, and some others. But as we said before, all men are not of one temper, nor equally affected to, and with music: nor all music of one kind: and there be degrees of sorrow too. As true therefore, (to be generally spoken) that music, or singing of songs, (which for the most part are a testimony of a light heart:) is very unseasonable to a heavy heart: and to this effect, it seems were Solomon's words understood by Sirach, the author of Ecclesiasticus: of whose imitation of Solomon, we have spoken in the preface. For so he: A tale out of season, is as Music in mourning. Chapter 22:6. The ancient Grecians also had a proverb to this very purpose, as by Drusius upon this very place is very well observed: *Οὐ ταῦτα κωμῳδοῖσιν*, *οὐ λύρᾳ φίλα*. And Plutarch in his *Symposia*, or Table talks, excepts against Euripides for his contrary judgment. Symp. 7. Quæst. 7. as I find him cited by the same Drusius, in the same place.

**Verse 21.** *{If thine enemy be hungry, give him bread to eat: and if he be thirsty, give him water to drink}* Exodus 23:4. Romans 12.20.

**Verse 22.** *{For thou shalt heap coals of fire upon his head}* Psalm 140:10. You shall bring God's vengeance on his head, and God's blessing on your own. So most interpreters, both upon this place; and upon Romans 12.20. where this proverb is used: or rather, this very passage, cited by the Apostle. So most, later interpreters; and so divers, if not most ancients too, collected by Salazar upon this place. But this seems to be too uncharitable an interpretation, and contrary to what in this very book of Proverbs, Chapter 20:22. & 24.29. and elsewhere, (whereof see more upon Psalm 26. *So will I compass thine altar*, &c. and Psalm 35:4. Let them be confounded, &c.) is commanded. For what greater revenge, or more malicious hatred can there be, than for a man either to wish or to use means, that his enemy may incur the wrath of God; the utmost of evils, and most direful imprecations? Divers things I know, are answered by divers; but nothing that satisfies me so much, as Saint Chrysostom's answer; which is, that Solomon and after him Saint Paul's intention was not by these words to allow, much less to command such vindicative policy; but because most men in the heat of their anger, are incapable of any other reason or consideration, but what tends to revenge; this by way of condescension is proposed unto them for the time, that if they must needs be revenged, and nothing else will content them, this is the only way to turn the wrath of God from themselves upon their enemies, not to revenge ourselves, but to leave them to God, and rather to requite good for evil: that afterwards when the edge of their anger, and the danger of their fury, is taken away; they may be persuaded to do that out of charity, which before they did out of mere policy. But if there be danger in this interpretation, as certainly there is, and matter of scandal too; why should not we rather prefer that which may be as proper to the words, and much safer, for the sense? Which is according to Saint Austin's golden rule, whereof he gives an instance, these very words of Solomon: *Si præceptiva locutio est*, says he, *aut flagitium aut facinus vetans*, &c. that is, If it be a precept forbidding any lewdness, or wickedness, commanding something that is profitable or beneficial; there is no figure in the words, if otherwise it is, a figure. For example, *If thine enemy*, &c. water to drink. Here goodness and bounty is enjoined: no figure here therefore. But in the next verse; *For thou shalt heap coals*, &c. here malice seems to be commanded: Doubt not therefore but that the words are figurative; and whereas they admit of two several interpretations; the one, tending to evil; the other to good: let your charity rather embrace that which is to good; that these coals of fire you may understand to be nothing else, but the burning groans of hearty repentance, whereby his pride is healed, who grieves with himself, that he has been his enemy, who was so good as to endeavor his good, whilst he did him evil; So Saint Augustine. Others by coals of fire, understand such, by which the fire of love and charity is kindled in the heart. Did not our hearts burn: Luke 24:22. in a different sense, it is true, but that too, figurative; and in a good sense too. Of a live coal, taken from the altar, to take away iniquity, we read Isa. 6:6, 7. And the word *חתה* here used, seems to have particular relation to the altar, or censer, which from thence was called *מחתה*. But of this metaphor, of coals of fire, that the words may be taken as well in a good as in a bad sense, there is no question. All that can be objected is, why these coals should be said to be heaped upon the head, which sounds more like an imprecation, (*εἰς κεφαλήν σοι*, the ancient Grecians were wont to say;) than in the bosom, (as before Chapter 6:7.) or heart; the proper seat of love and charity. Some think the ground of the speech might be, a way that ancient physicians had to cure apoplexies and frenzies, that proceeded from cold causes, by applying coals to the head. Saint Augustine indeed, in one of his sermons, seems to apprehend it so. Others would have it a comparison taken from founders, or casters of metal, who by adding much fire, will make the hardest iron, or steel at last to melt. A probable conjecture; but that it does not satisfy, why the head particularly, not the heart, or any other part, should be mentioned. But it is a very difficult thing, to give a reason of old proverbs, when at some times some extraordinary chance, for which no reason can be given, but that it has so happened, is enough to give occasion to a proverb. If a house both a great and high, be on fire, coals and cinders, as I have seen it myself, will fly very far, so that if a man be near, he shall be forced to stand further off, or to cover his head with somewhat, more than ordinary; as I think Pliny does relate of himself, or his uncle, in the burning of mount Vesuvius. Some such thing spoken of some one at first, that was not willing to go from his house, though on fire, till the coals fell so thick upon his head, that he must either burn or go; some such thing, I say, might be occasion enough to make this a proverbial speech, applicable to them, who in a manner against their wills are forced to do somewhat or other, which of themselves they are most averse from. Such a thing is, when a malicious enemy's obstinacy is overcome with goodness, and unexpected kindness. And does not Saint Paul himself interpret it so, when he adds presently, (which to me seems to make the business very clear,) Be not overcome of evil, but overcome evil with good? How is evil overcome with good, but when the malice of an implacable (of himself) enemy is overcome; so that of a foe, he becomes our friend? Besides, there be passages in divers ancient authors, of best account, so pat and pregnant to this very sense, of overcoming the most obstinate and malicious, by constant unconquerable goodness; that it is very likely the same thing might be intended by Solomon also. There is a very pregnant passage in Xenophon to that purpose. But I will content myself with one, out of a Greek author too, but already done in English to my hands: Consider, (says he, exhorting himself to goodness, and patience,) that meekness is a thing unconquerable, (or, irresistible; that is his meaning,) if it be true and natural, and not affected (or, not scoffingly) or hypocritical. For how shall even the most fierce and malicious be able to hold on against you, if you shall still continue meek and loving unto him and that even at that time when he is about to do you wrong, you shall be well disposed and in good temper with all meekness to teach and instruct him better? Not so my son, &c. So he, Seneca: *Vincit malos pertinax bonitas, nec quisquam tam duri infestique adversus diligenda animi est, ut etiam vi tractus bonos non amet*: that is, Pertinacious goodness will overcome evil men: neither is any man so rigid and perverse against that which of itself is lovely, that even when he is drawn by force he should not love them that are good. Among later interpreters, (though most, as has been said, for the contrary,) yet Mercer is for this interpretation: and likewise Drusius in his Hebrew Proverbs, Classe 11. l. 1. *Beneficentia erga inimicos.* See also upon Ps. 35:4. Let them be confounded, &c. And as for that main objection, why the head rather than any other part, which makes it to sound as an imprecation; whatever the Greek phrase may be; yet in the Hebrew, in matter of blessings, as well as curses and imprecations, we find the head mentioned as in this very book of Proverbs, Chapter 10:6. blessings are upon the head of the just, and 11.26. but blessing, &c. It may be also that upon his head, is no more than upon him: which is not without example. See more upon Ps. 140:9, 10.

**Verse 23.** *{The North wind driveth away rain}* Job 37:22.

*{The North wind driveth away rain: so doth an angry countenance a backbiting tongue}* Or, *The north wind bringeth forth rain*: *so doth a backbiting tongue an angry countenance*. This, as to the second part, or member of the verse, I take to be the truest translation, because *לשון סתר* here, seems to answer to *צפון* in the first part, the North, which in Hebrew comes from a root that signifies to hide. And certain it is, that the Hebrew *המחולל*, may be translated, *bringeth, or begetteth*, as well as *driveth away*. It signifies both. But then the question will be, how the North wind can be said to bring out rain, which by most authors, (as by Homer, and divers others) is said to drive it away. Some Rabbins tell us, that it does so in Jerusalem, because of the sea, though in most parts of the world otherwise. They say so, but contradicted by some other Rabbins: *neque ego crediderim*, says Mercer; and so say I too. If then we translate, as we think most probable, *The Northwind driveth away the rain, so doth a secret tongue* (for that is the proper signification of the Hebrew words) *an angry countenance*: by a secret tongue, we must understand, a soft, insinuating, close; opposed to a rough and plain tongue: to the same purpose, as before verse 15. and a soft tongue, &c. and Chapter 15:1. *A soft answer turneth away wrath*, &c. and Chapter 21:14. A gift in secret (*בסתר*: as here *לשון סתר*) *pacifieth anger*, &c. So secret love, Chapter 27:5.

**Verse 24.** *{It is better to dwell in a corner of the housetop than with a brawling woman and in a wide house}* Chapter 19:13. and 21.9.

**Verse 25.** *{from a far country}* I see no antithesis in the words which should make Solomon, (as Mercer would have it) though he intended near, as well as far, rather to say far. I take his meaning to be, *from a far country*, that is, altogether unexpected; and the more unexpected, the more welcome.

**Verse 26.** *{is as a troubled fountain and a corrupt spring}* Which is a thing to be grieved at by all men, as being a public mischief. Or rather, *falling*, not to be understood of any outward calamity; but *falling*, that is, yielding to him through timorousness, in an evil thing, when he should generously have opposed him: compared before to a well of life Chapter 10:11. for his good instruction, and other good fruits of his mouth.

**Verse 27.** *{good}* Or, *hurtful*, Chapter 10:2.

*{to eat much honey}* verse 16.

*{so for men to search their own glory is not glory}* Heb. *וחקר כבדם כבוד* that is, and to seek (or search) their glory, glory. Which most understand of too much curiosity in the search of things abstruse and divine; as of God's nature, counsels, &c. By their glory, some understand God particularly: called their glory, Psalm 106:20. Thus they changed their glory, &c. Others, refer it to honey, (as if plural, honeys) but to the same purpose: as they that understand God. And so, glory, the last word, some supply, or repeat the negative, is not glory: others, but to the same purpose, expound it, as it is used sometimes, is grievous, or heavy. And to this purpose the vulgar Latin is not amiss, *et scrutator Majestatis opprimetur a gloria.*

**Verse 28.** *{He that hath no rule over his own spirit, is like a city that is broken down, and without walls}* Chapter 16:32.

*{is like a city that is broken down}* Which lies open to all assailants, and so is in extreme danger.

## CHAPTER 26

**Verse 1.** *{As snow in summer}* For these things are then unseasonable, 1 Sam. 12:17.

**Verse 2.** *{As the bird by wandering, as the swallow}, &c.* *Sicut passeris est vagari*, &c. that is, As it is natural to the bird (or, the sparrow, particularly; as the word is interpreted by others;) to wander; and to the swallow, to fly; a thing not to be wondered at, &c. so causeless cursing is as little to be regarded. But if we read *לו* according to the Hebrew margin: then; As the sparrow, and swallow, after some wandering, return often to the house they are used; so causeless curses will return, or fall upon him that is the author of them.

**Verse 3.** *{A whip for the horse, a bridle for the ass, and a rod for the fool's back}* Ps. 32:9. Chapter 10:13.

**Verse 4.** *{Answer not a fool according to his folly}, &c.* Answer him not according to his folly: That is, as foolishly as he speaks with vain speeches, and railings: or, consent not unto him in his sayings or doings, but, answer him, to convince him, though you have no hope to instruct him: or, observe a fit time and place to answer him: or, answer him briefly, and be not full of words, as he is. See also in the preface: and upon Chapter 10:2. Treasures of wickedness, &c.

**Verse 5.** *{his own conceit}* Heb. *his own eyes*.

**Verse 6.** *{cutteth off the feet}* As he that cuts off his legs, or the legs of his messenger, receives loss thereby; so does he that employs a fool in his business. Or, He that sends a fool about his business, is as if he sent a man on an errand that has no legs; and he is sure to suffer for it. Yet I must freely acknowledge, that I am not very well satisfied with anything that I have read upon this verse. Further time and consideration may bring somewhat to light, that as yet is hidden. The understanding reader, upon this warning, if he please, may take it into his consideration.

*{damage}* Or *violence*.

**Verse 7.** *{The legs of the lame are not equal}* One goes up, and the other down, which is an unseemly thing.

*{are not equal}* Heb. *are lifted up*. But I think their opinion very probable, that take *דליו* here, not for a verb, but for a noun substantive, there being examples of such forms (not to speak how frequent it is among the Rabbins:) in Scripture. If so, the sense will be; As the lifting up of the legs in one that is lame, so, &c. that is, As dancing does not become a lame man, &c. a most elegant and apposite similitude, as any can be imagined. For as dancing is an extraordinary kind of going, or motion of the feet, not natural, but according to art; so is a proverb, or sentence, an extraordinary kind of speech; the right using, and application whereof, according to several circumstances, of time, place, person, &c. does require much judgment and discretion; and therefore very ill becoming the mouth of a fool. Yea, though we should make an imperative of the word, attollite, lift up, as some do; or an indicative, are unequal (to wit, in; or, for dancing:) as others; yet the same sense may be made of it, and will fit much better, than anything else, that I have lighted upon.

**Verse 8.** *{As he that bindeth a stone in a sling}* That is, *Sicut aliquis lapidem alligat vel applicat balistæ, ut eum ejaculetur*, &c. As he that does bind, or apply a stone to a sling, or stone-engine, to cast it off; so does the honor, that is given to a fool, soon pass away. So Buxtorf in his dictionary.

*{As he that bindeth a stone in a sling}* Or, *As he that putteth a precious stone in a heap of stones* (where it is not seen but trampled upon, with other stones,) *so is he*, &c. Some think that he alludes to those heaps of stones, used in ancient times, as your *Mercuriales statuæ*, for directions of ways, and therefore sacred, and worshiped by travelers, by casting of a stone into the heap, &c. Some would have *מרגמה* to be the same as *ארגמן* that is, purple: *Ut est ligatio lapidis vilis in re pretiosa*, &c. As the binding of an ordinary stone in purple, (or, a precious thing:) so is honor to a fool. See Buxtorfius who before.

**Verse 9.** *{As a thorn goeth up into the hand of a drunkard}* That runs into his hand and hurts himself: or, which he takes up to hurt others. Or, *as a thorn coming*, (some, *growing*:) into the hand of a drunkard, &c. The handling of thorns, for any use, does require, we know, much circumspection; else, it may do more hurt, than good: so a parable, &c. And here it may be observed, that by a parable, or proverb, any divine sentence, or piece of Scripture, may be understood. It is the sottishness of many: they think if they can utter many places of Scripture upon any occasion, with expedition; they are great clerks, and expect to be admired for it by others: whereas it is well known, that fools, and frantic men, because they want judgment, have often a great advantage of memory above other men. Scriptures, in the hands of such, are very thorns indeed: they do themselves much hurt, by their ignorant glosses and applications; and give great scandal to others. But yet worse than these, (if worse can be,) are those, who affect profane jests, to make themselves, and others (not much better than themselves:) sport, by scurrilous applications, or detortions of Scripture phrases and speeches: who if magistrates were as zealous of God's honor; as they should; would have their tongues, some; and others, their ears: (the hearers and approvers of such profane mirth:) bored with hot irons, that they might be examples unto others.

*{The great God that served all things, both rewardeth the fool}* The best sense that can be made of this translation of the words, is, that God has created all things for himself, and *rewardeth, or hireth*: (the most proper and immediate signification of the word,) even the fools and transgressors. See upon Chapter 6:4. *The Lord hath made all things for himself*. Some make this sense (without any great violence to the original words:) of the words: *A wise, (or skillful man) doth form all things*, (that he goes about,) *rightly, or, is also to do anything*. But he that is not, is forced to hire others; or, *but he that hireth others, shall have his work done foolishly*, (that is, unskillfully) and negligently: or superficially. How this may arise from the words, they that have any knowledge of the tongue, will easily see: and it were impertinent, to endeavor to satisfy others, who are in no capacity to judge.

*{the great God that formed all things, both rewardeth the fool, and rewardeth transgressors}* Or, *A great man grieveth all, and he hireth the fool, he hireth also transgressors*: That is, Wicked men when they are in place and power, make use of all sorts of wicked men for the oppressing of others. So some. Others, Great men; that is, Kings and princes (such as are tyrannical, and fear not God,) often make no conscience what burdens they lay upon their poor subjects, that they may have the more to bestow upon such as they settle their affections upon, as fit instruments of their pleasures, or cruelty, &c. Junius his translation; *Ut plurimum dolore afficit omnes ebrius*, though I find none that follow him, yet since that obscurity of the place, is such, that it is not easy to determine anything certainly; may deserve to be taken into consideration: He makes it a continued sense, with the foregoing verse, concerning drunkards; to this effect: *For the most part [the drunkard] grieveth all men*. *As he rewardeth*, (that is, *punisheth*:) *himself, that is a fool*, (or, through his folly:) *so others also that pass by*.

*{The great God that formed all things}* God rewards all manner of transgressors, whether they sin, of folly, or of malice, Chapter 11:31.
