**Verse 11.** *{As a dog returneth to his vomit}* 2 Pet. 2:22. That is, A fool, who after correction, promises, &c. returns to his folly, is as a dog, &c.

*{returneth to his folly}* Heb. *iterateth his folly*.

**Verse 12.** *{there is more hope of a fool than of him}* For the fool will rather be counseled than he, and so may sooner come to true wisdom. Matt. 21:31. To this purpose Thucydides has an excellent saying, *Ἀμαθία μετὰ σωφροσύνης ὠφελιμώτερον*, *ἢ δεξιότης μετὰ ἀκολασίας*; that is, Simplicity with sobriety (or humility) is to be preferred before unruly wit, or dexterity.

**Verse 13.** *{The slothful man saith, there is a lion in the way, a lion in the streets}* Chapter 22:13.

*{in}* Heb. *between*.

**Verse 14.** *{As the door turneth upon his hinges}* And never goes off the hinges: so a fool tosses to and fro in his bed, but will not out.

**Verse 15.** *{The slothful hideth his hand in his bosom, it grieveth him to bring it again to his mouth}* Chapter 19:24. See there.

*{it grieveth him}* Or, *he is weary*.

**Verse 17.** *{meddleth}* Or, *is enraged*.

*{is like one that taketh a dog by the ears}* He that being not called, goes about to end strife, may sooner catch hurt, than do good, by bringing himself to danger on both sides.

**Verse 18.** *{a mad man}* Or, *one that feigns himself mad, 1 Samuel 21*:13.

*{firebrands}* Heb. *flames, or sparks*.

**Verse 19.** *{So is the man that deceiveth his neighbour}* Wrong done in sport may work much mischief.

**Verse 20.** *{Where no wood is}* Heb. *without wood*.

*{where there is no talebearer, there strife ceaseth}* Chapter 22:10.

*{talebearer}* Or, *whisperer*.

*{ceaseth}* Heb. *is silent*.

**Verse 21.** *{As coals are to burning coals, and wood to fire; so is a contentious man to kindle strife}* Chapter 15:18. & 29.22.

*{so is a contentious man to kindle strife}* Heb. *a man of contentions*. That is, a very contentious man.

**Verse 22.** *{The words of a talebearer are as wounds, and they go down in the innermost parts of the belly}* Chapter 18:8. See there.

*{innermost parts}* Heb. *chambers*.

**Verse 23.** *{Burning lips, and a wicked heart}* Burning with affection, and making profession of much love to us, when evil is intended, as an earthen vessel covered over with drossy silver, which makes a show, as if it were good massive silver.

**Verse 24.** *{dissembleth}* Or, *is known*.

**Verse 25.** *{speaketh fair}* Heb. *maketh his voice gracious*.

*{for there are seven abominations in his heart}* Meaning many. He uses a certain number for an uncertain, Chapter 24:16.

**Verse 26.** *{Whose hatred is covered}* Or, *hatred is covered in secret*: or, his hatred.

*{his wickedness shall be shewed before the whole congregation}* God will give him over to some great sin, that he may be publicly punished.

**Verse 27.** *{Whoso diggeth a pit, shall fall therein: and he that rolleth a stone, it will return upon him}* Ps. 7:15, 16. & 9.15. Eccles. 10:8.

*{he that rolleth a stone, it will return upon him}* Up a hill, which falls down again on his own head.

**Verse 28.** *{A lying tongue hateth those that are afflicted by it}* Seeks to do them more mischief, whom he has hurt before. Some (making the verb to be active here; which is more commonly passive:) *Lingua fallax odit atterentes se*: (or, *qui illam reprimunt*: Vatablus:) *A lying tongue hateth those that crush it*, (or, *repress it*:) which is very plausible. Some, turning the passive plural into a singular, invert the order of the words, thus: *Attritus (i.e. modestus) homo linguam odit mendacem*: that is, *A contrite (modest) man hateth a lying tongue*. Indeed, lying must needs be very odious to a mortified man, and it is a question, whether any man can avoid the occasions of lying sometimes; that is not altogether dead to the world, and all worldly vanities. Hence, to lie, in Scripture, for, to submit; because they that are afraid to offend superiors, of whom they depend, and whom they seek to please in all things, are forced to this base shift oftentimes: according to the old saying, *Obsequium, amicos, veritas, odium parit*, that is, Compliance, begets love; truth, envy.

*{and a flattering mouth worketh ruin}* A flatterer seeks to undo men, as well as false witness: but the one does it secretly, and the other openly.

## CHAPTER 27

**Verse 1.** *{Boast not thyself of tomorrow}* James 4:13. &c.

*{tomorrow}* Heb. *tomorrow-day*.

*{thou knowest not what a day may bring forth}* Every day is as it were child of something. Tomorrow may bring forth that which may cross your business, or your comfort: therefore presume not of either, if you neglect the present occasion, Matt. 6:34.

**Verse 3.** *{heavy}* Heb. *heaviness*.

**Verse 4.** *{Wrath is cruel, and anger is outrageous}* Heb. *wrath is cruelty, and anger an overflowing*.

*{but who is able to stand before envy?}* For the envious are obstinate, and cannot be reconciled.

*{envy}* Or, *jealousy*; Chapter 6:34.

**Verse 5.** *{Open rebuke is better than secret love}* He does us more good that reveals his hatred to us by railing on us, than he that professes he loves us, and dare not reprove. See upon Chapter 25:23. a secret tongue, &c. and Chapter 28:23. But the meaning may be also, That open rebuke from a friend that deals freely with us upon occasion, is better than much professed love, that never shows itself, (though there want not opportunity,) by any effects. This seems to me the better of the two.

**Verse 6.** *{Faithful are the wounds of a friend}* Ps. 141:5. The rebukes of a friend that pierce deep, are better than the fawning of an enemy.

*{deceitful}* Or, *earnest, or frequent*. Or, *deprecanda*; as Junius and Buxtorf.

**Verse 7.** *{The full soul loatheth an honeycomb}* Want sometimes is better than plenty.

*{loatheth}* Heb. *treadeth under foot*.

*{to the hungry soul every bitter thing is sweet}* Job 6:7.

**Verse 8.** *{As a bird that wandereth from her nest}* And so is unsettled, and in danger: So is a man out of his dwelling, or calling. Vita desultoria: in Latin, noted for an unhappy life, Seneca has much to this purpose, in his second epistle. And we have had somewhat before too, out of him, that would do well here too.

**Verse 9.** *{Ointment and perfume rejoice the heart}* See upon Ps. 45:8. *whereby they have made thee glad*.

*{sweetness of a man's friend by hearty counsel}* Ps. 55:24.

*{by hearty counsel}* Heb. *from the counsel of the soul*.

**Verse 10.** *{neither go into}* Or, *but go not into*.

*{neither go into thy brother's house in the day of thy calamity}* Seek help from an ancient friend in time of trouble, rather than from your kindred; for these will sooner fail you, than he, who loves you better, and dwells nearer to you.

*{better is a neighbour that is near, than a brother far off}* Chapter 17:17. & 18.24. Old Hesiod, whom we have been beholding to already more than once, gives a reason, which it is probable, that Solomon might have in his mind: *Εἰ γάρ τοι καὶ χρῆμ᾽ ἐγχώριον ἄλλο γένηται*, *Γείτονες ἄζωστοι ἔκιον*, *ζώσαντο δὲ πηοί*. If any sudden occasion (says he) call for help; neighbors will run half naked to your assistance, when kindred, that live further off, must take time to dress and prepare themselves.

**Verse 11.** *{My son, be wise, and make my heart glad; that I may answer him that reproacheth me}* Chapter 10:1. & 23.24. See there.

*{that I may answer him that reproacheth me}* See notes on Ps. 127:5.

**Verse 12.** *{A prudent man foreseeth the evil, and hideth himself}* Chapter 22:3. See there.

**Verse 13.** *{Take his garment that is surety for a stranger, and take a pledge of him for a strange woman}* Chapter 20:16. See there.

*{for}* Or, *that is surety for*.

**Verse 14.** *{He that blesseth his friend with a loud voice}* He that praises a man before others continually to his face, is as tedious to a wise man, as if he cursed him.

*{with a loud voice, rising early in the morning}* If we take it literally, it must be supposed that the custom of salutationum matutinarum, (of morning salutations) was in use among the Hebrews, as well as among the Romans. But it may be, that all these words tend but to this, to tell us, that unseasonable praises are as odious and troublesome, as a loud voice or noise, early in the morning, to many that sleep best then, and would not willingly be disturbed.

**Verse 15.** *{A continual dropping in a very rainy day, and a contentious woman are alike}* Chapter 19:13. See there.

*{a contentious woman}* Heb. *a woman of contentions*: That is, a very contentious woman.

**Verse 16.** *{Whosoever hideth her, hideth the wind}* Though husbands, many times, to cover their own shame, do their best, that their wives may not be known abroad to be such scolds; yet it is to as much purpose, as if they would hide some blustering wind (not louder perchance than some women's tongues) that it might not be heard; or keep in the scent and fragrancy of some precious ointment, which they have about them, which is impossible.

*{bewrayeth}* Heb. *proclaimeth*.

**Verse 17.** *{Iron sharpeneth iron: so man sharpeneth}, &c.* By mutual friendly conference (and sometimes emulation) the wits of men grow commonly more vigorous and quick; and their industry too. There be other interpretations too, I know: as *Ferrum cudit ferrum, & homo cudit hominem*: commended unto us by Constantius the Emperor: and well approved of as to the sense, by some others. But since this is the most literal, and not inferior to any, for the sense; why should we *nodum in scirpo quærere*? There be divers proverbs (in ancient authors) to the same purpose: as, *Unus homo, nullus homo* *Σύν τε δύω ἐρχομένω*, &c.

**Verse 18.** *{Whoso keepeth the fig tree, shall eat the fruit thereof}* 1 Cor. 9:7. It should be as natural to a good master, to recompense a faithful servant, as it is to a fig tree that is well kept, to yield good fruit. On the other side, servants should not be discouraged, if they be not presently rewarded; but must wait patiently, as the husbandman does, who takes a great deal of pains, before he reaps any benefit: especially they that plant young trees, they must expect a great while before they can eat of the fruit.

*{waiteth on}* Or, *observeth*.

*{shall be honoured}* That is, rewarded: or plentifully provided for. So honor, is often taken; as 1 Tim. 5:3, 4. Honour widows, &c. where see learned expositors, Grotius, and others.

**Verse 19.** *{As in water face answereth to face}* As a man can guess at his own face by seeing the shadow of it in the water, though he cannot see it immediately: so may he guess at another man's thoughts by his actions, although he cannot directly see his heart. Or it may be understood of a man's own conscience, which the true looking-glass, wherein every man may see and know himself. Or he might mean perchance, that a man may *ex suo ingenio* (as some ancient Latin authors express it) *aliorum ingenia æstimare*: an honest man may easily guess by himself, what is in another honest man, like himself, his mind about a business: and likewise a wicked man judge of another like himself, by himself. By not a few, this sense, (as literal indeed, as any other:) is most approved: That as a man carries himself towards another, so he may expect to find him towards himself: if friendly, sincerely, liberally, &c. it falls out so very commonly: and good use may be made of it, by them that are wise. However, the contrary (as in most moral sayings; as before often has been observed;) is as true sometimes, and it has been the undoing of many, not to know it in time. Some men, (such natures there be:) the more they have been obliged, the greater and fiercer enemies they prove. King David found it so in his particular, as he complains in divers places: and there be other kings, (not to speak of private persons:) besides David, that have found it so too. And therefore this caution was necessary.

**Verse 20.** *{never}* not.

*{the eyes of man are never satisfied}* Eccles. 1:8.

**Verse 21.** *{As the fining pot for silver, and the furnace for gold}* Chapter 17:3.

*{so is a man to his praise}* True praise is gotten by well-doing, as gold is made praiseworthy by the fire. Or, a man may be known by his praises, according as he is affected when he is praised, or dispraised by others. He that admits of all praises, whether deserved, or undeserved; that greedily hunts after popular applause; that easily swells when he is commended; he is a vain glorious weak man. A pusillanimous man, that can bear no reproach patiently, though undeserved; and the like. Some would have this the meaning; A man may be known what he is, by whom he is commended: He good and virtuous, whom good and virtuous men commend; He sober, or intemperate; as those are, that speak well or ill of him; and the like. They that forsake the Law, praise the wicked, Chapter 28:4. See also 29.27. Some would make a more general sense of the words, That every man may be known what he is, in point of virtue, or vice, by common fame, and report. This too, may be true sometimes, and wise men (as we said a little before) may make good use of it; but subject to so many limitations and restrictions, as the contrary may seem the more true of the two. Neither do I believe, that Solomon intended it.

**Verse 22.** *{yet will not his foolishness depart from him}* A fool will be beaten to death, before he will leave his folly, Jer. 6:29. This must be understood chiefly, of those that are grown in wickedness by long continuance, and past the years of ordinary correction: that it may be reconciled, to Chapter 22:15. Foolishness is bound, &c. Yet Horace delivers it more absolutely, *Nemo adeo ferus est, qui non mansuescere possit; si modo culturæ*, &c. I believe him, as he does express himself: If men will lend a patient ear, and will be attentive to good counsel, and instruction. For in this case, though long custom, or natural inclination, may resist for a time: yet at last, the tenet of best philosophers will prove true, that: *Voluntas determinatur ab intellectu*: the will will be overswayed by reason. But it falls out sometimes, that the will is so strongly set before; and the affections, so flagrant; that the power of ratiocination being quite blunted and obstructed by it, and them; whatsoever comes to the outward ear, though never so often inculcated, sticks there, and makes no impression at all, I will not say upon the heart, but not upon the understanding; and for want of such impression, never comes seriously to be considered of: and in that case, no wonder if all the reasoning in the world, though of itself never so solid, and convincing; prove altogether ineffectual. I speak this of them, that have the power of reason and ratiocination in all other things, but what concerns their amendment. I do not think that Solomon intended here natural fools.

**Verse 23.** *{look well}* Heb. *set thy heart*.

**Verse 24.** *{For riches are not for ever}* If you were never so rich, or had a crown, yet you may be poor; therefore look well to be educated in some trade that may maintain you, if wealth fail. More probable, that Solomon by these words, from the 23. verse, to the end of the chapter, intended to commend husbandry unto us, as the most natural, most profitable, and most certain occupation: and withal, to give them that follow it, (though the use of it may be extended further) a caveat that if they mean to thrive by it, they must not altogether trust to the care of others, under them; but take good notice themselves, of all things; and trust their own eyes most.

*{riches}* Heb. *strength*.

*{to every generation}* Heb. *generation and generation*.

**Verse 25.** *{The hay appeareth}* This declares the great goodness of God towards man, who gives him food for himself, and his servants, and his cattle, summer and winter: and by consequence, requires care in man to preserve all these things for their several uses, Gen. 1:28, 29. Ps. 104:14. & 147.8,9. This tends also to the commendation of husbandry, which affords so many commodities; and should not therefore be neglected, though a man think himself otherwise never so well provided for. This may help when other things fail.

**Verse 26.** *{are}* Or, *which are*.

**Verse 27.** *{maintenance}* Heb. *life*.

## CHAPTER 28

**Verse 1.** *{The wicked flee when no man pursueth}* Lev. 26:36. Because their own conscience accuses them.

*{as bold as a lion}* That is, in time of peace, and under a just government, according to laws established; not arbitrary: which is to be presupposed in many of these sentences: as we have noted before. See also verse 12,28. and chapter 29:2. Yet even in times of greatest trouble, confusion, and tyranny; religious men, if they see themselves called to it, may and ought to be bold and resolute, upon sure confidence of God's love and mercy; and (as to the world, and man's judgment:) their innocency. However, boldness in troubles, is no certain evidence, either of a good cause, or a good conscience. Great malefactors, (besides notorious schismatics and heretics,) have been known, some in all ages, to behave themselves with great resolution, to the last. It does often proceed (as timorousness, in some truly godly) from the temper of the body, besides divers other circumstances, in particular cases; which wise men, and well versed in matters of the world, will easily understand.

*{bold}* Heb. *confident*.

**Verse 2.** *{many are the princes thereof}* The princes and kings thereof live but a little while; they are soon taken away, so that within the compass of a little time, they be many that reign successively; whereby the state of the commonwealth is oftentimes changed; especially if they be not princes of one stock and family: or, if of very different (as often) tempers and dispositions. Or, many are the princes and governors of it at one time; which is not accounted, generally, the best of governments.

*{but by a man of understanding}* It may be read, So because of men of understanding and knowledge, the princes' life shall be prolonged. Which is a great benefit to the land, for many troubles come where the government is often altered. Or, by a man of understanding, a wise king, or prince may be understood.

*{by a man of understanding and knowledge the state thereof shall be prolonged}* Or, *by men of understanding and wisdom shall they likewise be prolonged*.

**Verse 3.** *{like a sweeping rain which leaveth no food}* Which sweeps all the corn away.

*{which leaveth no food}* Heb. *without food*.

**Verse 4.** *{such as keep the law, contend with them}* Not only condemn them, but resist them also.

**Verse 5.** *{understand all things}* This may be understood of prudence and discretion in matters of life and conversation; and daily occurrences. But rather, (which will agree better with the former verse) of a right judgment, concerning God's providence: whereof, see upon Ps. 25:14. The secret of the Lord, &c. and Ps. 28:5. Because they regard not the works of the Lord, &c. See also Job 2:20. & 27. and Psal. 111. last verse.

**Verse 6.** *{Better is the poor that walketh in his uprightness}* Chapter 19:1.

*{perverse in his ways}* Heb. *in two ways*: That is, which goes out of the way to the right hand, or to the left, or that runs out of the mean into either extreme, Deut. 5:32. Josh. 1:7.

**Verse 7.** *{Whoso keepeth the law, is a wise son}* Chapter 29:3.

*{is a companion of riotous men}* Or, *feedeth gluttons*.

**Verse 8.** *{He that by usury and unjust gain increaseth his substance, he shall gather it for him that will pity the poor}* Chapter 13:22. Eccles. 2:26.

*{by usury}* See upon Ps. 15:5. *He that putteth not out his money to usury*.

*{unjust gain increaseth his substance}* Heb. *by increase*.

*{he shall gather it for him that will pity the poor}* For God will take away the riches of the wicked usurer, and give them to him that shall do good with them.

**Verse 9.** *{even his prayer shall be abomination}* He that will not be taught by God, must not think that God will supply his need. Chapter 15:8. Zech. 7:11, 12, 13. See also John 9:31. Or, he that lives scandalously, and does not apply himself to the keeping of God's commandments; though he often pray, and make long prayers with great show of fervency of spirit, (which seduced, and seducing people, who in opposition to set prayers will not allow the Lord's prayer; call, praying by the spirit:) yet his prayer shall be, &c.

**Verse 10.** *{Whoso causeth the righteous to go astray in an evil way, he shall fall himself into his own pit}* He that seeks to draw good men into a trap, shall fall into it himself, while they hold their own.

**Verse 11.** *{in his own conceit}* Heb. *in his eyes*.

*{the poor that hath understanding searcheth him out}* All his outward pomp and stateliness removed, he considers him in himself, and judges of him, that his condition is miserable; rather to be pitied, than envied by any that is wise; and does understand, what true happiness is.

**Verse 12.** *{When righteous men do rejoice, there is great glory}* **Verse 28.** Chapter 11:10. & 29.2. Eccl. 10:6.

*{when the wicked rise, a man is hidden}* Good men hide themselves for fear of oppression. See upon verse 1. *as bold as a lion*.

*{hidden}* Or, *sought for*.

**Verse 13.** *{He that covereth his sins, shall not prosper}* Ps. 32:5. 1 John 1:9, 10.

*{and forsaketh them}* It is not enough to confess our sins, unless we also forsake them.

**Verse 14.** *{happy is the man that feareth alway}* Which constantly stands in awe of God, and is afraid to offend him at any time, Chapter 23:17.

*{but he that hardeneth his heart shall fall into mischief}* Rom. 11:20.

**Verse 15.** *{so is a wicked ruler over the poor people}* For he leaves nothing, but undoes them utterly.

**Verse 16.** *{understanding}* Heb. *understandings*: That is, void of all kind of wisdom.

*{is also a great oppressor}* And therefore shall soon be cut off: as appears by the contrary in the end of the verse. It may also be translated, *The prince that wanteth understanding*, (the *ו* in *ורב* may *abundare*, as elsewhere sometimes) *is a great oppressor*: that is, *The prince that greatly oppresseth, wanteth understanding.* Some think it great wisdom, to find out ways how to extort money: but that wisdom is mere folly. The event will show it, in time; though it may do for awhile.

*{great oppressor}* Heb. *great of oppressions*: That is, a very great oppressor.

**Verse 17.** *{A man that doth violence, to the blood of any person, shall flee to the pit, let no man stay him}* Gen. 9:6. Exod. 21:14.

*{A man that doth violence to the blood of any person}* Or, *is oppressed with*. For blood lies heavy on a murderer, and will bring him to an untimely end, in despite of all his friends.

*{doth violence to the blood}* The Hebrew word *עשוק* is properly, of a passive form: but there be divers others of that kind, which nevertheless are interpreted actively: So that it may be translated either way.

*{person}* Heb. *soul*.

**Verse 18.** *{Whoso walketh uprightly, shall be saved}* Chapter 10:25.

*{but he that is perverse in his ways}* See notes on verse 6.

*{shall fall at once}* Heb. *in one*. He that uses to halt now on one side, and then on another, will be sure to fall on one side or other. Or, *דרכים* (his ways:) though it have the form of a dual, may be (as sometimes) for a plural; which is most likely. And so *באחת* may as well be translated at once. Of the sudden ruin of the wicked, so often threatened in the law, &c. see more upon Ps. 37:1. &c. Fret not thyself, &c.

**Verse 19.** *{He that tilleth his land, shall have plenty of bread}* Chapter 12:11. See there.

**Verse 20.** *{A faithful man}* Heb. *a man of faithfulnesses*: That is, a very faithful man.

*{A faithful man shall abound with blessings}* God will bless them that are true in their contracts, and curse them that deal falsely, that they may be rich quickly.

*{but he that maketh haste to be rich, shall not be innocent}* Chapter 13:11. & 23.4. 1 Tim. 6:9.

*{innocent}* Or, *unpunished*.

**Verse 21.** *{To have respect of persons, is not good}* Chapter 18:5. & 24.23.

*{for a piece of bread that man will transgress}* He that uses to take bribes, will easily be corrupted, and do unjustly for the smallest reward that is, Ezek. 13:19.

**Verse 22.** *{He that hasteth to be rich, hath an evil eye}* Or, *he that hath an evil eye, hasteth to be rich*, verse 20.

*{hath an evil eye}* That is, a covetous eye, Chapter 23:6.

*{poverty}* Or, *reproach*. Not that the same word in the original text, does afford both those senses; but because the original text does afford, according to variety of copies, two several words, or readings: *חסר* and *חסד*: the one whereof signifies want; (or poverty:) and the other, reproach. But *חסר* poverty, is the reading of most copies. Neither would the sense be amiss, if the other were embraced.

**Verse 23.** *{He that rebuketh a man, afterwards shall find more favour than he that flattereth with the tongue}* Chapter 27:4, 5, 6. See there.

*{He that rebuketh a man, afterwards shall find more favour}* It may be the party reproved will be angry at first, but when he has considered how justly he was reproved, he will love the reprover, 1 Pet. 2:12. But flatterers please at first, till men perish by following their counsel, and then they curse them.

**Verse 24.** *{and saith, it is no transgression}* That either says so within himself, so to dispense with his conscience, as if he did not offend God therein: or being found, and charged, makes that his pretense to save his credit. But the very heathens might have taught such a one more conscience: witness him in the comedy; *Egon' patri surripere quicquam possim, cano seni? Atque adeo si facere possem, pietas prohibet.* And, *Qui mentiri aut fallere insuerit patrem, aut Audebit; tanto magis audebit cæteros,*

*{a destroyer}* Heb. *a man destroying*. *לאיש משחית*, as before, Chapter 18:9. by which may be understood, a highway thief and robber: a companion unto such; that is, either like to become one of them himself; or, conversant with such wicked persons, from whence he learns this wicked doctrine.

**Verse 25.** *{He that is of a proud heart, stirreth up strife}* Chapter 13:10. He that trusts in his own strength, shall waste his goods at law: but he that trusts in God shall be rich. See also upon Chapter 21:4. an high look, and a proud heart, &c.

**Verse 26.** *{He that trusteth in his own heart, is a fool}* And shall bring himself into many dangers, which he cannot get out of, for not considering wisely of his ways.

**Verse 27.** *{He that giveth unto the poor, shall not lack}* Deut. 15:8. Chapter 22:9.

*{but he that hideth his eyes}* Isa. 58:7.

*{shall have many a curse}* Chapter 11:26.

**Verse 28.** *{When the wicked rise, men hide themselves: but when they perish, the righteous increase}* Chapter 29:2. and verse 12. of this chapter. See the reference there.

## CHAPTER 29

**Verse 1.** *{He that being often reproved}* Heb. *A man of reproofs*.

*{without remedy}* See before, Chapter 6:15.

**Verse 2.** *{When the righteous are in authority, the people rejoice}* Chapter 11:10. & 28.28. Eccl. 20:5.

*{in authority}* Or, *increased*.

**Verse 3.** *{Whoso loveth wisdom, rejoiceth his father}* Chapter 10:1. & 15.20. & 27.11. See there.

*{but he that keepeth company with harlots, spendeth his substance}* Chapter 5:9. & 28.7. Luke 15:13. And so grieves his father.

*{keeps company with harlots}* Heb. *feeds harlots*.

**Verse 4.** *{he that receiveth gifts}* Heb. *a man of oblations*.

**Verse 5.** *{spreadeth a net for his feet}* He that gives ear to the flatterer, is in danger, as the bird is, for which the net is laid.

**Verse 6.** *{In the transgression of an evil man there is a snare}* To catch the righteous by their bad example: but the righteous avoid it, and rejoice. Or (according to best expositors) the meaning may be, that a wicked man (what by terror of conscience, inwardly; what by chances and casualties, outwardly) is never out of fear and danger: when as the just and righteous, may enjoy themselves in full security: of which security, see upon Ps. 91:5. *Thou shalt not be afraid for the terror by night*, &c. See also Chapter 10:9. *He that walketh uprightly*, &c.

**Verse 7.** *{The righteous considereth the cause of the poor}* Job 29:16, 17. I was a father to the poor, and the cause which I knew not, I searched out. And I brake the jaws of the wicked, &c. To consider of the poor, Ps. 41:1. in another sense; of which see there.

**Verse 8.** *{Scornful men bring a city into a snare}* Ambitious persons, which scorn all but themselves, as Chapter 3:34. do by seeking their own ends bring mischief upon a state: which men of better temper, help to turn away. Or, more generally, and according to the most usual signification of the word, *scornful men*; that is, proud wicked men, that make a mock of godliness, and contemn them that fear God, and make a conscience of their ways; as idiots, or superstitious fools.

*{bring a city into a snare}* Or, *set a city on fire*.

**Verse 9.** *{whether he rage or laugh, there is no rest}* Whether the fool look angrily or merrily, he that contends with him, shall never be quiet. Others; whether the wise man deal with him roughly, or more gently, and as it were laughing, (*Ridentem dicere verum, Quid vetat?*) it comes all to one; there is no end: neither fair means, nor foul, can do any good upon him. This therefore must be understood of one that is hardened in his wickedness, and past feeling. See upon Chapter 27:22. *Though thou shouldst bray*, &c.

**Verse 10.** *{The bloodthirsty}* Heb. *men of blood*.

*{but the just seek his soul}* Or, *and seek the soul of every just man*.

*{the just seek his soul}* Take care to preserve other men's lives, Ps. 142:4. Or, those that are just and upright themselves, know how to value the life of a righteous man, and will not be wanting unto him at any time, to bring him out of danger. They interpret his danger, to be theirs, and as they would do for their own lives, so they will for his. Others: *The just seek his soul*, that is, they require it (the life of the upright:) at the hands of the bloodthirsty, and will not suffer them to escape unpunished. Or, (which comes to one) they seek the soul (or life) of the bloodthirsty, to bring him to just punishment. The original words will afford any of these senses.

**Verse 11.** *{A fool uttereth all his mind}* Chapter 14:23. *Wisdom resteth*, &c. and 17.27. *He that hath knowledge*, &c. See there.

*{mind}* Heb. *spirit*; or, wrath, Isa. 25:4.

**Verse 12.** *{all his servants are wicked}* They will tell him lies daily to please him. Regis ad exemplum: such as the ruler is, such will the servants be: All books are full of observations to that purpose. See also Ecclesiasticus 10:2. As the judge of the people is himself, &c.

**Verse 13.** *{The poor and the deceitful man meet together}* Heb. *man of deceits*: That is, the most deceitful man. But here, comparing with Chapter 22:2. (*The rich and the poor meet together*: *the Lord is the maker of them all*) it is most probable, that by a man of deceits, is meant an usurer; and by an usurer, a rich man, in general: because, as most usurers, (those that make it their profession) are unconscionable and fraudulent; (See more upon Ps. 72:14. He shall redeem their souls, &c.) so, most, rich.

*{the Lord lighteneth both their eyes}* Both to the one, and to the other, (and by consequent, unto all) God is the author of life and being. *He maketh his sun to rise* (here, *he lighteneth the eyes*) *on the evil, and on the good* &c. and John 1:9. of Christ: *That was the true light which lighteth every man that cometh into the world*: being spoken of natural light, but whether rational, or vital, we will not here dispute. The same God also, that has appointed that some (though not any by unlawful means) should be rich; and others poor: by which diversity of states and conditions, commonwealths are maintained. To enlighten the eyes also, in Scripture, is sometimes to comfort and cherish; to revive: as Ezra 9:8. that our God may lighten our eyes, and give us a little reviving in our bondage. Poor men oftentimes, whom we account miserable for their poverty; by a special blessing, have a greater share of cheerfulness, and inward contentment; than those that we account very happy, judging by the external appearance only.

*{the deceitful man}* Or, *the usurer*.

**Verse 14.** *{The king that faithfully judgeth the poor, his throne shall be established for ever}* Chapter 20:28.

**Verse 15.** *{The rod and reproof give wisdom}* **Verse 17.**

*{a child left to himself, bringeth his mother to shame}* Chapter 10:1. & 17.21,25.

**Verse 16.** *{but the righteous shall see their fall}* Ps. 37:36. and 58.10. & 91.8.

**Verse 17.** *{Correct thy son, and he shall give thee rest}* Chapter 13:24. & 22.15. & 23.13,14.

*{he shall give delight unto thy soul}* Heb. *delights, or, dainties*. That is, abundance of delight.

**Verse 18.** *{Where there is no vision}, &c.* That is, no prophecy: for prophets were called Seers: and because it was the office of prophets also, to teach and instruct the people, by prophecies is sometimes intended, (as here) public teaching and preaching.

*{perish}* Or, *is made naked*.

*{he that keepeth the law, happy is he}* Want of preaching may damn men, but store of it cannot save them, unless they be obedient to it, 1 Sam. 3:1. And, Not the hearers of the law, are just before God, but the doers of the law shall be justified, Rom. 2:13. and James 1:22. Or, as some others: Though, where no preaching is, the church be in a manner dissolved, and the generality of people be in great danger; yet it follows not, but that they that live conscionably, and practice what they formerly have learned, and know; may do well enough, and be saved. Another interpretation, (in reference to those times under the law) not less literal, offers itself also: Where no vision is; that is, When God does withdraw himself from his people for their sins, so that no oracle, no signs of his appearance, (as Ps. 74:9. *We see not our signs, there is no more any Prophet, neither is there among us any that knoweth how long*) are left; no wonder, if through multiplicity of public woes and confusions, many, both good and bad, do perish: yet even then this may be their comfort, that are pious and godly; that through their perseverance in good, they shall be happy in the end. See upon Ps. 9:6. *O thou enemy*, &c. and upon Ps. 91:5. *Thou shalt not be afraid*, &c. Some ancient heathens also did maintain, that *ἡ μαντικὴ*, that is, oracles, were necessary to the preservation of a commonwealth: as Socrates, in Xenophon, disputes; and Plutarch somewhere does confirm out of experience.

**Verse 19.** *{A servant will not be corrected by words}* He that is of a servile, and surly nature, will not amend without blows.

*{though he understand, he will not answer}* He will not answer to your call, but make as though he did not hear you. Or, though he know his master's will, Luke 12:47. (which is a great aggravation of his disobedience: because masters sometimes expect that servants should do their duty, without speaking to them.) and be told of it never so much; yet he will not obey. So some take answering here; for execution, or performance: and Job 19:16. I called my servant, and he gave me no answer: I intreated him with my mouth.

**Verse 20.** *{Seest thou a man that is hasty in his words}* Rather, in rebus suis; as Mercer, and others: that is, in his businesses. It agrees with 26.12. *Seest thou a man wise in his own conceit*? there is more hope, &c. For a self-conceited man, uses not to deliberate long: but trusting to his own wisdom, and thinks it a disparagement to take advice from others: hence is his haste and precipitancy in businesses.

*{in his words}* Or, *in his matters*.

**Verse 21.** *{shall have him become his son at the length}* He will think much to do any servile work.

**Verse 22.** *{An angry man stirreth up strife, and a furious man aboundeth in transgression}* Chapter 15:18. & 26.21.

**Verse 23.** *{a man's pride shall bring him low}* Job 22:29. Chapter 15:33. & 18.12. Matt. 23:12. Luke 14:11.

**Verse 24.** *{he heareth cursing, and bewrayeth it not}* Some refer these words to that public examination of persons suspected, mentioned, Exod. 22:8. which was wont to be, say they, (grounding upon Lev. 6:4, 5.) with tender of an oath with imprecations to the party: but more likely it is, (with some others) that Lev. 5:1. And if a soul sin, and hear the voice of swearing, &c. is intended. But of the right meaning there too, there is some controversy among expositors, some translating, the voice of adjuration; others, the voice of blasphemy.

*{cursing}* Or, *an oath,* Isa. 24:18.

**Verse 25.** *{The fear of man bringeth a snare}* He that fears man more than God, falls into a snare (which he seeks to avoid) and is destroyed, Chapter 10:24. Or, by the fear of man, may be understood a continual worldly fear and trepidation, which they that fear not God, and put their trust in him, are subject to: opposed to that security of the godly, of which, see before upon verse 6. In the transgression of an evil man, &c. Neither is this contrary to Chapter 28:14. *Happy is the man that feareth always*: because that is spoken of another kind of fear; a religious, or conscientious fear; upon which a godly man's security is chiefly grounded.

*{shall be safe}* Heb. *shall be set on high*.

**Verse 26.** *{Many seek the ruler's favour, but every man's judgment cometh from the Lord}* Chapter 19:6.

*{the ruler's favour}* Heb. *the face of a ruler*.

*{every man's judgment cometh from the Lord}* He needs not to flatter the ruler, but rather pray to God, who can incline the judge to pass sentence as he please, Chapter 21:1. Or, *by judgment* (Heb. *משפט*) may be understood, state and condition of life; riches, or poverty; greatness, or lowness, in point of dignity: which though they seem to depend from the power and will of princes, (where absolute) especially: yet the wise man tells us here, that it is God in heaven that does rule, and overrules all things.

## CHAPTER 30

**Verse 1.** *{The words of Agur, the son of Jakeh}, &c.* Though these names be found nowhere else in Scripture, which has made some men to interpret them, as mystical: (so the vulgar Latin, and divers ancients:) yet it is more probable, that such a man there was really, famous in his time for his wisdom; whose disciples, among others, are conceived to have been these two here named, Ithiel, and Ucal. But when, or where he lived, is altogether uncertain. Most Rabbins would have Solomon himself to be the object of these several names: and they make strange interpretations of them to that purpose: but there is no appearance of any solidity in anything they deliver concerning this matter.

*{even the prophecy}* Any instruction may be called a prophecy, (and so in the New Testament sometimes) because it was the office of prophets to teach and instruct, as well as to prophesy: as has been said already upon the 18. verse of the former chapter. But some would have the word to signify here, comparatio; that is, say they, *συλλογὴ*, a collection: and these proverbs here inserted, to have been collected out of other writings of this Agur: or out of his sayings, and sentences, then extant. It is but a conjecture, for which I see but little ground.

*{unto Ithiel and Ucal}* Which were Agur's scholars, or friends.

**Verse 2.** *{Surely I am more brutish than any man}* It may be read thus; Surely I am too foolish to be a great man, nay I have not the knowledge of an ordinary man in me. So the words are used, Ps. 49:2. & 62.9. It seems Agur had not his knowledge by human institution, but by divine inspiration, as Amos had, Amos 7:14. Herein he declares his great humility, who would not attribute any wisdom to himself, but all unto God, Ps. 73:22.

**Verse 3.** *{I neither learned wisdom}* Or this, *I have not learned wisdom, but I know the knowledge of the holy ones*; That is, I have not been taught by men, but by God, as the angels of heaven are, by a more immediate illumination. They are called holy ones, Dan. 4:13, 17. & 8.17. Or, *I have not attained to any great measure of true wisdom, or, of the knowledge of holy and heavenly things*. There is a not, in the first member, which some (as in ordinary constructions;) would have to belong to the second member also; some confine it to the first only: whence this variety is.

*{the knowledge of the holy}* See upon Chapter 9:10. *and the knowledge of the holy is understanding*.

**Verse 4.** *{Who hath ascended up into heaven; or descended?}* This, and what else follows in this verse, tends only to this, to set out the difficulty, or rather impossibility of divine heavenly wisdom to man: to which purpose there is a whole chapter in Job, to wit, the 28. Surely there is a vein for the silver, &c. of which, see what is said upon Chapter 1:20. *Wisdom crieth without*, &c. as for those particular words, *who hath ascended* &c. see John 3:13. Rom. 10:6, 7. and upon Chapter 17:24. *but the eyes of a fool are in the ends of the earth*.

*{Who hath ascended up into heaven}* Who is everywhere, &c. None but God.

*{who hath gathered the wind in his fist?}* Job 38:4. &c. Ps. 104:3. &c. Isa. 40:12. &c.

*{what is his name, and what is his son's name, if thou canst tell?}* If any such man, that could do these things, ever were in the world, tell us somewhat of his generation, or pedigree? either father, or son. The word father is not here, but it may be included in the word name, because it was, and is yet the fashion among the Hebrews, and most Orientals, to name every man by his father; as here; Agur, the son of Jakeh, and the like. Or thus; if yet alive and in being; what is his name? Or if dead; where are any of his posterity? what is his son's name? Of Christ, Isa. 58:8. Who shall declare his generation? And it seems to have been a proverbial speech among the Jews, of one that was but an ordinary man, that they knew whence he was; (that is, his generation, father, mother &c.) as of one that was not, that they knew not; as of the Messias John 7:27. (though verse 42. of the same chapter, that Christ was to be of the seed of David, they knew well enough,) they speak.

**Verse 5.** *{Every word of God is pure}* Ps. 12:6. and 18.30. and 19.8.119.140.

*{pure}* Heb. *purified*.

*{he is a shield unto them that put their trust in him}* Ps. 18:30. & 84.11,12.

**Verse 6.** *{Add thou not unto his words, lest he reprove thee and thou be found a liar}* Deut. 4:2. & 12.32. Rev. 22:18, 19. To add, here, is as much as to counterfeit, or to impose: as when a man pretends to have had a revelation, or to deliver some message from God; which, if not true, he does impose upon God, and at the same time, but in another sense, does impose upon men too: a right impostor, in either sense.

*{lest he reprove thee:} &c.* God sometimes suffers impostors, through the credulity of men, and some false signs, (though true sometimes in the event, and that, by a special providence of God, to try us Deut. 13:1, 2. If there arise among you a prophet, &c.) to prevail very far: sometimes he speedily reproves them, when their forgeries and impostures, and pretended enthusiasms are soon discovered, and come to light: for which they are justly abhorred by all that have any solidity of either faith, or judgment in them.

**Verse 7.** *{Two things have I required of thee}* O God.

*{deny me them not}* Heb. *withhold not from me*.

*{deny me them not before I die}* So long as I live.

**Verse 8.** *{Remove far from me vanity and lies}* Take care of my soul and body, so as to keep me from falling into any vain or deceitful course, and to afford me a competent livelihood, fit for my estate and charge.

*{feed me with food convenient for me}* Matt. 6:11. Heb. *of my allowance, or, my part*. For in well-ordered families, every servant has his proportion daily, Chapter 31:15.

**Verse 9.** *{Lest I be full, and deny thee, and say, Who is the Lord?}* Deut. 32:15.

*{Lest I be full, and deny thee}* Meaning, that they that put their trust in their riches, forget God, and that by too much wealth, men have occasion to do so.

*{deny thee}* Heb. *belie thee*.

*{and take the name of my God in vain}* By false oaths.

**Verse 10.** *{Accuse not a servant unto}* Heb. *hurt not with thy tongue*.

*{Accuse not a servant unto his master}* For no fault, or for a light one. A servant's good will is not to be contemned.

*{and thou be found guilty}* To wit, Before God. Or, *and thou be made desolate*. That is, lest the fruit of his ill wishes fall upon you.

**Verse 11.** *{There is a generation that curseth their father}, &c.* Here begin his quaternaries, that is, his coupling, or comparing of things, by four in number: to which kind of expression his own genius, it seems, and fancy did lead him; and being not ordinary, it is the more emphatical, and apt to make the more impression in the fancy of the reader; as also, easier to be committed to memory. Of these quaternaries, from this to the end of the chapter, there be six in all. In this first, he proposes four kind of men or sinners, which of all others he conceived most abominable both in the sight of God and men. It is not so fully expressed here; but that to be the aim, may be confirmed from, Chapter 6:16. *These six things doth the Lord hate*, &c.

*{that curseth their father, and doth not bless}* The sense of this is further expressed verse 17. *The eye that mocketh at his father, and despiseth to obey his mother*, &c. So that, to curse, and, not to bless, is in effect no more, than to slight, and disobey.

**Verse 12.** *{that are pure in their own eyes, and yet not washed}, &c.* There be two kinds of pride; spiritual, and worldly pride. Sometimes they meet together in one: as when a man has a great opinion of his own goodness and excellent parts: and thereupon in his ordinary conversation openly ascribes to himself, and despises others; as the proud Pharisee did in the Gospel: and those in Esay, Chapter 65:5. Stand by thyself; come not near to me, for I am holier, &c. And sometimes they are severed: as if a man be humble and lowly, outwardly; but proud in himself, for this very humility, and whatsoever else he fancies good and commendable in himself: whether real, or imaginary. For distinction sake, we may call the one internal, and the other external pride: though commonly neither the one be without some external evidences; nor the other, ever found, but from some inward cause and distemper. The first kind seems to be specially intended here; as the second, in the next verse. By worldly pride, we comprehend that also, which is altogether grounded upon external things, as wealth, honor, beauty, and the like.

**Verse 13.** *{lofty are their eyes}* Chapter 6:17.

**Verse 14.** *{There is a generation, whose teeth are as swords, and their jaw-teeth as knives}, &c.* Job 29:17. Ps. 52:2. & 57.4.

**Verse 15.** *{The horseleech hath two daughters}, &c.* It does not appear clearly what this, and the next verse tend unto. For to take them barely, as if he intended only to tell us of some natural things that are unsatiable, is more proper to a naturalist, (as by some is well observed;) than one that takes upon him to teach and prophesy, verse 1. This has moved some of the Rabbins, to seek out strange interpretations, and they have found a way to make coherence of sense between the three verses here following; and those four before, from ver. 11. *There is a generation that curseth their father*, &c. They would have *עלוקה*, by inversion of letters, (*עלק*, for *עקל*) to signify here, not hirudinem, or horseleech: but perversitatem, that is, wickedness; and so make these four mentioned insatiable things, to be intended as particular punishments to those four kinds, or generations of sinners insisted upon in the former verses. To make this good and plausible, what they say of every particular word, except I apprehended more solidity in it than I do, would not, I think, be much to the purpose. Yet if any be so curious, they may read Aben Ezra, either in his own language; printed with divers Bibles: or, if they will, in Latin, as it is set out by Antonius Giggeius, a doctor of the Ambrosian College in Italy. Most expositors are of opinion, that by these several examples, the covetous unmerciful extortioner, and oppression of the poor, mentioned in the 14. verse, is aimed at: which is very probable.

*{the horseleech hath two daughters}* The leech has two forks in her tongue, which here he calls her *two daughters*, wherewith she sucks the blood, and is never satiate: even so are the covetous extortioners unsatiable. But this of two forks is denied by some, who quote authors, that have written of that subject. Not two they say: but three: and therefore by *two daughters* here, they understand any that resemble, in their conversation, the unsatiableness of the horseleech; such as are particularly mentioned in the following verse. A certain number, for an uncertain, as they would have it. Or, *two daughters*, because of the word, give, give; doubled.

**Verse 16.** *{and the barren womb}* Some; and the narrow womb. And there be reasons from nature, given for both. But I will not entertain my reader with such discourses.

*{It is enough}* Heb. *Wealth*.

**Verse 17.** *{the raven}* Such children shall come to an untimely end, and be denied burial. Those horrible judgments which, as of all sinners the most inhumane and unnatural, they must expect one day at the hands of God, are here set out by such temporal evils, as among men are commonly most dreaded. See before upon ver. 11. *There is a generation that curseth*, &c. Some such saying also ancient Grecians had concerning tyrants, the worst of men.

*{of the valley}* Which haunt the valleys to seek for carrion.

*{the valley}* Or, *the brook*.

**Verse 19.** *{The way of an eagle in the air, the way of a serpent}* Others instead of via, that is, the way: they translate, vestigium, the footstep. The words may seem to differ but little: but the difference of sense, that is made of these different words, is more apparent, and considerable. If we say footsteps, in the ordinary notion; it is sure enough, that neither, Eagle in the air; nor serpent upon a rock; nor ship, upon the sea, leave any print of either feet, bulk; or body, by which they may be traced. But then say they, how shall this be verified of, the way of a man with a maid: is not impregnation, or being with child (the natural and ordinary consequence) a very sufficient token or argument, by which the way of a man, in this kind may be known & descried? They may be thought to say somewhat: but upon further consideration, the objection will appear but weak and impertinent. For the question is not of consequents, (whether probable or necessary:) by which antecedent actions may in time be discovered; for so it may be objected of the rest; as for example, if a serpent pass by, though he may not be known by his footsteps, yet by some noise, (*sibila lambebant linguis vibrantibus ora*: in the poet:) or by some effect of his fierceness, or the like he may perchance: and so of the rest: but of certain signs and marks, in the place, in the nature of footsteps. But this will bring us to a physical consideration, never (though handled by many physicians and naturalists, at large;) to be determined; whether there be any certain natural signs of virginity, by which a maid may be known from her that is not. As for those signs mentioned in Scripture, most agree, that it was by a special dispensation of God, for a time, and no general case otherwise. They that will be so curious, as to desire further resolution in this particular, may read (not to mention others at this time:) Joh. Beverovicii *Epistolicas Quæstiones*, where they shall find this matter agitated both by a physician, and a divine; both learned men: yea and this very place we are now upon, considered of upon this occasion. Now those that are against this translation of the word, by vestigium, or footstep; and maintain it must be, way: (the most literal, to the original word:) they are put to it to tell us, what it is that is so extraordinary in the flight of an eagle, &c. that Solomon should here insist upon them, as things of such abstruseness, and not to be comprehended by the wit of man. Of an eagle, they say, because of all birds she only flies straight upwards: of a serpent, because of his swift motions without feet: of a ship, because of her structure, masts, sails: and especially her rudder, by which so vast a body is guided and turned at will; and lastly, for the way of a man in a maid, they tell us of some mysteries, affections, passions, secret wooings and communications, by which the most watchful are often deceived. I will forbear further arguing: and deliver my opinion as briefly as I can. When I have well weighed all circumstances and particulars, what is said by others, and what my own judgment does suggest unto me; my opinion is, that of the three first particulars, being but an introduction, as it were, to the last; footsteps will do best: of the last, ways. I conceive that Solomon his aim was, to set out the secret ways of young lovers, (not easily to be discerned or prevented by the greatest care of parents, or others, that have charge of them:) by things that have some affinity, in that they leave no print, or footstep after them; and consequently, to warn such whom it might concern, to be the more careful: not to trust words, countenance, protestations, and the like; but according to the custom of those times and places, to keep their virgins out of all sight, and access: which from this very custom of close keeping, and reclusedness, were called *עלמות* in the Hebrew tongue. Many such warnings we may find in ancient authors. And there may probably be some emphasis here in the very word *עלמה* Solomon intimating by that word, that though virgins be recluse, and out of sight, according to the ordinary custom; yet for all this, if there be not some extraordinary care and observation; a man may find a way, or make his way, to compass his designs. Of the secret ways and communications of lovers, besides comedies; Ovid, *De arte amandi* will sufficiently satisfy them that desire further satisfaction. And as for the nature of love, in reference to secrecy, there is an ancient inscription in some part of the world, which begins with *Ælia Lælia Crispis*: written so mystically and enigmatically, that it has posed the wits of many learned men to find out the meaning; and divers expositions have been made of it; both philosophical; and others: some supposing the philosopher's stone to be intended; others, some other thing: until at last some happily lighted upon the truth, and did so thoroughly unriddle it, that no further question, I think, can be made about it. As for the Jews' exception, and objection against Christians, that the word *עלמה* does not properly signify a maid; it can have no ground at all from this place, since it is ordinary, in all languages, and among all men, to call things by their names, and titles, according to outward appearance and profession; though in reality of truth it be quite contrary. That the same word *דרך* in the original text should be taken for footstep, properly, and for way more generally; there being so much affinity in the thing; can be no wonder at all: and it has been observed before more than once, that the same word, or phrase, where there is no such affinity in the thing, is sometimes used in different senses, in a very little compass of words: sometimes, in the same verse, or line. Besides, there is no necessity that *עלמה* here should be taken of a tainted, or corrupted maid: according to that interpretation of, the ways of man; of secret wooing, communications, &c. that has been insisted upon.

**Verse 20.** *{of an adulterous woman}* Though not the same spoken of in the former verse; yet the same thing, in effect, is still prosecuted: the mystical hidden courses of love, whether a maid, or a married woman be the object.

*{midst}* Heb. *heart*.

*{and wipeth her mouth}* She has her desire, and after counterfeits, as though she were an honest woman: as a licorish, and filching servant, eats stolen bread, and wipes his mouth after it, and appears as if he had not stolen it.

**Verse 22.** *{For a servant when he reigneth}, &c.* Chapter 19:10. These commonly abuse the state whereunto they are called; and the comforts they receive. There is no obscurity in the words; and no man, that has any knowledge of the world, will require further confirmation or illustration of the sense.

**Verse 23.** *{an handmaid that is heir to her mistress}* Which is married to her master after the death of her mistress.

**Verse 24.** *{but they are exceeding wise}* They contain matter of great instruction in them.

*{exceeding wise}* Heb. *wise, made wise*. See upon Chapter 6:6. *consider her ways and be wise*.

**Verse 25.** *{The ants are a people not strong, yet they prepare their meat in the summer}* Chapter 6:6. &c.

*{ants are a people}* Or, *ants a people*.

**Verse 26.** *{The conies are but a feeble folk}* So God's people, though feeble, have God as a powerful rock to protect them, Ps. 18:2. & 40.17. See upon Ps. 104:18. and the rocks for the conies.

*{conies are but feeble}* Or, *conies feeble*.

**Verse 27.** *{The locusts have no king}* How much more should those that have governors over them, live orderly under them.

*{have}* Or, *which have*.

*{by bands}* Heb. *gathered together*.

**Verse 28.** *{The spider taketh hold with her hands, and is in kings' palaces}* Chapter 22:29.

*{taketh}* Or, *which taketh*.

**Verse 31.** *{greyhound}* Or, *horse*. Heb. *זרזיר* of which word, not elsewhere to be found in the Scripture, there be divers other translations; as, cock, &c. but not any grounded upon so much probability as either of these, greyhound, or horse. See (who can, and desires further satisfaction) Fulleri Miscellanea, lib. 5. cap. 12.

*{greyhound}* Heb. *girt in the loins*.

*{against whom there is no rising up}* Eccles. 8:4. A king whose power is uncontrollable: or a king that keeps his subjects in due order, so that there is no insurrection in the state. This sense is not to be found fault with, as very proper to the place, and agreeable to the Hebrew words, *מלך אלקום עמו* Yet the learned reader may take notice from a very learned man, Mr. Edward Pocock, (as for his other abilities, but especially for his singular knowledge of the Oriental tongues, a great ornament to the University of Oxford:) that the same words according to the idiotism of the Arabic tongue, according to which divers places of the Scripture; in Job especially; (see one example upon Ps. 104:26. *that Leviathan whom thou hast made*, &c.) by learned expositors are interpreted: may also be translated, *Rex cum quo populus, est: vel, Quem sequitur populus suus.* That is, A king with his people about him: or, *whom his people doth follow*. And this would agree well with Chapter 14:28. *In the multitude of people is the King's honour*, &c.

**Verse 32.** *{If thou hast done foolishly in lifting up thyself}* If you have evil thoughts against your king, give them over, for you will not prevail, verse 31. Eccles. 8:3. & 10.20. The word also (according to best expositors) may more generally be interpreted, of any evil, which through error, or rashness, we are in the pursuit of. Many are of that nature. They would not willingly trespass, but if once they have entered upon a business, (or opinion;) though the unreasonableness of it does appear to them afterwards, they are so rigid and inflexible, (which they interpret constancy and resolution) they will hardly be persuaded to go back. This advice may belong to such.

*{lay thine hand upon thy mouth}* Job 21:5. & 40.4. Mic. 7:16.

**Verse 33.** *{so the forcing of wrath bringeth forth strife}* For, churning, wringing, and forcing, there is but one word in the original, *מיץ* and but one word (varied in numbers only, *אף* and *אפים*) for nose, and wrath; which makes the whole expression much sweeter, and proverb-like in the original Hebrew, than it can be in any translation. And so it is, not here only but in many others of these proverbs. By forcing of wrath, or forcing wrath, as some render it, is meant long, and reiterated provocations, able to extort an indignation even from the most gentle and patient. Then by contention, we must understand such as St. James writes of where envying and strife is, there is confusion and every evil work, James 3:16. Provocation of wrath inevitably brings forth strife and mischief.

## CHAPTER 31

**Verse 1.** *{The words of king Lemuel}* That is, of Solomon, who was called Lemuel, that is, of God, because God had ordained him to be king over Israel, rather than any of his elder brethren, 1 Kings 2:15, 22. Or, it may be, was so called of mother, in his infancy, with some inversion or transposition of letters, for more easy pronunciation: from his right name Solomon. It is very ordinary, in all places of the world.

*{the prophecy that his mother taught him}* The doctrine, see upon Chapter 30:1. even the prophecy) which his mother Bathsheba taught him.

**Verse 2.** *{What my son! and what the son of my womb}, &c.* This abrupt beginning, and often repetition of the word son, does well set out the affectionate care of a tender and wise mother, for her dearest child, in this main business. Many mothers, yea most, have affection enough to their children (an affection of fondness and cockering:) but they use it not aright, for their good and virtuous education. Less of that affection, would do their children more good.

*{the son of my vows}* Whom I made many vows to God for, before I had you, as 1 Sam. 1:11. Or, *that you might be king*.

**Verse 3.** *{Give not thy strength unto women}* Solomon was forewarned to avoid wantonness by his mother here, and by his father, Chapter 5:9. yet he fell into it. Wisdom and instruction fail of their fruit oftentimes.

*{nor thy ways to that which destroyeth kings}* Meaning, that women are the destruction of kings, if they haunt them, Chapter 7:26.

**Verse 4.** *{it is not for kings to drink wine}* That is, the king must not give himself unto immoderate drinking, and so neglect his office, which is to execute judgment. See Ecclesiastes 10:16, 17.

*{to drink wine}* Either, to drink wine without mixture, a thing not used in those hot countries, (whereof see upon Ps. 75:8. full of mixture:) but by intemperate men, in Latin properly called merobii: or to drink wine not for strength (for which use it was ordained by God, and for comfort in time of need:) but for drunkenness: Eccles. 10:17.

**Verse 5.** *{Lest they drink and forget the law}* *Quid non ebrietas designat?* as the Latin poet. There is no manner of wickedness that drunkenness is not capable of, and they guilty, in some measure, that willfully, and wittingly, expose themselves to the danger of it.

*{pervert}* Heb. *alter*.

*{of any of the afflicted}* Heb. *of all the sons of affliction*.

**Verse 6.** *{Give strong drink to him that is ready to perish, and wine to those that be of heavy hearts}* Upon this place, (as is conceived by many,) especially, was that custom of the Jews grounded, to give wine to them that were condemned to death: as we read in Christ's case, Matt. 27:24. (according to some copies: for others read there *ὄξος*, *vinegar*; not *οἶνον*, wine: but see Beza there;) and Mark 15:23. called therefore Amos 2:8. the wine of the condemned: according to some; though otherwise, and more probably interpreted, by most. But that there was such a custom among the Jews, no man does question: and all Rabbins bear witness: but whether at all, or how justly grounded upon these words of Solomon, is another question; concerning which all are not of one opinion. But this is not a place for it. Certain it is, that Solomon's words, (and that according to best expositors) will admit of a more general interpretation: ready to perish, that is, through poverty, &c. as Deut. 26:5. a Syrian ready to perish was my father, &c. which is confirmed by the verse following, Let him drink, and forget his poverty, &c. and that wine is proper and natural, to revive the heart of the distressed and afflicted, whether through want, or any other cross and affliction, we are told, Ps. 104:15.

*{of heavy heart}* Heb. *bitter of soul*.

**Verse 8.** *{Open thy mouth for the dumb}* Defend their cause that are in great danger, and unable to help themselves, Chapter 24:11.

*{such as are appointed to destruction}* Heb. *the sons of destruction*. See upon Chapter 24:11, 12.

**Verse 9.** *{judge righteously, and plead the cause of the poor and needy}* Lev. 19:15. Deut. 1:16. See also upon Ps. 72:4. He shall judge the poor of the people, &c.

**Verse 10.** *{Who can find a virtuous woman}* Chapter 12:4. This commendation of a good wife is set out elegantly in the Hebrew. Every verse to the end of the chapter begins with a several Hebrew letter, according to the alphabet: as divers choice psalms do. See upon Psalm 25. at the end.

**Verse 11.** *{The heart of her husband doth safely trust in her}* Commits with confidence the household affairs to her, as Potiphar did to Joseph, Gen. 39:4, 6.

*{he shall have no need of spoil}* He shall not need to get riches by indirect means, as many soldiers do; for his wife will fill his house with goods, as the camp is full after the spoil of a city: Or, spoils may be put here for all manner of provision: which is the more likely, and, by most, approved interpretation. See the like Ps. 111:5. and verse 15. here, *giveth meat to her household*.

**Verse 14.** *{She bringeth her food from afar}* She sells her cloth to the merchant for things needful, verse 24. Or, rather, she provides for all necessaries for her house, and housekeeping, (whereof divers things come from far places) long before hand; and in due season; that she may not be to seek, when she should use them.

**Verse 15.** *{giveth meat to her household}* As a lion rises betimes to get a prey, that may serve him and his all the day; so does she rise herself, and raise her maids betimes, to get food for her family by labor.

*{meat}* Or, *prey,* Ps. 111:5. See before upon 11. no need of spoil.

*{a portion to her maidens}* To appoint every one their stint of work, or, of meat.

**Verse 16.** *{and buyeth it}* She purchases it with the gains of her travail.

*{buyeth}* Heb. *taketh*.

*{with the fruit of her hands she planteth a vineyard}* She not only looks that her servants work, but works herself also, verse 27.

**Verse 17.** *{She girdeth her loins with strength}* She uses vigilancy and diligence, in her labor, 2 Kings 4:29.

**Verse 18.** *{She perceiveth}* Heb. *she tasteth*.

*{She perceiveth}* Or, *When she perceiveth*.

*{She perceiveth that her merchandise is good}* Her tractatio, or negotiatio; as the word *סחרה* is also translated; that is, her dealings, doings, negotiation. She considers of it upon her pillow, (which is the best time for consideration) and finding that all matters thrive with her, according to her own heart's desire; she reaps this fruit of her labors, proper to herself, and, and greater than any other: a complacency within herself, and sweet contentment of mind. This is likely to be in the night, especially, when the thoughts are most free: which, as I conceive, gave Solomon occasion immediately upon it, to mention, her candle burning in the night. Most others make this to be the coherence, (which is very good so too,) that finding how all things thrive with her, she is thereby encouraged to increase her industry, and to labor night and day; which nevertheless must be understood as spoken hyperbolically, or figuratively, implying no more, in effect, but that she plies her business with great constancy and alacrity. Were it not for this coherence between the two members of the verse; this her tasting of her merchandise, might very literally be understood of her both skill, and care in tasting and trying her several wares and commodities, before she paid for them; no contemptible commendation, in point of economy, and domestic providence. *Ὥσπερ οὖν* (says an ancient author to this purpose) *ἐπειδάν τις καταπλεύσῃ τῶν ἐμπόρων*, — *ἀλλὰ πρότερον γευσάμενος*, &c. that is, As when an unknown merchant comes to you with a ship laden with wares, (or provisions) you do not presently discredit him, because unknown, but having first tasted of his wine, or whatever else it is, that he brings, having made trial of it, if it please you, you buy of it; if it does not, you leave it: So, &c.

**Verse 19.** *{She layeth her hands to the spindle}* There be divers things in this description, that seem to belong to an industrious poor woman, such a one as the best of Latin poets does describe at large in that excellent poem of his called Moretum: one, who by her labor, and industry, makes a hard shift to get her living: other things again, that set out an industrious indeed, but very wealthy woman; a woman, of great rank and quality. It may be that Solomon might of purpose, mix both; to show, that whether rich or poor, a woman may be virtuous and industrious in her kind, according to her temporal estate and condition. But withal, we must know, that divers things have reference to particular customs of those times, much different from ours; (at least in England,) as that which is written here of the spindle, or distaff. For certain it is, (as by divers testimonies of ancient authors may further appear.) that time was, when even kings' daughters were not ashamed of that employment.

*{her hands}* Or, *then her hands*.

**Verse 20.** *{She stretcheth}* Heb. *she spreadeth*.

*{yea, she reacheth forth her hands}* She not only gets to maintain her family, but also to give to the poor, which she does liberally, not with one hand alone, but with both, when need is.

**Verse 21.** *{scarlet}* Or, *double garments*.

*{her clothing is silk and purple}* This may be understood, as spoken hyperbolically, or metaphorically, for choice and good. Byssus (which may be translated, either silk, as here; or, fine linen, as in Luke: the Hebrew *שש* does comprehend both) & purpura, are an expression of excess, Luke 16:19. However, rich and honorable women may be clothed with silk and purple; it becomes them well enough; so they do not make that their chiefest ornament, and be proud of it, which does very ill become true nobility.

*{silk and purple}* Some learned men (see Bochartus, in his *Geographia Sacra*:) would have silk and purple, by a figure called *ἓν διὰ δυοῖν* (often mentioned in these observations) to be *byssum purpura tinctam*; that is, silk dyed with purple. I will not dispute it, because it is not material to the sense.

**Verse 22.** *{She maketh herself coverings of tapestry}* For beds.

**Verse 23.** *{Her husband is known}* Noted, because of his wife's good housewifery, or by the good clothes she makes him.

*{in the gates}* In the assemblies and places of judgment, Chapter 1:21. Ruth 4:1.

**Verse 24.** *{and selleth it}* She not only provides cloth for her family, but also to sell, and for a great price.

**Verse 25.** *{and she shall rejoice in time to come}* Or, *and she laugheth at the time to come*. That is, she is so well provided of all things necessary for her family, that she fears no want. See Job 5:21, 22.

**Verse 26.** *{She openeth her mouth with wisdom}* She teaches her children and servants, not only the fear of God, but also how to labor, and get a living, and also how to be provident, that they may be bountiful unto the poor in future times.

*{and in her tongue is the law of kindness}* *Et instructio gratiæ super lingua ejus*; according to some: that is, And instruction of grace is in, or upon her tongue. Instruction of grace, for, gracious instruction; according to the Hebrew idiotism, very frequent in all parts of Scripture.

**Verse 28.** *{Her children arise up}* To do reverence to her, when she comes in, and they speak highly of her, as well living as dead.

*{her}* Or, *her sayings*, as Ps. 105:15.

**Verse 29.** *{Many daughters have done virtuously}* This is the commendation her husband and children give her.

*{have done virtuously}* Or, *have gotten riches*.

*{excellest them all}* Heb. *ascendest above them all*.

**Verse 30.** *{a woman that feareth the Lord}* Before he spoke of the apparel of her body, now he declares the apparel of her mind, which is the fear of God, and that is her greatest ornament, 1 Pet. 3:4, 5. whether joined with beauty, or severed from it.

*{she shall be praised}* Heb. *shall praise herself*. That is, shall give just cause to all men to praise her, verse 31.

**Verse 31.** *{Give her of the fruit of her hands}* Confess her diligent labors, and commend her therefore.

*{let her own works praise her in the gates}* Forasmuch as the most honorable are clad in the apparel that she made, Acts 9:39. Or, let them speak in the public assemblies of her curious works, to her great praise, when she is dead and gone. She shall have praise, both in public and private.
